Both children retain the cumulative score prefix and only update the coordinate offset for future score increments. At cap-free accepting leaves and resident blocks, the query scans candidates and verifies true Euclidean distances.

At all scan points, candidates are verified in the original metric. Therefore approximate clustering can affect performance but not correctness.

\subsection{Certified covers and the role of k-means}

The experiments suggest that ordinary-space $k$-means routing is a strong
practical primitive, especially when points are assigned to several nearby
centers. The theoretical lesson is more specific: the useful object is not a
partition, but an overlapping certified filter cover with controlled query
stabbing number.

A certified ball filter is a triple $(P_i,o_i,R_i)$ with
\[
   P_i\subseteq P\cap \Ball(o_i,R_i).
\]
Its query region is the inflated ball
\[
   Q_i=\{q:\|q-o_i\|\le R_i+r\}.
\]
If $p\in P_i$ and $\|p-q\|\le r$, then $q\in Q_i$ by the triangle inequality.
Thus any cover in which every data point belongs to at least one stored filter
is automatically routing-correct if the query probes all filters whose inflated
balls contain $q$.

For a flat terminal cover the relevant load is
\[
   \operatorname{Stab}_{\rm scan}(q)
   =
   \sum_{i:q\in Q_i}|P_i|.
\]
For a recursive score-and-ball cover the theory suggests the power-stabbing
load
\[
   \operatorname{Stab}_F(q)
   =
   \sum_{i:q\in Q_i}|P_i|^{\rho}\Psi(R_i),
   \qquad
   \operatorname{Store}_F
   =
   \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i).
\]

\begin{lemma}[Certified cover compiler]\label{lem:certified-cover-compiler}
   Suppose a family of certified ball filters covers $P$ and satisfies, for
   every query $q$,
   \[
      \operatorname{Stab}_F(q)\le L,
      \qquad
      \operatorname{Store}_F\le S_0.
   \]
   If every active filter is searched by a terminal or recursive subroutine with
   query cost $O(|P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)\polylog n)$ and storage
   $O(|P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)\polylog n)$, then the resulting node is
   routing-correct for every near pair and contributes query work
   $O(L\polylog n)$ and storage $O(S_0\polylog n)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $\|p-q\|\le r$ and choose any filter $i$ containing $p$. Since
   $P_i\subseteq\Ball(o_i,R_i)$,
   \[
      \|q-o_i\|\le\|q-p\|+\|p-o_i\|\le r+R_i,
   \]
   so the query activates filter $i$. Therefore one active subproblem contains
   the true near point. The work and storage bounds are exactly the displayed
   stabbing and storage sums, multiplied by the polylogarithmic overhead of the
   subroutines. Final candidates are verified in the original metric.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Priced certified cover compiler]
   \label{lem:priced-cover-compiler}
   Let $\mathcal C$ be a retained family of certified filters.  Each filter
   $C\in\mathcal C$ has a point set $P_C\subseteq P$, an activation predicate
   $a_C(q)\in\{0,1\}$, and certified child prices $Q_C,S_C\ge0$.  The
   activation predicates are routing-safe:
   \[
      p\in P_C,\quad \|p-q\|\le r
      \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
      a_C(q)=1 .
   \]
   The price $Q_C$ certifies the worst-case query work of the retained
   subindex stored for $P_C$, and $S_C$ certifies its storage.  Suppose
   $\mathcal C$ covers $P$ and
   \[
      \sup_q\sum_{C\in\mathcal C}a_C(q)Q_C\le L,
      \qquad
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal C}S_C\le S .
   \]
   Then the node obtained by querying every active child is routing-correct and
   has certified query work $O(L+D(q))$ and storage $O(S+M)$, where $D(q)$ is
   the work needed to decode the active predicates and $M$ is the retained
   metadata size.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $\|p-q\|\le r$ and choose a retained filter $C$ with $p\in P_C$.  By
   routing-safety, $a_C(q)=1$, so the query invokes a certified subindex
   containing the near point.  Verification in the original metric accepts any
   valid candidate returned by that subindex.  The work after decoding is at
   most the sum of the certified prices of active children, and the storage is
   the sum of the retained child prices plus metadata.
\end{proof}

The closed-form power compiler above is the special case
\[
   Q_C=|P_C|^\rho\Psi(R_C)\polylog n,
   \qquad
   S_C=|P_C|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_C)\polylog n .
\]
The priced form is often the better offline object.  A child that is terminal,
pure, low-guard solved, or admits a stronger descendant certificate should be
charged by that actual certified price, not by the pessimistic surrogate
$F_4(|P_C|,R_C)$.  Thus the standard certified ball recursion, score-filter
recursion, terminal sampling, and residual scans are all columns in the same
priced cover verifier; $F_4$ is a convenient universal feasible price, not a
black-box theorem.

\begin{lemma}[Sampled priced cover compiler]
   \label{lem:sampled-priced-cover}
   Let $\mathcal C$ be a retained family of certified filters as in
   \cref{lem:priced-cover-compiler}, with certified child query prices $Q_C$
   and storage prices $S_C$.  For every query $q$, suppose the decoder can
   sample a child from a distribution $\pi_q$ supported on active children
   $(\pi_q(C)>0\Rightarrow a_C(q)=1)$ in work $D(q)$, and suppose
   \[
      \sum_C\pi_q(C)Q_C\le L .
   \]
   Give each child a certified success credit $\sigma_C\in[0,1]$: if
   $p\in P_C$ and $\|p-q\|\le r$, then one invocation of child $C$ returns a
   verified $cr$-neighbor with probability at least $\sigma_C$.  Define the
   route mass of a near pair by
   \[
      h_{p,q}
      =
      \sum_{C:p\in P_C}\pi_q(C)\sigma_C .
   \]
   If $h_{p,q}\ge h_0$ for every near pair not handled by an always-scanned
   residual block, then $B$ independent sampled child invocations, followed by
   verification in the original metric, fail with probability at most
   $\exp(-Bh_0)$.  Their expected query work is
   \[
      O\bigl(B(D(q)+L)\bigr),
   \]
   and the retained storage is $\sum_CS_C$ plus metadata.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Fix a near pair $(p,q)$ not handled by the residual block.  A single trial
   chooses an active child $C$ containing $p$ and succeeds inside that child
   with probability at least $\sigma_C$.  Summing over the choices of $C$ gives
   success probability at least $h_{p,q}\ge h_0$ for one trial.  Independent
   trials therefore miss with probability at most $(1-h_0)^B\le e^{-Bh_0}$.
   The expected child work in one trial is
   $\sum_C\pi_q(C)Q_C\le L$, and sampling the child costs $D(q)$; linearity of
   expectation gives the work bound.  Storage is independent of the number of
   sampled trials.
\end{proof}

Thus active children need not always be listed.  If a query can sample from a
distribution whose expected certified price is small and whose total mass on
children containing the true near point is inverse-polylogarithmic, then
polylogarithmically many trials replace an all-reporting active-power bound.
The deterministic priced compiler is the limiting case where every active child
is queried, while terminal pure sampling is the case $\sigma_C=1$ for pure
terminal alternatives and zero for impure ones.

\begin{lemma}[Existential sampled priced cover compiler]
   \label{lem:existential-sampled-priced-cover}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:sampled-priced-cover}, but fix a query
   \(q\) and let \(N(q)\) be the unresolved near set after the always-scanned
   residual block has been removed.  Suppose the sampled child distribution
   satisfies the same support and expected-price conditions, and define the
   near-set route mass
   \[
      h_q^{\exists}
      =
      \sum_{C:\ P_C\cap N(q)\ne\emptyset}\pi_q(C)\sigma_C .
   \]
   If \(N(q)\ne\emptyset\) and \(h_q^{\exists}\ge h_0\), then
   \(B\) independent sampled child invocations, followed by verification in the
   original metric, fail with probability at most \(\exp(-Bh_0)\).  Their
   expected query work is \(O(B(D(q)+L))\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   In one trial, whenever the sampled child \(C\) has
   \(P_C\cap N(q)\ne\emptyset\), choose any
   \(p\in P_C\cap N(q)\).  By the definition of \(N(q)\), \(\|p-q\|\le r\);
   by the child certificate, invoking \(C\) returns a verified
   \(cr\)-neighbor with probability at least \(\sigma_C\).  Summing over these
   useful children gives one-trial success probability at least
   \(h_q^{\exists}\).  Independent trials and linearity of expectation give
   the displayed bounds.
\end{proof}

The existential form is weaker than the per-witness route-mass condition in
\cref{lem:sampled-priced-cover}.  When \(N(q)\) is a singleton the two coincide,
but when several near points are present the sampler may spend all of its route
mass on whichever witness is easiest to route.  This is the correct ANN
objective; per-point route mass is only a sufficient robustness certificate.

\begin{lemma}[Prefix-list decoding compiler]
   \label{lem:prefix-list-decoder}
   Fix one query \(q\) and a finite rooted trie of route prefixes.  Each
   terminal route \(z\) has a certified output procedure with price \(Q_z\),
   a resident set \(P_z\subseteq P\), and a score \(S_q(z)\).  Each prefix
   \(u\) has a computable upper certificate \(U_q(u)\), with
   \(U_q(z)=S_q(z)\) on terminal routes, satisfying
   \[
      S_q(z)\le U_q(u)
      \qquad
      \text{for every terminal descendant }z\succeq u .
   \]
   Fix a threshold \(\Theta\).  Suppose the query decoder, when given any
   prefix \(u\), can enumerate exactly the children \(v\) with
   \(U_q(v)\ge\Theta\), and that the total decoder work is \(D(q)\) plus a
   constant per enumerated prefix.  If
   \[
      L_\Theta(q)=|\{u:U_q(u)\ge\Theta\}|,
   \]
   then the deterministic list decoder that starts at the root, keeps only
   prefixes with \(U_q\ge\Theta\), and invokes every terminal route it reaches,
   visits at most \(L_\Theta(q)\) prefixes and has query work
   \[
      O\left(D(q)+L_\Theta(q)+\sum_{z:\ S_q(z)\ge\Theta}Q_z\right).
   \]
   Moreover, suppose that for every unresolved near pair \((p,q)\) there is a
   terminal route \(z\) with \(p\in P_z\), \(S_q(z)\ge\Theta\), and whose
   output procedure is certified to find a verified \(cr\)-neighbor whenever
   invoked on \(q\).  Then this prefix-list decoder is routing-correct.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   A terminal route with \(S_q(z)\ge\Theta\) has every prefix \(u\preceq z\)
   satisfying \(U_q(u)\ge S_q(z)\ge\Theta\).  Therefore the child-enumeration
   rule never prunes any prefix of such a terminal route, and the decoder
   eventually reaches and invokes it.  Conversely, every prefix the decoder
   keeps has \(U_q\ge\Theta\), so the number of kept prefixes is at most
   \(L_\Theta(q)\).  The work bound follows from the assumed output-sensitive
   prefix enumeration and from invoking the terminal procedures reached by the
   decoder.  The final correctness statement is immediate from the existence
   of a certified terminal route containing the true near point; all reported
   candidates are verified in the original metric.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Priority prefix-list decoder]
   \label{lem:priority-prefix-list-decoder}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:prefix-list-decoder}.  Assume the route
   trie has finite out-degree and that the query can compute \(U_q(v)\) for
   every child \(v\) of a visited prefix \(u\).  Run the following stack
   decoder: keep a priority queue ordered by \(U_q\), initialized with the root
   if \(U_q(\varnothing)\ge\Theta\).  Repeatedly pop the largest-\(U_q\) prefix;
   discard it if \(U_q<\Theta\), invoke its output procedure if it is terminal,
   and otherwise compute its children and insert exactly those with
   \(U_q\ge\Theta\).

   Let
   \[
      \mathcal L_\Theta(q)=\{u:U_q(u)\ge\Theta\}
   \]
   be the set of live prefixes, and let \(G_q(u)\) be the work needed to
   generate and test the children of \(u\).  Then the decoder invokes every
   terminal route \(z\) with \(S_q(z)\ge\Theta\), invokes no terminal route
   outside \(\mathcal L_\Theta(q)\), and has query work
   \[
      O\!\left(
         D(q)
         +\sum_{u\in\mathcal L_\Theta(q)}(1+G_q(u))
         +|\mathcal L_\Theta(q)|\log(2+|\mathcal L_\Theta(q)|)
         +\sum_{z:\ S_q(z)\ge\Theta}Q_z
      \right).
   \]
   In particular, for bounded score branching and output-sensitive geometric
   child generation, the overhead beyond terminal invocations is
   \(D(q)+|\mathcal L_\Theta(q)|\log^{O(1)}|\mathcal L_\Theta(q)|\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   If a terminal route \(z\) has \(S_q(z)\ge\Theta\), then every prefix
   \(u\preceq z\) has \(U_q(u)\ge\Theta\), because \(U_q(u)\) upper-bounds all
   terminal descendants.  Thus no prefix on the path to \(z\) is discarded, and
   induction on depth shows that \(z\) is eventually inserted and popped.
   Conversely, the algorithm inserts only prefixes with \(U_q\ge\Theta\), so
   every popped prefix lies in \(\mathcal L_\Theta(q)\).  The generation cost
   is therefore the displayed sum over live internal prefixes, and the priority
   queue contributes a logarithmic factor per insertion and deletion.  Finally,
   terminal prices are paid exactly for the terminal routes reached by the
   decoder.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Exponential list-size certificate]
   \label{lem:exponential-list-size}
   Fix one query \(q\), a finite route trie, and upper certificates \(U_q(u)\).
   Suppose there are nonnegative level weights \(M_\ell(q)\) and a parameter
   \(\beta>0\) such that, for every level \(\ell\),
   \[
      \sum_{|u|=\ell}\exp(\beta U_q(u))\le M_\ell(q).
   \]
   Then for every threshold \(\Theta\),
   \[
      |\{u:U_q(u)\ge\Theta\}|
      \le
      e^{-\beta\Theta}\sum_\ell M_\ell(q).
   \]
   More generally, if every prefix \(u\) has a nonnegative generation weight
   \(g(u)\) and
   \[
      \sum_{|u|=\ell}g(u)\exp(\beta U_q(u))\le M_\ell^g(q),
   \]
   then
   \[
      \sum_{u:U_q(u)\ge\Theta}g(u)
      \le
      e^{-\beta\Theta}\sum_\ell M_\ell^g(q).
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The first bound is Markov's inequality applied deterministically:
   \[
      \mathbf 1\{U_q(u)\ge\Theta\}
      \le e^{-\beta\Theta}e^{\beta U_q(u)}.
   \]
   Sum this inequality over all prefixes and group by level.  The weighted
   statement is identical after multiplying by \(g(u)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Prefix-list resource certificate]
   \label{cor:prefix-list-resources}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:priority-prefix-list-decoder}.  Let
   \(g_q(u)\ge1+G_q(u)\) be a generation-cost weight.  Suppose that, for every
   query \(q\) in the promised domain,
   \[
      \sum_u g_q(u)\exp(\beta U_q(u))
      \le
      e^{\beta\Theta}L_{\rm pref},
      \qquad
      \sum_{z:\ S_q(z)\ge\Theta}Q_z\le L_{\rm term}.
   \]
   Then the priority prefix-list decoder is routing-correct under the near-route
   hypothesis of \cref{lem:prefix-list-decoder} and has query work
   \[
      O\!\left(
         D(q)
         +(L_{\rm pref}+1)\log(2+L_{\rm pref})
         +L_{\rm term}
      \right).
   \]
   Thus, at a local node with target price \(F_4(m,R)\), the deterministic
   list-decoding route has only polylogarithmic overhead if
   \[
      D(q),L_{\rm pref},L_{\rm term}
      \le F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply the weighted form of \cref{lem:exponential-list-size} with the single
   level sum replaced by the sum over all prefixes.  It gives
   \[
      \sum_{u:U_q(u)\ge\Theta}g_q(u)\le L_{\rm pref}.
   \]
   In particular, \(|\mathcal L_\Theta(q)|\le L_{\rm pref}\), since
   \(g_q(u)\ge1\).  Substitute these two bounds into
   \cref{lem:priority-prefix-list-decoder}.  Correctness is inherited from the
   same near-route hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Continuation-mass list certificate]
   \label{lem:continuation-list-certificate}
   Fix a finite route trie for one query \(q\).  Let \(H_q(u)>0\) be any
   positive prefix potential, let \(\mathsf{acc}(z)\in\{0,1\}\) mark terminal
   routes whose certified output procedure should be invoked, and define the
   terminal decoding score
   \[
      S_q(z)=
      \begin{cases}
         \displaystyle \min_{u\preceq z}\log H_q(u),
            & \mathsf{acc}(z)=1,\\[0.6em]
         -\infty,& \mathsf{acc}(z)=0 .
      \end{cases}
   \]
   For an internal prefix put \(U_q(u)=\log H_q(u)\), and for a terminal route
   put \(U_q(z)=S_q(z)\).  Let the threshold be \(\Theta=\log\tau\), where
   \(0<\tau\le1\).  If \(g_q(u)\ge1+G_q(u)\), then
   \[
      \sum_u g_q(u)H_q(u)\le L_H,
      \qquad
      \sum_{z:\ S_q(z)\ge\log\tau}Q_z\le L_T
   \]
   implies that the priority prefix-list decoder has query work
   \[
      O\!\left(
         D(q)
         +(\tau^{-1}L_H+1)\log(2+\tau^{-1}L_H)
         +L_T
      \right).
   \]
   It invokes exactly the accepting terminal routes whose every prefix has
   \(H_q(u)\ge\tau\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For any terminal descendant \(z\succeq u\), the definition of \(S_q(z)\)
   gives \(S_q(z)\le\log H_q(u)=U_q(u)\) when \(u\) is internal, and
   \(U_q(z)=S_q(z)\) when \(u=z\).  Thus the hypotheses of
   \cref{lem:prefix-list-decoder} hold.  With \(\beta=1\) and
   \(\Theta=\log\tau\),
   \[
      \sum_u g_q(u)\exp(U_q(u))
      \le
      \sum_u g_q(u)H_q(u)
      \le L_H ,
   \]
   so \cref{cor:prefix-list-resources} applies with
   \(L_{\rm pref}=\tau^{-1}L_H\) and \(L_{\rm term}=L_T\).  Finally,
   \(S_q(z)\ge\log\tau\) is exactly the conjunction of \(\mathsf{acc}(z)=1\)
   and \(H_q(u)\ge\tau\) for every prefix \(u\preceq z\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Continuation pruning as list decoding]
   \label{cor:continuation-as-list-decoding}
   In a budgeted continuation score block of depth \(k\), let
   \[
      H_q(u)=H_{|u|}(S_u(q))
   \]
   be the analytic continuation mass from \cref{lem:continuation-work}, mark a
   terminal route accepting when its leaf score is at least \(kt\), and take
   \(\tau=k^{-A}\).  The priority prefix-list decoder of
   \cref{lem:continuation-list-certificate} invokes exactly the accepting leaves
   that survive continuation-mass pruning.  For every fixed query,
   \[
      \E\sum_u H_q(u)
      \le
      k\,N^\rho\log^{O_{a,b}(1)}N,
   \]
   where \(N\) is the budget parameter of \cref{cor:budgeted-block}.  Hence the
   expected prefix-list work of the score block is
   \[
      N^\rho\log^{O_{a,b}(1)}N .
   \]
   The same statement holds for the adaptive predictable score schedules of
   \cref{lem:adaptive-continuation}, with \(H_q(u)\) equal to the exact
   conditional continuation mass for the remaining schedule.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The first claim is just the definition of the terminal decoding score in
   \cref{lem:continuation-list-certificate}: a leaf has score at least
   \(\log\tau\) exactly when it accepts and every prefix has continuation mass
   at least \(\tau\).

   At a fixed level \(\ell\), summing \(H_\ell(S_u(q))\) over all prefixes
   gives the conditional expected number of accepting descendant leaves from
   that frontier.  Taking expectation over the realized score tree and using
   the tower property gives the unconditional expected number of accepting
   leaves in the block, which is \(N^\rho\log^{O_{a,b}(1)}N\) by
   \cref{cor:budgeted-block}.  Summing over the \(k\) levels gives the displayed
   bound.  Since \(\tau^{-1}=k^A=\log^{O_{a,b}(1)}N\), the work bound follows
   from \cref{lem:continuation-list-certificate}.  The adaptive case is
   identical because its continuation mass is the exact conditional expected
   number of accepting descendants for the remaining predictable schedule, as
   used in \cref{lem:adaptive-continuation}.
\end{proof}

This is the literal list-decoding analogue of the score tree.  The retained
tree defines an implicit Euclidean code or lattice of route leaves, the query is
the received word, and the decoder must output a short list of plausible route
leaves before final metric verification.  The sampled-route compiler above is a
soft randomized version of this principle; \cref{lem:prefix-list-decoder} and
\cref{lem:priority-prefix-list-decoder} are deterministic versions.  The
priority form is the direct analogue of lattice sphere enumeration: keep a
stack of partial routes whose admissible remaining score can still reach the
decoding threshold, and stop when the stack certificate drops below threshold.
In this interpretation the lattice is the lifted route lattice, not a
black-box lattice over the resident data points.  A branch contributes both a
query reliability increment and a certified volume or budget price, so the
decoded metric is the Fano-biased score rather than raw Euclidean distance to a
center.  Thus an existing code or lattice decoder is useful only insofar as it
exposes three certified objects: an admissible prefix upper score, a compact
transcript or state-DAG envelope for the visited prefixes, and an exact final
filter for the reported terminal buckets.  Barnes--Wall, Construction-A/D,
polar, trellis, GMD/Chase, and ordered-statistics decoders are different ways
to implement this same reporter interface; none of them should be invoked as a
black-box nearest-center theorem.
\Cref{lem:exponential-list-size} and \cref{cor:prefix-list-resources} give the
corresponding proof target.  Instead of storing activation-pattern tables, prove
a moment or potential bound on \(\sum g_q(u)\exp(\beta U_q(u))\) over prefixes;
thresholding then gives a short list and a query-work bound automatically.  The
\(\beta=1\) special case is not new machinery:
\cref{lem:continuation-list-certificate} and
\cref{cor:continuation-as-list-decoding} show that ordinary continuation-mass
pruning is already a stack/list decoder whose certificate is
\(\sum_u H_q(u)\).  The open deterministic route is to find similarly tight
admissible prefix potentials after geometric recentering and budget-thinned
child generation.  The useful coding-theory lesson from lattice and code list
decoding is therefore not a black-box decoder, but the separation of three
tasks: prove a
combinatorial list-size or route-cut bound, design an output-sensitive prefix
enumerator for the structured tree, and use reliability or posterior scores to
prune the list.  This mirrors Barnes--Wall lattice list decoding,
finite-trellis lattice sequential decoding, lattice sphere enumeration,
successive-cancellation list decoding of polar codes, and soft-decision
Reed--Solomon decoding~\cite{GP17,MP20,TVZ97,FP85,SE94,GNR10,TV12,KV03}.
The additional lesson from pruned lattice enumeration is that the pruning
profile should be an optimized certificate rather than a fixed level schedule:
one can spend more search budget on prefixes that have high near-route
reliability and prune elsewhere, as long as the resulting profile has certified
expected work.  \Cref{lem:pruned-budget-thinning} records the corresponding
self-contained budget rule for the score tree.

Sequential decoding gives a sharper way to phrase the same rule.  The classical
Fano and stack metrics do not rank a partial codeword only by its raw
likelihood; they subtract a rate or volume bias so that the expected number of
competing prefixes is controlled below the computational cutoff
rate~\cite{WR61,Fano63,Jel69}.  In the present notation the analogue is to
decode by a biased reliability score
\[
   \text{query score}
   \;-\;
   \text{certified branch price}.
\]
The branch price may be the canonical continuation mass, the budget-stable
Bellman price, or the water-filled allocation toll.  The prefix moment
\(\sum g_q(u)\exp(\beta U_q(u))\) is then the deterministic version of the
cutoff-rate calculation: it bounds the stack size before any metric
verification is performed.  Thus the code/lattice replacement for the score
tree should not be a nearest-raw-center decoder.  It should be a Fano-biased
list decoder whose bias is chosen by the same transition-aware water-filling
certificate used for budget thinning.  This explains why lattice extreme
pruning and the transition-aware allocation normal form are the same object at
different levels of abstraction.

\begin{lemma}[Fano-biased prefix-list compiler]
   \label{lem:fano-biased-prefix-list}
   Fix one query \(q\) and a finite route trie.  Each terminal route \(z\) has
   a raw reliability score \(A_q(z)\), terminal price \(Q_z\), resident set
   \(P_z\), and certified output procedure.  Each prefix \(u\) has a computable
   raw upper certificate \(\overline A_q(u)\), with
   \[
      A_q(z)\le \overline A_q(u)
      \qquad(z\succeq u).
   \]
   Let every edge \(e\) have a nonnegative decodable branch price
   \(\pi_q(e)\), and put
   \[
      \Pi_q(u)=\sum_{e\preceq u}\pi_q(e).
   \]
   Define the Fano-biased terminal and prefix scores by
   \[
      S_q(z)=A_q(z)-\Pi_q(z),
      \qquad
      U_q(u)=\overline A_q(u)-\Pi_q(u)
   \]
   on internal prefixes, and \(U_q(z)=S_q(z)\) on terminals.  Then \(U_q\) is
   an admissible upper certificate for \(S_q\).

   Suppose children with \(U_q\ge\Theta\) can be generated as in
   \cref{lem:priority-prefix-list-decoder}.  For \(\beta>0\), define
   \[
      L_{\rm Fano}
      =
      e^{-\beta\Theta}
      \sum_u g_q(u)
      \exp\!\left(\beta(\overline A_q(u)-\Pi_q(u))\right),
   \]
   and
   \[
      T_{\rm Fano}
      =
      e^{-\beta\Theta}
      \sum_z Q_z
      \exp\!\left(\beta(A_q(z)-\Pi_q(z))\right).
   \]
   Then the priority decoder invokes every terminal with
   \(A_q(z)-\Pi_q(z)\ge\Theta\), and its query work is
   \[
      O\!\left(
         D(q)
         +(L_{\rm Fano}+1)\log(2+L_{\rm Fano})
         +T_{\rm Fano}
      \right).
   \]

   Moreover, let \(\mathcal G\) be a set of terminal routes whose certified
   output procedures are valid for the current unresolved near-pair objective.
   If a route law \(\Lambda\) on \(\mathcal G\) satisfies
   \[
      \Lambda\{z:\ A_q(z)\ge\Theta+B\}\ge h
      \quad\text{and}\quad
      \E[\Pi_q(V)\mid A_q(V)\ge\Theta+B]\le B/2,
   \]
   then the Fano decoder lists a valid route whenever the conditional event has
   positive probability; quantitatively,
   \[
      \Lambda\{z\in\mathcal G:\ A_q(z)-\Pi_q(z)\ge\Theta\}
      \ge h/2 .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   If \(z\succeq u\), then \(\Pi_q(z)\ge\Pi_q(u)\) because the branch prices
   are nonnegative.  Hence
   \[
      S_q(z)
      =
      A_q(z)-\Pi_q(z)
      \le
      \overline A_q(u)-\Pi_q(u)
      =
      U_q(u),
   \]
   so \(U_q\) is admissible.  The work bound is
   \cref{cor:prefix-list-resources} applied to this \(U_q\), using the
   exponential majorants
   \[
      \mathbf 1\{U_q(u)\ge\Theta\}
      \le e^{-\beta\Theta}e^{\beta U_q(u)}
   \]
   for prefixes and the analogous terminal bound for
   \(\sum_{z:S_q(z)\ge\Theta}Q_z\).

   For the last claim, condition on
   \(G_0=\{A_q(V)\ge\Theta+B\}\).  Markov's inequality gives
   \[
      \Pr[\Pi_q(V)>B\mid G_0]\le\frac12.
   \]
   Therefore \(G_0\cap\{\Pi_q(V)\le B\}\) has \(\Lambda\)-mass at least
   \(h/2\).  Every route in this intersection satisfies
   \(A_q(z)-\Pi_q(z)\ge\Theta\), so the decoder lists it.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Water-filled Fano bias]
   \label{cor:waterfilled-fano-bias}
   Consider a set of required route edges with normalized recursive charges
   \(a_e\), a decodable allocation \(y_e\), and, optionally, a decodable
   transition envelope \(\lambda_e\).  Use the Fano branch prices
   \[
      \pi_q(e)=\log_+\frac{a_e}{y_e}
      \quad\text{or, in the transition-aware form,}\quad
      \pi_q(e)=\log_+\frac{\lambda_ea_e}{y_e}.
   \]
   The zero-charge and zero-allocation conventions are the same as in
   \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation}.
   Let \(A_q,\overline A_q\) be any raw route score and raw admissible upper
   score.  If the unpriced prefix moment satisfies
   \[
      \sum_u g_q(u)e^{\beta\overline A_q(u)}
      \le e^{\beta\Theta_0}F\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   then the Fano-biased decoder run at threshold \(\Theta_0-B\) has prefix work
   at most
   \[
      e^{\beta B}F\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   up to the priority-queue logarithm.  The same statement holds for terminal
   prices \(Q_z\) whenever the analogous unpriced terminal exponential moment is
   bounded by \(e^{\beta\Theta_0}F\log^{O_c(1)}m\).

   Consequently, a water-filled allocation defect \(B=O_c(\log\log m)\) costs
   only a polylogarithmic factor in deterministic stack-decoding work.  In the
   transition-aware case this is precisely the defect
   \(\sum_e\ell_e\log_+(\lambda_ea_e/y_e)\) from
   \cref{cor:transition-waterfilled-allocation}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Since all branch prices are nonnegative,
   \[
      e^{\beta(\overline A_q(u)-\Pi_q(u))}
      \le e^{\beta\overline A_q(u)}.
   \]
   Substituting \(\Theta=\Theta_0-B\) in
   \cref{lem:fano-biased-prefix-list} gives
   \[
      L_{\rm Fano}
      \le
      e^{-\beta(\Theta_0-B)}
      \sum_u g_q(u)e^{\beta\overline A_q(u)}
      \le
      e^{\beta B}F\log^{O_c(1)}m.
   \]
   The terminal-price estimate is identical.  The final sentence is the
   definition of the allocation and transition-aware defects, combined with
   \cref{lem:fano-biased-prefix-list}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Robust water-filled Fano compiler]
   \label{cor:robust-fano-allocation-compiler}
   Fix one query state and a finite family of witness route laws
   \(\{\Lambda_i\}_{i\in I}\) on the same route trie.  For each witness
   \(i\), let \(\mathcal G_i\) be terminal routes whose output procedures are
   valid for the corresponding unresolved near objective.  Suppose
   \(\Lambda_i\) is supported on \(\mathcal G_i\), and suppose every route in
   this support has raw reliability margin
   \[
      A_q(z)\ge\Theta+2B .
   \]
   Let a single decodable allocation \(y\) define nonnegative Fano branch
   prices
   \[
      \pi_q(e)=\log_+\frac{a_e}{y_e}
      \quad\text{or}\quad
      \pi_q(e)=\log_+\frac{\bar\lambda_ea_e}{y_e}
   \]
   for a decodable transition envelope \(\bar\lambda_e\), with the usual
   zero-charge conventions.  Assume this one allocation has expected price at
   most \(B\) for every witness:
   \[
      \E_{V\sim\Lambda_i}\sum_{e\preceq V}\pi_q(e)\le B
      \qquad(i\in I).
   \]
   Finally assume that the unpriced prefix and terminal moments satisfy
   \[
      \sum_u g_q(u)e^{\beta\overline A_q(u)}
      \le
      e^{\beta(\Theta+2B)}F\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_z Q_z e^{\beta A_q(z)}
      \le
      e^{\beta(\Theta+2B)}F\log^{O_c(1)}m.
   \]
   Then the Fano-biased priority decoder at threshold \(\Theta\) has query work
   \[
      e^{2\beta B}F\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   up to the priority-queue logarithm, and for every witness \(i\) the listed
   valid routes have \(\Lambda_i\)-mass at least \(1/2\).  In particular, if
   \(B=O_c(\log\log m)\) and \(\beta=O_c(1)\), the deterministic biased
   decoder has only polylogarithmic overhead over \(F\).

   The price hypothesis is supplied by
   \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax} whenever the corresponding robust
   water-filled defect is at most \(B\); equivalently it may be certified by
   the robust tail, moment, raw-log, raw-moment, or transition-envelope
   certificates developed later in this section.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The work estimate follows from
   \cref{lem:fano-biased-prefix-list} and the fact that subtracting the
   nonnegative branch price can only reduce the two displayed exponential
   moments:
   \[
      L_{\rm Fano},T_{\rm Fano}
      \le
      e^{-\beta\Theta}
      e^{\beta(\Theta+2B)}
      F\log^{O_c(1)}m
      =
      e^{2\beta B}F\log^{O_c(1)}m.
   \]

   Fix a witness \(i\).  Markov's inequality and the expected-price hypothesis
   give
   \[
      \Lambda_i\left\{
         z:\sum_{e\preceq z}\pi_q(e)\le 2B
      \right\}\ge\frac12.
   \]
   On this event, the raw margin assumption gives
   \[
      A_q(z)-\sum_{e\preceq z}\pi_q(e)\ge\Theta .
   \]
   Since \(\Lambda_i\) is supported on valid terminal routes, the Fano decoder
   lists valid routes with \(\Lambda_i\)-mass at least \(1/2\).

   The last sentence is just the minimax and certificate chain:
   \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax} produces one allocation whose defect
   is at most \(B\) against every witness occupation profile, and the
   subsequent robust certificates are sufficient conditions for that minimax
   defect bound.  In the transition-envelope case,
   \(\bar\lambda_e\) upper-bounds the witness transition probabilities, so the
   envelope branch price dominates each witness's transition-aware toll.
\end{proof}

The closest existing template is the product-code locality-sensitive filtering
construction of Becker, Ducas, Gama, and Laarhoven~\cite{BDGL16}.  They replace
independent spherical-cap filters by an enormous structured product family and
find the relevant filters by a decoding oracle based on lattice-enumeration
ideas.  In our notation this suggests a concrete alternative to \(k\)-means or
Lloyd routing.  After local centering and a logarithmic-dimensional projection,
split the coordinates into blocks, choose a spherical or Construction-A code
\(\mathcal C_j\) in each block, and let a route
\(g=(c_1,\ldots,c_h)\in\prod_j\mathcal C_j\) have additive reliability score
\[
   S_g(x)=\sum_{j=1}^h \phi_j(x_j,c_j).
\]
The query decoder is then a stack decoder on partial codewords, using an
admissible upper bound
\[
   U_q(c_1,\ldots,c_\ell)
   =
   \sum_{j\le\ell}\phi_j(q_j,c_j)
   +R_{\ell+1:h}(q)
\]
for the best remaining suffix score, and a pruning profile supplied by
\cref{lem:pruned-budget-thinning} or the water-filled allocation below.  This is
literally a lattice decoder: the score tree is replaced by a product lattice or
code, and query work is the size of the decoded prefix list.  The proof target
is therefore not Lloyd convergence.  It is to prove, for such a structured
panel, the same random-like cap spectrum and the prefix moment bound
\[
   \sum_u g_q(u)\exp(\beta U_q(u))
   \le e^{\beta\Theta}m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
\]
while ensuring that a near pair has an inverse-polylogarithmic decoded common
route.  If this succeeds, the product panel would be both a practical
replacement for \(k\)-means routing and a self-contained proof object: the
large filter family is implicit, build time may optimize or reject panels, and
query time pays only for output-sensitive list decoding.

\begin{lemma}[Fano-biased scores are lifted Euclidean decoding metrics]
   \label{lem:fano-lifted-euclidean-decoding}
   Let a product panel have Euclidean block symbols
   \(\mathcal C_j\subseteq\mathbb R^{b_j}\), projected query blocks \(q_j\),
   and nonnegative query-independent branch prices
   \(\pi_j:\mathcal C_j\to\mathbb R_{\ge0}\).  Put
   \[
      \phi_j(q,c)=\ip{q_j}{c}-\frac12\|c\|^2,\qquad
      S_q(c_1,\ldots,c_h)=\sum_{j=1}^h\phi_j(q,c_j),
   \]
   and \(\Pi(c_1,\ldots,c_h)=\sum_j\pi_j(c_j)\).  Define the lifted symbol
   and lifted query block by
   \[
      \widehat c_j=(c_j,\sqrt{2\pi_j(c_j)})\in\mathbb R^{b_j+1},
      \qquad
      \widehat q_j=(q_j,0).
   \]
   Then every route \(c=(c_1,\ldots,c_h)\) satisfies the exact identity
   \[
      S_q(c)-\Pi(c)
      =
      \frac12\sum_{j=1}^h\|q_j\|^2
      -
      \frac12\sum_{j=1}^h\|\widehat q_j-\widehat c_j\|^2.
      \tag{LD}
   \]
   Consequently, for every threshold \(\Theta\), listing all routes with
   Fano-biased score \(S_q(c)-\Pi(c)\ge\Theta\) is exactly list decoding the
   lifted product code
   \[
      \widehat{\mathcal C}
      =
      \prod_{j=1}^h
      \{(c,\sqrt{2\pi_j(c)}):c\in\mathcal C_j\}
   \]
   around the received word \(\widehat q=(\widehat q_1,\ldots,\widehat q_h)\)
   to squared radius \(\sum_j\|q_j\|^2-2\Theta\).  Moreover, sampling routes
   with probability proportional to
   \(\exp(\beta(S_q(c)-\Pi(c)))\) is the discrete Gaussian law on the same
   lifted code with center \(\widehat q\) and inverse temperature \(\beta/2\),
   up to the query-only normalizing factor
   \(\exp((\beta/2)\sum_j\|q_j\|^2)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For each block,
   \[
      \frac12\|q_j\|^2-\frac12\|\widehat q_j-\widehat c_j\|^2
      =
      \frac12\|q_j\|^2
      -
      \frac12\left(\|q_j-c_j\|^2+2\pi_j(c_j)\right)
      =
      \ip{q_j}{c_j}-\frac12\|c_j\|^2-\pi_j(c_j).
   \]
   Summing over \(j\) gives \((LD)\).  The threshold and sampling statements
   are immediate because the omitted factor depends only on the query.
\end{proof}

\Cref{lem:fano-lifted-euclidean-decoding} makes the lattice interpretation
literal.  Continuation mass, rate penalties, and extreme-pruning prices are not
decorations on top of nearest-center search; they are extra coordinates in the
effective Euclidean decoding metric.  If the prices are fixed by the retained
panel, the score tree has been replaced by an ordinary lifted code or lattice
list decoder.  If the pruning profile is query-dependent, the same identity is
still the correct stack metric, but the lifted metric is part of the query
algorithm rather than a fixed retained lattice.  This is why a useful
code/lattice alternative should support either threshold enumeration in the
lifted metric or discrete-Gaussian sampling from it; a Lloyd or nearest-raw-center
partition ignores the bias coordinates that certify the list size.

\begin{lemma}[Additive product-panel decoder]
   \label{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}
   Fix one query \(q\).  Let
   \(\mathcal C=\mathcal C_1\times\cdots\times\mathcal C_h\) be a finite
   product code.  A terminal route \(c=(c_1,\ldots,c_h)\) has query score
   \[
      S_q(c)=\sum_{j=1}^h\phi_j(q,c_j)
   \]
   and certified terminal price \(Q_c\ge0\).  For each suffix let
   \(R_{\ell+1}(q)\), \(0\le\ell\le h\), satisfy \(R_{h+1}(q)=0\) and
   \[
      \sum_{j=\ell+1}^h\phi_j(q,c_j)\le R_{\ell+1}(q)
      \qquad
      \text{for every suffix }(c_{\ell+1},\ldots,c_h).
   \]
   For a prefix \(u=(c_1,\ldots,c_\ell)\), put
   \[
      U_q(u)=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\phi_j(q,c_j)+R_{\ell+1}(q).
   \]
   Suppose that block \(j\) has a threshold reporter which, for any threshold
   \(\theta\), lists exactly the symbols
   \[
      \{c\in\mathcal C_j:\phi_j(q,c)\ge\theta\}
   \]
   in work \(D_j(q)\) plus a constant per listed symbol.  Run the priority
   prefix-list decoder with threshold \(\Theta\), using the reporter at a live
   prefix \(u=(c_1,\ldots,c_\ell)\) to generate precisely the children
   satisfying
   \[
      \phi_{\ell+1}(q,c)\ge
      \Theta-\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\phi_j(q,c_j)-R_{\ell+2}(q).
   \]
   For \(\beta>0\), define
   \[
      Z_j(q)=\sum_{c\in\mathcal C_j}\exp(\beta\phi_j(q,c)),
      \qquad
      Z_{\le\ell}(q)=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Z_j(q),
   \]
   with \(Z_{\le0}(q)=1\), and
   \[
      P_\beta(q)=
      \sum_{\ell=0}^{h}
      a_\ell(q)\exp(\beta R_{\ell+1}(q))Z_{\le\ell}(q),
   \]
   where \(a_h(q)=1\) and
   \(a_\ell(q)=1+D_{\ell+1}(q)\) for \(0\le\ell<h\).  Also put
   \[
      T_\beta(q)=
      \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}Q_c\exp(\beta S_q(c)).
   \]
   Then the decoder visits every terminal \(c\) with \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta\), and
   its query work is
   \[
      O\!\left(
         (L_\beta(q)+1)\log(2+L_\beta(q))
         +e^{-\beta\Theta}T_\beta(q)
      \right),
      \qquad
      L_\beta(q)=e^{-\beta\Theta}P_\beta(q).
   \]
   Consequently the decoder has local target work
   \(F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m\) whenever
   \[
      P_\beta(q),T_\beta(q)
      \le
      e^{\beta\Theta}F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The suffix condition makes \(U_q\) admissible: every terminal descendant
   \(z\succeq u\) has \(S_q(z)\le U_q(u)\).  The displayed threshold for the
   next symbol is exactly the inequality \(U_q(u,c)\ge\Theta\).  Thus the block
   reporters implement the child-generation rule required by
   \cref{lem:priority-prefix-list-decoder}.

   At level \(\ell\),
   \[
      \sum_{|u|=\ell}\exp(\beta U_q(u))
      =
      \exp(\beta R_{\ell+1}(q))
      \prod_{j=1}^{\ell}
      \sum_{c\in\mathcal C_j}\exp(\beta\phi_j(q,c))
      =
      \exp(\beta R_{\ell+1}(q))Z_{\le\ell}(q).
   \]
   Multiplying by the level weight \(a_\ell(q)\), summing over levels, and
   applying \cref{lem:exponential-list-size} gives
   \[
      \sum_{u:U_q(u)\ge\Theta}a_{|u|}(q)
      \le e^{-\beta\Theta}P_\beta(q)=L_\beta(q).
   \]
   This bounds the base reporter work and the number of live prefixes.  The
   constant per listed child is charged to the child prefix itself, except for
   the root initialization, which is absorbed by the \(+1\).  The priority
   queue contributes the logarithmic factor.

   Finally,
   \[
      \sum_{c:S_q(c)\ge\Theta}Q_c
      \le
      e^{-\beta\Theta}
      \sum_c Q_c\exp(\beta S_q(c))
      =
      e^{-\beta\Theta}T_\beta(q),
   \]
   so substituting the prefix and terminal bounds into
   \cref{lem:priority-prefix-list-decoder} proves the work estimate.  The last
   statement is the displayed estimate with the local target budget substituted.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Superset product-panel decoder]
   \label{lem:superset-product-panel-decoder}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}, but
   replace the exact block reporters by certified superset reporters.  Thus,
   for every block \(j\) and threshold \(\theta\), the reporter returns a
   finite candidate set \(\widehat L_j(q,\theta)\subseteq\mathcal C_j\)
   containing every \(c\in\mathcal C_j\) with
   \(\phi_j(q,c)\ge\theta\).  The algorithm evaluates \(\phi_j(q,c)\) on every
   returned candidate and pushes only the children satisfying the exact
   threshold.  Suppose the reporter work at threshold \(\theta\), including
   candidate generation and these exact block-score evaluations, is at most
   \[
      A_j(q)+B_j(q)e^{-\beta\theta},
      \tag{SR1}
   \]
   where \(A_j(q),B_j(q)\ge0\).  Define \(Z_j(q)\), \(Z_{\le\ell}(q)\), and
   \(T_\beta(q)\) as in \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}, and put
   \[
      \widehat P_\beta(q)
      =
      \sum_{\ell=0}^{h}
      e^{\beta R_{\ell+1}(q)}Z_{\le\ell}(q)
      +
      \sum_{\ell=0}^{h-1}
      A_{\ell+1}(q)e^{\beta R_{\ell+1}(q)}Z_{\le\ell}(q)
   \]
   \[
      \hspace{2.2cm}
      +
      \sum_{\ell=0}^{h-1}
      B_{\ell+1}(q)e^{\beta R_{\ell+2}(q)}Z_{\le\ell}(q).
   \]
   Then the superset priority decoder visits every terminal
   \(c\) with \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta\), and its query work is
   \[
      O\!\left(
         (\widehat L_\beta(q)+1)\log(2+\widehat L_\beta(q))
         +
         e^{-\beta\Theta}T_\beta(q)
      \right),
      \qquad
      \widehat L_\beta(q)=e^{-\beta\Theta}\widehat P_\beta(q).
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Correctness is unchanged from
   \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}.  If a terminal descendant of a
   live prefix can still reach threshold, then its next block symbol satisfies
   the displayed child threshold and is therefore included in the superset
   candidate list.  Since the algorithm evaluates the exact block score before
   pushing a child, the priority queue contains exactly the same live children
   as the exact-reporter decoder, possibly after doing extra candidate work.

   The number of live prefixes is bounded as before:
   \[
      \sum_{|u|=\ell,\ U_q(u)\ge\Theta}1
      \le
      e^{-\beta\Theta}
      e^{\beta R_{\ell+1}(q)}Z_{\le\ell}(q).
   \]
   The fixed part \(A_{\ell+1}(q)\) of the block reporter is paid once for
   every live prefix at level \(\ell\), giving the second sum in
   \(\widehat P_\beta\).

   For the threshold-dependent part, a live prefix
   \(u=(c_1,\ldots,c_\ell)\) invokes block \(\ell+1\) at threshold
   \[
      \theta(u)=
      \Theta-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_i(q,c_i)-R_{\ell+2}(q).
   \]
   By \((SR1)\), its extra candidate work is at most
   \[
      B_{\ell+1}(q)
      e^{-\beta\Theta}
      \exp\!\left(\beta\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_i(q,c_i)\right)
      e^{\beta R_{\ell+2}(q)}.
   \]
   Summing this over all live prefixes and then upper-bounding by the sum over
   all prefixes of length \(\ell\) gives
   \[
      e^{-\beta\Theta}
      B_{\ell+1}(q)e^{\beta R_{\ell+2}(q)}Z_{\le\ell}(q),
   \]
   which is the third sum.  The priority-queue and terminal-price terms are
   bounded exactly as in \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Product-panel spectrum compiler]
   \label{prop:product-panel-spectrum-compiler}
   Fix a terminal localized ANN subproblem of size \(m\), approximation \(c\),
   and target exponent \(\rho=\rho_c\).  Let \(L_m=\log^{A_c}m\).  A random
   product panel consists of a product code
   \(\mathcal C=\prod_{j=1}^h\mathcal C_j\), data block scores
   \(\psi_j(x,c_j)\), query block scores \(\phi_j(q,c_j)\), and thresholds
   \(\theta_P,\theta_Q\).  Write
   \[
      S_P(x,c)=\sum_{j=1}^h\psi_j(x,c_j),
      \qquad
      S_Q(q,c)=\sum_{j=1}^h\phi_j(q,c_j),
   \]
   and store \(x\) in bucket \(c\) when \(S_P(x,c)\ge\theta_P\).  On query
   \(q\), list buckets with \(S_Q(q,c)\ge\theta_Q\) using the product decoder
   of \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}.  Suppose that, for every
   promised query \(q\), the one-panel expected decoder work is
   \[
      \E_{\mathcal G}W_{\rm dec}(q;\mathcal G)
      \le dL_m+m^\rho L_m,
      \tag{PP1}
   \]
   where \(W_{\rm dec}\) is the work bound certified by
   \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder} with terminal prices \(Q_c=0\).
   Suppose further that there are \(\alpha,\beta>0\) such that
   \[
      \E_{\mathcal G}
      \sum_{x\in P}
      e^{-\alpha\theta_P}
      \prod_{j=1}^h
      \sum_{a\in\mathcal C_j}e^{\alpha\psi_j(x,a)}
      \le m^{1+\rho}L_m,
      \tag{PP2}
   \]
   and, for every promised query \(q\),
   \[
      \E_{\mathcal G}
      \sum_{\substack{x\in P\\ \|x-q\|>cr}}
      e^{-\alpha\theta_P-\beta\theta_Q}
      \prod_{j=1}^h
      \sum_{a\in\mathcal C_j}
      e^{\alpha\psi_j(x,a)+\beta\phi_j(q,a)}
      \le m^\rho L_m .
      \tag{PP3}
   \]
   Finally assume that every near pair \((p,q)\), \(\|p-q\|\le r\), satisfies
   the one-panel hit condition
   \[
      \Pr_{\mathcal G}\!\left[
         \exists c\in\mathcal C:
         S_P(p,c)\ge\theta_P,\quad S_Q(q,c)\ge\theta_Q
      \right]\ge L_m^{-1}.
      \tag{PP4}
   \]
   Then \(O(L_m)\) independent product panels give a terminal ANN data
   structure with query work
   \[
      d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   and space
   \[
      dm+m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   For one panel, the query decoder lists every bucket with
   \(S_Q(q,c)\ge\theta_Q\) by \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}, and
   \((PP1)\) gives the expected decoder work.

   The storage bound follows from the elementary exponential majorant
   \[
      \mathbf 1\{S_P(x,c)\ge\theta_P\}
      \le e^{\alpha(S_P(x,c)-\theta_P)} .
   \]
   Summing over \(c=(c_1,\ldots,c_h)\) factors the product:
   \[
      \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}e^{\alpha S_P(x,c)}
      =
      \prod_{j=1}^h\sum_{a\in\mathcal C_j}e^{\alpha\psi_j(x,a)}.
   \]
   Therefore \((PP2)\) upper-bounds the expected number of stored point-bucket
   incidences in one panel by \(m^{1+\rho}L_m\).

   Similarly, the number of invalid point-bucket incidences scanned by query
   \(q\) is at most
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{x\in P\\ \|x-q\|>cr}}
      \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}
      \mathbf 1\{S_P(x,c)\ge\theta_P\}
      \mathbf 1\{S_Q(q,c)\ge\theta_Q\}.
   \]
   Using
   \[
      \mathbf 1\{S_P(x,c)\ge\theta_P\}
      \mathbf 1\{S_Q(q,c)\ge\theta_Q\}
      \le
      e^{\alpha(S_P(x,c)-\theta_P)+
         \beta(S_Q(q,c)-\theta_Q)}
   \]
   and the product factorization
   \[
      \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}e^{\alpha S_P(x,c)+\beta S_Q(q,c)}
      =
      \prod_{j=1}^h
      \sum_{a\in\mathcal C_j}
      e^{\alpha\psi_j(x,a)+\beta\phi_j(q,a)}
   \]
   gives \((PP3)\), so the one-panel expected invalid scan work is
   \(m^\rho L_m\).

   Store \(B=\lceil C L_m\rceil\) independent panels.  If \(p\) is a true near
   neighbor of \(q\), \((PP4)\) implies that all panels miss a common bucket
   containing \(p\) with probability at most
   \[
      (1-L_m^{-1})^B\le e^{-C}.
   \]
   Taking \(C\) large gives constant success probability.  The expected decoder
   work, invalid scan work, and storage are multiplied by \(B=\log^{O_c(1)}m\),
   giving the displayed resources.  The query verifies every scanned point in
   the original metric and stops after finding a \(cr\)-neighbor, so valid
   candidates cannot cause false positives.  As in
   \cref{prop:flat-spherical-code-terminal}, the capped-expectation convention
   converts these expectations to capped query and space resources after a
   constant-factor loss in success probability.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Block tensorization for product panels]
   \label{lem:product-panel-tensorization}
   Work in the product-panel setting of
   \cref{prop:product-panel-spectrum-compiler}.  Suppose the thresholds split
   as
   \[
      \theta_P=\sum_{j=1}^h\theta_{P,j},
      \qquad
      \theta_Q=\sum_{j=1}^h\theta_{Q,j},
   \]
   and for each block choose a suffix bound
   \(M_j(q)\ge\max_{a\in\mathcal C_j}\phi_j(q,a)\).  Use
   \(R_{\ell+1}(q)=\sum_{j=\ell+1}^hM_j(q)\) in
   \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}.  Define
   \[
      Z_j^Q(q)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal C_j}e^{\beta\phi_j(q,a)},
      \qquad
      Z_j^P(x)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal C_j}e^{\alpha\psi_j(x,a)},
   \]
   and
   \[
      Z_j^{PQ}(x,q)=
      \sum_{a\in\mathcal C_j}
      e^{\alpha\psi_j(x,a)+\beta\phi_j(q,a)} .
   \]
   Then the query-list partition certificate factors as
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      e^{-\beta\theta_Q}P_\beta(q)
      &=
      \sum_{\ell=0}^{h}
      a_\ell(q)
      \prod_{j\le\ell}
      \left(e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}Z_j^Q(q)\right)
      \prod_{j>\ell}
      e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})}.
   \end{aligned}
   \tag{BT1}
   \]
   The storage and far-load exponential majorants factor as
   \[
      e^{-\alpha\theta_P}
      \prod_{j=1}^hZ_j^P(x)
      =
      \prod_{j=1}^h
      \left(e^{-\alpha\theta_{P,j}}Z_j^P(x)\right),
      \tag{BT2}
   \]
   and
   \[
      e^{-\alpha\theta_P-\beta\theta_Q}
      \prod_{j=1}^h Z_j^{PQ}(x,q)
      =
      \prod_{j=1}^h
      \left(
      e^{-\alpha\theta_{P,j}-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}
      Z_j^{PQ}(x,q)
      \right).
      \tag{BT3}
   \]
   Consequently, if the block panels are sampled independently, expectations of
   the products in \((BT2)\) and \((BT3)\) are products of one-block
   expectations, and the same holds term-by-term in \((BT1)\), with the
   \((\ell+1)\)-st factor read as
   \[
      \E\!\left[
      a_\ell(q)e^{\beta(M_{\ell+1}(q)-\theta_{Q,\ell+1})}
      \right]
   \]
   when \(\ell<h\).

   Finally, for a near pair \((p,q)\), let
   \[
      H_j(p,q)=
      \left\{
      \exists a\in\mathcal C_j:
      \psi_j(p,a)\ge\theta_{P,j},\
      \phi_j(q,a)\ge\theta_{Q,j}
      \right\}.
   \]
   If the block panels are independent, then
   \[
      \Pr\!\left[
      \exists c\in\mathcal C:
      S_P(p,c)\ge\theta_P,\ S_Q(q,c)\ge\theta_Q
      \right]
      \ge
      \prod_{j=1}^h\Pr[H_j(p,q)].
      \tag{BT4}
   \]
   Thus the global product-panel certificates \((PP1)\)--\((PP4)\) follow from
   the corresponding blockwise bounds obtained by summing \((BT1)\)--\((BT3)\)
   over prefixes, data points, and far pairs, together with
   \(\prod_j\Pr[H_j(p,q)]\ge L_m^{-1}\) for every near pair.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The identity \((BT1)\) is obtained by substituting
   \(R_{\ell+1}(q)=\sum_{j>\ell}M_j(q)\) and
   \(\theta_Q=\sum_j\theta_{Q,j}\) into the definition of \(P_\beta\):
   \[
      e^{-\beta\theta_Q}
      a_\ell e^{\beta R_{\ell+1}}\prod_{j\le\ell}Z_j^Q
      =
      a_\ell
      \prod_{j\le\ell}
      \left(e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}Z_j^Q\right)
      \prod_{j>\ell}
      e^{\beta(M_j-\theta_{Q,j})}.
   \]
   Summing over \(\ell\) gives \((BT1)\).  The storage and far identities
   \((BT2)\) and \((BT3)\) are the same calculation with
   \(\theta_P=\sum_j\theta_{P,j}\) and the additive scores
   \(S_P,S_Q\).

   If the block panels are independent, every displayed product involves one
   random object from each block.  Therefore its expectation factors across
   blocks.  In \((BT1)\), the coefficient \(a_\ell\) is either \(1\) when
   \(\ell=h\), or \(1+D_{\ell+1}(q)\) when \(\ell<h\); this depends on the
   next block and is therefore grouped with the \((\ell+1)\)-st suffix factor.

   On the event \(\bigcap_jH_j(p,q)\), choose one witnessing block symbol
   \(a_j\) for each \(j\), and put \(c=(a_1,\ldots,a_h)\).  Then
   \[
      S_P(p,c)=\sum_j\psi_j(p,a_j)\ge\sum_j\theta_{P,j}=\theta_P,
      \qquad
      S_Q(q,c)=\sum_j\phi_j(q,a_j)\ge\sum_j\theta_{Q,j}=\theta_Q .
   \]
   Hence the global hit event contains \(\bigcap_jH_j(p,q)\).  Independence
   gives \((BT4)\).  The final sentence is just the substitution of these
   factored estimates into \((PP1)\)--\((PP4)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Product-panel rate bookkeeping]
   \label{cor:product-panel-rate-bookkeeping}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:product-panel-tensorization}, and assume
   the number of blocks satisfies \(h\le\log^{O_c(1)}m\).  For every query
   \(q\), suppose there are deterministic block factors \(\mathfrak q_j(q)\)
   such that, for every realization of block \(j\),
   \[
      e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}Z_j^Q(q)\le\mathfrak q_j(q),
      \qquad
      e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})}\le\mathfrak q_j(q),
   \]
   and
   \[
      (1+D_j(q))e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})}
      \le\mathfrak q_j(q).
   \]
   For data and far pairs put
   \[
      \mathfrak s_j(x)=
      \E\!\left[e^{-\alpha\theta_{P,j}}Z_j^P(x)\right],
      \qquad
      \mathfrak f_j(x,q)=
      \E\!\left[
      e^{-\alpha\theta_{P,j}-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}
      Z_j^{PQ}(x,q)
      \right],
   \]
   and for near pairs put
   \[
      \mathfrak h_j(p,q)=\Pr[H_j(p,q)].
   \]
   If
   \[
      \prod_{j=1}^h\mathfrak q_j(q)
      \le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \qquad\text{for every promised query }q,
      \tag{RB1}
   \]
   \[
      \prod_{j=1}^h\mathfrak s_j(x)
      \le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \qquad\text{for every data point }x,
      \tag{RB2}
   \]
   \[
      \prod_{j=1}^h\mathfrak f_j(x,q)
      \le m^{-(1-\rho)}\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \qquad
      \text{whenever }\|x-q\|>cr,
      \tag{RB3}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \prod_{j=1}^h\mathfrak h_j(p,q)
      \ge \log^{-O_c(1)}m
      \qquad
      \text{whenever }\|p-q\|\le r,
      \tag{RB4}
   \]
   then the product panel satisfies the certificates \((PP1)\)--\((PP4)\) of
   \cref{prop:product-panel-spectrum-compiler}, after increasing the
   polylogarithmic exponent.

   Equivalently, it is enough to prove one-block log-rate bounds
   \[
      \mathfrak q_j(q)\le m^{u_j},\qquad
      \mathfrak s_j(x)\le m^{v_j},\qquad
      \mathfrak f_j(x,q)\le m^{-w_j},\qquad
      \mathfrak h_j(p,q)\ge e^{-b_j},
   \]
   with
   \[
      \sum_j u_j\le\rho+O_c\!\left(\frac{\log\log m}{\log m}\right),
      \quad
      \sum_j v_j\le\rho+O_c\!\left(\frac{\log\log m}{\log m}\right),
   \]
   \[
      \sum_j w_j\ge1-\rho-O_c\!\left(\frac{\log\log m}{\log m}\right),
      \quad
      \sum_j b_j=O_c(\log\log m).
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \((BT1)\) and the definition of the factors \(\mathfrak q_j\), every
   prefix term in \(e^{-\beta\theta_Q}P_\beta(q)\) is at most
   \(\prod_j\mathfrak q_j(q)\).  There are \(h+1=\log^{O_c(1)}m\) terms, so
   \[
      e^{-\beta\theta_Q}P_\beta(q)
      \le
      (h+1)\prod_j\mathfrak q_j(q)
      \le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
   The additive product-panel decoder with terminal prices \(Q_c=0\) therefore
   has work
   \[
      O\!\left(
      (m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m+1)\log(2+m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m)
      \right)
      =
      m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   plus the explicit \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\) work needed to compute the block
   query scores.  This is \((PP1)\).

   For storage, independence and \((BT2)\) give, for each fixed \(x\),
   \[
      \E e^{-\alpha\theta_P}\prod_j Z_j^P(x)
      =
      \prod_j\mathfrak s_j(x)
      \le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
   Summing over the \(m\) data points gives \((PP2)\).  Similarly, for each far
   pair \((x,q)\), independence and \((BT3)\) give
   \[
      \E e^{-\alpha\theta_P-\beta\theta_Q}\prod_j Z_j^{PQ}(x,q)
      =
      \prod_j\mathfrak f_j(x,q)
      \le m^{-(1-\rho)}\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
   Summing over at most \(m\) far points gives \((PP3)\).  Finally, \((BT4)\)
   and \((RB4)\) give \((PP4)\).

   The log-rate formulation is obtained by multiplying the displayed one-block
   bounds and using
   \[
      m^{O_c((\log\log m)/\log m)}=\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Superset product-panel rate bookkeeping]
   \label{cor:superset-product-panel-rate-bookkeeping}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:product-panel-tensorization}, but use the
   superset block reporters of \cref{lem:superset-product-panel-decoder}.  Thus
   block \(j\)'s candidate work satisfies \((SR1)\) with functions
   \(A_j(q)\) and \(B_j(q)\).  For every query \(q\), suppose there are
   deterministic block factors \(\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q)\) such that, for
   every realization of block \(j\),
   \[
      e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}Z_j^Q(q)
      \le\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q),
      \qquad
      e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})}
      \le\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q),
      \tag{SRB1}
   \]
   \[
      A_j(q)e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})}
      \le\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q),
      \qquad
      B_j(q)e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}
      \le\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q).
      \tag{SRB2}
   \]
   If
   \[
      \prod_{j=1}^h\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q)
      \le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \qquad\text{for every promised query }q,
      \tag{SRB3}
   \]
   then the superset product decoder has one-panel query work
   \(m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m\), up to the explicit block score-evaluation
   preprocessing already included in \(A_j,B_j\).

   In particular, for the OSD block reporter of
   \cref{cor:osd-superset-block-reporter}, where
   \(B_j(q)=\exp(\beta M_j(q)+\Lambda_j(q))\), it is enough to choose
   \(\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q)\) satisfying
   \[
      e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}Z_j^Q(q)
      \le\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q),
      \qquad
      \bigl(1+A_j(q)+e^{\Lambda_j(q)}\bigr)
      e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})}
      \le\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q).
      \tag{SRB4}
   \]
   Thus the OSD query-rate penalty is exactly the entropy multiplier
   \(e^{\Lambda_j(q)}\) at the decoded block, plus the chart-construction term
   \(A_j(q)\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   We bound \(e^{-\beta\theta_Q}\widehat P_\beta(q)\).  The first sum in
   \(\widehat P_\beta\) is the live-prefix sum from \((BT1)\) with coefficient
   \(1\), so every term is bounded by
   \(\prod_j\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q)\) using \((SRB1)\).

   For the fixed reporter-work term at level \(\ell\), let \(r=\ell+1\).
   After multiplying by \(e^{-\beta\theta_Q}\), the term factors as
   \[
      A_r(q)
      \prod_{j<r}\left(e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}Z_j^Q(q)\right)
      e^{\beta(M_r(q)-\theta_{Q,r})}
      \prod_{j>r}e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})}.
   \]
   This is at most \(\prod_j\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q)\) by \((SRB1)\) and
   the first inequality in \((SRB2)\).

   For the threshold-dependent candidate term at level \(\ell\), again put
   \(r=\ell+1\).  Multiplication by \(e^{-\beta\theta_Q}\) gives
   \[
      B_r(q)e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,r}}
      \prod_{j<r}\left(e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}Z_j^Q(q)\right)
      \prod_{j>r}e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})},
   \]
   which is also at most the product of the
   \(\widehat{\mathfrak q}_j(q)\)'s.  There are \(2h+1=\log^{O_c(1)}m\) sums,
   so \((SRB3)\) gives
   \[
      e^{-\beta\theta_Q}\widehat P_\beta(q)
      \le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
   Substituting this into \cref{lem:superset-product-panel-decoder}, with
   terminal prices \(Q_c=0\), proves the query-work claim.

   For an OSD block,
   \[
      B_j(q)e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}
      =
      e^{\Lambda_j(q)}e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})}.
   \]
   Combining this with the live-prefix and fixed-work requirements gives
   \((SRB4)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Soft list-recovery blocks are threshold reporters]
   \label{lem:soft-list-recovery-reporter}
   Fix one product block \(j\).  Let
   \(\mathcal C_j\subseteq\Sigma_j^{b_j}\) be a code, and suppose the query
   score decomposes coordinatewise:
   \[
      \phi_j(q,c)=\sum_{i=1}^{b_j}\omega_{j,i}(q,c_i)
      \qquad(c=(c_1,\ldots,c_{b_j})\in\mathcal C_j).
   \]
   Suppose there is a certified soft list-recovery decoder which, for every
   query \(q\) and threshold \(\theta\), returns exactly
   \[
      \mathsf L_j(q,\theta)
      =
      \left\{
      c\in\mathcal C_j:
      \sum_{i=1}^{b_j}\omega_{j,i}(q,c_i)\ge\theta
      \right\}
   \]
   in work \(D_j(q)+O(|\mathsf L_j(q,\theta)|)\).  Then block \(j\) supplies
   the threshold reporter required by
   \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}.  Consequently, any
   code or lattice family with such certified soft reporters and with the
   one-block rate bounds of \cref{cor:product-panel-rate-bookkeeping}
   implements the product-panel terminal engine.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The threshold reporter in \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder} is
   precisely the map \((q,\theta)\mapsto\mathsf L_j(q,\theta)\).  Substituting
   the certified work bound for \(D_j(q)\) gives the decoder work estimate.
   The final statement is the composition with
   \cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder},
   \cref{prop:product-panel-spectrum-compiler}, and
   \cref{cor:product-panel-rate-bookkeeping}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Euclidean list decoders are soft-score reporters]
   \label{cor:euclidean-list-decoder-reporter}
   Let a block code \(\mathcal C\subseteq\Sigma^h\) have symbol embeddings
   \(a\mapsto v_i(a)\in\mathbb R^{d_i}\).  For a query block
   \(y=(y_1,\ldots,y_h)\), define the Euclidean score
   \[
      S_y(c)
      =
      \sum_{i=1}^h
      \left(\ip{y_i}{v_i(c_i)}-\frac12\|v_i(c_i)\|^2\right).
   \]
   Suppose a certified Euclidean list decoder for the embedded code
   \(V(c)=(v_1(c_1),\ldots,v_h(c_h))\) returns, for every radius \(R\), every
   codeword satisfying
   \[
      \sum_{i=1}^h\|y_i-v_i(c_i)\|^2\le R^2
   \]
   in work \(D_{\rm Euc}(y,R)+O(|L(y,R)|)\).  Then, for every threshold
   \(\theta\), calling this decoder with
   \[
      R^2=\sum_{i=1}^h\|y_i\|^2-2\theta
   \]
   gives a certified threshold reporter for
   \[
      \{c\in\mathcal C:\ S_y(c)\ge\theta\}.
   \]
   Consequently, Euclidean Reed--Solomon, Barnes--Sloane, Construction-A, or
   lattice quotient decoders can be used directly as block reporters whenever
   their list-size and work certificates match the product-panel rates.  No
   repeated-coordinate discretization is needed for scores of this form.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   For every codeword \(c\),
   \[
      S_y(c)
      =
      \frac12\sum_{i=1}^h\|y_i\|^2
      -
      \frac12\sum_{i=1}^h\|y_i-v_i(c_i)\|^2 .
   \]
   Thus \(S_y(c)\ge\theta\) is exactly the displayed Euclidean-radius
   condition with \(R^2=\sum_i\|y_i\|^2-2\theta\).  The work statement is the
   assumed certified decoder work, and the composition with the product-panel
   framework is the same as in \cref{lem:soft-list-recovery-reporter}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Hard list-recovery lift for soft scores]
   \label{lem:hard-list-recovery-lift}
   Let \(\mathcal C\subseteq\Sigma^h\) be a code and let a query \(q\)
   assign bounded nonnegative symbol scores
   \[
      0\le\omega_i(q,a)\le M
      \qquad(i\in[h],\ a\in\Sigma).
   \]
   For \(c\in\mathcal C\), put
   \[
      S_q(c)=\sum_{i=1}^h\omega_i(q,c_i).
   \]
   Fix a mesh \(\Delta>0\), let \(L=\lceil M/\Delta\rceil\), and define
   threshold lists
   \[
      \Gamma_{i,\ell}(q)=
      \{a\in\Sigma:\ \omega_i(q,a)\ge \ell\Delta\},
      \qquad i\in[h],\ \ell\in[L].
   \]
   Suppose there is a certified list-recovery oracle for the repeated-coordinate
   lift of \(\mathcal C\) which, for any integer \(A\), returns a superset of
   \[
      \left\{
      c\in\mathcal C:\
      \sum_{i=1}^h\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}
      \mathbf 1\{c_i\in\Gamma_{i,\ell}(q)\}\ge A
      \right\}
   \]
   and includes every codeword in this set, in work
   \(D_{\rm LR}(q,A)+O(|\mathsf O|h)\), where \(\mathsf O\) is the returned
   output list.  Then, for every score threshold \(\Theta\), running the oracle
   with
   \[
      A=\max\left\{0,\left\lceil \frac{\Theta}{\Delta}-h\right\rceil\right\}
   \]
   and filtering the returned codewords by the exact score \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta\)
   gives a certified threshold reporter for
   \[
      \{c\in\mathcal C:\ S_q(c)\ge\Theta\}.
   \]
   Its work is \(D_{\rm LR}(q,A)+O(|\mathsf O|h)\), plus the work needed to
   build the lists \(\Gamma_{i,\ell}(q)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a fixed coordinate with score \(w=\omega_i(q,c_i)\),
   \[
      \sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbf 1\{w\ge \ell\Delta\}
      =
      \left\lfloor \frac{w}{\Delta}\right\rfloor
      \ge
      \frac{w}{\Delta}-1 .
   \]
   Summing over coordinates gives
   \[
      \sum_{i=1}^h\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}
      \mathbf 1\{c_i\in\Gamma_{i,\ell}(q)\}
      \ge
      \frac{S_q(c)}{\Delta}-h .
   \]
   Hence every \(c\) with \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta\) is included by the oracle at the
   displayed agreement threshold \(A\).  The final exact-score filter removes
   any lower-score codewords from the oracle's superset output, so the reporter
   is certified.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Reliability-erasure sweep reporter]
   \label{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep}
   Let \(\mathcal C\subseteq\Sigma^h\) have Hamming distance \(\Delta\), and
   suppose an errors-and-erasures decoder returns the unique codeword \(c\)
   whenever the received word \(y\in(\Sigma\cup\{?\})^h\) satisfies
   \[
      2e(c,y)+s(y)<\Delta,
   \]
   where \(e(c,y)\) is the number of unerased disagreements and \(s(y)\) is
   the number of erasures.  For a query \(q\), let
   \(a_i=a_i(q)\in\Sigma\) be a hard symbol and
   \(\gamma_i=\gamma_i(q)\in[0,1]\) be a confidence for coordinate \(i\).
   For every threshold
   \[
      \theta\in\{0,1\}\cup\{\gamma_i:i\in[h]\},
   \]
   form the erasure word \(y^\theta\) by setting
   \(y^\theta_i=a_i\) when \(\gamma_i\ge\theta\), and
   \(y^\theta_i=?\) otherwise.  Run the errors-and-erasures decoder on all
   distinct erasure words \(y^\theta\), and output the distinct decoded
   codewords.

   Then the reporter makes at most \(h+2\) decoder calls, outputs at most
   \(h+2\) distinct codewords, and includes every codeword
   \(c\in\mathcal C\) with weighted agreement margin
   \[
      \sum_{i:c_i=a_i}\gamma_i
      -
      \sum_{i:c_i\ne a_i}\gamma_i
      >
      h-\Delta .
      \tag{GMD}
   \]
   Consequently this sweep is an output-sensitive threshold reporter with a
   decoder transcript set of size \(O(h)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Fix \(c\in\mathcal C\) satisfying \((GMD)\), and draw
   \(\theta\) uniformly from \([0,1]\).  If \(c_i=a_i\), the coordinate
   contributes one erasure exactly when \(\theta>\gamma_i\), so its expected
   contribution to \(2e+s\) is \(1-\gamma_i\).  If \(c_i\ne a_i\), the
   coordinate contributes two when kept and one when erased, so its expected
   contribution is \(1+\gamma_i\).  Therefore
   \[
      \E_\theta[2e(c,y^\theta)+s(y^\theta)]
      =
      h
      -
      \sum_{i:c_i=a_i}\gamma_i
      +
      \sum_{i:c_i\ne a_i}\gamma_i
      <\Delta .
   \]
   Some threshold \(\theta\in[0,1]\) has
   \(2e(c,y^\theta)+s(y^\theta)<\Delta\).  The erasure pattern changes only
   when \(\theta\) crosses one of the values \(\gamma_i\), so the finite sweep
   contains a threshold with the same pattern.  The decoder returns \(c\) on
   that pattern.  There are at most \(h+2\) thresholds and hence at most that
   many decoder calls and distinct decoded words.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Soft-score margin compiler for GMD]
   \label{lem:soft-score-gmd-margin}
   Let \(\mathcal C\subseteq\Sigma^h\) have Hamming distance \(\Delta\), and
   let a query assign coordinate scores \(\omega_i(a)\).  Choose a hard symbol
   \(a_i\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{a\in\Sigma}\omega_i(a)\), numbers
   \(\gamma_i\in[0,1]\), and a scale \(\kappa>0\) such that
   \[
      \omega_i(a_i)-\omega_i(a)\ge\kappa\gamma_i
      \qquad(a\ne a_i).
      \tag{GMS1}
   \]
   Put
   \[
      M(q)=\sum_{i=1}^h\omega_i(a_i),
      \qquad
      \Gamma(q)=\sum_{i=1}^h\gamma_i,
   \]
   and \(S_q(c)=\sum_i\omega_i(c_i)\).  Then the
   reliability-erasure sweep of
   \cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep} includes every codeword satisfying
   \[
      S_q(c)>
      M(q)-\frac{\kappa}{2}\bigl(\Gamma(q)-h+\Delta\bigr).
      \tag{GMS2}
   \]
   Consequently, for any soft threshold \(\Theta\) with
   \[
      \Theta\ge
      M(q)-\frac{\kappa}{2}\bigl(\Gamma(q)-h+\Delta\bigr),
      \tag{GMS3}
   \]
   the GMD sweep followed by exact filtering \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta\) is a
   certified threshold reporter for all codewords of score at least
   \(\Theta\), using at most \(h+2\) errors-and-erasures decoder calls.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a codeword \(c\), let \(I(c)=\{i:c_i\ne a_i\}\).  By \((GMS1)\),
   \[
      M(q)-S_q(c)
      =
      \sum_{i\in I(c)}
      \bigl(\omega_i(a_i)-\omega_i(c_i)\bigr)
      \ge
      \kappa\sum_{i\in I(c)}\gamma_i .
   \]
   If \((GMS2)\) holds, then
   \[
      \sum_{i\in I(c)}\gamma_i
      <
      \frac{\Gamma(q)-h+\Delta}{2}.
   \]
   Since
   \[
      \sum_{i:c_i=a_i}\gamma_i-\sum_{i:c_i\ne a_i}\gamma_i
      =
      \Gamma(q)-2\sum_{i\in I(c)}\gamma_i,
   \]
   condition \((GMD)\) follows.  The inclusion is therefore
   \cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep}.  If \((GMS3)\) holds, every codeword
   with \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta\) satisfies \((GMS2)\), and the final exact filter
   removes any lower-score decoder outputs.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[GMD block certificate]
   \label{cor:gmd-block-certificate}
   Let \(\mathcal C\subseteq\Sigma^h\) have Hamming distance \(\Delta\) and a
   certified errors-and-erasures decoder.  For a query \(q\), let
   \(S_q(c)=\sum_i\omega_i(q,c_i)\), choose hard symbols \(a_i(q)\),
   confidences \(\gamma_i(q)\in[0,1]\), and a scale \(\kappa(q)>0\) satisfying
   \((GMS1)\), and define \(M(q)\) and \(\Gamma(q)\) as in
   \cref{lem:soft-score-gmd-margin}.  If a requested threshold \(\Theta(q)\)
   satisfies
   \[
      \Theta(q)\ge
      M(q)-\frac{\kappa(q)}2\bigl(\Gamma(q)-h+\Delta\bigr),
   \]
   then the GMD erasure sweep followed by exact filtering \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta(q)\)
   returns exactly
   \[
      \{c\in\mathcal C:S_q(c)\ge\Theta(q)\}
   \]
   using at most \(h+2\) errors-and-erasures decoder calls and at most \(h+2\)
   reported codewords.  Equivalently, the query-side sweep has only \(O(h)\)
   erasure-threshold transcripts before the compact outer decoder output; in
   the output-sensitive decoder-DAG certificate, scan work is charged only to
   the distinct decoded codewords actually reported.

   Therefore, in the finite-signature or decoder-DAG rate-coverage
   certificates, a retained block only has to certify the top-gap inequality
   above on each query signature; no repeated-coordinate list-recovery oracle
   is needed in such high-margin signatures.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The inclusion of every codeword with \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta(q)\) is exactly
   \cref{lem:soft-score-gmd-margin}.  The final exact filter guarantees that
   no lower-score codeword is output.  The decoder-call and output bounds are
   those of \cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep}.  The transcript statement is
   the same sweep viewed as a compact decoder: one branch for each distinct
   erasure threshold, followed by the certified errors-and-erasures decoder
   call and duplicate removal.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Chase--GMD reporter]
   \label{lem:chase-gmd-reporter}
   Let \(\mathcal C\subseteq\Sigma^h\) have Hamming distance \(\Delta\) and a
   certified errors-and-erasures decoder as in
   \cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep}.  Fix hard symbols \(a_i\in\Sigma\)
   and confidences \(\gamma_i\in[0,1]\).  Let \(T\subseteq[h]\) be a branch
   set, and for each \(i\in T\) let \(L_i\subseteq\Sigma\) be a candidate
   symbol list.  For every branch
   \(b=(b_i)_{i\in T}\in\prod_{i\in T}L_i\), set
   \[
      a^b_i=
      \begin{cases}
         b_i, & i\in T,\\
         a_i, & i\notin T,
      \end{cases}
      \qquad
      \gamma^b_i=
      \begin{cases}
         1, & i\in T,\\
         \gamma_i, & i\notin T.
      \end{cases}
   \]
   Run the reliability-erasure sweep of
   \cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep} for every pair
   \((a^b,\gamma^b)\), and output the distinct decoded codewords.

   This reporter makes at most
   \[
      (h+2)\prod_{i\in T}|L_i|
   \]
   errors-and-erasures decoder calls and outputs at most that many distinct
   codewords.  It includes every \(c\in\mathcal C\) such that
   \(c_i\in L_i\) for all \(i\in T\) and
   \[
      |T|
      +
      \sum_{\substack{i\notin T\\ c_i=a_i}}\gamma_i
      -
      \sum_{\substack{i\notin T\\ c_i\ne a_i}}\gamma_i
      >
      h-\Delta .
      \tag{CH1}
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a codeword \(c\) satisfying the displayed hypotheses, choose the branch
   \(b_i=c_i\) on \(T\).  With respect to \((a^b,\gamma^b)\), every coordinate
   in \(T\) is a correct hard decision with confidence \(1\).  Thus the GMD
   weighted agreement margin for this branch is exactly the left-hand side of
   \((CH1)\), so \cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep} outputs \(c\).  The
   number of calls is the number of branches times the \(h+2\) erasure
   thresholds per branch, and each call returns at most one codeword.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Chase--GMD soft-score block certificate]
   \label{cor:chase-gmd-block-certificate}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:chase-gmd-reporter}, and let a query assign
   coordinate scores \(\omega_i(a)\).  For \(i\notin T\), choose
   \(a_i\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{a\in\Sigma}\omega_i(a)\),
   \(\gamma_i\in[0,1]\), and a scale \(\kappa>0\) such that
   \[
      \omega_i(a_i)-\omega_i(a)\ge\kappa\gamma_i
      \qquad(i\notin T,\ a\ne a_i).
      \tag{CH2}
   \]
   For a branch \(b\in\prod_{i\in T}L_i\), put
   \[
      M_b(q)=
      \sum_{i\in T}\omega_i(b_i)
      +
      \sum_{i\notin T}\omega_i(a_i),
      \qquad
      \Gamma_T(q)=\sum_{i\notin T}\gamma_i,
   \]
   and \(S_q(c)=\sum_i\omega_i(c_i)\).  Then the Chase--GMD reporter includes
   every codeword \(c\) with \(c_i\in L_i\) for \(i\in T\) and
   \[
      S_q(c)>
      M_{c_T}(q)
      -
      \frac{\kappa}{2}\bigl(\Gamma_T(q)+|T|-h+\Delta\bigr).
      \tag{CH3}
   \]
   Consequently, if a requested threshold \(\Theta(q)\) satisfies
   \[
      \Theta(q)\ge
      \max_{b\in\prod_{i\in T}L_i}M_b(q)
      -
      \frac{\kappa}{2}\bigl(\Gamma_T(q)+|T|-h+\Delta\bigr),
      \tag{CH4}
   \]
   then the Chase--GMD reporter followed by exact filtering
   \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta(q)\) returns every codeword of score at least
   \(\Theta(q)\) whose symbols on \(T\) lie in the candidate lists.  If
   \(L_i=\Sigma\) for all \(i\in T\), it is a certified threshold reporter for
   the whole code.  Its query-side transcript count is
   \[
      O\!\left(h\prod_{i\in T}|L_i|\right).
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Fix \(c\) with \(c_i\in L_i\) on \(T\), and let
   \(I(c)=\{i\notin T:c_i\ne a_i\}\).  By \((CH2)\),
   \[
      M_{c_T}(q)-S_q(c)
      =
      \sum_{i\in I(c)}
      \bigl(\omega_i(a_i)-\omega_i(c_i)\bigr)
      \ge
      \kappa\sum_{i\in I(c)}\gamma_i .
   \]
   If \((CH3)\) holds, then
   \[
      \sum_{i\in I(c)}\gamma_i
      <
      \frac{\Gamma_T(q)+|T|-h+\Delta}{2}.
   \]
   Hence
   \[
      |T|
      +
      \sum_{\substack{i\notin T\\ c_i=a_i}}\gamma_i
      -
      \sum_{\substack{i\notin T\\ c_i\ne a_i}}\gamma_i
      =
      |T|+\Gamma_T(q)-2\sum_{i\in I(c)}\gamma_i
      >
      h-\Delta .
   \]
   \Cref{lem:chase-gmd-reporter} therefore includes \(c\).  If \((CH4)\)
   holds, then every codeword with \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta(q)\) and \(c_T\in\prod_iL_i\)
   satisfies \((CH3)\), and exact filtering removes lower-score outputs.  The
   transcript count is the call bound in \cref{lem:chase-gmd-reporter}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Top-list Chase completeness]
   \label{cor:top-list-chase-completeness}
   Work in the setting of
   \cref{cor:chase-gmd-block-certificate}.  Assume that every list
   \(L_i\), \(i\in T\), contains a maximizer of \(\omega_i\), and define
   \[
      M_*(q)=
      \sum_{i\in T}\max_{a\in\Sigma}\omega_i(a)
      +
      \sum_{i\notin T}\omega_i(a_i).
   \]
   Let
   \[
      \delta_{\rm list}(q)
      =
      \min_{i\in T}
      \left(
         \max_{a\in\Sigma}\omega_i(a)
         -
         \max_{a\notin L_i}\omega_i(a)
      \right),
   \]
   with the convention that the inner maximum is \(-\infty\) when
   \(L_i=\Sigma\), and that \(\delta_{\rm list}(q)=+\infty\) when
   \(T=\emptyset\).  If
   \[
      \Theta(q)>M_*(q)-\delta_{\rm list}(q)
      \tag{TL1}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \Theta(q)\ge
      M_*(q)
      -
      \frac{\kappa}{2}\bigl(\Gamma_T(q)+|T|-h+\Delta\bigr),
      \tag{TL2}
   \]
   then the Chase--GMD reporter followed by exact filtering
   \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta(q)\) is a certified threshold reporter for the entire
   code:
   \[
      \{c\in\mathcal C:S_q(c)\ge\Theta(q)\}.
   \]
   Its transcript count is \(O(h\prod_{i\in T}|L_i|)\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Because each \(L_i\) contains a maximizer of \(\omega_i\), the quantity
   \(\max_{b\in\prod_{i\in T}L_i}M_b(q)\) in \((CH4)\) is exactly \(M_*(q)\).
   Thus \((TL2)\) is the Chase margin condition \((CH4)\).

   It remains only to show that every codeword with score at least
   \(\Theta(q)\) has its \(T\)-coordinates in the lists.  If \(c_j\notin L_j\)
   for some \(j\in T\), then
   \[
      S_q(c)
      \le
      M_*(q)
      -
      \left(
         \max_{a\in\Sigma}\omega_j(a)
         -
         \max_{a\notin L_j}\omega_j(a)
      \right)
      \le M_*(q)-\delta_{\rm list}(q)
      <\Theta(q).
   \]
   Hence every codeword with \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta(q)\) satisfies
   \(c_i\in L_i\) for all \(i\in T\).  Applying
   \cref{cor:chase-gmd-block-certificate} and then exact filtering proves the
   claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Information-set threshold reporter]
   \label{lem:information-set-threshold-reporter}
   Let \(\mathcal C\subseteq\Sigma^h\) be a code and let
   \(I\subseteq[h]\).  Write
   \(D_I=\{c_I:c\in\mathcal C\}\), and assume that the projection
   \(c\mapsto c_I\) is injective on \(\mathcal C\).  Let
   \(E_I:D_I\to\mathcal C\) be its certified inverse.  For query scores
   \(\omega_i(q,a)\), define
   \[
      S_q(c)=\sum_{i=1}^h\omega_i(q,c_i),
      \qquad
      m_i(q)=\max_{a\in\Sigma}\omega_i(q,a),
   \]
   and for \(b\in D_I\)
   \[
      U_I(q,b)
      =
      \sum_{i\in I}\omega_i(q,b_i)
      +
      \sum_{i\notin I}m_i(q).
   \]
   Suppose a query routine returns a set \(\mathsf B_I(q,\Theta)\subseteq D_I\)
   containing every \(b\in D_I\) with \(U_I(q,b)\ge\Theta\).  Then extending
   all \(b\in\mathsf B_I(q,\Theta)\) by \(E_I\) and filtering by
   \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta\) returns exactly
   \[
      \{c\in\mathcal C:S_q(c)\ge\Theta\}.
   \]

   In particular, when \(D_I=\Sigma^I\), the standard best-first product
   enumerator over the sorted coordinate symbol lists supplies such a routine.
   If
   \[
      N_I(q,\Theta)=
      \left|
      \left\{
      b\in\Sigma^I:
      \sum_{i\in I}\omega_i(q,b_i)
      \ge
      \Theta-\sum_{i\notin I}m_i(q)
      \right\}
      \right|,
   \]
   then its work is
   \[
      O\!\left(
      |I||\Sigma|\log|\Sigma|
      +
      N_I(q,\Theta)\log(2+N_I(q,\Theta))
      \right)
   \]
   plus the cost of extending and scoring the listed words.  For every
   \(\beta>0\),
   \[
      N_I(q,\Theta)
      \le
      \exp\!\left(
         -\beta\Theta
         +
         \beta\sum_{i\notin I}m_i(q)
      \right)
      \prod_{i\in I}
      \sum_{a\in\Sigma}\exp(\beta\omega_i(q,a)).
      \tag{IS1}
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let \(c\in\mathcal C\) satisfy \(S_q(c)\ge\Theta\), and put
