   \(b=c_I\).  Since each coordinate outside \(I\) is bounded above by
   \(m_i(q)\),
   \[
      U_I(q,b)
      \ge
      \sum_{i\in I}\omega_i(q,c_i)
      +
      \sum_{i\notin I}\omega_i(q,c_i)
      =
      S_q(c)
      \ge\Theta .
   \]
   Hence \(b\) is listed and \(E_I(b)=c\) is tested.  The exact score filter
   removes every extended word below threshold, proving exactness.

   For the product case, sort the symbols in each coordinate by decreasing
   score and use a heap on rank vectors, starting from the all-top vector and
   inserting each unvisited neighbor obtained by increasing one rank.  The heap
   always pops the largest remaining information-set score, so stopping below
   the displayed threshold lists exactly the \(N_I(q,\Theta)\) tuples.  The
   stated work is the sorting and heap cost.  Finally,
   \[
      \mathbf 1\left\{
      \sum_{i\in I}\omega_i(q,b_i)
      \ge
      \Theta-\sum_{i\notin I}m_i(q)
      \right\}
      \le
      \exp\!\left(
         \beta\sum_{i\in I}\omega_i(q,b_i)
         -
         \beta\Theta
         +
         \beta\sum_{i\notin I}m_i(q)
      \right),
   \]
   and summing over \(b\in\Sigma^I\) gives \((IS1)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Ordered-statistics information-set certificate]
   \label{cor:ordered-statistics-information-set}
   Work in the product information-set case of
   \cref{lem:information-set-threshold-reporter}.  For \(i\in I\), choose
   \(a_i(q)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{a\in\Sigma}\omega_i(q,a)\), and put
   \[
      D_I(q,b)=
      \sum_{i\in I}
      \bigl(m_i(q)-\omega_i(q,b_i)\bigr),
      \qquad
      \Delta_I(q,\Theta)=
      \sum_{i=1}^h m_i(q)-\Theta .
   \]
   Enumerating all \(b\in\Sigma^I\) with
   \(D_I(q,b)\le\Delta_I(q,\Theta)\), extending by \(E_I\), and exact filtering
   is a certified threshold reporter for \(\mathcal C\).

   Moreover, let
   \[
      \delta_i(q)=
      \min_{a\ne a_i(q)}
      \bigl(m_i(q)-\omega_i(q,a)\bigr),
      \qquad i\in I,
   \]
   with \(\delta_i(q)=+\infty\) if \(|\Sigma|=1\), and let
   \(\delta_{(1)}(q)\le\cdots\le\delta_{(|I|)}(q)\) be these gaps in
   nondecreasing order.  The order-\(s\) ordered-statistics enumeration of all
   information words differing from \(a_I(q)\) in at most \(s\) positions is
   complete whenever
   \[
      \Delta_I(q,\Theta)
      <
      \sum_{\ell=1}^{s+1}\delta_{(\ell)}(q),
      \tag{IS2}
   \]
   with the right-hand side interpreted as \(+\infty\) when \(s=|I|\).  Its
   number of candidate information words is at most
   \[
      \sum_{\ell=0}^s \binom{|I|}{\ell}(|\Sigma|-1)^\ell .
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Since
   \[
      U_I(q,b)
      =
      \sum_{i=1}^h m_i(q)-D_I(q,b),
   \]
   the condition \(D_I(q,b)\le\Delta_I(q,\Theta)\) is exactly
   \(U_I(q,b)\ge\Theta\).  The first claim is therefore
   \cref{lem:information-set-threshold-reporter}.

   If \(b\) differs from \(a_I(q)\) in at least \(s+1\) positions, then
   \(D_I(q,b)\) is at least the sum of the \(s+1\) smallest coordinate gaps.
   Under \((IS2)\), no such \(b\) can satisfy the required discrepancy bound.
   Hence order-\(s\) enumeration contains every threshold-relevant information
   word, and the displayed count is the number of possible support sets and
   non-top symbols on those sets.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Minimum-entropy information-set reporter]
   \label{lem:min-entropy-information-set}
   Let \(\mathcal C\le\mathbb F^h\) be a \(k\)-dimensional linear code with
   generator matrix \(G\in\mathbb F^{k\times h}\).  For an information set
   \(I\subseteq[h]\), \(|I|=k\), write \(G_I\) for the \(k\times k\) submatrix
   of columns in \(I\), and assume \(G_I\) is nonsingular.  Then
   \(D_I=\mathbb F^I\), and the certified extension map is
   \[
      E_I(b)=bG_I^{-1}G .
   \]

   Fix \(\beta>0\), query scores \(\omega_i(q,a)\), and threshold \(\Theta\).
   Put
   \[
      m_i(q)=\max_{a\in\mathbb F}\omega_i(q,a),
      \qquad
      \lambda_i(q)=
      \log\sum_{a\in\mathbb F}
      \exp\bigl(\beta(\omega_i(q,a)-m_i(q))\bigr),
   \]
   and
   \[
      \Delta(q,\Theta)=\sum_{i=1}^hm_i(q)-\Theta .
   \]
   Let
   \[
      \Lambda_*(q)=
      \min_{\substack{I\subseteq[h]\\ |I|=k,\ \operatorname{rank}G_I=k}}
      \sum_{i\in I}\lambda_i(q).
   \]
   Choose an information set \(I_*(q)\) attaining this minimum and run the
   information-set reporter of
   \cref{lem:information-set-threshold-reporter} with \(I=I_*(q)\).  It is a
   certified threshold reporter for
   \[
      \{c\in\mathcal C:S_q(c)\ge\Theta\},
   \]
   and its information-word list size satisfies
   \[
      N_{I_*}(q,\Theta)
      \le
      \exp\bigl(\beta\Delta(q,\Theta)+\Lambda_*(q)\bigr).
      \tag{ME1}
   \]

   The minimizing information set is found by the usual greedy basis rule:
   sort the coordinates by nondecreasing \(\lambda_i(q)\), scan them in that
   order, and keep a coordinate exactly when its column of \(G\) increases the
   rank of the kept columns.  If \(h\), \(|\mathbb F|\), and the linear-algebra
   work are polylogarithmic in the local subproblem size \(m\), then the
   reporter has polylogarithmic query overhead whenever
   \[
      \beta\Delta(q,\Theta)+\Lambda_*(q)=O(\log\log m).
      \tag{ME2}
   \]

   Two useful upper bounds on \(\Lambda_*(q)\) are immediate.  If
   \(\mathcal C\) is MDS, then \(\Lambda_*(q)\) is the sum of the \(k\)
   smallest values among \(\lambda_1(q),\ldots,\lambda_h(q)\).  More generally,
   for any distribution \(\nu\) on information sets,
   \[
      \Lambda_*(q)
      \le
      \sum_{i=1}^h \pi_i\lambda_i(q),
      \qquad
      \pi_i=\Pr_{I\sim\nu}[i\in I].
      \tag{ME3}
   \]
   In particular, a coordinate-transitive family with a uniform information-set
   distribution satisfies
   \[
      \Lambda_*(q)\le\frac{k}{h}\sum_{i=1}^h\lambda_i(q).
      \tag{ME4}
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Since \(G_I\) is nonsingular, every information word \(b\in\mathbb F^I\)
   determines the unique message \(u=bG_I^{-1}\), hence the unique codeword
   \(uG=E_I(b)\).  Thus the information-set reporter applies.

   For any information set \(I\), the exponential bound \((IS1)\) becomes
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      N_I(q,\Theta)
      &\le
      \exp\!\left(
         -\beta\Theta+\beta\sum_{i\notin I}m_i(q)
      \right)
      \prod_{i\in I}
      \sum_{a\in\mathbb F}\exp(\beta\omega_i(q,a))\\
      &=
      \exp\!\left(
         \beta\sum_{i=1}^hm_i(q)-\beta\Theta
         +
         \sum_{i\in I}\lambda_i(q)
      \right).
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   Minimizing over information sets gives \((ME1)\).  The polylogarithmic
   claim follows by substituting \((ME2)\) into this bound and adding the
   stated coordinate sorting, symbol sorting, rank-test, extension, and exact
   scoring costs.

   It remains to justify the greedy minimization.  The columns of \(G\) form a
   rank-\(k\) linear matroid, whose bases are exactly the information sets.
   Let \(B\) be the greedy basis and \(O\) any minimum-weight basis.  Order the
   elements of \(B\) as they are chosen, \(b_1,\ldots,b_k\).  By induction,
   after the first \(r-1\) greedy choices there exists a minimum-weight basis
   \(O_{r-1}\) containing \(b_1,\ldots,b_{r-1}\).  At step \(r\), if
   \(b_r\in O_{r-1}\), take \(O_r=O_{r-1}\).  Otherwise, because
   \(O_{r-1}\) is a basis, \(b_r\) has a linear representation in the columns
   of \(O_{r-1}\).  Since \(b_r\) is independent of
   \(\{b_1,\ldots,b_{r-1}\}\), there is a column
   \(o\in O_{r-1}\setminus\{b_1,\ldots,b_{r-1}\}\) that appears with nonzero
   coefficient in this representation.  Replacing that column by \(b_r\)
   preserves the span, so
   \[
      O_r=O_{r-1}-\{o\}+\{b_r\}
   \]
   is still a basis.  Also \(o\) is not in the span of
   \(\{b_1,\ldots,b_{r-1}\}\), otherwise \(O_{r-1}\) would be dependent.  Thus
   \(o\) was feasible for the greedy scan at step \(r\), so the sorted greedy
   rule gives
   \(\lambda_{b_r}(q)\le\lambda_o(q)\).  Hence \(O_r\) is no heavier than
   \(O_{r-1}\), and it is also minimum-weight.  After \(k\) steps the greedy
   basis itself is minimum-weight.

   If \(\mathcal C\) is MDS, every \(k\)-subset is an information set, so the
   minimum is the sum of the \(k\) smallest weights.  For a distribution
   \(\nu\) on information sets,
   \[
      \Lambda_*(q)
      \le
      \E_{I\sim\nu}\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_i(q)
      =
      \sum_i\Pr[i\in I]\lambda_i(q),
   \]
   proving \((ME3)\).  The transitive specialization has
   \(\pi_i=k/h\) for every coordinate, giving \((ME4)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[OSD blocks are superset reporters]
   \label{cor:osd-superset-block-reporter}
   Let a block code \(\mathcal C\le\mathbb F^h\) and coordinate scores
   \(\omega_i(q,a)\) be as in \cref{lem:min-entropy-information-set}, and
   write
   \[
      S_q(c)=\sum_{i=1}^h\omega_i(q,c_i),
      \qquad
      M(q)=\sum_{i=1}^h\max_{a\in\mathbb F}\omega_i(q,a).
   \]
   For a fixed \(\beta>0\), choose the minimum-entropy information set
   \(I_*(q)\) and let \(\Lambda_*(q)\) be as in
   \cref{lem:min-entropy-information-set}.  For every threshold \(\theta\),
   enumerate all information words \(b\in\mathbb F^{I_*(q)}\) satisfying
   \[
      U_{I_*}(q,b)\ge\theta,
   \]
   extend them to codewords, and exact-filter by \(S_q(c)\ge\theta\).
   This is a certified threshold reporter for
   \(\{c\in\mathcal C:S_q(c)\ge\theta\}\).  Before exact filtering, the number
   of candidate extensions is at most
   \[
      \exp\bigl(\beta(M(q)-\theta)+\Lambda_*(q)\bigr).
      \tag{OSD1}
   \]
   Hence the block supplies the superset reporter required by
   \cref{lem:superset-product-panel-decoder} with
   \[
      B(q)=\exp\bigl(\beta M(q)+\Lambda_*(q)\bigr),
      \tag{OSD2}
   \]
   and \(A(q)\) equal to the polylogarithmic chart-construction, sorting, and
   heap-initialization cost.

   In a product panel whose block upper bounds split as
   \(R_{\ell+1}(q)=\sum_{j>\ell}M_j(q)\), the OSD candidate term for block
   \(j=\ell+1\) in \(\widehat P_\beta(q)\) is exactly
   \[
      \exp(\Lambda_j(q))\,e^{\beta R_{\ell+1}(q)}Z_{\le\ell}(q).
      \tag{OSD3}
   \]
   Therefore the query-list certificate for an OSD product panel is the usual
   product-panel prefix moment, with the extra multiplier
   \(\exp(\Lambda_j(q))\) only at the block being decoded.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The first claim is \cref{lem:min-entropy-information-set} applied with
   \(\Theta=\theta\).  The candidate count bound \((OSD1)\) is \((ME1)\) with
   \(\Delta(q,\theta)=M(q)-\theta\).  Thus the threshold-dependent part of the
   reporter work is bounded by \(B(q)e^{-\beta\theta}\), proving \((OSD2)\).
   The remaining operations do not depend on \(\theta\) except through the heap
   enumeration already charged by the candidate count, so they are absorbed
   into \(A(q)\).

   For the product-panel identity, substitute
   \(B_j(q)=\exp(\beta M_j(q)+\Lambda_j(q))\) into the third sum of
   \cref{lem:superset-product-panel-decoder}:
   \[
      B_{\ell+1}(q)e^{\beta R_{\ell+2}(q)}Z_{\le\ell}(q)
      =
      e^{\Lambda_{\ell+1}(q)}
      e^{\beta(M_{\ell+1}(q)+R_{\ell+2}(q))}
      Z_{\le\ell}(q).
   \]
   Since \(M_{\ell+1}(q)+R_{\ell+2}(q)=R_{\ell+1}(q)\), this is \((OSD3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[GMD and Chase as compressed list decoders]
   \label{rem:gmd-chase-compressed-decoder}
   \Cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep} is the self-contained form of the
   generalized minimum-distance idea of Forney and of its Euclidean-code and
   lattice extensions by Forney--Vardy~\cite{For66,FV96}.  It is weaker than a
   full soft list-recovery oracle, because each coordinate contributes one hard
   symbol and one confidence, but it has a much smaller transcript space than
   the repeated-coordinate lift of \cref{lem:hard-list-recovery-lift}: the
   query tries only \(O(h)\) erasure patterns.  This is exactly the kind of
   decoder DAG charged by
   \cref{cor:output-sensitive-decoder-dag-rate-coverage}.

   Chase decoding enlarges the same template by branching over a few
   low-confidence coordinates or low-confidence symbols before running the
   outer decoder~\cite{Cha72}.  In the present language, those trials are just
   additional branches of a compact decoder DAG, as formalized in
   \cref{lem:chase-gmd-reporter} and
   \cref{cor:chase-gmd-block-certificate}.  The useful theoretical target is
   therefore not Lloyd-style cell convergence, but a reliability map for which
   the true near route satisfies the soft top-gap inequality \((GMS2)\), or the
   Chase margin \((CH4)\) after branching over a polylogarithmic ambiguity set,
   while the retained outputs pass the same rate-coverage scan and storage
   certificates as the other panels.  \Cref{cor:top-list-chase-completeness}
   makes the usual practical restriction to top lists self-contained: symbols
   outside the lists are harmless whenever their score deficit already puts the
   codeword below the reporting threshold.

   \Cref{lem:information-set-threshold-reporter} gives the ordered-statistics
   alternative inspired by Prange information sets and the Fossorier--Lin OSD
   decoder~\cite{Pra62,FL95}.  Instead of asking an outer decoder to correct
   low-confidence coordinates, one chooses a reliable information set, lists
   only high-score information words, extends them through the systematic chart,
   and filters by the exact Euclidean score.  This is often a better match for
   expensive preprocessing: the build stage may search for good systematic
   charts, rotations, or quotient bases, while the retained query algorithm pays
   only for the short discrepancy list certified by
   \cref{cor:ordered-statistics-information-set}.
   \Cref{lem:min-entropy-information-set} makes this search objective exact:
   for a linear block, the right chart is the minimum-weight information set
   with coordinate weights equal to the local soft entropy
   \(\lambda_i(q)\).  For MDS or Reed--Solomon style blocks this is just the
   \(k\) most reliable coordinates, while for a general retained linear block
   it is a greedy minimum-basis computation.  The remaining geometric spectrum
   theorem can therefore ask for the scalar entropy margin \((ME2)\), instead
   of a full soft list-recovery oracle.
   \Cref{lem:superset-product-panel-decoder} and
   \cref{cor:osd-superset-block-reporter} then connect this scalar margin back
   to the product-panel accounting: false OSD candidates are not hidden in the
   output-sensitive scan term, but are charged by an explicit
   \(e^{\Lambda_j(q)}\) multiplier in the prefix moment.
   \Cref{cor:superset-product-panel-rate-bookkeeping} is the rate form of the
   same statement: the query exponent only has to absorb
   \((1+A_j(q)+e^{\Lambda_j(q)})
   e^{\beta(M_j(q)-\theta_{Q,j})}\) block-by-block.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Outer list recovery as the clean codebook target]
   \label{rem:outer-list-recovery-codebook}
   \Cref{cor:euclidean-list-decoder-reporter},
   \cref{lem:hard-list-recovery-lift}, and
   \cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep} are the most useful transfers from
   Reed--Solomon, expander-code, polar-code, and Euclidean-code list decoding
   to the present data-structure problem.  The codebook need not be the full
   Cartesian product
   \(\prod_j\mathcal C_j\).  It may be a sparse outer code
   \(\mathcal C\subseteq\prod_j\Sigma_j\), a Construction-A or Construction-D
   lattice coset, or a Barnes--Wall style recursive lattice family.  The query
   supplies soft Euclidean reliabilities for the symbols.  If those
   reliabilities are actual embedded Euclidean scores, then
   \cref{cor:euclidean-list-decoder-reporter} turns any Euclidean-metric list
   decoder into the threshold reporter directly.  For more general bounded
   scores, the lift converts reliabilities into ordinary list-recovery
   constraints at \(h\lceil M/\Delta\rceil\) repeated positions, and the
   decoder returns a short certified list of high-score global codewords before
   metric verification.  When one symbol per coordinate is dominant, the
   reliability-erasure sweep gives the cheaper \(O(h)\)-transcript GMD
   surrogate instead.  Signed scores are handled by adding a query-dependent
   constant to all symbols in a coordinate and shifting the final threshold.

   This is a sharper alternative to \(k\)-means or Lloyd cells.  \(k\)-means++
   or other expensive build-time procedures can still be used to propose
   rotations, cosets, scalings, or inner score panels, but the retained index is
   certified by a code/list-recovery theorem and by the spectrum inequalities
   \((PP1)\)--\((PP4)\), not by convergence of the clustering heuristic.  When
   \(M/\Delta=\log^{O_c(1)}m\), the repeated-coordinate lift costs only a
   polylogarithmic factor; direct Euclidean decoding removes even this
   discretization overhead when it applies.  Thus folded Reed--Solomon/list-recovery
   constructions~\cite{GR08}, Koetter--Vardy soft decoding~\cite{KV03},
   Barnes--Sloane lattice decoding via soft Reed--Solomon scores~\cite{MP20},
   expander-code list recovery~\cite{ST25}, and polar-code multiprobing
   \cite{TV12,TH23} all point to the same theorem object: a compact panel whose
   soft-score threshold reporter is output-sensitive and whose retained buckets
   have the random-like ANN spectrum.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[List decoding as learned routing without Lloyd analysis]
   \label{rem:list-decoding-routing}
   The useful transfer from list decoding is not a black-box decoding radius.
   It is the interface in \cref{lem:soft-list-recovery-reporter} and
   \cref{cor:euclidean-list-decoder-reporter}: a structured code or lattice gives an
   implicit family of candidate centers together with a certified
   output-sensitive enumerator for the centers relevant to a query.
   \Cref{lem:hard-list-recovery-lift} is the fallback that reduces arbitrary
   bounded soft scores to ordinary list recovery with only polylogarithmic mesh
   loss.
   This is a cleaner theorem object than Lloyd or \(k\)-means routing.  Lloyd
   may find excellent empirical centers, but its iterations do not by
   themselves provide a worst-case list-size majorant for every query.  A
   code/lattice panel instead stores a compact seed, coset, syndrome, or
   frozen-set description, and the query runs a list decoder whose stack size is
   charged by the partition certificates \(P_\beta\) and \(T_\beta\).

   Since preprocessing time is not charged in the model, the build routine may
   search aggressively over random rotations, cosets, Construction-A moduli,
   block scalings, frozen sets, evaluation points, and pruning profiles.  Only
   the retained panel descriptions, bucket incidences, and query decoder are
   charged.  The proof obligation remains exactly the rate bookkeeping above:
   random-like point load, random-like far co-load, output-sensitive decoding,
   and inverse-polylogarithmic near-pair hit probability.  Recent Euclidean
   list-decoding work gives relevant building blocks and benchmarks for this
   target: random spherical and Construction-A Euclidean codes give the list
   size baseline~\cite{ZV21}, Reed--Solomon and related soft decoders give
   coordinatewise Euclidean list-recovery mechanisms~\cite{KV03,MP20,PH26},
   and polar-code nearest-neighbor search shows the practical value of using a
   high-dimensional latent code plus list decoding for multiprobe
   search~\cite{TV12,TH23}.  Recent expander-code list-decoding and
   list-recovery results also make it plausible to use an explicit outer code
   to combine many small soft blocks without paying a linear scan over all
   products~\cite{ST25}.  What is still missing here is the simultaneous
   ANN spectrum theorem at the critical exponent, not the decoder interface.

   The sharpest formulation of that missing object is the smooth decodable
   subcode problem isolated by Becker--Ducas--Gama--Laarhoven.  Their random
   product-code construction gives the right spherical filtering intuition and
   a lattice-enumeration decoder, but the random-behavior guarantee carries a
   \(2^{\widetilde O(\sqrt d)}\) slack.  In logarithmic dimension this is
   \(m^{o(1)}\), which is enough for the traditional \(o(1)\)-exponent
   accounting but not for the polylogarithmic overhead target pursued here.
   Thus the self-contained replacement for \(k\)-means is not a black-box
   lattice decoder.  It is a block code or lattice subcode of the prescribed
   size whose wedge/cap loads satisfy the one-block rate inequalities of
   \cref{cor:product-panel-rate-bookkeeping} with only polylogarithmic slack,
   and whose threshold reporter is output-sensitive.  Expensive preprocessing
   may search over random rotations, Construction-A or Construction-D
   parameters, Barnes--Wall or Reed--Solomon derived blocks, or repulsive
   spherical codebooks; after certification, the retained object is just a
   compact decodable panel.  In this sense the cumulative score tree is an
   online randomized lattice decoder, and the product-panel program asks for a
   fixed implicit lattice whose decoder has the same moment certificates.

   The route-lattice viewpoint also clarifies how such a panel can simplify the
   near-value side of the proof.  A black-box approximate closest-vector answer
   is too weak, because it gives neither pointwise domination nor a cut-mass
   certificate.  A threshold list decoder with admissible prefix upper scores
   is strong enough: every decoded continuation is a unit-success edge in the
   route trie.  After the contraction in
   \cref{lem:port-skeleton-saturated-comparison}, the decoded block contributes
   only its Fano/list-decoder prefix moment and its polylogarithmic depth, not a
   separate detector-vote or cohort-materialization loss.  Thus the lowest
   overhead code/lattice replacement is one that turns nonunit probabilistic
   ports into certified deterministic threshold enumeration, or else into a
   sampler with an explicit state-DAG envelope and the same cut certificate.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Sampled structured-panel compiler]
   \label{prop:sampled-structured-panel}
   Fix a terminal localized ANN subproblem of size \(m\), approximation \(c\),
   and target exponent \(\rho=\rho_c\).  Let \(L_m=\log^{A_c}m\).  A random
   sampled panel \(\mathcal P\) consists of a finite label set \(\mathcal L\),
   data predicates \(A_g(x)\in\{0,1\}\), and, for every promised query \(q\),
   a probability law \(\pi_q\) on \(\mathcal L\) from which the query can sample in
   \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\) time per trial.  Suppose that
   \[
      \E_{\mathcal P}\sum_{x\in P}\sum_{g\in\mathcal L}A_g(x)
      \le m^{1+\rho}L_m ,
      \tag{SP1}
   \]
   and, for every promised query \(q\),
   \[
      \E_{\mathcal P}
      \sum_{\substack{x\in P\\ \|x-q\|>cr}}
      \sum_{g\in\mathcal L}\pi_q(g)A_g(x)
      \le m^\rho L_m .
      \tag{SP2}
   \]
   Finally assume that every near pair \((p,q)\), \(\|p-q\|\le r\), satisfies
   the one-sample hit condition
   \[
      \E_{\mathcal P}
      \sum_{g\in\mathcal L}\pi_q(g)A_g(p)
      \ge L_m^{-1}.
      \tag{SP3}
   \]
   Then \(O(L_m)\) independent sampled panels give a terminal ANN data
   structure with query work
   \[
      d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   and space
   \[
      dm+m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Store \(B=\lceil C L_m\rceil\) independent panels.  In each panel, put
   every point \(x\) in every bucket \(g\) with \(A_g(x)=1\).  The expected
   stored point-bucket incidences are bounded by \(B m^{1+\rho}L_m\), which is
   \(m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m\); the raw coordinates add \(dm\).

   On query \(q\), draw one label \(g\sim\pi_q\) from each panel, scan the
   corresponding bucket, and verify all reported points in the original metric.
   By \((SP2)\), the expected number of invalid scanned points per panel is at
   most \(m^\rho L_m\), and the sampling overhead is
   \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\).  Multiplying by \(B\) gives the displayed query bound
   after increasing the polylogarithmic exponent.

   If \(p\) is a true near neighbor of \(q\), the probability that one random
   panel and one label sample hit a bucket containing \(p\) is at least
   \(L_m^{-1}\) by \((SP3)\).  Hence all \(B\) independent trials miss \(p\)
   with probability at most \((1-L_m^{-1})^B\le e^{-C}\).  Taking \(C\) large
   gives constant success probability, and verification prevents false
   positives.  The capped-expectation convention from the preliminaries gives
   the stated capped resources after the usual constant-factor loss.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Gibbs sampled product-panel certificate]
   \label{cor:gibbs-sampled-product-panel}
   Fix the terminal setting of \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel}.  A random
   product panel has labels
   \(\mathcal C=\prod_{j=1}^h\mathcal C_j\), data scores
   \[
      S_P(x,c)=\sum_{j=1}^h\psi_j(x,c_j),
      \qquad
      S_Q(q,c)=\sum_{j=1}^h\phi_j(q,c_j),
   \]
   and stores \(x\) in bucket \(c\) when \(S_P(x,c)\ge\theta_P\).  For a
   promised query \(q\), the query samples one label from the Gibbs law
   \[
      \pi_q(c)=\frac{e^{\beta S_Q(q,c)}}{Z_Q(q)},
      \qquad
      Z_Q(q)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal C}e^{\beta S_Q(q,a)}.
   \]
   Assume this law can be sampled in \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\) time.  Suppose that
   for some \(\alpha,\beta>0\),
   \[
      \E
      \sum_{x\in P}
      e^{-\alpha\theta_P}
      \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}e^{\alpha S_P(x,c)}
      \le m^{1+\rho}L_m,
      \tag{GS1}
   \]
   and, for every promised query \(q\),
   \[
      \E
      \sum_{\substack{x\in P\\ \|x-q\|>cr}}
      \frac{
         e^{-\alpha\theta_P}
         \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}
         e^{\alpha S_P(x,c)+\beta S_Q(q,c)}
      }{Z_Q(q)}
      \le m^\rho L_m .
      \tag{GS2}
   \]
   Finally assume that every near pair \((p,q)\), \(\|p-q\|\le r\), satisfies
   \[
      \E
      \frac{
         \sum_{c\in\mathcal C:\,S_P(p,c)\ge\theta_P}
         e^{\beta S_Q(q,c)}
      }{Z_Q(q)}
      \ge L_m^{-1}.
      \tag{GS3}
   \]
   Then \(O(L_m)\) independent Gibbs sampled product panels give the terminal
   ANN bounds of \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel}.

   If the labels are a full product and
   \(Z_j^Q(q),Z_j^P(x),Z_j^{PQ}(x,q)\) are as in
   \cref{lem:product-panel-tensorization}, then
   \[
      Z_Q(q)=\prod_jZ_j^Q(q),
      \qquad
      \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}e^{\alpha S_P(x,c)}
      =\prod_jZ_j^P(x),
   \]
   and
   \[
      \frac{
         \sum_c e^{\alpha S_P(x,c)+\beta S_Q(q,c)}
      }{Z_Q(q)}
      =
      \prod_j\frac{Z_j^{PQ}(x,q)}{Z_j^Q(q)}.
      \tag{GS4}
   \]
   Thus \((GS1)\) and \((GS2)\) factor block-by-block.  If
   \(\theta_P=\sum_j\theta_{P,j}\), then the near condition is implied by
   \[
      \prod_j
      \E\left[
      \frac{
         \sum_{a\in\mathcal C_j:\,\psi_j(p,a)\ge\theta_{P,j}}
         e^{\beta\phi_j(q,a)}
      }{Z_j^Q(q)}
      \right]
      \ge L_m^{-1}
      \tag{GS5}
   \]
   whenever the block panels are independent.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   For a realized panel, set the sampled-panel label set to
   \(\mathcal L=\mathcal C\), put
   \(A_c(x)=\mathbf 1\{S_P(x,c)\ge\theta_P\}\), and use the displayed Gibbs
   law \(\pi_q\).  The storage condition \((SP1)\) follows from
   \[
      A_c(x)\le e^{\alpha(S_P(x,c)-\theta_P)}
   \]
   and \((GS1)\).  For far sampled co-load,
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      \sum_c\pi_q(c)A_c(x)
      &\le
      \frac{
         e^{-\alpha\theta_P}
         \sum_c e^{\alpha S_P(x,c)+\beta S_Q(q,c)}
      }{Z_Q(q)} ,
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   so \((SP2)\) follows from \((GS2)\).  The near sampled mass condition
   \((SP3)\) is exactly \((GS3)\).  Therefore
   \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel} applies.

   The factor identities in \((GS4)\) follow by expanding the additive product
   scores and summing independently over the coordinates \(c_j\).  Finally, on
   the block event
   \(\psi_j(p,c_j)\ge\theta_{P,j}\) for every \(j\), the full label satisfies
   \(S_P(p,c)\ge\theta_P\).  Since the Gibbs law and the random panel factor
   across independent blocks, the expected Gibbs mass of this sufficient event
   is the product in \((GS5)\), which proves the last claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Gibbs sampled product-panel rate bookkeeping]
   \label{cor:gibbs-sampled-rate-bookkeeping}
   Work in the full-product setting of
   \cref{cor:gibbs-sampled-product-panel}, and assume the number of blocks is
   \(h\le\log^{O_c(1)}m\).  The query Gibbs sampler is the product sampler
   obtained from the one-block laws proportional to
   \(e^{\beta\phi_j(q,a)}\), and its sampling time is
   \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\).  For data points, far pairs, and near pairs define
   \[
      \mathfrak s_j(x)=
      \E\!\left[e^{-\alpha\theta_{P,j}}Z_j^P(x)\right],
   \]
   \[
      \mathfrak f_j(x,q)=
      \E\!\left[
         e^{-\alpha\theta_{P,j}}
         \frac{Z_j^{PQ}(x,q)}{Z_j^Q(q)}
      \right],
   \]
   and
   \[
      \mathfrak h_j(p,q)=
      \E\!\left[
      \frac{
         \sum_{a\in\mathcal C_j:\,\psi_j(p,a)\ge\theta_{P,j}}
         e^{\beta\phi_j(q,a)}
      }{Z_j^Q(q)}
      \right].
   \]
   If
   \[
      \prod_{j=1}^h\mathfrak s_j(x)
      \le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \qquad\text{for every data point }x,
      \tag{GR1}
   \]
   \[
      \prod_{j=1}^h\mathfrak f_j(x,q)
      \le m^{-(1-\rho)}\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \qquad
      \text{whenever }\|x-q\|>cr,
      \tag{GR2}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \prod_{j=1}^h\mathfrak h_j(p,q)
      \ge \log^{-O_c(1)}m
      \qquad
      \text{whenever }\|p-q\|\le r,
      \tag{GR3}
   \]
   then the Gibbs sampled product panel satisfies the terminal ANN bounds of
   \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel}.

   Equivalently, it is enough to prove one-block log-rate bounds
   \[
      \mathfrak s_j(x)\le m^{v_j},\qquad
      \mathfrak f_j(x,q)\le m^{-w_j},\qquad
      \mathfrak h_j(p,q)\ge e^{-b_j},
   \]
   with
   \[
      \sum_jv_j\le\rho+O_c\!\left(\frac{\log\log m}{\log m}\right),
      \qquad
      \sum_jw_j\ge1-\rho-O_c\!\left(\frac{\log\log m}{\log m}\right),
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_jb_j=O_c(\log\log m).
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Since the product sampler chooses block symbols independently with
   probabilities proportional to \(e^{\beta\phi_j(q,a)}\), its law on
   \(\mathcal C\) is exactly the Gibbs law in
   \cref{cor:gibbs-sampled-product-panel}.  For a fixed data point \(x\),
   independence and \((GS4)\) give
   \[
      \E e^{-\alpha\theta_P}
      \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}e^{\alpha S_P(x,c)}
      =
      \prod_j\mathfrak s_j(x).
   \]
   Summing over \(x\in P\) and applying \((GR1)\) gives \((GS1)\).  For a
   fixed far pair \((x,q)\), the mixed/query partition ratio factors as
   \[
      \E\,
      \frac{
         e^{-\alpha\theta_P}
         \sum_c e^{\alpha S_P(x,c)+\beta S_Q(q,c)}
      }{Z_Q(q)}
      =
      \prod_j\mathfrak f_j(x,q).
   \]
   Summing over the at most \(m\) far points and using \((GR2)\) gives
   \((GS2)\).  Finally, \((GR3)\) is exactly the sufficient product lower bound
   \((GS5)\) for the near Gibbs mass condition \((GS3)\).  Hence
   \cref{cor:gibbs-sampled-product-panel} applies.

   The log-rate form is obtained by multiplying the one-block bounds and using
   \(m^{O_c((\log\log m)/\log m)}=\log^{O_c(1)}m\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Tilted moment lower bound for Gibbs near mass]
   \label{lem:gibbs-near-moment-lower-bound}
   Fix one block of a Gibbs sampled product panel, one query \(q\), and one
   near point \(p\).  Let
   \[
      \Pi_q(a)=\frac{e^{\beta\phi(q,a)}}{Z^Q(q)}
   \]
   be the block query Gibbs law on the block labels, and let
   \(\psi(p,a)\) be the block data storage score.  For \(\gamma>0\) put
   \[
      M_\gamma(p,q)
      =
      e^{-\gamma\theta_P}
      \sum_a\Pi_q(a)e^{\gamma\psi(p,a)}
      =
      \frac{
         e^{-\gamma\theta_P}
         \sum_a e^{\gamma\psi(p,a)+\beta\phi(q,a)}
      }{Z^Q(q)} .
   \]
   Then, for every realized block,
   \[
      \Pi_q\{\psi(p,a)\ge\theta_P\}
      \ge
      \frac{(M_\gamma(p,q)-1)_+^2}{M_{2\gamma}(p,q)}.
      \tag{GM1}
   \]
   Consequently, in the notation of
   \cref{cor:gibbs-sampled-rate-bookkeeping},
   \[
      \mathfrak h_j(p,q)
      \ge
      \E\left[
      \frac{(M_{j,\gamma}(p,q)-1)_+^2}{M_{j,2\gamma}(p,q)}
      \right],
      \tag{GM2}
   \]
   where \(M_{j,\gamma}\) is the corresponding block quantity.  In
   particular, the near-rate condition \((GR3)\) follows if the product over
   blocks of the right-hand side of \((GM2)\) is at least
   \(\log^{-O_c(1)}m\).  A simpler sufficient deterministic margin is
   \[
      M_{j,\gamma}(p,q)\ge 1+\delta_j,
      \qquad
      M_{j,2\gamma}(p,q)\le U_j
   \]
   on an event of probability \(1-\varepsilon_j\), which gives
   \[
      \mathfrak h_j(p,q)
      \ge
      (1-\varepsilon_j)\frac{\delta_j^2}{U_j}.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let \(Y=e^{\gamma(\psi(p,A)-\theta_P)}\), where
   \(A\sim\Pi_q\).  Then \(\E Y=M_\gamma(p,q)\) and
   \(\E Y^2=M_{2\gamma}(p,q)\).  Since
   \[
      \E(Y-1)_+\ge(\E Y-1)_+,
   \]
   and since \((Y-1)_+\le Y\mathbf 1_{\{Y\ge1\}}\), Cauchy's inequality gives
   \[
      (M_\gamma-1)_+^2
      \le
      \left(\E Y\mathbf 1_{\{Y\ge1\}}\right)^2
      \le
      \E Y^2\cdot \Pr[Y\ge1].
   \]
   The event \(Y\ge1\) is exactly
   \(\{\psi(p,A)\ge\theta_P\}\), proving \((GM1)\).  Averaging \((GM1)\) over
   the random block gives \((GM2)\).  The product and deterministic-margin
   statements are immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Critical tilted-Gaussian sampled spectrum]
   \label{lem:critical-tilted-gaussian-sampled-spectrum}
   Fix \(c>1\), and put
   \[
      a_0=1-\frac1{c^2},
      \qquad
      \rho=\frac1{2c^2-1}.
   \]
   Let \(m\ge2\), \(L=\log m\), and choose a storage threshold \(T\) and query
   tilt \(\beta\) by
   \[
      T^2=2(1-\rho)L,
      \qquad
      \beta=\frac{T}{a_0}.
   \]
   Consider the ideal continuous Gaussian filter family with base label
   \(G\sim N(0,I_d)\), label intensity \(m\), and storage predicate
   \[
      A_G(x)=\mathbf 1\{\ip{G}{x}\ge T\}
      \qquad(x\in\mathbb S^{d-1}).
   \]
   For a query \(q\), sample the filter label from the exponentially tilted
   law \(G_q\sim N(\beta q,I_d)\).  Then the following spectrum bounds hold.
   For every data point \(x\),
   \[
      m\,\Pr[\ip{G}{x}\ge T]\le m^\rho.
      \tag{TG1}
   \]
   For every far pair with \(\ip{x}{q}\le0\),
   \[
      \Pr[\ip{G_q}{x}\ge T]\le m^{-(1-\rho)}.
      \tag{TG2}
   \]
   For every near pair with \(\ip{p}{q}\ge a_0\),
   \[
      \Pr[\ip{G_q}{p}\ge T]\ge\frac12.
      \tag{TG3}
   \]
   Thus the ideal sampled Gaussian spectrum has storage rate \(\rho\), sampled
   far-separation rate \(1-\rho\), and constant near sampled mass at the
   critical Euclidean normalization.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a unit vector \(x\), \(\ip{G}{x}\sim N(0,1)\).  The elementary Gaussian
   tail bound gives
   \[
      m\,\Pr[\ip{G}{x}\ge T]
      \le m e^{-T^2/2}
      =
      m^\rho,
   \]
   proving \((TG1)\).

   If \(\ip{x}{q}\le0\), then
   \(\ip{G_q}{x}\sim N(\beta\ip{x}{q},1)\) has mean at most zero, so
   \[
      \Pr[\ip{G_q}{x}\ge T]\le e^{-T^2/2}=m^{-(1-\rho)},
   \]
   which is \((TG2)\).  If \(\ip{p}{q}\ge a_0\), then
   \(\ip{G_q}{p}\sim N(\beta\ip{p}{q},1)\) has mean at least
   \(\beta a_0=T\).  Therefore
   \[
      \Pr[\ip{G_q}{p}\ge T]\ge \Pr[N(T,1)\ge T]=\frac12,
   \]
   proving \((TG3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the tilted-Gaussian spectrum supplies]
   \label{rem:tilted-gaussian-spectrum}
   \Cref{lem:critical-tilted-gaussian-sampled-spectrum} is not by itself a
   finite index: a literal continuous filter family has no stored bucket for a
   newly sampled \(G_q\).  Its role is to identify the exact spectrum that a
   structured code, lattice, trellis, or product panel should emulate.  A
   compact panel that realizes this Gaussian quadrature and supports sampling
   from the query tilt would plug into
   \cref{cor:gibbs-sampled-rate-bookkeeping}.  The calculation also explains
   why sampling is potentially easier than reporting here: at the critical
   normalization the query does not need to enumerate the
   \(m^\rho\) active filters.  One tilted sample already lands in a near
   filter with constant probability, while its expected far bucket size is
   \(m^\rho\).
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Naive Gaussian-label quadrature barrier]
   \label{lem:naive-gaussian-quadrature-barrier}
   In the setting of
   \cref{lem:critical-tilted-gaussian-sampled-spectrum}, let
   \(M=m^\nu\) independent base labels \(G_1,\ldots,G_M\sim N(0,I_d)\) be used
   as an ordinary Monte Carlo discretization of the query tilt.  More generally
   take a threshold \(T^2=2\tau\log m\) and the near-critical tilt
   \(\beta=T/a_0\).  For a fixed query \(q\), the Monte Carlo estimator of the
   tilted partition function
   \[
      Z_q=\E e^{\beta\ip{G}{q}}=e^{\beta^2/2}
   \]
   is
   \[
      \widehat Z_q=\frac1M\sum_{i=1}^M e^{\beta\ip{G_i}{q}},
   \]
   and its relative variance is
   \[
      \frac{\operatorname{Var}\widehat Z_q}{Z_q^2}
      =
      \frac{e^{\beta^2}-1}{M}
      =
      m^{2\tau/a_0^2-\nu+o(1)}.
      \tag{NQ1}
   \]
   Hence bounded-relative-variance Monte Carlo quadrature requires
   \[
      \nu\ge \frac{2\tau}{a_0^2}-o(1).
      \tag{NQ2}
   \]
   On the other hand, the ANN storage and sampled far-load powers force
   \[
      \nu\le\tau+\rho+o(1),
      \qquad
      \tau\ge1-\rho-o(1).
      \tag{NQ3}
   \]
   These three inequalities are incompatible for every fixed \(c>1\).
   Consequently the ideal tilted-Gaussian sampler cannot be materialized by a
   plain iid Gaussian label pool whose correctness is proved by ordinary
   self-normalized Monte Carlo concentration; the finite construction must use
   additional structure, such as a code, lattice, trellis, or deterministic
   quadrature.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Since \(\ip{G}{q}\sim N(0,1)\),
   \[
      \E e^{\beta\ip{G}{q}}=e^{\beta^2/2},
      \qquad
      \E e^{2\beta\ip{G}{q}}=e^{2\beta^2}.
   \]
   Thus
   \[
      \frac{\operatorname{Var}\widehat Z_q}{Z_q^2}
      =
      \frac1M
      \frac{e^{2\beta^2}-e^{\beta^2}}{e^{\beta^2}}
      =
      \frac{e^{\beta^2}-1}{M}.
   \]
   Since \(\beta^2=2\tau\log m/a_0^2\), this is \((NQ1)\), and \((NQ2)\)
   follows.

   The expected number of stored labels for one point is
   \[
      M\Pr[N(0,1)\ge T]=m^{\nu-\tau+o(1)},
   \]
   so keeping the per-point storage within \(m^{\rho+o(1)}\) gives
   \(\nu\le\tau+\rho+o(1)\).  For a far point with correlation zero to the
   query, the continuous tilted law leaves its score distributed as \(N(0,1)\).
   Thus a sampled far bucket has probability \(m^{-\tau+o(1)}\), and summing
   over \(m\) far points gives the target \(m^\rho\) only if
   \(\tau\ge1-\rho-o(1)\).  This proves \((NQ3)\).

   It remains to check incompatibility.  Write \(C=c^2>1\).  Then
   \(a_0=(C-1)/C\) and \(\rho=1/(2C-1)\).  At the smallest possible
   \(\tau=1-\rho\),
   \[
      \frac{2\tau}{a_0^2}-(\tau+\rho)
      =
      \frac{2C^2+3C-1}{(C-1)(2C-1)}
      >0.
   \]
   The left-hand side is increasing in \(\tau\), so the gap remains positive
   for every \(\tau\ge1-\rho\).  Thus \((NQ2)\) and \((NQ3)\) cannot both hold.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Weighted Gaussian quadrature panel verifier]
   \label{cor:weighted-gaussian-quadrature-panel}
   Fix a terminal spherical instance of size \(m\) at the critical parameters
   of \cref{lem:critical-tilted-gaussian-sampled-spectrum}.  Let \(L_m\) be a
   sufficiently large power of \(\log m\).  A retained weighted quadrature
   panel consists of labels \(g\in\mathcal L\subset\mathbb R^d\), positive
   weights \(w_g\), a storage threshold \(T\), and a query tilt \(\beta\).
   Store \(x\) in bucket \(g\) when
   \[
      \ip{g}{x}\ge T.
   \]
   On query \(q\), sample one label from
   \[
      \pi_q(g)=
      \frac{w_g e^{\beta\ip{g}{q}}}
           {\sum_{h\in\mathcal L}w_h e^{\beta\ip{h}{q}}},
      \tag{WQ0}
   \]
   assuming this sampling operation is supported in \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\) time.

   If the retained panel satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P}
      |\{g\in\mathcal L:\ip{g}{x}\ge T\}|
      \le m^{1+\rho}L_m,
      \tag{WQ1}
   \]
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{x\in P\\ \|x-q\|>cr}}
      \sum_{\substack{g\in\mathcal L\\ \ip{g}{x}\ge T}}
      \pi_q(g)
      \le m^\rho L_m
      \qquad\text{for every promised query }q,
      \tag{WQ2}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{g\in\mathcal L\\ \ip{g}{p}\ge T}}
      \pi_q(g)
      \ge L_m^{-1}
      \qquad
      \text{whenever }\|p-q\|\le r,
      \tag{WQ3}
   \]
   then \(O(L_m)\) independent retained panels give the terminal ANN bounds of
   \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel}.

   The same statement holds for labels drawn from a structured code, lattice,
   trellis, or product panel after replacing \(\ip{g}{x}\) by the certified
   data score and \(\ip{g}{q}\) by the certified query score.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   This is \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel} with
   \(A_g(x)=\mathbf 1\{\ip{g}{x}\ge T\}\) and the query sampling law
   \((WQ0)\).  The storage, far sampled co-load, and near sampled mass
   hypotheses are exactly \((SP1)\), \((SP2)\), and \((SP3)\).  The final
   sentence is only a change of notation for the same sampled-panel compiler.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Bucket-quotient weighted quadrature]
   \label{cor:bucket-quotient-quadrature}
   Fix the terminal setting of \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel}.  Let
   \(\mathcal L\) be any finite or compact label family with base weights
   \(w_g\), data predicates \(A_g(x)\in\{0,1\}\), and positive query scores
   \(R_q(g)\).  Partition the labels into bucket classes \(b\in\mathcal B\) by
   the stored point set
   \[
      C_b=\{x\in P:A_g(x)=1\},
      \qquad g\in b.
   \]
   For a query \(q\), define the aggregate tilted bucket weight
   \[
      W_q(b)=\sum_{g\in b}w_gR_q(g),
      \qquad
      \Pi_q(b)=\frac{W_q(b)}{\sum_{a\in\mathcal B}W_q(a)} .
   \]
   The same definition applies with integrals when a bucket class contains a
   continuum of labels.  Suppose that the query can sample \(b\sim\Pi_q\) in
   \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\) time, and that
   \[
      \sum_{b\in\mathcal B}|C_b|\le m^{1+\rho}L_m,
      \tag{BQ1}
   \]
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{x\in P\\\|x-q\|>cr}}
      \sum_{b:x\in C_b}\Pi_q(b)
      \le m^\rho L_m
      \qquad\text{for every promised query }q,
      \tag{BQ2}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{b:p\in C_b}\Pi_q(b)
      \ge L_m^{-1}
      \qquad\text{whenever }\|p-q\|\le r.
      \tag{BQ3}
   \]
   Then \(O(L_m)\) independent quotient panels give the terminal ANN bounds of
   \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Store one bucket \(C_b\) per bucket class \(b\).  Use the sampled-panel label
   set \(\mathcal B\), predicate
   \[
      A_b(x)=\mathbf 1\{x\in C_b\},
   \]
   and query law \(\Pi_q\).  The three displayed assumptions are exactly
   \((SP1)\), \((SP2)\), and \((SP3)\) for this quotient panel.  Therefore
   \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel} applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the quotient buys]
   \label{rem:bucket-quotient}
   \Cref{cor:bucket-quotient-quadrature} separates two entropies that were
   conflated in \cref{cor:weighted-gaussian-quadrature-panel}.  A trellis,
   code, lattice coset, or continuous Gaussian rule may have many internal
   labels, but the index stores point copies only for distinct bucket sets.
   Query time then depends on whether the decoder can compute or sample the
   aggregate weights \(W_q(b)\) without enumerating all labels in the bucket
   class.  Thus a structured decoder can win in either of two ways: by keeping
   the number of distinct stored buckets small, or by representing many labels
   with one bucket and sampling their total tilted mass directly.  The
   remaining certification is still the same three quantities: bucket storage,
   far sampled co-load, and near sampled mass.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Compressed aggregate sampler]
   \label{lem:compressed-aggregate-sampler}
   Let a finite acyclic directed graph have source \(s\), terminal states
   \(\mathcal T\), and edges \(e=(u,v)\).  A label is a path from \(s\) to a
   terminal \(t\), and each terminal has a bucket class \(b(t)\in\mathcal B\).
   Suppose the tilted weight of a path factors as
   \[
      \prod_{e\in\pi}r_e(q),
      \qquad r_e(q)>0.
   \]
   For a query \(q\), define suffix partition functions by the backward
   recursion
   \[
      H_q(t)=1\quad(t\in\mathcal T),
      \qquad
      H_q(u)=\sum_{(u,v)}r_{(u,v)}(q)H_q(v)
   \]
   at nonterminal states.  Then a top-down walk that, at state \(u\), chooses
   the edge \((u,v)\) with probability
   \[
      \frac{r_{(u,v)}(q)H_q(v)}{H_q(u)}
   \]
   outputs a terminal bucket \(b\) with probability proportional to the
   aggregate tilted weight of all labels ending in that bucket:
   \[
      \Pr[b(t)=b]
      =
      \frac{
         \sum_{\pi:s\leadsto t,\ b(t)=b}\prod_{e\in\pi}r_e(q)
      }{
         \sum_{\pi:s\leadsto t}\prod_{e\in\pi}r_e(q)
      } .
   \]
   The preprocessing may attach to each bucket class \(b\) its stored point set
   \(C_b\).  If the graph partition functions and the sampled path can be
   computed in \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\) time, and the induced bucket law satisfies
   \((BQ1)\)--\((BQ3)\), then the terminal ANN bounds of
   \cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel} follow.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For any state \(u\), the quantity \(H_q(u)\) is exactly the total tilted
   weight of all suffix paths from \(u\) to a terminal.  Therefore the displayed
   transition probabilities are normalized and sample a path with probability
   equal to its tilted weight divided by \(H_q(s)\).  Summing over all sampled
   paths whose terminal has bucket class \(b\) gives the displayed bucket
   probability.  The final statement is precisely
   \cref{cor:bucket-quotient-quadrature}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Trellis target for codes and lattices]
   \label{rem:trellis-quotient-target}
   \Cref{lem:compressed-aggregate-sampler} is the sampled analogue of the
   Fano/list-decoding DAG certificates earlier in the paper.  A code or lattice
   construction need not expose its labels as an explicit list.  It may expose
   a compact state graph whose path weights are the query reliabilities, whose
   terminals map to stored buckets, and whose dynamic program samples the
   quotient bucket law.  The remaining theorem for such a trellis is then only
   the spectrum certificate \((BQ1)\)--\((BQ3)\) plus the graph-size/query-time
   bound for evaluating \(H_q\).
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Tilted weight feasibility is linear]
   \label{lem:tilted-weight-linear-feasibility}
   Fix a finite retained label set \(\mathcal L\) and storage predicate
   \(A_g(x)\in\{0,1\}\).  Let \(\mathcal Q\) be a finite set of promised
   queries and let \(\mathcal N\subseteq P\times\mathcal Q\) be a finite set
   of near-pair witnesses.  For a label \(g\) and query \(q\), write
   \[
      R_q(g)=e^{\beta S_Q(q,g)},
      \qquad
      F_q(g)=\sum_{\substack{x\in P\\\|x-q\|>cr}}A_g(x),
   \]
   and fix targets \(B>0\), \(h>0\).  There are nonnegative weights
   \((w_g)_{g\in\mathcal L}\), \(\sum_gw_g=1\), whose tilted query law
   \[
      \pi_q(g)=\frac{w_gR_q(g)}{\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal L}w_\ell R_q(\ell)}
   \]
   satisfies
   \[
      \sum_g\pi_q(g)F_q(g)\le B
      \qquad(q\in\mathcal Q)
      \tag{TW1}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_g\pi_q(g)A_g(p)\ge h
      \qquad((p,q)\in\mathcal N)
      \tag{TW2}
   \]
   if and only if the same weights satisfy the homogeneous linear inequalities
   \[
      \sum_gw_gR_q(g)\bigl(F_q(g)-B\bigr)\le0
      \qquad(q\in\mathcal Q),
      \tag{TW3}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_gw_gR_q(g)\bigl(h-A_g(p)\bigr)\le0
      \qquad((p,q)\in\mathcal N).
      \tag{TW4}
   \]
   Equivalently, the finite retained pool fails to admit such weights iff
   there exist nonnegative dual prices
   \(\lambda_q\) for \(q\in\mathcal Q\) and
   \(\mu_{p,q}\) for \((p,q)\in\mathcal N\), not all zero, such that every
   retained label \(g\in\mathcal L\) has strictly positive reduced obstruction
   \[
      \sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}
      \lambda_qR_q(g)\bigl(F_q(g)-B\bigr)
      +
      \sum_{(p,q)\in\mathcal N}
      \mu_{p,q}R_q(g)\bigl(h-A_g(p)\bigr)
      >0.
      \tag{TW5}
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For any \(q\), the denominator
   \(\sum_gw_gR_q(g)\) is positive because the weights have total mass one and
   \(R_q(g)>0\).  Multiplying \((TW1)\) by this denominator gives \((TW3)\),
   and multiplying \((TW2)\) by the same denominator gives \((TW4)\).  This
   proves the first equivalence.

   Let \(a_g\in\mathbb R^{|\mathcal Q|+|\mathcal N|}\) be the column whose
   coordinates are the left-hand coefficients in \((TW3)\) and \((TW4)\).  The
   linear feasibility problem asks whether
   \[
      \operatorname{conv}\{a_g:g\in\mathcal L\}
   \]
   intersects the nonpositive orthant.  By the finite separating hyperplane
   theorem, equivalently Farkas' lemma, this intersection is empty iff there is
   a nonnegative separating vector, not identically zero, whose inner product
   with every column \(a_g\) is strictly positive.  Writing the coordinates of
   that separating vector as \(\lambda\) and \(\mu\) gives \((TW5)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Continuous Gaussian weights have finite test witnesses]
   \label{lem:continuous-gaussian-finite-witness}
   Work in the critical spherical normalization of
   \cref{lem:critical-tilted-gaussian-sampled-spectrum}.  Thus
   \(a_0=1-1/c^2\), \(\rho=1/(2c^2-1)\),
   \(T^2=2(1-\rho)\log m\), and \(\beta=T/a_0\).  Let
   \(P\subset\mathbb S^{d-1}\) have \(|P|=m\).  Let
   \(\mathcal Q\subset\mathbb S^{d-1}\) be a finite set of promised queries
   such that every invalid point for \(q\) has \(\ip{x}{q}\le0\), and let
   \(\mathcal N\subseteq P\times\mathcal Q\) be a finite set of near witnesses
   with \(\ip{p}{q}\ge a_0\).  For a Gaussian label \(g\in\R^d\), put
   \[
      A_g(x)=\mathbf 1\{\ip{g}{x}\ge T\},
      \qquad
      R_q(g)=e^{\beta\ip{g}{q}},
   \]
   and
   \[
      F_q(g)=
      \sum_{\substack{x\in P\\ x\text{ invalid for }q}}A_g(x).
   \]
   Then there are labels \(g_1,\ldots,g_s\in\R^d\) and nonnegative weights
   \(w_1,\ldots,w_s\), with
   \[
      s\le |\mathcal Q|+|\mathcal N|+1,
      \qquad
      \sum_iw_i=1,
   \]
   such that the tilted laws
   \[
      \pi_q(i)=
      \frac{w_iR_q(g_i)}{\sum_{\ell=1}^sw_\ell R_q(g_\ell)}
   \]
   satisfy
   \[
      \sum_i\pi_q(i)F_q(g_i)\le 2m^\rho
      \qquad(q\in\mathcal Q),
      \tag{CG1}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_i\pi_q(i)A_{g_i}(p)\ge\frac13
      \qquad((p,q)\in\mathcal N).
      \tag{CG2}
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let \(G\sim N(0,I_d)\), and let \(\gamma_q\) be the tilted law with density
   proportional to \(R_q(g)\) with respect to the law of \(G\).  Equivalently,
   \(G_q\sim N(\beta q,I_d)\).  By
   \cref{lem:critical-tilted-gaussian-sampled-spectrum}, for every
   \(q\in\mathcal Q\),
   \[
      \E_{\gamma_q}F_q(G)
      \le
      m\cdot m^{-(1-\rho)}
      =
      m^\rho,
   \]
   and, for every \((p,q)\in\mathcal N\),
   \[
      \E_{\gamma_q}A_G(p)\ge\frac12 .
   \]
   If \(Z_q=\E R_q(G)\), then the corresponding homogeneous coordinates from
   \cref{lem:tilted-weight-linear-feasibility} have expectations
   \[
      \E R_q(G)\bigl(F_q(G)-2m^\rho\bigr)
      =
      Z_q\bigl(\E_{\gamma_q}F_q(G)-2m^\rho\bigr)
      \le
      -Z_qm^\rho,
   \]
   and
   \[
      \E R_q(G)\left(\frac13-A_G(p)\right)
      =
      Z_q\left(\frac13-\E_{\gamma_q}A_G(p)\right)
      \le
      -\frac{Z_q}{6}.
   \]
   All these finitely many random variables are integrable, because
   \(F_q(G)\le m\) and \(R_q(G)\) is lognormal.  Therefore a sufficiently long
   finite iid sample \(G_1,\ldots,G_N\) has, with positive probability, all
   empirical averages of the displayed homogeneous coordinates nonpositive.
   Fix such a sample.  Its empirical column average lies in the convex hull of
   the sampled columns in
   \(\R^{|\mathcal Q|+|\mathcal N|}\).  Caratheodory's theorem represents the
   same average as a convex combination of at most
   \(|\mathcal Q|+|\mathcal N|+1\) sampled columns.  Applying
   \cref{lem:tilted-weight-linear-feasibility} to these labels and weights
   gives \((CG1)\) and \((CG2)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the finite witness lemma does not solve]
   \label{rem:continuous-witness-caveat}
   \Cref{lem:continuous-gaussian-finite-witness} says that the ideal Gaussian
   label universe has no tilted-weight dual obstruction on any finite test
   set.  Equivalently, if a Farkas certificate of the form \((TW5)\) exists,
   it is caused by restricting the labels to a proposed code, lattice, trellis,
   or stored panel, not by the Gaussian spectrum itself.

   This is only an offline separation statement.  It does not give the charged
   ANN index: the number of finite tests may be enormous, the selected labels
   need not have a compact decoder or sampler, and the unweighted bucket
   incidences in \((WQ1)\) are not controlled for the full all-query problem.
   Thus the remaining code/lattice theorem is precisely a low-storage,
   query-decodable quadrature theorem for the continuous tilted Gaussian
   calculation, with polylogarithmic rather than \(m^{o(1)}\) slack.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Small-signature Gaussian quadrature compiler]
   \label{cor:small-signature-gaussian-quadrature}
   Work in the setting of
   \cref{lem:continuous-gaussian-finite-witness}.  Let
   \(D=|\mathcal Q|+|\mathcal N|+1\), and suppose
   \[
      D\le m^\rho L_m
   \]
   for a polylogarithmic factor \(L_m\).  Then there is a retained weighted
   Gaussian panel with at most \(D\) labels such that
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P}
      |\{g:\ip{g}{x}\ge T\}|
      \le m^{1+\rho}L_m,
      \tag{SQ1}
   \]
   and, for every \(q\in\mathcal Q\),
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{x\in P\\ x\text{ invalid for }q}}
      \sum_{\substack{g\\\ip{g}{x}\ge T}}\pi_q(g)
      \le 2m^\rho,
      \tag{SQ2}
   \]
   while every \((p,q)\in\mathcal N\) satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{g\\\ip{g}{p}\ge T}}\pi_q(g)
      \ge\frac13 .
      \tag{SQ3}
   \]
   The query can sample from \(\pi_q(g)\propto w_g e^{\beta\ip{g}{q}}\) by a
   direct scan over the retained labels in \(O(d\,m^\rho L_m)\) arithmetic
   operations.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:continuous-gaussian-finite-witness} and keep the
   \(s\le D\) labels supplied there, after discarding any zero-weight labels.
   The far and near conditions are exactly \((CG1)\) and \((CG2)\).  For
   storage, each retained label stores at most all \(m\) data points, so the
   total number of point-label incidences is at most
   \(ms\le mD\le m^{1+\rho}L_m\).  Finally, direct sampling computes all \(s\)
   tilted weights \(w_g e^{\beta\ip{g}{q}}\), forms their prefix sums, and
   draws one label; since \(s\le m^\rho L_m\), this is within the stated work.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Signature entropy is the charged obstruction]
   \label{rem:signature-entropy-obstruction}
   \Cref{cor:small-signature-gaussian-quadrature} is deliberately finite.  It
   shows that once the continuum of promised queries and near witnesses is
   compressed to \(m^\rho\polylog m\) exact signatures, storage and sampling no
   longer require a sophisticated lattice decoder: a direct retained weighted
   panel already fits the terminal \(m^\rho\) budget.  For the full ANN problem
   the signature family is not known to be this small.  A code, lattice,
   trellis, or list-recovery construction should therefore be judged by the
   entropy of the exact certificates it represents: if it exposes too many
   query/witness signatures, then Caratheodory support alone blows up storage;
   if it represents them compactly and samples without scanning more than
   \(m^\rho\polylog m\) labels, the Gaussian quadrature part is already
   certified.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Metric nets are not the right signatures]
   \label{rem:metric-net-signature-barrier}
   The small-signature condition should not be interpreted as a standard net
   over query directions.  At the critical parameters,
   \[
      \beta=\frac{T}{a_0}=\Theta_c(\sqrt{\log m}).
   \]
   For a critical near pair with \(\ip{p}{q}=a_0\), the ideal tilted label
   satisfies
   \[
      \Pr[\ip{G_q}{p}\ge T]=\frac12.
   \]
   If a representative query \(q'\) has
   \(\ip{p}{q'}\le a_0-\Delta\), then
   \[
      \Pr[\ip{G_{q'}}{p}\ge T]
      =
      \Pr[N(0,1)\ge \beta\Delta].
   \]
   Thus a purely metric representative argument that must preserve the
   constant near mass for every possible critical witness needs
   \(\Delta=O(1/\beta)\), hence angular mesh \(O(1/\sqrt{\log m})\) up to
   constants.  A spherical net at that mesh in dimension \(d\) has size at
   least
   \[
      (c_0\sqrt{\log m})^{d-1}.
   \]
   In the usual terminal dimension \(d=\Theta(\log m)\), this is
   \(m^{\Theta(\log\log m)}\), far above the
   \(m^\rho\polylog m\) budget in
   \cref{cor:small-signature-gaussian-quadrature}.  Hence the desired
   signatures cannot be ordinary metric cells.  They must be reliability,
   rank-prefix, code, lattice, or trellis states whose entropy is tied to the
   active certificate rather than to a uniform cover of the sphere.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Lattice Gaussian panels as sampled decoders]
   \label{rem:lattice-gaussian-panels}
   \Cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel} is the sampler analogue of the
   threshold-reporter interface in
   \cref{lem:soft-list-recovery-reporter}.  It matches the coding-theoretic
   view even more literally: labels \(g\) may be codewords, lattice points,
   coset representatives, or states of a finite trellis, and the query samples
   a likely label instead of listing every active label.  A typical target is a
   discrete Gaussian or Gibbs law
   \[
      \pi_q(g)\propto
      \exp\!\left(-\tau\|Pq-g\|^2\right)
   \]
   on a structured code or lattice in the projected block.  Randomized nearest
   plane and lattice Gaussian samplers~\cite{Kle00,GPV08,Pei10} are relevant
   because they supply exactly this kind of law for decodable lattices; product
   filters and lattice sieving already exploit the same idea at the level of
   implicit filter families~\cite{BDGL16,GNR10}.

   This avoids the black-box weakness of ordinary lattice CVP or ANN.  The
   theorem does not ask the lattice decoder to return the nearest center, and
   it does not require an additive Gumbel optimizer over the data points.  It
   asks for the three directly checkable spectrum quantities \((SP1)\)--\((SP3)\):
   stored redundancy, far sampled co-load, and near sampled mass.  In
   particular, middle-density near sets are not an all-reporting obstruction:
   many valid buckets improve the sampling mass in \((SP3)\) without forcing
   the query to enumerate all of them.  This is the formal version of the
   empirical lesson that redundancy is the useful part of \(k\)-means
   multi-assignment, while Lloyd convergence itself is not a proof object.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Construction-A theta quotient]
   \label{lem:construction-a-theta-quotient}
   Let \(p\ge2\), let \(\Sigma=\mathbb Z/p\mathbb Z\), and choose representatives
   \(\iota(a)\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}\) for \(a\in\Sigma\).  For a code
   \(C\subseteq\Sigma^b\), put
   \[
      \Lambda(C,p)
      =
      \{\iota(c)+pz:\ c\in C,\ z\in\mathbb Z^b\}\subseteq\mathbb R^b .
   \]
   Fix a center \(y\in\mathbb R^b\) and inverse temperature \(\tau>0\).  For
   \(a\in\Sigma\) define the one-coordinate theta weight
   \[
      \Theta_a(y_i)
      =
      \sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}
      \exp\!\left(
         -\frac{\tau}{2}(y_i-\iota(a)-pk)^2
      \right),
   \]
   and for \(c\in C\) put
   \[
      \Omega_y(c)=\sum_{i=1}^b\log\Theta_{c_i}(y_i).
   \]
   Then the lattice discrete-Gaussian partition function factors through the
   residue code:
   \[
      \sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda(C,p)}
      \exp\!\left(-\frac{\tau}{2}\|y-\lambda\|^2\right)
      =
      \sum_{c\in C}\exp(\Omega_y(c)).
      \tag{CA1}
   \]
   Moreover exact sampling from the left-hand law is obtained by first sampling
   \(c\in C\) with probability proportional to \(\exp(\Omega_y(c))\), and then,
   independently over coordinates, sampling \(K_i\in\mathbb Z\) with
   \[
      \Pr[K_i=k\mid c]
      =
      \frac{
         \exp\!\left(
            -\frac{\tau}{2}(y_i-\iota(c_i)-pk)^2
         \right)}
      {\Theta_{c_i}(y_i)}
   \]
   and outputting \(\lambda_i=\iota(c_i)+pK_i\).

   The score form used in the product-panel certificates is the same identity:
   for
   \[
      S_y(\lambda)=\ip{y}{\lambda}-\frac12\|\lambda\|^2,
   \]
   the law proportional to \(\exp(\beta S_y(\lambda))\) is the
   \(\tau=\beta\) instance above, multiplied by the query-only factor
   \(\exp(\beta\|y\|^2/2)\).  Finally, if
   \[
      M_a(y_i)=
      \max_{k\in\mathbb Z}
      \left\{-\frac{\tau}{2}(y_i-\iota(a)-pk)^2\right\},
      \qquad
      M_y(c)=\sum_iM_{c_i}(y_i),
   \]
   then every lattice point with
   \(-\tau\|y-\lambda\|^2/2\ge\Theta\) has residue \(c=\lambda\bmod p\)
   satisfying \(M_y(c)\ge\Theta\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Expanding the lattice sum by residue class gives
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      \sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda(C,p)}
      e^{-\tau\|y-\lambda\|^2/2}
      &=
      \sum_{c\in C}
      \sum_{z\in\mathbb Z^b}
      \prod_{i=1}^b
      e^{-\tau(y_i-\iota(c_i)-pz_i)^2/2}  \\
      &=
      \sum_{c\in C}\prod_{i=1}^b\Theta_{c_i}(y_i)
      =
      \sum_{c\in C}e^{\Omega_y(c)} ,
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   proving \((CA1)\).  The displayed conditional sampler is exactly the same
   factorization normalized first over residues and then over the independent
   integer coordinates.  The score identity follows from
   \[
      \beta S_y(\lambda)
      =
      \frac{\beta}{2}\|y\|^2
      -
      \frac{\beta}{2}\|y-\lambda\|^2 .
   \]
   For the threshold statement, each coordinate contribution of a lattice point
   with residue \(c_i\) is at most \(M_{c_i}(y_i)\), so its total score is at
   most \(M_y(c)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Construction-A reduces lattice sampling to soft list recovery]
   \label{rem:construction-a-soft-decoding}
   \Cref{lem:construction-a-theta-quotient} is the most direct way to use
   Construction-A or Construction-D ideas here.  A lattice decoder does not have
   to search over all integer lifts first.  It can compute the theta
   reliabilities \(\log\Theta_a(y_i)\), run a soft decoder or partition-function
   recursion for the residue code \(C\), and then sample the one-dimensional
   integer lifts independently.  For threshold reporting, the max-residue scores
   \(M_a(y_i)\) give a certified soft list-recovery superset of residues that
   could contain any close lattice point; a standard sphere decoder can then
   enumerate the remaining integer lifts inside each reported residue.

   This matches the list-decoding literature more closely than a black-box CVP
   formulation.  Euclidean Reed--Solomon and Barnes--Sloane decoders use
   Koetter--Vardy style soft information exactly in this sense, while random and
   nested Construction-A list-decoding results describe the achievable list-size
   spectrum for the residue/lattice ensemble~\cite{MP20,ZV21}.  Expander-based
   list-recovery results are relevant as possible outer-code engines because
   the residue problem is now an ordinary soft list-recovery or partition
   problem on symbol reliabilities~\cite{ST25}.  The remaining ANN theorem is
   still the same one: after choosing rotations, moduli, scalings, and bucket
   thresholds, the retained Construction-A panel must satisfy the storage,
   far sampled co-load, and near sampled-mass certificates of
   \cref{cor:weighted-gaussian-quadrature-panel} or
   \cref{cor:bucket-quotient-quadrature}.  The theta quotient only removes an
   unnecessary lattice-search layer.
\end{remark}

\begin{assumption}[Flat structured spherical-code decoder]
   \label{asm:flat-spherical-code-decoder}
   Fix a localized terminal spherical gap with \(m\) resident points.  There is
   a distribution over finite structured code panels \(\mathcal G\).  Each
   \(g\in\mathcal G\) has a data predicate \(A_g(x)\), a query predicate
   \(B_g(q)\), and a bucket storing the points with \(A_g(x)=1\).  For every
   realized panel, the query decoder lists exactly
   \[
      \mathsf L(q)=\{g\in\mathcal G:B_g(q)=1\}
   \]
   in work \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m+O(|\mathsf L(q)|)\).  For every data set and
   every promised query, the panel distribution satisfies
   \[
      \E|\mathsf L(q)|\le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m,
      \qquad
      \E\sum_{x\in P}\sum_{g\in\mathcal G}A_g(x)
      \le m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   and the far co-load bound
   \[
      \E\sum_{\substack{x\in P\\ \|x-q\|>cr}}
      \sum_{g\in\mathcal G}A_g(x)B_g(q)
      \le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
   Finally, if \(p\) is a true near neighbor of \(q\), then one panel hits it
   with inverse-polylogarithmic probability:
   \[
      \Pr_{\mathcal G}\!\left[
         \exists g\in\mathcal G:\ A_g(p)B_g(q)=1
      \right]\ge \log^{-O_c(1)}m .
   \]
\end{assumption}

\begin{proposition}[Flat code decoder gives a terminal spherical engine]
   \label{prop:flat-spherical-code-terminal}
   Under \cref{asm:flat-spherical-code-decoder}, a terminal localized
   spherical instance of size \(m\) has a data structure with query work
   \[
      d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   and space
   \[
      dm+m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Store \(B=\log^{O_c(1)}m\) independent panels, where the exponent is large
   enough that \(B\) independent near-hit trials succeed with constant
   probability.  In each panel, put every data point \(x\) in every bucket
   \(g\) with \(A_g(x)=1\).  The expected total bucket storage is
   \(m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m\), plus the raw \(dm\) coordinates used for final
   metric verification.

   On query \(q\), decode \(\mathsf L(q)\) in every panel, scan all listed
   buckets, and verify candidate distances in the original metric.  The
   expected decoder work over all panels is
   \(d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m\), after increasing the polylogarithmic exponent
   to absorb \(B\).  The far co-load bound gives the same expected order for
   invalid candidates.  If \(p\) is a true near neighbor, the independent panels
   miss every bucket containing \(p\) with probability at most
   \((1-\log^{-O_c(1)}m)^B\), which is at most a fixed constant strictly below
   one for the chosen \(B\).  The standard capped-expectation convention from the
   preliminaries turns the expected work and storage bounds into the displayed
   capped resources after another constant-factor loss in success probability.
   Any reported point is verified, so the structure never returns a false
   answer.
\end{proof}

The flat-code target is a possible replacement for the terminal score tree, not
another proof of it.  A random Gaussian filter family has the desired spectrum
but no compact decoder.  Existing structured-code work gives pieces of the
right picture: spherical-code hashing analyzes angular nearest-neighbor
partitions induced by polytopes, root lattices, and spherical codes, while
polar-code nearest-neighbor search uses list decoding to multiprobe a
high-dimensional binary LSH space~\cite{Laa20,TH23}.  The missing theorem for
our purposes is the simultaneous combination of random-like cap spectrum,
Euclidean soft scores, and output-sensitive list enumeration at the critical
large-deviation scale.  Construction-A lattices, group-orbit spherical codes,
or concatenated/list-recoverable codes are useful candidates precisely because
they would turn the branching score tree into one decoding problem with the
resource conditions stated in \cref{asm:flat-spherical-code-decoder}.

\begin{lemma}[Finite sampled-cover dual]
   \label{lem:finite-sampled-cover-dual}
   Fix one query state $q$, a finite retained family of certified children
   $\mathcal C$, and a finite unresolved near-point set $N_q\subseteq P$.  For
   each child $C$, let $a_C(q)\in\{0,1\}$ be its activation indicator, let
   $Q_C\ge0$ be its certified query price, and let $\sigma_C\in[0,1]$ be its
   certified success credit.  Given route-mass targets $h_p\ge0$ for
   $p\in N_q$ and a query-price budget $L_q$, consider the sampled-cover
   feasibility problem
   \[
   \begin{array}{ll}
   \text{\rm find} & \pi_C\ge0\qquad(C\in\mathcal C)\\[0.3em]
   \text{\rm subject to}
      & \pi_C=0\qquad(a_C(q)=0),\\[0.4em]
      & \displaystyle \sum_C\pi_C\le1,\\[0.8em]
      & \displaystyle \sum_C\pi_CQ_C\le L_q,\\[0.8em]
      & \displaystyle
        \sum_{C:p\in P_C}\pi_C\sigma_C\ge h_p
        \qquad(p\in N_q).
   \end{array}
   \tag{$\mathsf S_q$}
   \]
   If $\mathsf S_q$ is infeasible, then there are route-demand prices
   $\beta_p\ge0$ $(p\in N_q)$, a query-work price $\lambda\ge0$, and a
   normalization price $\mu\ge0$ such that
   \[
      \sum_{p\in N_q}\beta_ph_p>\lambda L_q+\mu,
      \tag{SD1}
   \]
   while every active child has nonpositive sampled reduced gain,
   \[
      \sigma_C\sum_{p\in P_C\cap N_q}\beta_p
      \le
      \lambda Q_C+\mu
      \qquad(C:a_C(q)=1).
      \tag{SD2}
   \]
   Conversely, the existence of such prices certifies infeasibility of
   $\mathsf S_q$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The converse is immediate: if $\pi$ were feasible, then multiplying the
   route constraints by $\beta_p$ and summing gives
   \[
      \sum_p\beta_ph_p
      \le
      \sum_C\pi_C\sigma_C\sum_{p\in P_C\cap N_q}\beta_p
      \le
      \lambda\sum_C\pi_CQ_C+\mu\sum_C\pi_C
      \le
      \lambda L_q+\mu,
   \]
   contradicting \((SD1)\).

   For the forward implication, apply the finite-dimensional separating
   hyperplane theorem, equivalently Farkas' lemma, to the polyhedron
   $\mathsf S_q$.  Since all constraints are linear and the variables are
   restricted to active children by setting inactive variables to zero,
   infeasibility is exactly the existence of nonnegative multipliers for the
   lower-bound route constraints and upper-bound budget constraints such that
   the resulting linear combination has a strictly positive right-hand side
   but a nonpositive coefficient on every active variable.  Written with
   multipliers $(\beta,\lambda,\mu)$, those two conditions are precisely
   \((SD1)\) and \((SD2)\).
\end{proof}

Thus a finite sampled decoder has the same column-generation interpretation as
the all-listing cover, but with a different obstruction.  A bad query assigns
prices to unresolved near points and asks for one active child whose certified
success credit times its priced point mass beats its query price and the unit
normalization price.  If every surplus sampled dual certificate has such a
positive-gain child in the candidate universe, unbounded preprocessing can add
that child and repeat until the sampled route-mass LP is feasible.

\begin{lemma}[Finite existential sampled-cover dual]
   \label{lem:finite-existential-sampled-cover-dual}
   Fix one query state \(q\), a finite retained family of certified children
   \(\mathcal C\), and a finite unresolved near set
   \(N_q\subseteq P\). Assume \(N_q\ne\emptyset\).  Given a target \(h_0>0\)
   and query-price budget \(L_q\), consider the feasibility problem
   \[
   \begin{array}{ll}
   \text{\rm find} & \pi_C\ge0\qquad(C\in\mathcal C)\\[0.3em]
   \text{\rm subject to}
      & \pi_C=0\qquad(a_C(q)=0),\\[0.4em]
      & \displaystyle \sum_C\pi_C\le1,\\[0.8em]
      & \displaystyle \sum_C\pi_CQ_C\le L_q,\\[0.8em]
      & \displaystyle
        \sum_{C:\ P_C\cap N_q\ne\emptyset}\pi_C\sigma_C\ge h_0 .
   \end{array}
   \tag{$\mathsf E_q$}
   \]
   If \(\mathsf E_q\) is infeasible, then there are prices
   \(\lambda,\mu\ge0\) such that
   \[
      h_0>\lambda L_q+\mu,
      \tag{ED1}
   \]
   while every active child has nonpositive existential reduced gain,
   \[
      \sigma_C\mathbf 1\{P_C\cap N_q\ne\emptyset\}
      \le
      \lambda Q_C+\mu
      \qquad(C:a_C(q)=1).
      \tag{ED2}
   \]
   Conversely, such prices certify infeasibility of \(\mathsf E_q\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The converse is immediate: multiplying the price and normalization
   constraints by \(\lambda\) and \(\mu\), respectively, and using \((ED2)\)
   gives, for any feasible \(\pi\),
   \[
      \sum_{C:\ P_C\cap N_q\ne\emptyset}\pi_C\sigma_C
      \le
      \lambda\sum_C\pi_CQ_C+\mu\sum_C\pi_C
      \le
      \lambda L_q+\mu
      <
      h_0,
   \]
   contradicting the route-mass constraint.

   For the forward implication, apply the same Farkas separation as in
   \cref{lem:finite-sampled-cover-dual}.  The infeasibility certificate has
   nonnegative multipliers \((\beta,\lambda,\mu)\), where \(\beta\) prices the
   single existential lower-bound constraint.  Since its right-hand side is
   \(h_0>0\), any separating certificate has \(\beta>0\); divide by \(\beta\).
   The resulting normalized inequalities are exactly \((ED1)\) and \((ED2)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite sampled-route verifier]
   \label{lem:finite-sampled-route-verifier}
   Fix a finite retained family of certified children $\mathcal C$, a target
   set $E\subseteq P$, a residual block $U_0\subseteq E$, and a query domain
   $\Omega$.  For $q\in\Omega$, define the unresolved near set
   \[
      N(q)=\{p\in E\setminus U_0:\|p-q\|\le r\},
   \]
   and the sampled-route signature
   \[
      \tau(q)=
      \left(
         (a_C(q))_{C\in\mathcal C},
         (\mathbf 1\{p\in N(q)\})_{p\in E\setminus U_0}
      \right).
   \]
   Let $\Sigma$ be any finite set of signatures containing
   $\{\tau(q):q\in\Omega\}$.  For each $\tau\in\Sigma$, let
   $A_\tau\subseteq\mathcal C$ be its active-child set and
   $N_\tau\subseteq E\setminus U_0$ its unresolved near-point set.  Suppose
   that, for every $\tau\in\Sigma$, the sampled-cover feasibility problem
   \(\mathsf S_\tau\) from \cref{lem:finite-sampled-cover-dual} is feasible
   with route targets $h_p=h_0$ for all $p\in N_\tau$ and query-price budget
   $L$, using activation indicators $\mathbf 1\{C\in A_\tau\}$.

   Store one feasible sampling vector $\pi^\tau$ for every realized signature,
   and suppose the query decoder computes $\tau(q)$ and samples from
   $\pi^{\tau(q)}$ in work $D(q)$.  Then the hypotheses of
   \cref{lem:sampled-priced-cover} hold uniformly over $\Omega$: every near
   pair with $q\in\Omega$, $p\in E\setminus U_0$, and $\|p-q\|\le r$ receives
   route mass at least $h_0$, and the expected sampled child price is at most
   $L$.  Hence $B$ independent sampled child invocations give failure
   probability at most $\exp(-Bh_0)$ and expected work
   $O(B(D(q)+L))$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Fix $q\in\Omega$ and write $\tau=\tau(q)$.  By construction, the active
   coordinates and unresolved near-point coordinates in $\tau$ agree with the
   actual query.  The stored vector $\pi^\tau$ is feasible for
   \(\mathsf S_\tau\), so it is supported only on active children,
   has $\sum_C\pi^\tau_CQ_C\le L$, and for every
   $p\in N(q)=N_\tau$ satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{C:p\in P_C}\pi^\tau_C\sigma_C\ge h_0 .
   \]
   These are exactly the support, expected-price, and route-mass hypotheses of
   \cref{lem:sampled-priced-cover}.  Applying that lemma gives the stated
   success and work bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite existential sampled-route verifier]
   \label{lem:finite-existential-sampled-route-verifier}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:finite-sampled-route-verifier}, but for
   each finite signature \(\tau\) with \(N_\tau\ne\emptyset\) require only the
   existential feasibility problem \(\mathsf E_\tau\) from
   \cref{lem:finite-existential-sampled-cover-dual}, with active set
   \(A_\tau\), unresolved near set \(N_\tau\), target \(h_0\), and query-price
   budget \(L\).  Store one feasible sampler \(\pi^\tau\) for every realized
   nonempty signature, and suppose the query decoder computes \(\tau(q)\) and
   samples from \(\pi^{\tau(q)}\) in work \(D(q)\).

   Then every query with \(N(q)\ne\emptyset\) receives near-set route mass at
   least \(h_0\).  Hence \(B\) independent sampled child invocations fail to
   return any verified \(cr\)-neighbor with probability at most
   \(\exp(-Bh_0)\), and have expected work \(O(B(D(q)+L))\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Fix \(q\in\Omega\) with \(N(q)\ne\emptyset\), and put
   \(\tau=\tau(q)\).  The signature records both the active-child pattern and
   the exact unresolved near set, so \(N_\tau=N(q)\) and \(A_\tau\) is the
   actual active set.  The stored sampler is feasible for
   \(\mathsf E_\tau\), hence it is supported on active children, has expected
   child price at most \(L\), and satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{C:\ P_C\cap N(q)\ne\emptyset}\pi^\tau_C\sigma_C\ge h_0 .
   \]
   Apply \cref{lem:existential-sampled-priced-cover}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two-child existential sampler]\label{lem:two-child-existential-sampler}
   Fix one finite signature with active child family
   \(\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal C\), nonempty unresolved near set \(N\),
   certified prices \(Q_C\ge0\), success credits \(\sigma_C\in[0,1]\), and
   target \(0<h_0\le1\).  Suppose the existential feasibility problem
   \(\mathsf E\) is feasible:
   \[
      \pi_C=0\quad(C\notin\mathcal A),\qquad
      \sum_C\pi_C\le1,\qquad
      \sum_C\pi_CQ_C\le L,
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{C:\ P_C\cap N\ne\emptyset}\pi_C\sigma_C\ge h_0 .
   \]
   Then there are two not-necessarily-distinct active useful children
   \(C_1,C_2\), where \(P_{C_i}\cap N\ne\emptyset\), and weights
   \(\theta_1,\theta_2\ge0\) with
   \[
      \theta_1+\theta_2\le1,\qquad
      \theta_1Q_{C_1}+\theta_2Q_{C_2}\le L,
   \]
   and
   \[
      \theta_1\sigma_{C_1}+\theta_2\sigma_{C_2}\ge h_0 .
   \]
   Moreover, for
   \[
      K\ge\left\lceil 4h_0^{-1}\right\rceil ,
   \]
   there are integers \(k_1,k_2\ge0\) with \(k_1+k_2\le K\) such that the
   uniform sampler on \(K\) records, consisting of \(k_i\) copies of \(C_i\)
   and \(K-k_1-k_2\) no-op records, has expected child price at most \(L\) and
   near-set route mass at least \(h_0/2\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Delete all probability mass on non-useful children and move it to a no-op
   record.  This cannot increase expected price and does not decrease the
   existential route mass.  Now consider the linear program that maximizes
   \[
      \sum_{C:\ P_C\cap N\ne\emptyset}\pi_C\sigma_C
   \]
   over useful active children under
   \(\sum_C\pi_C\le1\), \(\sum_C\pi_CQ_C\le L\), and \(\pi_C\ge0\).  It has a
   feasible solution of value at least \(h_0\), so an optimal extreme point also
   has value at least \(h_0\).  Since the LP has only the two nontrivial
   resource inequalities, every basic feasible solution has at most two
   positive variables.  These positive variables are the desired
   \(C_1,C_2\), with weights \(\theta_1,\theta_2\); if the support has only
   one child, duplicate it and put the second weight equal to zero.

   For the integer sampler, set \(k_i=\lfloor K\theta_i\rfloor\).  Then
   \(k_1+k_2\le K\), and rounding down only decreases expected price:
   \[
      \frac{k_1Q_{C_1}+k_2Q_{C_2}}{K}
      \le
      \theta_1Q_{C_1}+\theta_2Q_{C_2}
      \le L .
   \]
   Since \(\sigma_{C_i}\le1\),
   \[
      \frac{k_1\sigma_{C_1}+k_2\sigma_{C_2}}{K}
      \ge
      \theta_1\sigma_{C_1}+\theta_2\sigma_{C_2}-\frac2K
      \ge
      h_0/2 .
   \]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Activation-pattern sampled-route verifier]
   \label{lem:activation-sampled-route-verifier}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:finite-sampled-route-verifier}, but group
   queries only by their active-child pattern
   \[
      a(q)=(a_C(q))_{C\in\mathcal C}.
   \]
