   If $\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal Q}$ has value $< |P|-L_0$, then there exist
   point prices $\alpha_x\in[0,1]$, query prices $\lambda_q\ge0$, and a storage
   price $\sigma\ge0$ such that
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P}\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S,
      \tag{D1}
   \]
   while every proposal satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
      \le
      \sigma s_C+
      w_C\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q} \lambda_q a_C(q).
      \tag{D2}
   \]
   Conversely, if such prices do not exist, then the fractional pool contains a
   cover of value at least $|P|-L_0$ under the displayed query and storage
   budgets.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The dual of $\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal Q}$ has variables $\alpha_x\ge0$ for the
   constraints $v_x\le\sum_{C:x\in P_C}z_C$, variables $\mu_x\ge0$ for
   $v_x\le1$, query variables $\lambda_q\ge0$, and storage variable
   $\sigma\ge0$.  It is
   \[
   \begin{array}{ll}
   \text{\rm minimize}
      & \displaystyle
        \sum_{x\in P}\mu_x
        +\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S\\[0.8em]
   \text{\rm subject to}
      & \alpha_x+\mu_x\ge1
        \qquad (x\in P),\\[0.4em]
      & \displaystyle
        \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
        \le
        \sigma s_C+
        w_C\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_q a_C(q)
        \qquad (C\in\mathcal C).
   \end{array}
   \]
   Since the primal is feasible and bounded, strong duality applies.  In an
   optimal dual solution we may assume $0\le\alpha_x\le1$ and
   $\mu_x=1-\alpha_x$.  Therefore the dual objective is
   \[
      |P|-\sum_x\alpha_x+
      \sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S .
   \]
   Having primal value below $|P|-L_0$ is exactly the existence of a dual
   feasible solution for which this objective is below $|P|-L_0$, which is
   equivalent to \((D1)\).  The remaining dual constraints are precisely
   \((D2)\).  The converse is the same duality implication read backward.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Shadow finite-pool dual]\label{cor:shadow-finite-pool-dual}
   Fix a target set $E\subseteq P$, a finite low-guard query constraint set
   $\mathcal Q$, and a finite proposal pool whose children are hereditary under
   taking subsets.  Replace every proposal $C$ by the exposed subchild with
   point set $P_C\cap E$, discard empty subchildren, and compute its query and
   storage weights using the exposed cardinality:
   \[
      w_{C,E}=|P_C\cap E|^\rho\Psi(R_C),
      \qquad
      s_{C,E}=|P_C\cap E|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_C).
   \]
   Consider the fractional cover program that maximizes
   $\sum_{x\in E}v_x$, covers only points of $E$, and uses the active and
   storage weights $w_{C,E},s_{C,E}$.  If its value is below $|E|-L_0$, then
   there are prices $\alpha_x\in[0,1]$ supported on $E$, query prices
   $\lambda_q\ge0$, and a storage price $\sigma\ge0$ such that
   \[
      \sum_{x\in E}\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S,
   \]
   while every proposal satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P_C\cap E}\alpha_x
      \le
      \sigma s_{C,E}
      +
      w_{C,E}\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_q a_C(q).
   \]
   Conversely, if no such supported price system exists, the exposed pool has a
   fractional cover of $E$ with residual value at most $L_0$ under the displayed
   budgets.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} with ground set $E$ and with the proposal
   pool consisting of the exposed subchildren $P_C\cap E$.  Heredity guarantees
   that each exposed subchild keeps the same radius and activation predicate
   and is still certified.  The displayed inequalities are exactly the dual
   certificate from \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} after extending the point prices
   by zero on $P\setminus E$.
\end{proof}

The dual gives a concrete way to attack the remaining geometric problem.  A
failure of the finite fractional cover produces a weighted residual set
$\alpha$, a query-load distribution $\lambda$, and a storage price $\sigma$ such
that every certified proposal has less $\alpha$-mass than its charged storage
and query footprint.  Thus the desired local theorem can be phrased without any
reference to Lloyd dynamics: for a rich enough proposal pool, rule out all
price systems satisfying \((D1)\)--\((D2)\), or the shadow-supported version in
\cref{cor:shadow-finite-pool-dual}, then use the offline optimizer and
deterministic verifier to retain only a low-stabbing cover.  In the
guard-restricted route the relevant target set is the low-guard shadow
$E_\gamma$, so dense regions outside $E_\gamma$ never need to be bought by a
dual witness or stored inside an exposed child.

\begin{corollary}[Dual-price proposal separation]\label{cor:dual-price-separation}
   Fix a finite universe $\mathcal C_\star$ of certified candidate children and
   the same finite query constraint set $\mathcal Q$.  For prices
   $(\alpha,\lambda,\sigma)$ define the reduced gain of a child $C$ by
   \[
      \operatorname{gain}_{\alpha,\lambda,\sigma}(C)
      =
      \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
      -
      \sigma s_C
      -
      w_C\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_q a_C(q).
   \]
   The full fractional program over $\mathcal C_\star$ has value at least
   $|P|-L_0$ if and only if every price system with
   \[
      \sum_x\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\lambda_qL_q+\sigma S,
      \qquad
      0\le\alpha_x\le1,\quad \lambda_q,\sigma\ge0,
   \]
   has some child $C\in\mathcal C_\star$ with
   \[
      \operatorname{gain}_{\alpha,\lambda,\sigma}(C)>0.
   \]
   Consequently, with unbounded preprocessing, a column-generation routine that
   repeatedly solves the current finite-pool LP and adds any positive-gain child
   from $\mathcal C_\star$ terminates with value at least $|P|-L_0$ whenever the
   full universe satisfies this separation property.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} to the full pool
   $\mathcal C_\star$.  Failure of value at least $|P|-L_0$ is exactly the
   existence of prices satisfying the displayed surplus inequality and
   \(\operatorname{gain}_{\alpha,\lambda,\sigma}(C)\le0\) for every
   $C\in\mathcal C_\star$.  Negating this statement gives the equivalence.

   For the algorithmic statement, $\mathcal C_\star$ is finite.  Each positive
   reduced-gain child violates the current dual certificate, so adding it either
   increases the current pool or the procedure has already reached the target
   value.  The routine therefore terminates after at most $|\mathcal C_\star|$
   additions, and the equivalence rules out termination below value
   $|P|-L_0$ under the separation property.
\end{proof}

This is the proof-aligned analogue of $k$-means++ seeding.  Instead of sampling
from squared-distance residuals, preprocessing may solve the current fractional
cover, read the dual prices, and search the very large proposal universe for a
positive-gain cap, medoid ball, pair-direction filter, or learned score.  The
search may be arbitrarily expensive; the retained index still pays only for the
children that survive the final deterministic certificate.

\begin{lemma}[Finite activation-pattern verifier]\label{lem:finite-activation-patterns}
   Fix a finite retained family of certified children $\mathcal C$ and a query
   domain $\Omega$.  Each child $C$ has an activation predicate
   $a_C(q)\in\{0,1\}$.  Let
   \[
      \mathcal A(\mathcal C)
      =
      \bigl\{
         (a_C(q))_{C\in\mathcal C}:q\in\Omega
      \bigr\}
      \subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathcal C}
   \]
   be the set of realizable activation patterns.  For any nonnegative child
   weights $w_C$,
   \[
      \sup_{q\in\Omega}\sum_{C\in\mathcal C}a_C(q)w_C
      =
      \max_{a\in\mathcal A(\mathcal C)}
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal C}a_Cw_C .
   \]
   Consequently an all-query active-power certificate for a finite retained
   family is exactly a finite list of linear constraints, one for each
   realizable activation pattern.  Since
   $|\mathcal A(\mathcal C)|\le2^{|\mathcal C|}$, unbounded preprocessing may
   verify the certificate by enumerating realizable patterns.  It is also safe
   to enumerate any finite superset of the realizable patterns; this can only
   make the certificate stricter.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The displayed equality is just grouping queries by their activation vector:
   the summand depends on $q$ only through $(a_C(q))_{C\in\mathcal C}$.  The
   remaining claims follow because the set of binary activation vectors of a
   finite family is finite, and a finite superset of the realizable vectors
   contains the maximum over the realizable ones.
\end{proof}

For the cap, shell-normalized cap, and ball predicates above, the activation
sets are finite Boolean combinations of explicit inequalities.  Thus the
pattern enumeration is a preprocessing certification problem.  The paper does
not need this enumeration to be efficient: after the certificate is accepted,
the query algorithm only evaluates its ordinary decoder and pays for the listed
children.

\begin{lemma}[Dominated child pruning]\label{lem:dominated-child-pruning}
   Let $\mathcal R$ be a finite retained family of certified children over a
   query domain $\Omega$.  Each child $C$ has a point set $P_C$, activation
   predicate $a_C(q)\in\{0,1\}$, active weight
   $w_C=|P_C|^\rho\Psi(R_C)$, and storage weight
   $s_C=|P_C|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_C)$.  Suppose two children $C,D\in\mathcal R$
   satisfy
   \[
      P_C\subseteq P_D,
      \qquad
      a_C(q)\le a_D(q)\quad(q\in\Omega).
   \]
   Then deleting $C$ preserves every routing guarantee witnessed by
   $\mathcal R$, and weakly decreases both the active-power certificate
   \[
      \sup_{q\in\Omega}\sum_{E\in\mathcal R}a_E(q)w_E
   \]
   and the storage-power certificate
   \[
      \sum_{E\in\mathcal R}s_E .
   \]
   Repeating this deletion process terminates with an antichain under the
   domination order
   \[
      C\preceq D
      \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
      P_C\subseteq P_D\text{ and }a_C\le a_D\text{ on }\Omega .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The active and storage sums only lose the nonnegative terms contributed by
   $C$, so the two certificates cannot increase.  For routing, consider a near
   pair whose previous witness was child $C$: the near point lies in $P_C$ and
   the query satisfies $a_C(q)=1$.  Since $P_C\subseteq P_D$ and
   $a_C(q)\le a_D(q)$, the same query also activates $D$, which contains the
   same near point.  Thus $D$ replaces $C$ as a valid witness.  The family is
   finite, so repeated deletions terminate, and termination means no dominated
   pair remains.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Shared-predicate decoder accounting]
   \label{lem:shared-predicate-decoder}
   Consider a retained family of nonempty certified children
   $\mathcal R$.  Partition it into parent groups $\mathcal G$ so that every
   child $D\in\mathcal R_g$ has the same activation predicate $a_g(q)$ as its
   parent group; write $a_D=a_g$ for $D\in\mathcal R_g$.  Suppose the query
   evaluates the distinct parent predicates in work $E(q)$ and, for each active
   group, enumerates the retained children in that group.  With
   \[
      w_D=|P_D|^\rho\Psi(R_D),
      \qquad
      s_D=|P_D|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_D),
   \]
   the child-record enumeration at query $q$ is bounded by
   \[
      |\{D\in\mathcal R:a_D(q)=1\}|
      \le
      \sum_{D\in\mathcal R:a_D(q)=1} w_D,
   \]
   and the retained child metadata is bounded by
   \[
      |\mathcal R|\le \sum_{D\in\mathcal R}s_D .
   \]
   Hence the query overhead of atom-safe subchildren is
   $O(E(q)+\sum_{D:a_D(q)=1}w_D)$, and their metadata is charged by the same
   storage-power certificate as their point copies.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Every retained child is nonempty, so $|P_D|\ge1$.  The padded radius factor
   satisfies $\Psi(R_D)=1+(R_D/r)^4\ge1$.  Therefore
   $w_D\ge1$ and $s_D\ge1$ for every retained child.  Summing these unit lower
   bounds over the active children gives the query-record inequality, and
   summing over all retained children gives the metadata inequality.  The
   decoder evaluates each distinct parent predicate once, then pays one record
   per active retained child, so the displayed work bound follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Atom-safe subchild separation]\label{lem:atom-safe-subchild}
   Fix finite query-pattern budgets $L_q>0$ and a storage budget $S>0$.  Let
   $C$ be any certified child with point set $P_C$, radius $R_C$, and activation
   vector $a_C(q)$ on the finite query set.  Write
   \[
      \Lambda_C=\sum_q\lambda_q a_C(q),
      \qquad
      \psi_C=\Psi(R_C).
   \]
   A subchild $D\subseteq P_C$ inherits the same radius and activation rule and
   has weights
   \[
      w_D=|D|^\rho\psi_C,
      \qquad
      s_D=|D|^{1+\rho}\psi_C .
   \]
   It is atom-safe if
   \[
      a_C(q)w_D\le L_q\quad(q\in\mathcal Q),
      \qquad
      s_D\le S .
   \]
   For fixed dual prices $(\alpha,\lambda,\sigma)$, the maximum reduced gain
   over all atom-safe subchildren of $C$ is obtained by sorting the points of
   $P_C$ in decreasing order of $\alpha_x$ and trying the prefixes of all
   atom-safe cardinalities.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Any $D\subseteq P_C$ remains certified because the ball and score
   constraints are hereditary under taking subsets.  For a subchild of
   cardinality $k$, the non-point part of the reduced gain is fixed:
   \[
      -\sigma k^{1+\rho}\psi_C
      -
      k^\rho\psi_C\Lambda_C .
   \]
   Thus, among all subchildren of size $k$, the one maximizing reduced gain is
   exactly the set of the $k$ largest point prices $\alpha_x$ inside $P_C$.
   The atom-safety constraints also depend only on $k$, $R_C$, and the activation
   vector of $C$.  Trying all feasible cardinalities therefore finds the best
   atom-safe subchild.
\end{proof}

Thus the bounded-atom hypothesis in
\cref{lem:fractional-cover-materialization} is not a new geometric object.  It
is a preprocessing normal form: replace a rich proposal universe by the finite
universe of all atom-safe certified subchildren.  Under dual prices, separation
against this expanded universe reduces to the same geometric proposal search,
followed by the prefix test of \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild}.
\Cref{lem:shared-predicate-decoder} shows that this expansion does not force a
query to rescore every retained subchild separately: subchildren inherit their
parent activation predicate, and their record enumeration is paid by the same
active-power sum.
\Cref{lem:dominated-child-pruning} gives the complementary retained-cover
normal form: along any nested chain of subchildren with inherited activation,
only maximal retained members need to be stored.

\begin{corollary}[Price-density subchild certificate]
   \label{cor:price-density-subchild}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild}, write the point prices in
   $P_C$ in nonincreasing order
   \[
      \alpha_1\ge\alpha_2\ge\cdots\ge\alpha_M,
      \qquad M=|P_C|,
   \]
   and put $A_k=\sum_{j\le k}\alpha_j$.  If some atom-safe cardinality $k$
   satisfies
   \[
      A_k>
      \psi_C\bigl(\sigma k^{1+\rho}+\Lambda_C k^\rho\bigr),
      \tag{PD}
   \]
   then the expanded atom-safe universe contains a positive-gain child.
   Conversely, if no atom-safe subchild of $C$ has positive gain, then every
   atom-safe cardinality $k$ satisfies
   \[
      \frac{A_k}{k}
      \le
      \psi_C\bigl(\sigma k^\rho+\Lambda_C k^{\rho-1}\bigr).
      \tag{PD$^\ast$}
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild}, the best atom-safe subchild of size $k$ is
   the set of the $k$ largest point prices.  Its reduced gain is
   \[
      A_k-\sigma\psi_C k^{1+\rho}-\Lambda_C\psi_C k^\rho .
   \]
   This is positive exactly when \((PD)\) holds.  Dividing the nonpositivity of
   this expression by $k$ gives \((PD^\ast)\).
\end{proof}

In the low-guard shadow program, \cref{cor:price-density-subchild} is applied to
the exposed parent $P_C\cap E_\gamma$ and to prices supported on
$E_\gamma$.  Unexposed points are discarded before the prefix test.  Thus a
candidate parent proposal is useful exactly when some prefix of its exposed
high-price points beats the storage and active-pattern charge after the
cardinality, query weight, and storage weight have all been computed on that
exposed prefix.  This prevents dense but guard-solved points from diluting the
price density of a proposal.

\begin{lemma}[Averaged proposal separation]\label{lem:averaged-proposal-separation}
   Fix any dual price system and any finite certified child universe
   $\mathcal C_\star$.  Let $\Pi$ be a probability distribution on
   $\mathcal C_\star$, and write
   \[
      \Lambda_C=\sum_q\lambda_qa_C(q)
   \]
   for the query-price load of $C$.  If
   \[
      \E_{C\sim\Pi}\left[
         \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
         -\sigma s_C
         -w_C\Lambda_C
      \right]>0,
   \]
   then some child in $\mathcal C_\star$ has positive reduced gain.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   If every child had nonpositive reduced gain, every average of reduced gains
   would be nonpositive.  The displayed strict positivity contradicts this.
\end{proof}

Thus a randomized build-time proposal mechanism can be used as a dual
separator without analyzing its convergence as a stored algorithm.  Given dual
prices, it is enough to produce a distribution over verified parent proposals
whose best atom-safe price prefix has positive expected gain.  Unbounded
preprocessing then samples or enumerates until it finds the positive-gain
certificate, adds that column, and discards all failed attempts.  The remaining
geometric theorem for cap or learned-score proposals can therefore be stated in
the price-density form \((PD)\): every surplus dual price system must expose a
certified radius-contracting proposal with enough high-price prefix mass to
beat its storage price and active-pattern price.

\begin{lemma}[Fractional cover materialization]\label{lem:fractional-cover-materialization}
   In the finite program of \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual}, assume $L_q>0$ for all
   $q\in\mathcal Q$, $S>0$, and the following atom bounds:
   \[
      a_C(q)w_C\le L_q \qquad(C\in\mathcal C,\ q\in\mathcal Q),
      \qquad
      s_C\le S \qquad(C\in\mathcal C).
   \]
   Suppose there is a feasible fractional solution $(z_C,v_x)$ of value at
   least $|P|-L_0$.  Let
   \[
      B\ge
      \max\left\{
         1,\log(4|P|),\log(8(|\mathcal Q|+1))
      \right\}.
   \]
   Then there exists an integral subfamily $\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal C$ and a
   residual set
   \[
      U_0=P\setminus\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal A}P_C
   \]
   such that
   \[
      |U_0|\le 4(L_0+1),
   \]
   and, simultaneously for every $q\in\mathcal Q$,
   \[
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal A}a_C(q)w_C\le 3B L_q,
      \qquad
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal A}s_C\le 3B S .
   \]
   Thus a finite fractional score cover can be stored as an ordinary certified
   cover with only logarithmic budget inflation and a comparable residual scan.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Put
   \[
      y_x=\sum_{C:x\in P_C}z_C .
   \]
   Since $v_x\le1$ and $v_x\le y_x$, the value assumption implies
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P}\bigl(1-\min(1,y_x)\bigr)
      \le
      \sum_{x\in P}(1-v_x)
      \le L_0 .
   \]
   Independently keep each child $C$ with probability
   \[
      p_C=\min(1,Bz_C).
   \]
   If no child containing $x$ has $p_C=1$, then
   \[
      \Pr[x\notin\cup_{C\in\mathcal A}P_C]
      \le
      \exp(-B y_x).
   \]
   If some containing child has $p_C=1$, the probability is zero.  Hence in all
   cases
   \[
      \Pr[x\in U_0]
      \le
      1-\min(1,y_x)+e^{-B}.
   \]
   Indeed, for $y_x\ge1$ the exponential bound is at most $e^{-B}$, while for
   $y_x\le1$ convexity gives
   $e^{-B y_x}\le 1-y_x+y_xe^{-B}\le1-y_x+e^{-B}$.
   Summing over $x$ and using $B\ge\log(4|P|)$ gives
   \[
      \E |U_0|\le L_0+\frac14 .
   \]
   By Markov's inequality,
   \[
      \Pr[|U_0|>4(L_0+1)]<\frac14 .
   \]

   For a fixed query $q$, define
   \[
      X_q=\sum_{C\in\mathcal A}a_C(q)w_C .
   \]
   The atom bound makes $X_q/L_q$ a sum of independent random variables in
   $[0,1]$, and
   \[
      \E X_q\le
      \sum_C a_C(q)w_C Bz_C
      \le B L_q .
   \]
   The standard Chernoff bound for sums of independent $[0,1]$ variables gives
   \[
      \Pr[X_q>3B L_q]\le e^{-B}.
   \]
   The same argument applied to
   $X_{\rm store}=\sum_{C\in\mathcal A}s_C$ gives
   \[
      \Pr[X_{\rm store}>3B S]\le e^{-B}.
   \]
   Since $B\ge\log(8(|\mathcal Q|+1))$, a union bound over all query constraints
   and the storage constraint has failure probability at most $1/4$.  With
   positive probability neither the residual event nor any budget event fails,
   so an integral subfamily with the displayed guarantees exists.  Unbounded
   preprocessing may find it by trying the random choices until the deterministic
   certificate passes, or by exhaustive search over the retained subfamilies.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Pattern-complete fractional cover]\label{lem:pattern-complete-cover}
   Fix a finite universe $\mathcal C_\star$ of certified children and a query
   domain $\Omega$.  Let
   \[
      \mathcal A_\star
      =
      \{(a_C(q))_{C\in\mathcal C_\star}:q\in\Omega\}
      \subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathcal C_\star}
   \]
   be the realizable activation patterns of the full proposal universe, or let
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}\supseteq\mathcal A_\star$ be any finite superset of
   patterns.  Consider the fractional cover program of
   \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} with one query constraint for each
   $a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}$,
   \[
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal C_\star} a_C w_C z_C\le L,
   \]
   and with storage budget $S$ and residual budget $L_0$.  If this program has
   value at least $|P|-L_0$ and the atom bounds
   \[
      a_Cw_C\le L\qquad(a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A},\,C\in\mathcal C_\star),
      \qquad
      s_C\le S
   \]
   hold, then an integral stored subfamily $\mathcal R\subseteq\mathcal C_\star$
   and residual block $U_0$ exist with
   \[
      |U_0|\le4(L_0+1),
      \qquad
      \sup_{q\in\Omega}\sum_{C\in\mathcal R}a_C(q)w_C\le3BL,
      \qquad
      \sum_{C\in\mathcal R}s_C\le3BS,
   \]
   where
   \[
      B\ge
      \max\{1,\log(4|P|),\log(8(|\widetilde{\mathcal A}|+1))\}.
   \]
   Conversely, if the fractional value is below $|P|-L_0$, then there are point
   prices $\alpha_x\in[0,1]$, pattern prices $\lambda_a\ge0$, and a storage
   price $\sigma\ge0$ such that
   \[
      \sum_x\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+L\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}}\lambda_a+\sigma S
   \]
   and every proposal has nonpositive pattern-priced gain,
   \[
      \sum_{x\in P_C}\alpha_x
      \le
      \sigma s_C
      +
      w_C\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}}\lambda_a a_C
      \qquad(C\in\mathcal C_\star).
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Treat each activation pattern $a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ as one query
   constraint in \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} and
   \cref{lem:fractional-cover-materialization}.  The rounding lemma gives an
   integral subfamily with residual, storage, and pattern-load bounds
   simultaneously for every $a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}$.  Since each actual
   query $q\in\Omega$ realizes a pattern in
   $\mathcal A_\star\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal A}$, the same bound holds for
   $\sup_q\sum_Ca_C(q)w_C$.  The displayed dual certificate is exactly
   \cref{lem:finite-pool-dual} with the query index set replaced by
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ and all query budgets equal to $L$.
\end{proof}

The factor $B$ is an index-size and query-time inflation, not a preprocessing
cost.  Thus this compiler feeds the polylogarithmic theorem only when the
retained activation class has polylogarithmic pattern entropy, for example when
after localization the relevant cap or score predicates have an arrangement
bound with $\log|\widetilde{\mathcal A}|=\log^{O_c(1)}m$.  If the exact pattern
set is much larger, the same LP and dual are still useful as an offline
certificate and separation diagnostic, but direct randomized materialization
would not by itself preserve polylogarithmic overhead; one would then need an
exact integral certificate or a sharper entropy-sensitive rounding theorem.
By contrast, an exact integral search as in
\cref{cor:offline-pareto-compression} pays no rounding factor at all.  Its
pattern enumeration may be enormous, but that cost is purely preprocessing.
Thus large pattern entropy is an obstacle only to proving an integral cover from
a fractional LP with the rounding lemma above; it is not an index-size obstacle
once an integral low-stabbing cover has been certified.

\begin{lemma}[Linear score pattern entropy]\label{lem:linear-pattern-entropy}
   Let $\Omega\subseteq\R^k$, and let the activation predicates be
   \[
      a_i(q)=\mathbf 1\{\langle u_i,q\rangle\ge b_i\},
      \qquad i=1,\ldots,N .
   \]
   Then the number of activation patterns
   \[
      \{(a_1(q),\ldots,a_N(q)):q\in\Omega\}
   \]
   is at most $N^{O(k)}$.  In particular,
   \[
      \log|\mathcal A|\le O(k\log(N+2)).
   \]
   The same bound holds if several certified children share the same
   activation predicate; duplicated predicates do not increase the number of
   realizable binary vectors except by duplicating coordinates.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The binary vector is constant on each relatively open face of the
   arrangement of the $N$ hyperplanes
   $\langle u_i,q\rangle=b_i$, with hyperplanes containing the face counted as
   active because of the weak inequality.  Thus it is enough to bound the number
   of faces of the arrangement.

   We use the standard induction.  When the $N$th hyperplane is inserted, the
   only new faces are obtained by cutting old faces that meet it.  Those cuts
   are controlled by the arrangement induced by the first $N-1$ hyperplanes on
   the new hyperplane, which has dimension at most $k-1$.  If $F(N,k)$ is the
   maximum number of relatively open faces of an arrangement of $N$ hyperplanes
   in $\R^k$, then
   \[
      F(N,k)\le F(N-1,k)+O(F(N-1,k-1)),
      \qquad
      F(N,0)=1,\quad F(0,k)=1 .
   \]
   Induction on $N+k$ gives $F(N,k)\le (CN)^k$ for a universal constant $C$.
   Restricting to $\Omega$ can only decrease the number of realized patterns.
   If predicates are duplicated, every duplicate coordinate is forced to equal
   its original predicate, so no new choices are created.
\end{proof}

Thus cap, shell-normalized cap, and affine score filters have polylogarithmic
pattern entropy after the standard projected localization, provided the number
of distinct activation predicates is $m^{\log^{O_c(1)}m}$ or smaller and the
projected dimension is $\log^{O_c(1)}m$.  The atom-safe subchildren used for
materialization inherit their parent's activation predicate, so they do not
increase this entropy.  Under these conditions \cref{lem:pattern-complete-cover}
contributes only another polylogarithmic factor.

Thus for modern regimes where preprocessing compute is relatively cheap, the
right theoretical target is even cleaner: generate a very large candidate pool
by residual sampling, all data directions, medoids, pair directions, or learned
scores; solve the active-power assignment problem as accurately as desired; and
store only a cover that passes the deterministic certificate.  Expensive build
time can amplify small discovery probabilities or replace greedy assignment by
exact integer programming over the candidate pool.  What remains for the
polylogarithmic ANN theorem is an existence-and-certification statement for a
small stored cover, not an efficient Lloyd-style training algorithm.
\Cref{lem:center-score-closure} means this candidate pool may be specified at
the template level: a seed proposes a score and center, while preprocessing
expands it into the finite set of data-score, data-radius superlevel parents
and then into atom-safe exposed prefixes.  More explicitly,
\cref{cor:offline-center-score-saturation} lets preprocessing saturate any
finite seed family under all data thresholds, data radii, and retained
memberships, run the deterministic certificates, and keep only the certified
cover on the desired query-memory frontier.  Finally,
\cref{cor:offline-recursive-pareto-pricing} lets this certification use the
actual recursive Pareto prices of candidate children rather than the closed-form
surrogate $|P_C|^\rho\Psi(R_C)$ whenever a better descendant certificate is
available.  If the retained family is equipped with a query sampler rather than
an all-listing decoder, \cref{lem:sampled-priced-cover} can certify it by
expected sampled price and near-pair route mass instead of active-power
stabbing.
\Cref{cor:seed-saturate-certify} packages the intended build loop.  A
$k$-means++ or spherical \(D^2\) run supplies only seed data; preprocessing then
saturates those seeds under all data thresholds, radii, submemberships,
recursive prices, and sampled-route certificates before choosing the retained
index.  The same wrapper supports a more theory-aligned alternative: use the
current cover dual prices, rather than squared-distance residuals, to sample
seeds that are likely to expose positive-gain score filters.  Thus Lloyd
updates are optional engineering refinements, while the theorem only needs a
positive-probability seeded separator plus deterministic certification.

\begin{lemma}[Shadow price-density compiler]\label{lem:shadow-price-density-compiler}
   Work in one bounded-diameter node with resident set $P$, $|P|=m$, radius
   $R$, guard threshold $\gamma$, low-guard domain $\Omega_{\rm low}$, and
   exposed shadow $E=E_\gamma$.  Let $\mathcal C_0$ be a finite universe of
   certified Lipschitz-score parent proposals.  Every parent $C\in\mathcal C_0$
   has point set $P_C$, radius $R_C\le R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}$, activation predicate
   $a_C(q)$ on $\Omega_{\rm low}$, and hereditary certification under taking
   subsets.  Let $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ be a finite superset of the
   low-guard activation patterns of these parents.  When parent proposals are
   expanded into atom-safe subchildren below, each parent coordinate is
   duplicated for all its subchildren, and we use the same symbol
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ for the lifted pattern set.  Set
   \[
      B\ge
      \max\{1,\log(4(|E|+1)),\log(8(|\widetilde{\mathcal A}|+1))\}.
   \]
   Fix budgets $L>0$, $S>0$, and $L_0\ge0$.

   Suppose the following shadow separation property holds.  For every choice
   of point prices $\alpha_x\in[0,1]$ supported on $E$, pattern prices
   $\lambda_a\ge0$, and storage price $\sigma\ge0$ with surplus
   \[
      \sum_{x\in E}\alpha_x
      >
      L_0+L\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}}\lambda_a+\sigma S,
      \tag{S1}
   \]
   there is a parent proposal $C\in\mathcal C_0$ and an atom-safe cardinality
   $k$ for its exposed set $P_C\cap E$ such that, after sorting the exposed
   points of $P_C\cap E$ by decreasing $\alpha_x$ and writing
   $A_k$ for the prefix sum,
   \[
      A_k>
      \Psi(R_C)
      \left(
         \sigma k^{1+\rho}
         +
         k^\rho
         \sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal A}}\lambda_a a_C
      \right).
      \tag{S2}
   \]
   Here atom-safe means that the exposed prefix of size $k$ has storage at most
   $S$ and active weight at most $L$ on every pattern in
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$.

   Then unbounded preprocessing can build an integral retained family
   $\mathcal R$ of exposed subchildren and a residual block $U_0\subseteq E$
   such that
   \[
      |U_0|\le4(L_0+1),
      \qquad
      \sup_{q\in\Omega_{\rm low}}
      \sum_{D\in\mathcal R}a_D(q)|P_D|^\rho\Psi(R_D)
      \le3BL,
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{D\in\mathcal R}|P_D|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_D)\le3BS .
   \]
   The retained children cover $E\setminus U_0$, are certified, and all have
   radius at most $R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Expand the proposal universe as follows.  For every parent
   $C\in\mathcal C_0$, intersect it with $E$, and include every atom-safe
   subset of $P_C\cap E$.  Since $P$ is finite, this expanded universe is
   finite; for any fixed price vector the best atom-safe subchild of a parent
   is the prefix identified by \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild}.  Each exposed
   subchild inherits the parent's activation predicate and radius certificate.

   Lift $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$ to the expanded universe by duplicating each
   parent coordinate for all exposed subchildren of that parent.  Run the
   pattern-complete fractional cover program on this expanded exposed universe,
   with the resident ground set in \cref{lem:pattern-complete-cover} replaced by
   $E$, pattern set $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$, active budget $L$, storage budget
   $S$, and residual budget $L_0$.  If its value were below $|E|-L_0$,
   \cref{cor:shadow-finite-pool-dual}, applied with query constraints indexed by
   the lifted patterns, would give prices satisfying \((S1)\) and having
   nonpositive gain on every exposed atom-safe subchild.  But by the assumed
   separation property, some parent has an atom-safe exposed prefix satisfying
   \((S2)\), which is exactly positive reduced gain for that subchild.  This
   contradiction proves that the fractional value is at least $|E|-L_0$.

   Apply \cref{lem:pattern-complete-cover} to materialize the fractional exposed
   cover.  The atom-safety hypotheses are part of the expanded universe, and
   duplicated activation predicates do not change the pattern set.  The
   materialized family covers all but $4(L_0+1)$ exposed points, has active
   pattern load at most $3BL$, and has storage at most $3BS$.  Since every
   actual low-guard query realizes a pattern in
   $\widetilde{\mathcal A}$, the same active bound holds uniformly over
   $\Omega_{\rm low}$.  Unbounded preprocessing may find the retained family by
   exhaustive search or by column generation using the positive-gain prefix
   oracle, and stores only the final certified family and residual block.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Las Vegas seeded shadow separation]
   \label{cor:seeded-shadow-separation}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:shadow-price-density-compiler}.  Let
   $\mathcal S$ be a finite seed space, and let a deterministic proposal map
   associate to every seed $s\in\mathcal S$ a finite family
   $\mathcal G(s)\subseteq\mathcal C_0$ of certified parent proposals.  Suppose
   that, for every surplus price system satisfying \((S1)\), preprocessing can
   sample seeds from a distribution
   $\mu_{\alpha,\lambda,\sigma}$ on $\mathcal S$ such that the set of successful
   seeds has positive probability.  Here $s$ is successful if some
   $C\in\mathcal G(s)$ has an atom-safe exposed prefix satisfying \((S2)\) for
   the current prices.

   Then the conclusion of \cref{lem:shadow-price-density-compiler} holds with
   parent universe $\bigcup_{s\in\mathcal S}\mathcal G(s)$.  Moreover,
   unbounded preprocessing may implement the separation oracle by repeated
   seed sampling and deterministic prefix verification; no lower bound on the
   success probability is charged to query time or index space.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Fix a surplus price system.  By assumption the successful seed set has
   positive $\mu_{\alpha,\lambda,\sigma}$-probability.  Repeated independent
   sampling therefore finds a successful seed almost surely.  For that seed,
   deterministic verification over the finite family $\mathcal G(s)$ and the
   atom-safe prefix test of \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild} finds a parent
   proposal satisfying \((S2)\).  Thus the shadow separation hypothesis of
   \cref{lem:shadow-price-density-compiler} holds for the finite parent
   universe generated by all seeds.

   A column-generation implementation solves the current finite-pool LP.  If
   the current pool is insufficient, its dual prices satisfy \((S1)\), so the
   repeated seeded search adds a positive-gain parent from the finite universe.
   Since the universe is finite, this can happen only finitely many times before
   the fractional cover value reaches the target.  The eventual
   materialization and certification are exactly those of
   \cref{lem:shadow-price-density-compiler}.  All failed samples, rejected
   proposals, and intermediate columns are discarded preprocessing artifacts.
\end{proof}

\begin{assumption}[Weak offline score-cover condition]\label{asm:weak-offline-score-cover}
   Fix $c>1$, $\rho=\rho_c$, and $\eta>0$.  In every bounded-diameter localized
   node with $m$ resident points in a certified ball of radius $R$, either the
   node is terminal, or expensive preprocessing can build the following
   certified object.  It is enough that a verifiable randomized build routine
   finds it with any positive probability, since preprocessing can then repeat
   until certification as in \cref{lem:las-vegas-offline}.

   Let
   \[
      \gamma_m=m^{-\rho}\log^{-O_c(1)}m,
      \qquad
      \Omega_{\rm low}=\{q:g(q)<\gamma_m\},
   \]
   and let $E_{\gamma_m}$ be the low-guard shadow from
   \cref{lem:low-guard-shadow}.  There is a residual block
   $U_0\subseteq E_{\gamma_m}$ and a query-decodable family of Lipschitz score
   filters
   \[
      (P_i,o_i,R_i,\phi_i,\theta_i,L_i)_{i\in I}
   \]
   covering $E_{\gamma_m}\setminus U_0$, with
   \[
      |U_0|\le F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m,
      \qquad
      R_i\le R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}.
   \]
   The decoder lists every filter satisfying
   $\phi_i(q)\ge\theta_i-L_i r$ in time
   $dF_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m$ plus output cost.  Unless the resident guard
   density at $q$ is at least $m^{-\rho}\log^{-O_c(1)}m$, the listed filters
   satisfy
   \[
      \sum_{i:\phi_i(q)\ge\theta_i-L_i r}
      |P_i|^\rho\Psi(R_i)
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R)
   \]
   for a constant $\kappa=\kappa(c)>0$, and the stored filters satisfy
   \[
      \sum_i |P_i|^{1+\rho}\Psi(R_i)
      \le
      mF_4(m,R).
   \]
   The cover is low-guard routing-correct: if $q\in\Omega_{\rm low}$,
   $\|p-q\|\le r$, and $p\in E_{\gamma_m}$, then $p\in U_0$ or $p$ lies in a
   listed filter.

   Finally, the preprocessing routine may obtain this object by any amount of
   offline computation.  In particular, it is enough that either a residual
   sampler as in \cref{lem:weak-residual-cover}, or exhaustive residual
   data-direction enumeration as in \cref{lem:exhaustive-residual-cover},
   together with a deterministic verifier satisfies the weak residual condition
   until the remaining residual has size at most
   $F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m$, and that the retained family passes the displayed
   deterministic active-power and storage certificates.  Equivalently, by
   \cref{lem:finite-pool-optimizer} and
   \cref{cor:offline-pareto-compression}, it is enough that a finite proposal
   pool generated during preprocessing contains one cover passing these
   certificates; preprocessing may then choose the memory-minimal or
   query-minimal accepted cover from that pool.  In the center-score model,
   \cref{cor:offline-center-score-saturation} reduces this finite-pool
   requirement further: the build routine only has to generate a finite family
   of centers and scores whose data-threshold, data-radius closure contains an
   accepted cover.  Equivalently, \cref{cor:seed-saturate-certify} allows the
   build routine to generate those centers and scores by $k$-means++,
   spherical \(D^2\) seeding, dual-weighted seeding, learned proposal
   generation, or exhaustive enumeration; the saturated universe is searched
   during preprocessing and only the final certified retained family is stored.
   The displayed $F_4$ weights are a sufficient universal
   price certificate; by \cref{lem:priced-cover-compiler} and
   \cref{cor:offline-recursive-pareto-pricing}, the verifier may instead charge
   any child by its actual certified recursive query and storage prices, and
   preprocessing may optimize over the resulting finite Pareto frontier.  A
   sampled decoder may replace the active-power certificate by the route-mass
   certificate of \cref{lem:sampled-priced-cover}: for every low-guard near
   pair not in the residual block, the sampling distribution must put
   inverse-polylogarithmic success mass on retained children containing the near
   point, while its expected certified child price remains
   $F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m$.  For any finite low-guard query certificate,
   \cref{lem:finite-sampled-cover-dual} turns this sampled route-mass condition
   into finite LP constraints, or equivalently into the absence of a
   route-demand dual price system satisfying \((SD1)\)--\((SD2)\).
   Since ANN only needs one valid near neighbor, the per-near-point route-mass
   constraints may be replaced by the existential near-set certificate of
   \cref{lem:existential-sampled-priced-cover}: for each query signature with
   nonempty unresolved near set, the sampler only has to put
   inverse-polylogarithmic mass on children that contain at least one of those
   near points.  The finite obstruction is then the simpler dual
   \((ED1)\)--\((ED2)\) of
   \cref{lem:finite-existential-sampled-cover-dual}.
   \Cref{lem:finite-sampled-route-verifier} gives the all-query version: it is
   enough to check these sampled LPs on the finite signatures consisting of the
   retained activation pattern together with the unresolved near-point set.
   \Cref{lem:finite-existential-sampled-route-verifier} is the corresponding
   existential version on the same signatures.
   By \cref{lem:two-child-existential-sampler}, each feasible exact
   existential signature can be retained as two useful child identifiers plus
   \(O(h_0^{-1})\) no-op/child cohort records, rather than as a full
   real-valued LP solution.
   The stronger certificate of
   \cref{lem:activation-sampled-route-verifier} avoids storing or decoding this
   near-point coordinate: for each retained activation pattern it checks the
   union of all unresolved near points compatible with that pattern.
   By \cref{lem:sampled-route-cohort}, a feasible sampled LP for a signature
   can be implemented by a uniform cohort of
   \(\log^{O_c(1)}m\) child records whenever the target route mass is
   inverse-polylogarithmic; arbitrary real sampling vectors need not be stored.
   In the activation-only case,
   \cref{lem:activation-cohort-sampled-compiler} combines these two steps into
   the retained object itself: one cohort per activation pattern, with no-op
   records representing unused probability mass.
   \Cref{cor:activation-cohort-resources} is the corresponding resource test:
   for inverse-polylogarithmic route mass, expected child price, and decoder
   work, the sampled route contributes only polylogarithmic query overhead
   exactly when the retained pattern decoder and cohort table fit within the
   local storage budget.  The preprocessing search for the LPs and cohorts may
   be arbitrarily expensive, but this retained metadata is not free.
   A deterministic prefix-list decoder may replace the sampled route
   certificate.  In that version, the retained score tree supplies admissible
   upper scores \(U_q(u)\), a threshold \(\Theta\), and an output-sensitive
   child generator satisfying \cref{cor:prefix-list-resources} with
   \(L_{\rm pref},L_{\rm term}\le F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m\); every low-guard
   near pair not in the residual block must have some certified terminal route
   above threshold.  This is the lattice-list-decoding formulation of the same
   local problem: prove a small threshold list of plausible routes, then verify
   candidates in the original metric.
   By \cref{lem:guard-restricted-cover}, these active-power constraints need
   only range over the low-guard query domain; high-guard queries are handled by
   the resident sample.  By \cref{lem:finite-activation-patterns}, the
   low-guard active-power certificate for any finite retained family is
   equivalently a finite set of activation-pattern constraints.  By
   \cref{cor:shadow-finite-pool-dual}, the corresponding fractional cover
   problem may use $E_{\gamma_m}$ as its ground set, intersect every proposal
   with this shadow, and put dual point prices only on exposed points.
   Equivalently, by \cref{lem:pattern-complete-cover}, it is enough to solve
   the fractional cover over a polylog-entropy finite superset of the low-guard
   activation patterns of the shadow-intersected finite proposal universe, with
   logarithmically smaller budgets and bounded atoms.  The bounded-atom normal
   form can be enforced by expanding the proposal universe to atom-safe exposed
   subchildren as in \cref{lem:atom-safe-subchild}; then the pattern-complete
   rounding converts the fractional solution into a stored cover and residual
   block, losing only another logarithmic factor.  In the equivalent
   price-density form of \cref{lem:shadow-price-density-compiler}, it is enough
   to prove that every surplus dual price system supported on $E_{\gamma_m}$
   exposes a certified parent proposal whose highest-price exposed prefix
   satisfies \((S2)\), after the active and storage budgets have been divided by
   the pattern-complete rounding factor.  By
   \cref{cor:seeded-shadow-separation}, this exposure theorem may be stated in
   seeded form: for the current dual prices, any seed distribution with positive
   probability of proposing such a parent can be amplified during preprocessing,
   and only the final certified cover is stored.
\end{assumption}

\begin{proposition}[Weak offline score covers imply the polylog endpoint]
   \label{prop:weak-offline-score-cover-polylog}
   If \cref{asm:weak-offline-score-cover} holds uniformly for
   \[
      \eta=\Theta_c\!\left(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\right),
   \]
   and terminal nodes are handled by resident scans or by
   \cref{thm:smooth-product-terminal}, then worst-case Euclidean ANN has
   \[
      Q(n,d)=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      S(n,d)=dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n .
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Las Vegas offline certification makes every constructed node satisfy the
   deterministic certificate; failed proposals are discarded before the index is
   stored.  At query time, first sample the resident guard as in
   \cref{prop:power-cover-polylog}.  If the guard-density alternative holds,
   the guard succeeds with constant probability after increasing the logarithmic
   factor.

   Otherwise scan $U_0$ and recurse into all listed score filters.  Correctness
   follows from \cref{lem:low-guard-shadow} and
   \cref{lem:lipschitz-score-cover}: on a low-guard query a near point lies in
   the low-guard shadow and is either in the scanned residual, or lies in a
   filter whose Lipschitz slack makes the query list it.
   The residual scan costs at most
   $dF_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}m$, and the listed recursive power contracts by
   $(1-\kappa\eta^2)$.

   The rest is the recurrence from \cref{prop:power-cover-polylog}.  Each
   recursive score-cover child decreases radius by
   $\sqrt{1-\eta^2}$, so there are only
   $O_c(\eta^{-2}\log(R_0/r))=\log^{O_c(1)}n$ radius levels after
   localization.  The per-level guard, decoder, and residual-scan costs are
   $dF_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}n$, while the active child sum contracts.  Summing the
   resulting geometric recurrence gives
   $dF_4(n,R_0)\log^{O_c(1)}n=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n$ query work.  The
   storage certificate and the residual blocks add only the same polylogarithmic
   level factor, giving $dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n$ space.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Simplicity and implementation}

The filtering work remains level-synchronous. At one checkpoint, all active score paths can be advanced by a batched matrix multiplication, followed by continuation-mass pruning, cap-test lookup, randomized child thinning, and resident scans. Recentering updates stored coordinate offsets; it does not start a fresh score tree.

The data structure stores only the tree metadata:
\[
   (\text{score seed},\text{bucket IDs},o_C,R_C,z_C,a_C,\text{resident block}).
\]
The same score kernels are used before and after recentering; only the coordinate offset and active bucket ID change.

\section{Why the one-tree route could remove compounded overhead}

The restart analysis pays for the same loss once per geometric level. The cumulative process is designed to avoid that multiplication in two ways.

\paragraph{The score process is global.}
Continuation-mass pruning controls the total number of explored prefixes in one cumulative tree. The budgeted form in \cref{cor:radius-budgeted-block} lets a node spend the full radius-weighted potential $F(m,R)$ inside the same score block by using the effective budget $N=m(R/r)^{2(1-\rho)/\rho}$. Recentered children inherit the score prefix and only change the coordinate offset for future increments. If the mixed stopping-frontier operator holds, there is no per-child independent spherical failure penalty in the target process.

\paragraph{Geometry is paid by randomized thinning.}
For an almost-total cap of relative size $z$ and radius ratio encoded by $\theta=\Psi(R_C)/\Psi(R)$, the query follows the cap child with probability $z^s\theta$ and always follows the residual. The expected recursive potential is
\[
   F_4((1-z)m,R)+z^s\theta F_4(zm,R_C)
   =
   F_4(m,R)\bigl((1-z)^\rho+z\theta^2\bigr),
\]
which contracts whenever $(1-z)^\rho\ll\eta^2$. The nontrivial part is summing these charges over all random cap-discovery frontiers of the branching decoder. Prefix-freeness alone is not enough, because the continuation decoder is a branching set of retained prefixes rather than one normalized path.

\paragraph{Terminal scans are low-correlation scans.}
At a cap-free accepting leaf, the query direction is not an almost-total cap. An inverse-polylogarithmic fraction of the bucket therefore has correlation at most $2\eta$ with the query, and the whole scan is charged to those ordinary low-correlation accepting-leaf collisions.

\section{An exponent-improvement ladder}

The proof program need not jump directly from the restart construction to the polylogarithmic target. A useful intermediate calculus is the following. Suppose the logarithm of the nonpolynomial overhead has the form
\[
   O_c\!\left(\eta^p L+\eta^{-q}L^\alpha\right),
   \qquad L=\log n,
\]
where:
\begin{itemize}
   \item $L^\alpha$ is the loss of one score block;
   \item $\eta^{-q}$ is the number of independently paid geometric phases;
   \item $\eta^p L$ is the exponent cost of geometric slack.
\end{itemize}
Balancing the two terms gives
\[
   \eta=L^{-(1-\alpha)/(p+q)}
\]
and overhead exponent
\[
   \theta(p,q,\alpha)
   =
   \frac{q+p\alpha}{p+q},
   \qquad
   \exp(O_c(L^\theta)).
\]

\begin{table}[t]
   \centering
   \caption{Useful milestones for reducing the nonpolynomial overhead.}
   \label{tab:ladder}
   \begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
      \toprule
      Milestone & New ingredient & Parameters & Exponent \\
      \midrule
      Restart & $k^{1/3}$ spherical loss & $(1,2,1/3)$ & $7/9$ \\
      Score-only & polylog spherical decoder & $(1,2,0)$ & $2/3$ \\
      One geometric gain & fewer phases or quadratic slack & $(1,1,0)$ or $(2,2,0)$ & $1/2$ \\
      Randomized one tree & cube-root geometric frontier bound & no restart phases; $\eta=L^{-2/3}$ & $1/3$ \\
      Adaptive continuation & all-scale mixed continuation operator & $\eta\asymp(\log\log n)/L$ & $\polylog$ \\
      Bellman cohorts & no independent phase payment & no $\eta^{-q}$ term & $\polylog$ \\
      \bottomrule
   \end{tabular}
\end{table}

This ladder is useful for stress testing. The continuation-mass spherical theorem already moves the restart architecture from exponent $7/9$ to roughly $2/3$. The randomized one-tree route below removes the independent phase count for almost-total cap splits; a cube-root-scale geometric frontier inequality gives a conditional $1/3$ rung, while proving the same continuation operator uniformly down to $\eta\asymp(\log\log n)/L$ would give the polylogarithmic rung. The Bellman cohort mechanism is the broader endpoint for arbitrary certified children: if cohort-or-leaf holds, recursive geometry is paid by the parent potential and the $\eta^{-q}$ term disappears rather than being optimized.

\section{A restartable slice route to the 2/3 rung}

There is a more concrete way to reach the $2/3$ milestone without proving the persistent route cohort-or-leaf lemma. The point is to restart deliberately after every recentering, but to recurse only on leaf-conditioned slices. This avoids the obstruction in which one atypical score prefix from a large child would have to fund a recursive search of the whole child.

Fix one spherical stage on a shell in a ball $\Ball(o,R)$. Let $\eta>0$ and set
\[
   \tau=\log^{-A_c} n.
\]
Run the continuation-mass spherical decoder and regard its accepting buckets as hard leaves for this stage only. At each leaf bucket $U$, repeatedly search for a heavy cap of margin $2\eta$, meaning a direction $w$ such that
\[
   \left|\left\{x\in U:\ip{(x-o)/R}{w}\ge 2\eta\right\}\right|
   \ge \tau |U|.
\]
When such a cap is found, store an $\eta$-margin slice
\[
   D[U,v]=\left\{x\in U:\ip{(x-o)/R}{v}\ge \eta\right\}
\]
as a certified child. Since every point in it has inner product at least $\eta R$ with direction $v$,
\[
   D[U,v]\subseteq \Ball(o+\eta Rv,\,R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}),
\]
and the implementation certifies the actual child radius by computing the maximum distance to the proposed center. After no heavy $2\eta$-cap remains, the residual bucket is scanned. Every certified child starts a fresh spherical stage in its own coordinates.

\begin{lemma}[Residual scan after slice peeling]\label{lem:slice-residual}
   In one restartable slice stage, scanning the final cap-free residual leaf buckets costs only a $(1+O(\tau))$ factor more than the ordinary far-candidate count of the spherical decoder with far correlation $2\eta$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Fix a query $q$ reaching a final residual bucket $U$, and let
   \[
      E_q=\left\{x\in U:\ip{(x-o)/R}{(q-o)/R}>2\eta\right\}.
   \]
   If $|E_q|\ge\tau |U|$, then the query direction itself defines a heavy $2\eta$-cap that should have been peeled. Hence $|E_q|<\tau |U|$. Therefore
   \[
      |U|\le \frac{1}{1-\tau}\,|U\setminus E_q|.
   \]
   Every point in $U\setminus E_q$ has correlation at most $2\eta$ with the query direction, so the spherical far-candidate bound controls its expected contribution. Since $(1-\tau)^{-1}=1+O(\tau)$, scanning all of $U$ has the same bound up to this factor.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Tuple cap finder]\label{lem:tuple-cap-finder}
   Let $U$ be a finite set of unit vectors, let $0<\eta<1/10$, and suppose some $2\eta$-cap
   \[
      C=\{x\in U:\ip{x}{w}\ge2\eta\}
   \]
   has size at least $\tau |U|$. There is a randomized proposal procedure which, using
   \[
      \tau^{-O(\eta^{-2})}\log(1/\delta)
   \]
   tuple proposals, finds with probability at least $1-\delta$ a direction $v$ whose $\eta$-cap contains at least $\Omega(\eta\tau)|U|$ points.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $X$ be uniform on $C$ and let $m_C=\E X$. Since every point of $C$ has projection at least $2\eta$ on $w$,
   \[
      \|m_C\|\ge \ip{m_C}{w}\ge2\eta.
   \]
   Sample
   \[
      k=\lceil C_0\eta^{-2}\rceil
   \]
   points from $U$ with replacement. With probability at least $\tau^k$, all sampled points lie in $C$. Conditioned on this event, their empirical mean $\widehat m$ satisfies
   \[
      \E\|\widehat m-m_C\|^2\le \frac1k.
   \]
   Taking $C_0$ large and applying Markov's inequality gives
   \[
      \|\widehat m-m_C\|\le\eta/4
   \]
   with probability at least a fixed positive constant. Let $v=\widehat m/\|\widehat m\|$. On this event,
   \[
      \ip{m_C}{v}
      \ge
      \|\widehat m\|-\|\widehat m-m_C\|
      \ge
      \|m_C\|-2\|\widehat m-m_C\|
      \ge
      3\eta/2.
   \]
   Therefore, for $X$ uniform on $C$,
   \[
      \E\ip{X}{v}\ge3\eta/2.
   \]
   Since $\ip{X}{v}\le1$, at least an $\Omega(\eta)$ fraction of $C$ must satisfy $\ip{X}{v}\ge\eta$. Hence the $\eta$-cap in direction $v$ contains $\Omega(\eta\tau)|U|$ points.

   A single random tuple is therefore successful with probability at least $c\tau^k$ for a constant $c>0$. Repeating $O(\tau^{-k}\log(1/\delta))$ times and verifying each proposed $\eta$-cap by scanning $U$ proves the lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Leaf-conditioned slice budget]\label{lem:slice-budget}
   Let a hard leaf $U$ have data fraction $\mu=|U|/m$, and let $\nu_q(U)\in\{0,1\}$ indicate whether query $q$ reaches it. Suppose the peeled slices inside $U$ have relative sizes
   \[
      z_j=\frac{|C_j|}{|U|}
   \]
   and certified radii $R_j=\lambda_jR$. With $\rho=\rho_c+O_c(\eta)$ and $s=1-\rho$,
   \[
      F(m,R)\,\mu^\rho\nu_q(U)^s\,z_j^\rho\lambda_j^{2s}
      =
      F(|C_j|,R_j)\nu_q(U)^s.
   \]
   If at most $J$ slices are peeled from $U$, then summing over the slices loses at most a factor $J^{1-\rho}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The identity follows by substituting $|C_j|=z_j\mu m$ and $R_j=\lambda_jR$ into
   \[
      F(m,R)=m^\rho\left(\frac Rr\right)^{2s}.
   \]
   The slices are disjoint inside $U$, so $\sum_j z_j\le1$. Concavity of $x^\rho$ gives
   \[
      \sum_{j=1}^J z_j^\rho\le J^{1-\rho}\left(\sum_{j=1}^J z_j\right)^\rho\le J^{1-\rho}.
   \]
\end{proof}

By \cref{lem:tuple-cap-finder}, every successful peeled slice removes an $\Omega(\eta\tau)$ fraction of the current bucket. A bucket of size at most $n$ therefore produces only
\[
   J=O((\eta\tau)^{-1}\log n)=\log^{O_c(1)}n
\]
sequential slices. Thus \cref{lem:slice-budget} costs only a polylogarithmic factor per restartable stage.
The rejected tuple proposals from \cref{lem:tuple-cap-finder} are preprocessing-only and are not stored. If preprocessing time is tracked, their logarithmic overhead per bucket is
\[
   O(\eta^{-2}\log(1/\tau)+\log\log n)
   =
   O_c(\eta^{-2}\log\log n),
\]
which is below the final $2/3$ overhead after the choice of $\eta$ in \cref{prop:slice-two-thirds}.

\begin{proposition}[Restartable slice milestone]\label{prop:slice-two-thirds}
   For fixed $c>1$, the restartable slice construction gives a worst-case Euclidean ANN structure with
   \[
      Q(n,d)
      =
      d\,n^{\rho_c}
      \exp\!\left(
         O_c\!\left((\log n)^{2/3}(\log\log n)^{2/3}\right)
      \right)
   \]
   and
   \[
      S(n,d)
      =
      dn+n^{1+\rho_c}
      \exp\!\left(
         O_c\!\left((\log n)^{2/3}(\log\log n)^{2/3}\right)
      \right).
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   First consider one restartable stage. The continuation-mass decoder supplies the hard accepting leaves and has expected path work, far-candidate occurrences, storage, and inverse near-pair success all bounded by the ideal spherical powers times $\log^{O_c(1)}n$, by \cref{lem:continuation-work} and \cref{cor:spherical-survival}. Amplifying the stage to failure probability $1/\log^{O_c(1)}n$ costs another polylogarithmic factor.

   The final residual scan is controlled by \cref{lem:slice-residual}. Heavy cap discovery is handled by \cref{lem:tuple-cap-finder}; rejected tuple proposals affect preprocessing only, and accepted slices are verified before storage. By \cref{lem:slice-budget}, the recursive budgets of all slices peeled from a leaf cost only a further polylogarithmic factor. A query follows every certified child ball from its reached leaf that is compatible with the query, which adds at most the same polylogarithmic factor because each leaf has only $J=\log^{O_c(1)}n$ accepted slices. Thus one stage has polylogarithmic overhead in query work, space, and inverse success probability relative to the ideal radius-weighted power-law budget.

   Put
   \[
      L=\log n,
      \qquad
      \ell=\log\log n.
   \]
   By \cref{lem:rho-stability} and \cref{lem:shell}, using far correlation $2\eta$ and inverse-polynomial shell widths changes the balanced exponent from $\rho_c$ to
   \[
      \rho_c+O_c(\eta),
   \]
   contributing
   \[
      \exp(O_c(\eta L))
   \]
   to the overhead.

   Each accepted slice has certified radius at most $R\sqrt{1-\eta^2}\le R(1-\eta^2/2)$ for small enough $\eta$. After bounded-diameter localization, the relevant ratio $R_0/r$ is at most $\log^{O_c(1)}n$. Hence a near-neighbor lineage has at most
   \[
      H=O_c\!\left(\eta^{-2}\log\frac{R_0}{r}\right)
      =
      O_c(\eta^{-2}\ell)
   \]
   restarts before a resident scan. Paying an independent polylogarithmic stage overhead at every restart contributes
   \[
      \log^{O_c(H)}n
      =
      \exp(O_c(\eta^{-2}\ell^2)).
   \]
   Therefore the logarithm of the nonpolynomial overhead is
   \[
      O_c(\eta L+\eta^{-2}\ell^2).
   \]
   Balance the two terms by taking
   \[
      \eta=\Theta_c\!\left(\left(\frac{\ell^2}{L}\right)^{1/3}\right).
   \]
   Then both terms are $O_c(L^{2/3}\ell^{2/3})$, proving the displayed bounds. \Cref{fig:balance} shows the balance.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[t]\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,>=stealth,xscale=1.5,yscale=1.0]
\draw[->] (0,0) -- (5.2,0) node[right]{$\eta$};
\draw[->] (0,0) -- (0,4.4) node[above]{overhead exponent $\log(\cdot)$};
% term1: slack, increasing line ~ eta L
\draw[blue,thick,domain=0:5,samples=2] plot (\x,{0.5*\x});
\node[blue,font=\scriptsize,right] at (4.3,2.35) {$\eta L$ (slack)};
% term2: restart phases, decreasing ~ eta^{-2} ell^2
\draw[red,thick,domain=0.5:5,samples=80,smooth] plot (\x,{2/\x});
\node[red,font=\scriptsize,right] at (3.55,0.75) {$\eta^{-2}\ell^2$ (restart phases)};
% envelope max (the actual overhead), thicker
\draw[black,very thick,domain=0.5:2,samples=60,smooth] plot (\x,{2/\x});
\draw[black,very thick,domain=2:5,samples=2] plot (\x,{0.5*\x});
% optimum marker
\fill (2,1) circle (1.6pt);
\draw[dashed] (2,0) -- (2,1) -- (0,1);
\node[below,font=\scriptsize] at (2,0) {$\eta^*\asymp(\ell^2/L)^{1/3}$};
\node[left,font=\scriptsize] at (0,1) {$L^{2/3}\ell^{2/3}$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Balancing the two overhead terms in the restartable-slice route (\cref{prop:slice-two-thirds}). The geometric slack contributes $\eta L$ (rising), while paying a fresh polylogarithmic stage cost at each of $\Theta(\eta^{-2}\ell)$ restarts contributes $\eta^{-2}\ell^2$ (falling). The realized overhead is their maximum (thick curve), minimized at $\eta^*\asymp(\ell^2/L)^{1/3}$, where both equal $L^{2/3}\ell^{2/3}$ with $L=\log n$, $\ell=\log\log n$. The Bellman-cohort mechanism removes the falling term entirely, collapsing the optimum to $\polylog$.}
\label{fig:balance}
\end{figure}

This result is not the final target: it still pays a fresh polylogarithmic factor after every certified radius reduction. Its value is that the recursive object is the prefix-conditioned slice $C[U,w]$, whose budget is exactly supplied by the hard leaf. The false full-child statement is no longer needed.

\section{A randomized one-tree route to the 1/3 rung}

The restartable construction still turns each recentered slice into a fresh spherical instance. The following variant does not restart or re-channelize. Geometric splits are randomized edges inside one cumulative score tree, exactly as in \cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction}. This removes the $\eta^{-2}$ independent phase payment from \cref{prop:slice-two-thirds}; what remains is a mixed stopping-frontier work inequality for the branching continuation process.

Fix parameters $\eta,\delta>0$ and put
\[
   \varepsilon=\frac{\eta\delta}{16}.
\]
Inside a score bucket $U$ on the current shell, call a direction $w$ almost total if its $2\eta$-cap contains all but an $\varepsilon$ fraction of $U$. If such a direction exists at a retained prefix, preprocessing stores an $\eta$-cap child containing all but a $\delta$ fraction of $U$ and leaves the rest as the residual child. If no almost-total direction exists at an intermediate prefix, the prefix simply keeps advancing the continuation-mass score process. A cap-free bucket is scanned only when it is an accepting leaf of the current score block, or when it is already small enough to be a resident block. This is the point that lets cap-free scans be charged to ordinary low-correlation accepting-leaf collisions.

\begin{lemma}[Hinge certificate for almost-total caps]\label{lem:almost-total-cap-finder}
   Let $U$ be a finite set of unit vectors. Suppose there is a unit vector $w$ such that
   \[
      \left|\{x\in U:\ip{x}{w}\ge2\eta\}\right|
      \ge
      (1-\varepsilon)|U|,
      \qquad
      \varepsilon\le\frac{\eta\delta}{16}.
   \]
   Define the empirical hinge loss over the unit ball by
   \[
      L(v)=\frac1{|U|}\sum_{x\in U}(2\eta-\ip{x}{v})_+,
      \qquad
      \|v\|_2\le1.
   \]
   Any $v$ with $L(v)\le4\varepsilon$ has an $\eta$-cap containing at least $(1-\delta)|U|$ points. Such a certificate is checkable by scanning $U$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The witness $w$ has loss at most $(1+2\eta)\varepsilon\le2\varepsilon$, since only the exceptional points contribute and each contribution is at most $1+2\eta$. Hence an approximate hinge minimizer with loss at most $4\varepsilon$ is enough.

   If $\ip{x}{v}<\eta$, then $(2\eta-\ip{x}{v})_+\ge\eta$. Therefore the fraction of points outside the $\eta$-cap of $v$ is at most $L(v)/\eta\le4\varepsilon/\eta\le\delta/4$. Normalizing $v$ can only increase positive projections when $\|v\|_2\le1$, so the same cap certificate holds for the stored unit direction.
\end{proof}

Thus a complete almost-total cap search can be implemented by minimizing a convex empirical hinge loss to inverse-polylogarithmic accuracy and then verifying the resulting cap size directly. Conversely, if no almost-total $2\eta$-cap exists, then for every query direction at least an $\varepsilon$ fraction of the terminal bucket has correlation at most $2\eta$ with the query. Scanning the whole terminal bucket is therefore charged to low-correlation points with only an $\varepsilon^{-1}$ loss.

In the one-tree route we take the residual allowance at the largest scale compatible with deterministic cap-split contraction,
\[
   \delta=\delta_0\eta^{2/\rho_c},
\]
where $\delta_0=\delta_0(c)>0$ is a sufficiently small constant. This improves the polynomial logarithmic factors in the residual scan and cap-finder accuracy compared with the safer but unnecessary choice $\eta^{3/\rho_c}$. Since $\rho_c<1$ for every fixed $c>1$, the lineage-thinning condition $H\delta=o(1)$ still holds for both the cube-root and polylogarithmic parameter choices below.

The information-time tube calculation is still a useful sanity check. For a fixed predictable mixed lineage of total information $D$, exponential tilting makes the log-likelihood fluctuation a centered Gaussian martingale with quadratic variation $2D$. Keeping it in a tube of width $w$ costs $\exp(-O(D/w^2))$, while the likelihood ratio varies by $\exp(O(w))$; optimizing $w=D^{1/3}$ gives a fixed-lineage survival loss $\exp(-O(D^{1/3}))$.

We do not use that tube estimate as a formal hypothesis. The adaptive continuation lemma proved in \cref{lem:adaptive-continuation} is stronger for this algorithm: with continuation-mass pruning, predictable mixed score schedules have inverse-polylogarithmic near survival. Thus the cube-root checkpoint below has one conditional ingredient, namely the mixed stopping-frontier work inequality.

\begin{lemma}[Cap-free terminal scan accounting]\label{lem:cap-free-scan-accounting}
   Work inside one bounded-diameter localized state with $m$ points in radius $R$, and choose the score budget $N$ by
   \[
      N^{\rho_c}=F_4(m,R).
   \]
   Suppose a query scans a prefix-free family $\mathcal L(q)$ of cap-free accepting leaves, and in every scanned bucket at least an $\alpha$ fraction of the resident points have correlation at most $2\eta$ with the query direction. Then the expected score work and terminal scans satisfy
   \[
      \E\left[
         \mathrm{score\ work}
         +
         \sum_{u\in\mathcal L(q)} |P[u]|
      \right]
      \le
      \alpha^{-1}
      \exp(O_c(\eta\log n))
      F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The score-work term is at most $F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}n$ by the budgeted continuation work bound, and $\alpha\le1$.

   For a scanned bucket $U$, let $U_{\mathrm{lo}}$ be its points whose shell-normalized correlation with the query is at most $2\eta$. By assumption, $|U|\le\alpha^{-1}|U_{\mathrm{lo}}|$. Summing over scanned leaves, the scan count is at most $\alpha^{-1}$ times the number of low-correlation accepting candidate occurrences.

   By \cref{lem:rho-stability}, these low-correlation pairs have spherical exponent at most $\rho_c+O_c(\eta)$. The budgeted far-candidate bound with depth chosen from $N$ gives expected low-correlation accepting occurrences at most
   \[
      mN^{-(1-\rho_c-O_c(\eta))}\log^{O_c(1)}N
      \le
      N^{\rho_c}\exp(O_c(\eta\log N))\log^{O_c(1)}N.
   \]
   In bounded-diameter localized states, $N\le n\log^{O_c(1)}n$, so $\log N=O_c(\log n)$ and $N^{\rho_c}=F_4(m,R)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{assumption}[Cube-root geometric frontier inequality]\label{asm:mixed-operator}
   In the randomized one-tree process with almost-total cap splitting at the cube-root parameters
   \[
      L=\log n,
      \qquad
      \eta=L^{-2/3},
      \qquad
      \delta=\delta_0\eta^{2/\rho_c},
   \]
   Let $\mathcal G(q)$ be the collection of cap children reached with their thinning probabilities included. Let
   \[
      \alpha=\Theta(\eta\delta),
      \qquad
      F_4(m,R)=m^{\rho_c}\Psi(R).
   \]
   The thinned recursive-potential frontier satisfies
   \[
      \E\left[
         \sum_{v\in\mathcal G(q)} a_vF_4(|P[v]|,R_v)
      \right]
      \le
      \alpha^{-1}
      \exp(O_c(\eta L))
      F_4(n,R_0)\log^{O_c(1)}n,
   \]
   where $a_v$ is the product of cap-edge thinning probabilities on the route to $v$.
\end{assumption}

The terminal term is charged by \cref{lem:cap-free-scan-accounting} to the $\alpha$ fraction of low-correlation points in every cap-free bucket. The geometric term should be controlled by the contraction in \cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction}. The unresolved point is to make this argument commute with the random prefix-free stopping frontiers, again without restarting the score clock. The all-parameter version needed for the polylogarithmic endpoint is isolated later as \cref{asm:mixed-continuation-operator}.

\begin{proposition}[Conditional randomized one-tree \(1/3\) rung]\label{prop:one-third}
   Assume \cref{asm:mixed-operator}. For fixed $c>1$, the randomized one-tree construction gives worst-case Euclidean ANN bounds
   \[
      Q(n,d)
      =
      d\,n^{\rho_c}
      \exp\!\left(O_c((\log n)^{1/3})\right)
      \log^{O_c(1)}n
   \]
   and
   \[
      S(n,d)
      =
      dn+n^{1+\rho_c}
      \exp\!\left(O_c((\log n)^{1/3})\right)
      \log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Work inside one bounded-diameter localized instance, so $R_0/r=\log^{O_c(1)}n$, and put
   \[
      L=\log n,
      \qquad
      \eta=L^{-2/3},
      \qquad
      \delta=\delta_0\eta^{2/\rho_c}.
   \]
   Choose $\delta_0$ small enough that $\delta^{\rho_c}$ is below the constant required in \cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction}. Then every almost-total cap split satisfies the contraction hypothesis. The storage potential is nonincreasing under the deterministic partition, and
   \[
      F_4(n,R_0)=n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]

   A cap edge decreases radius by a factor at most $\sqrt{1-\eta^2}$, so a lineage has at most
   \[
      H=O_c\!\left(\eta^{-2}\log\frac{R_0}{r}\right)
      =
      O_c(\eta^{-2}\log\log n)
   \]
   cap edges before the radius is terminal. With the chosen $\delta$,
   \[
      H\delta
      =
      O_c(\eta^{-2+2/\rho_c}\log\log n)
      =
      o(1).
   \]
   Therefore \cref{lem:thin-lineage} shows that all randomized cap-edge thinning along the true near lineage costs only $\log^{-O_c(1)}n$ success probability. Residual edges are followed with probability one.

   \Cref{lem:adaptive-continuation} supplies a common near score path with probability
   \[
      \log^{-O_c(1)}n.
   \]
   The score work and cap-free scans are bounded by \cref{lem:cap-free-scan-accounting}; the recursive cap-frontier term is bounded by \cref{asm:mixed-operator}. Since
   \[
      \eta L=L^{1/3},
      \qquad
      \alpha^{-1}=(\eta\delta)^{-1}=\eta^{-1-2/\rho_c}\delta_0^{-1}=\log^{O_c(1)}n,
   \]
   one copy has expected work and inverse success overhead
   \[
      \exp(O_c(L^{1/3}))\log^{O_c(1)}n
   \]
   over the $n^{\rho_c}$ scale. Amplifying by this factor and applying the truncation paragraph in the preliminaries gives the displayed query bound. The space bound follows from the storage part of \cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction}, the same global score-clock overhead, and the bounded-diameter localization factors.
\end{proof}

\section{A continuation-mass route to the polylog rung}

The cube-root checkpoint in \cref{prop:one-third} is not caused by the score survival analysis. The continuation-mass proof from the spherical section extends to predictable mixed score schedules, so the score side has only inverse-polylogarithmic loss. Cap-free leaves are charged to low-correlation collisions by \cref{lem:cap-free-scan-accounting}. The remaining work accounting is the thinned cap-split potential over random first-hit frontiers. If that mixed frontier inequality is proved uniformly at smaller $\eta$, the same randomized one-tree algorithm reaches the polylogarithmic endpoint.

\begin{definition}[Regular adaptive score schedule]\label{def:regular-adaptive-score}
   Fix a compact interval $I=[a_-,a_+]\subset(-1,1)$ and a constant branching factor $B\ge2$. A regular adaptive score schedule of depth $K$ is a rooted score tree in which every score node has $B$ score children, and before an edge $e$ is sampled its coordinate system and a value $a_e\in I$ are measurable with respect to the previous score prefix. Conditional on the past, the two score increments of a fixed near pair are centered Gaussian with covariance
   \[
      \begin{pmatrix}1&a_e\\ a_e&1\end{pmatrix}.
   \]
   The edge threshold is
   \[
      t_e=\sqrt{(1+a_e)\log B}.
   \]
   A leaf accepts the near pair if both accumulated scores lie in their unit endpoint windows around
   \[
      T_v=\sum_{e\preceq v}t_e .
   \]
   Prefixes are retained by the exact one-point continuation mass for the remaining adaptive schedule, with cutoff $K^{-A}$.
\end{definition}

The exact calibration $t_e^2/(1+a_e)=\log B$ is only for the proof. In the ANN application one uses a certified lower correlation and absorbs the resulting $O_c(\eta)$ perturbation through \cref{lem:rho-stability}; the estimates below are uniform on compact neighborhoods of the critical parameters.

\begin{lemma}[Adaptive continuation-mass survival]\label{lem:adaptive-continuation}
   For every compact $I\subset(-1,1)$ there is $A=A_I$ such that every regular adaptive score schedule of depth $K$ has the following property. For any fixed near pair following any predictable mixed geometric lineage, continuation-mass pruning produces a common accepting score leaf with probability at least
   \[
      K^{-O_I(1)}.
   \]
   The expected retained-prefix work and stored one-point occurrences are also bounded by the corresponding one-point accepting-leaf expectations times $K^{O_I(1)}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The work statement is identical to \cref{lem:continuation-work}. At any prefix, the continuation mass is the exact conditional expected number of accepting descendants for one point. Summing it over all prefixes at one score time counts accepting descendants, and retained prefixes have continuation mass at least $K^{-A}$.

   We prove the survival statement by the same first- and second-moment argument as \cref{lem:good-first-moment} and \cref{lem:tame-second-moment}. The only change is that the Gaussian increments form a predictable triangular array. All constants below are uniform for $a_e\in I$.

   Fix a score leaf $v$. Tilt each edge $e\preceq v$ by
   \[
      \theta_e=\frac{t_e}{1+a_e}(1,1).
   \]
   Under the tilted measure both coordinates have mean $t_e$, and
   \[
      \frac12\theta_e^\top
      \begin{pmatrix}1&a_e\\ a_e&1\end{pmatrix}
      \theta_e
      =
      \frac{t_e^2}{1+a_e}
      =
      \log B.
   \]
   Hence, on the endpoint unit square for $v$,
   \[
      B^K\frac{dP}{dQ_v}=\exp(O_I(1)).
   \]
   Under $Q_v$, the centered endpoint covariance is a sum of $K$ uniformly elliptic two-dimensional covariance matrices, so the endpoint unit square has probability $\Theta_I(K^{-1})$.

   The continuation lower barriers are one-point barriers written in the remaining conditional variance. Since every remaining variance increment is bounded above and below by constants depending only on $I$, the bridge estimate used in \cref{lem:good-first-moment} applies verbatim: far from the endpoint the barrier is linearly below the tilted mean, and during the final $O_I(\log K)$ variance units it is below by $\Omega_I(\sqrt{A r\log K})$. Taking $A$ large makes lower-barrier failure probability $o(1)$.

   Add the same analytical upper barrier in the centered common coordinate
   \[
      C_e=\frac{(X_e-t_e)+(Y_e-t_e)}{\sqrt{2(1+a_e)}}.
   \]
   These increments are standard normal under the tilted law, independently of $a_e$. For a Gaussian bridge of length $K$, the probability of staying below a fixed constant upper barrier, conditioned on a bounded endpoint, is $K^{-O_I(1)}$. Therefore the expected number $Z$ of adaptive tame-good leaves satisfies
   \[
      \E Z\ge K^{-O_I(1)}.
   \]

   For the second moment, decompose ordered pairs of score leaves by their lowest common ancestor after $j$ score edges. There are at most $B^{2K-j}$ such ordered pairs. Conditional on the predictable schedule up to the split, the two suffix schedules are independent after the split. The edgewise identity $t_e^2/(1+a_e)=\log B$ cancels the exponential growth from the $B^{2K-j}$ leaf pairs exactly as in the homogeneous calculation. The shared centered common coordinate is capped by the upper barrier at the split, so its integral contributes at most
   \[
      \frac{C_I}{\sqrt{j+1}\,(K-j)}
   \]
   after cancellation. The difference endpoint pair has covariance determinant $\Theta_I(K(K-j))$, hence density at most $C_I/\sqrt{K(K-j)}$. Thus the split depth $j<K$ contributes at most
   \[
      \frac{C_I K^{O_I(1)}}{\sqrt{j+1}\,(K-j)^{3/2}\sqrt K}.
   \]
   The sum over $j<K$ is $K^{O_I(1)}$, and the diagonal contribution is bounded by the first-moment endpoint calculation. Hence
   \[
      \E Z^2\le K^{O_I(1)}.
   \]
   Paley--Zygmund gives $\Pr[Z>0]\ge K^{-O_I(1)}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{assumption}[Mixed continuation operator]\label{asm:mixed-continuation-operator}
   In one randomized continuation score block at a state with $m$ points in radius $R$, choose the effective budget $N$ so that
   \[
      N^\rho=F_4(m,R)=m^\rho\Psi(R).
   \]
   Let $\mathcal G(q)$ be the prefix-free collection of first geometric splits reached by query $q$, including residual children with query weight $1$ and cap children with query weight $a_C=z^s\Psi(R_C)/\Psi(R)$. For
   \[
      \delta=\delta_0\eta^{2/\rho_c},
      \qquad
      \alpha=\Theta(\eta\delta),
      \qquad
      L=\log n,
   \]
   the weighted recursive child potentials satisfy, for an absolute $\kappa>0$,
   \[
      \E\left[
         \sum_{v\in\mathcal G(q)} w_vF_4(|P[v]|,R_v)
      \right]
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R),
   \]
   unless no geometric split is reached, in which case the left-hand side is zero.
   Here $w_v$ is the query weight of the child at its first split prefix.
\end{assumption}

The score-work and terminal-scan terms are handled by \cref{lem:cap-free-scan-accounting}. The remaining point is the geometric first-split sum. For a single split prefix the contraction in \cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction} is exact. What still needs proof is a power estimate for the random first-split frontier of the branching decoder, strong enough to replace the invalid normalized-path inequality
\[
