   \sum_{u\in\mathcal A}\nu_q(u)\mu(u)^\rho\le 1.
\]
That inequality would be immediate for a single sampled path, but the continuation decoder retains a set of prefixes; a data point or query may contribute to many prefix nodes. The desired proof should therefore be an optional-stopping or continuation-mass argument for the branching score process itself.

\begin{lemma}[Bellman certificate for first-split frontiers]\label{lem:bellman-frontier-certificate}
   Fix one continuation score block at a state with $m$ points and radius $R$. Suppose that, for every query $q$, there is a nonnegative predictable prefix potential $\mathcal B_q(u)$ on retained score prefixes with the following properties.
   \[
      \mathcal B_q(\varnothing)\le F_4(m,R).
   \]
   For every retained score prefix $u$ before a geometric split or terminal scan, if $\mathrm{ch}(u)$ denotes the next retained score children, then
   \[
      \E\left[\sum_{w\in\mathrm{ch}(u)}\mathcal B_q(w)\,\middle|\,u\right]
      \le
      \mathcal B_q(u).
   \]
   Finally, at every first geometric split prefix $u$, the weighted recursive potential of the children opened from that prefix is at most
   \[
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q(u).
   \]
   Then the geometric part of \cref{asm:mixed-continuation-operator} holds.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $\mathcal A(q)$ be the random prefix-free frontier of first geometric split prefixes reached by the query in this block. The second displayed inequality says that $\sum_{u\in\mathcal U}\mathcal B_q(u)$ is a supermartingale when $\mathcal U$ is the current active retained prefix frontier. Optional stopping at the bounded-depth frontier $\mathcal A(q)$ gives
   \[
      \E\sum_{u\in\mathcal A(q)}\mathcal B_q(u)
      \le
      \mathcal B_q(\varnothing)
      \le
      F_4(m,R).
   \]
   Multiplying each stopped prefix by its local split charge and using the last hypothesis gives
   \[
      \E\left[
         \sum_{v\in\mathcal G(q)}w_vF_4(|P[v]|,R_v)
      \right]
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R).
   \]
   If no split is reached, the child-potential sum is empty.
\end{proof}

This certificate is the local form of the remaining problem. It is analogous in proof shape to the robust Hamming hashing guarantee of Andoni and Beaglehole~\cite{AB22}: there a per-node min-max inequality over hash choices and adversarial query-neighbor pairs is enough, by induction, to lower-bound the success probability of the whole tree. Here the missing object is the continuation-mass analogue of that local Bellman potential. Proving such a potential would remove the global frontier assumption without replacing the branching decoder by a normalized one-path law.

\begin{lemma}[Canonical mixed continuation budget]\label{lem:canonical-mixed-budget}
   In one score block, let $H_0$ be the one-point continuation mass at the root for the chosen effective budget. For a retained prefix $u$, let $Q_q(u)$ be the exact query continuation mass from $u$, and let
   \[
      D(u)=\sum_{x\in P_u}H_x(u)
   \]
   be the aggregate data continuation mass of the resident bucket at $u$. Define
   \[
      \mathcal B_q^{\mathrm{mix}}(u)
      =
      \Psi(R)\frac{D(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}{H_0},
      \qquad s=1-\rho.
   \]
   Then $\mathcal B_q^{\mathrm{mix}}(\varnothing)=F_4(m,R)$ and $\mathcal B_q^{\mathrm{mix}}$ satisfies the prefix-supermartingale hypothesis of \cref{lem:bellman-frontier-certificate}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   At the root, $D(\varnothing)=mH_0$ and $Q_q(\varnothing)=H_0$, hence
   \[
      \mathcal B_q^{\mathrm{mix}}(\varnothing)
      =
      \Psi(R)m^\rho H_0^{\rho+s-1}
      =
      m^\rho\Psi(R)
      =
      F_4(m,R).
   \]

   Fix a retained prefix $u$ and reveal its retained score children $w$. For each realization, Hölder's inequality gives
   \[
      \sum_wD(w)^\rho Q_q(w)^s
      \le
      \left(\sum_wD(w)\right)^\rho
      \left(\sum_wQ_q(w)\right)^s .
   \]
   Before the next Gaussian children are realized, continuation mass is harmonic in expectation, and retention can only delete child mass. Therefore
   \[
      \E\!\left[\sum_wD(w)\,\middle|\,u\right]\le D(u),
      \qquad
      \E\!\left[\sum_wQ_q(w)\,\middle|\,u\right]\le Q_q(u).
   \]
   Since $(a,b)\mapsto a^\rho b^s$ is concave on $\R_+^2$, Jensen's inequality yields
   \[
      \E\left[\sum_wD(w)^\rho Q_q(w)^s\,\middle|\,u\right]
      \le
      D(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s.
   \]
   Multiplying by $\Psi(R)/H_0$ proves the supermartingale inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Budget-gated cap splitting]\label{cor:budget-gated-splits}
   Fix any predictable nonnegative budget process $\mathcal B_q$ satisfying the first two hypotheses of \cref{lem:bellman-frontier-certificate}. Modify the one-tree query algorithm as follows: at a retained prefix $u$, a stored almost-total cap split is live only if its thinned recursive charge
   \[
      F_4((1-z)|P[u]|,R_u)
      +
      z^s\frac{\Psi(R_C)}{\Psi(R_u)}F_4(z|P[u]|,R_C)
   \]
   is at most $(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q(u)$. If the gate fails, the query ignores that geometric split and continues the score block. Then the weighted recursive child-potential bound in \cref{asm:mixed-continuation-operator} holds for the live splits, without any additional first-frontier assumption.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The budget gate enforces the local domination hypothesis in \cref{lem:bellman-frontier-certificate}. Applying that lemma to the live first-split frontier gives the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Budget-stable prefix list]\label{lem:budget-stable-prefix-list}
   Fix one continuation score block of depth \(K\) at a state with local target
   \(F=F_4(m,R)\).  Let \(\mathcal B_q(u)\) be a predictable nonnegative
   prefix budget satisfying
   \[
      \mathcal B_q(\varnothing)\le F,
      \qquad
      \E\!\left[\sum_{w\in\mathrm{ch}(u)}\mathcal B_q(w)\,\middle|\,u\right]
      \le \mathcal B_q(u)
   \]
   for retained score children.  Let \(\mathcal T(q)\) be a predictable
   prefix-free family of offered geometric opportunities, and let \(\Gamma(u)\)
   be the certified recursive query-potential charge of opportunity \(u\).
   Fix a threshold \(0<\tau\le1\).  Call \(u\in\mathcal T(q)\)
   budget-stable if
   \[
      \mathcal B_q(v)\ge\tau \quad(v\preceq u),
      \qquad
      \Gamma(u)\le (1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q(u).
   \]

   Run the priority prefix-list decoder with prefix potential
   \(H_q(u)=\mathcal B_q(u)\), terminal opportunities marked accepting exactly
   when they are budget-stable, and threshold \(\tau\).  Then it invokes every
   budget-stable opportunity and no opportunity whose prefix budget ever drops
   below \(\tau\).  If the total number of possible prefixes in the block is
   \(M\), its expected prefix-enumeration work is
   \[
      O\!\left(\tau^{-1}KF\log(2+M)\right),
   \]
   and the expected recursive query-potential charge of invoked opportunities
   is at most
   \[
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Use \cref{lem:continuation-list-certificate} with \(H_q=\mathcal B_q\).
   Its terminal score is the minimum log-budget along the route, so a terminal
   opportunity is above threshold exactly when all prefixes have budget at
   least \(\tau\).  The additional accepting predicate is the local budget gate
   \(\Gamma(u)\le(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q(u)\), so the decoder invokes
   precisely the budget-stable opportunities.

   Let \(\mathcal U_\ell\) be the retained prefix frontier at level \(\ell\).
   Iterating the supermartingale inequality gives
   \[
      \E\sum_{u\in\mathcal U_\ell}\mathcal B_q(u)\le F .
   \]
   Summing over \(K+1\) levels,
   \[
      \E\sum_u\mathcal B_q(u)\le (K+1)F .
   \]
   Since every visited prefix has \(\mathcal B_q(u)\ge\tau\), the expected
   number of visited prefixes is at most \(\tau^{-1}(K+1)F\).  The priority
   queue contributes at most \(\log(2+M)\) per visited prefix.

   The invoked opportunities form a subfamily of the prefix-free set
   \(\mathcal T(q)\).  Optional stopping for the same budget supermartingale
   gives
   \[
      \E\sum_{u\in\mathcal T(q)}\mathcal B_q(u)\le F .
   \]
   On invoked opportunities,
   \(\Gamma(u)\le(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q(u)\), hence
   \[
      \E\sum_{\text{invoked }u}\Gamma(u)
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)
      \E\sum_{u\in\mathcal T(q)}\mathcal B_q(u)
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F .
   \]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Canonical budget-stable decoder]
   \label{cor:canonical-budget-stable-decoder}
   In one bounded-diameter localized score block, use the canonical mixed
   budget \(\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}\) of
   \cref{lem:canonical-mixed-budget}.  With
   \(\tau=\log^{-O_c(1)}n\), the budget-stable prefix-list decoder has expected
   prefix work
   \[
      F_4(m,R)\log^{O_c(1)}n
   \]
   and expected invoked recursive query-potential charge at most
   \[
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R).
   \]
   Thus all budget-stable recentering opportunities can be handled
   deterministically inside the list-decoding view.  The only geometric
   opportunities not covered by this deterministic compiler are those whose
   route passes through a prefix with subthreshold canonical budget, or whose
   local recursive charge exceeds the available budget; these are exactly the
   opportunities handled by the budget-thinned/value-delivery layer.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \cref{lem:canonical-mixed-budget}, the canonical mixed budget satisfies
   the hypotheses of \cref{lem:budget-stable-prefix-list} with
   \(F=F_4(m,R)\).  In bounded-diameter localized states, the score depth and
   the logarithm of the finite prefix universe are both \(\log^{O_c(1)}n\).
   Taking \(\tau^{-1}=\log^{O_c(1)}n\) gives the displayed prefix-work bound,
   and the recursive charge bound is the one in
   \cref{lem:budget-stable-prefix-list}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Budget-thinned first opportunities]\label{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities}
   Fix any predictable nonnegative budget process $\mathcal B_q$ satisfying the first two hypotheses of \cref{lem:bellman-frontier-certificate}. At a retained prefix $u$, let $\Gamma(u)$ denote the weighted recursive potential of the geometric children that would be opened from $u$; for the almost-total cap split above,
   \[
      \Gamma(u)=
      F_4((1-z)|P[u]|,R_u)
      +
      z^s\frac{\Psi(R_C)}{\Psi(R_u)}F_4(z|P[u]|,R_C).
   \]
   Let $\mathcal A(q)$ be the prefix-free frontier of first prefixes on which such a geometric opportunity is offered to query $q$. Modify the query rule at these first opportunities as follows: activate the geometric children at $u$ with probability
   \[
      \pi(u)=
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q(u)}{\Gamma(u)}
      \right\},
   \]
   with the convention $\pi(u)=1$ when $\Gamma(u)=0$. If the activation coin fails, this query branch opens no geometric child in the current score block; it may only continue toward a cap-free terminal scan. Then
   \[
      \E\left[
         \sum_{u\in\mathcal A(q)}
         \mathbf 1_{\{\text{$u$ activates}\}}\Gamma(u)
      \right]
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R).
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Condition on the realized score tree and on the first-opportunity frontier $\mathcal A(q)$. The activation coins are independent of the score randomness after $\mathcal A(q)$ is determined, and
   \[
      \E\!\left[
         \mathbf 1_{\{\text{$u$ activates}\}}\Gamma(u)
         \,\middle|\,\mathcal A(q),u
      \right]
      =
      \pi(u)\Gamma(u)
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q(u).
   \]
   Optional stopping for the budget supermartingale gives
   \[
      \E\sum_{u\in\mathcal A(q)}\mathcal B_q(u)
      \le
      \mathcal B_q(\varnothing)
      \le
      F_4(m,R).
   \]
   Combining the two displays proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Pruned budget-thinning profile]\label{lem:pruned-budget-thinning}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities}, let
   \(r_q(u)\ge0\) be any predictable multiplier on first-opportunity prefixes.
   Suppose the multiplier profile has the certified budget
   \[
      \E\sum_{u\in\mathcal A(q)}
      r_q(u)\mathcal B_q(u)
      \le F_4(m,R).
   \]
   Activate the geometric children at \(u\) with probability
   \[
      \pi_r(u)
      =
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{(1-\kappa\eta^2)r_q(u)\mathcal B_q(u)}
              {\Gamma(u)}
      \right\},
   \]
   with the convention \(\pi_r(u)=1\) when \(\Gamma(u)=0\). Then
   \[
      \E\left[
         \sum_{u\in\mathcal A(q)}
         \mathbf 1_{\{\text{$u$ activates}\}}\Gamma(u)
      \right]
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R).
   \]
   Taking \(r_q\equiv1\) recovers \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Condition on the realized first-opportunity frontier. For every
   \(u\in\mathcal A(q)\),
   \[
      \E\!\left[
         \mathbf 1_{\{\text{$u$ activates}\}}\Gamma(u)
         \,\middle|\,\mathcal A(q),u
      \right]
      =
      \pi_r(u)\Gamma(u)
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)r_q(u)\mathcal B_q(u).
   \]
   Summing and using the certified profile budget gives the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Budget-allocation thinning normal form]
   \label{lem:budget-allocation-thinning}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities}, let
   \(\mathcal Y_q(u)\ge0\) be any predictable allocation on first-opportunity
   prefixes. Suppose it has the certified budget
   \[
      \E\sum_{u\in\mathcal A(q)}\mathcal Y_q(u)
      \le F_4(m,R).
   \]
   Activate the geometric children at \(u\) with probability
   \[
      \pi_Y(u)
      =
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal Y_q(u)}
              {\Gamma(u)}
      \right\},
   \]
   with the convention \(\pi_Y(u)=1\) when \(\Gamma(u)=0\). Then
   \[
      \E\left[
         \sum_{u\in\mathcal A(q)}
         \mathbf 1_{\{\text{$u$ activates}\}}\Gamma(u)
      \right]
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R).
   \]
   The canonical rule is the special case
   \(\mathcal Y_q(u)=\mathcal B_q(u)\), and the pruned multiplier rule is the
   special case \(\mathcal Y_q(u)=r_q(u)\mathcal B_q(u)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Condition on the realized first-opportunity frontier. For every
   \(u\in\mathcal A(q)\),
   \[
      \E\!\left[
         \mathbf 1_{\{\text{$u$ activates}\}}\Gamma(u)
         \,\middle|\,\mathcal A(q),u
      \right]
      =
      \pi_Y(u)\Gamma(u)
      \le
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal Y_q(u).
   \]
   Summing and using the certified allocation budget proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Extreme-pruning interpretation]\label{rem:extreme-pruning-interpretation}
   \Cref{lem:pruned-budget-thinning} is the score-tree analogue of extreme
   pruning in lattice enumeration~\cite{GNR10}. The multiplier \(r_q(u)\) may
   exceed \(1\) on prefixes that are reliable under the intended near-route law
   and may be below \(1\) elsewhere; only the expected profile budget is charged.
   Since preprocessing time is not a scarce resource in our model, an offline
   build routine may search over finite multiplier profiles and retain a
   certified one, provided the query can decode the profile and the retained
   representation is charged to the index. The canonical budget-thinned rule is
   therefore just the uniform feasible profile \(r_q\equiv1\), not a forced
   algorithmic choice.  Equivalently, by
   \cref{lem:budget-allocation-thinning}, the query may work directly with a
   certified allocation \(\mathcal Y_q\) of its \(F_4(m,R)\) recursive budget.
   This removes the canonical budget from the activation formula; the canonical
   and pruned rules are merely two ways to parameterize the same allocation.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Budget-thinned one-tree work bound]
   \label{prop:budget-thinned-work}
   Work in one bounded-diameter localized instance with root state
   \((P_0,o_0,R_0)\), \(|P_0|=m_0\), and \(R_0/r=\log^{O_c(1)}n\).  Run the
   randomized one-tree construction with almost-total cap parameter \(\eta\),
   residual parameter \(\delta=\Theta_c(\eta^{2/\rho_c})\), and
   \[
      \alpha=\Theta_c(\eta\delta).
   \]
   At every geometric opportunity use the canonical budget-thinned activation
   rule of \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities}.  Assume each offered cap
   split is certified and satisfies the local storage and radius-contraction
   hypotheses of \cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction}, and scan a cap-free bucket
   only under the low-correlation condition of
   \cref{lem:cap-free-scan-accounting}.  Then, for every query, the expected
   score work, activated recursive child work, and terminal scan work in this
   localized instance are at most
   \[
      d\,F_4(m_0,R_0)\,
      \alpha^{-1}\eta^{-2}
      \exp(O_c(\eta\log n))\log^{O_c(1)}n .
      \tag{BW1}
   \]
   Consequently, with \(\eta=(\log n)^{-2/3}\) this work scale is
   \[
      d\,m_0^{\rho_c}
      \exp(O_c((\log n)^{1/3}))\log^{O_c(1)}n,
   \]
   while with
   \[
      \eta=\Theta_c\!\left(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\right)
   \]
   it is
   \[
      d\,m_0^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n .
   \]
   Thus the old mixed-frontier work assumption is not needed for the
   budget-thinned algorithm; the remaining issue is only near-route value
   delivery.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Let \(F=F_4(m,R)\) at a current state.  The local score work is bounded by
   the continuation-mass work theorem, and the cap-free terminal scans are
   bounded by \cref{lem:cap-free-scan-accounting}.  Since \(\alpha\le1\), both
   contributions are bounded by
   \[
      C_c\,d\,\alpha^{-1}\exp(O_c(\eta\log n))
      F\log^{O_c(1)}n .
      \tag{BW2}
   \]

   For activated geometric children, apply
   \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities} with the canonical mixed budget from
   \cref{lem:canonical-mixed-budget}.  The expected sum of child potentials is
   at most
   \[
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)F .
      \tag{BW3}
   \]
   Every activated certified cap child decreases the radius by a factor
   \(1-\Theta_c(\eta^2)\), so before the radius becomes terminal there are at
   most
   \[
      H=O_c\!\left(\eta^{-2}\log(R_0/r)\right)
      \tag{BW4}
   \]
   radius-decreasing generations.

   Let \(A_h\) be the smallest constant such that every state of remaining
   radius rank at most \(h\) and potential \(F\) has expected remaining work at
   most
   \[
      A_h\,d\,\alpha^{-1}\exp(O_c(\eta\log n))
      F\log^{O_c(1)}n .
   \]
   From \((BW2)\) and \((BW3)\), and by induction over the activated child
   states,
   \[
      A_h\le C_c+(1-\kappa\eta^2)A_{h-1}.
   \]
   Therefore
   \[
      A_h\le \frac{C_c}{\kappa\eta^2}.
   \]
   Since \(R_0/r=\log^{O_c(1)}n\), the rank bound \((BW4)\) is consistent with
   this geometric-series estimate, proving \((BW1)\).

   The two parameter substitutions use
   \[
      F_4(m_0,R_0)=m_0^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      \delta=\Theta_c(\eta^{2/\rho_c}),
      \qquad
      \alpha^{-1}=\eta^{-1-2/\rho_c}\Theta_c(1).
   \]
   For \(\eta=(\log n)^{-2/3}\), all powers of \(\eta^{-1}\) are
   polylogarithmic and \(\exp(O_c(\eta\log n))=\exp(O_c((\log n)^{1/3}))\).
   For \(\eta=\Theta_c((\log\log n)/\log n)\), both
   \(\alpha^{-1}\eta^{-2}\) and the exponential factor are polylogarithmic in
   \(n\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Canonical budget activation factor]\label{lem:canonical-budget-activation}
   Use the canonical mixed budget of \cref{lem:canonical-mixed-budget} inside
   one current geometric state. Thus the parent radius factor in the budget is
   the same $R_u$ that appears in the local split charge. Let $u$ be a retained
   prefix with nonempty bucket $P[u]$, let
   \[
      m_u=|P[u]|,
      \qquad
      \overline H(u)=\frac{D(u)}{m_u},
   \]
   and define the local thickness factor
   \[
      \beta_q(u)
      =
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{\overline H(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}{H_0}
      \right\}.
   \]
   If an almost-total cap opportunity at $u$ has weighted recursive charge
   \[
      \Gamma(u)\le F_4(m_u,R_u),
   \]
   then the budget-thinned activation probability from
   \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities} satisfies
   \[
      \pi(u)\ge (1-\kappa\eta^2)\beta_q(u).
   \]
   In particular, whenever
   \[
      \overline H(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s\ge H_0,
   \]
   the canonical budget activates the opportunity with probability
   $1-O(\eta^2)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   By definition of the canonical budget,
   \[
      \mathcal B_q^{\mathrm{mix}}(u)
      =
      \Psi(R_u)\frac{D(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}{H_0}
      =
      F_4(m_u,R_u)
      \frac{\overline H(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}{H_0}.
   \]
   The budget-thinned rule uses
   \[
      \pi(u)
      =
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\mathrm{mix}}(u)}
              {\Gamma(u)}
      \right\}.
   \]
   Since $\Gamma(u)\le F_4(m_u,R_u)$, this is at least
   \[
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         (1-\kappa\eta^2)
         \frac{\overline H(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}{H_0}
      \right\}
      \ge
      (1-\kappa\eta^2)\beta_q(u).
   \]
   The final statement is the special case $\beta_q(u)=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Canonical defect splits into affinity and resident surprise]
   \label{lem:canonical-defect-splitting}
   Use the canonical mixed budget of
   \cref{lem:canonical-mixed-budget} inside one bounded-diameter geometric
   state.  Let \(u\) be the tail of a budget-thinned recentering opportunity
   needed by a fixed near witness \(p\), and assume \(p\in P[u]\).  Write
   \(H_p(u)\) for the one-point continuation mass of \(p\) at \(u\),
   \(Q_q(u)\) for the query continuation mass, and
   \(\overline H(u)=D(u)/|P[u]|\).  Suppose the opportunity has certified
   recursive charge
   \[
      \Gamma(u)\le F_4(|P[u]|,R_u).
      \tag{CD0}
   \]
   Define the pair-score affinity defect and resident-surprise defect by
   \[
      \mathfrak a_{p,q}(u)
      =
      \log_+
      \frac{H_0}{H_p(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s},
      \qquad
      \mathfrak r_p(u)
      =
      \rho\log_+
      \frac{H_p(u)}{\overline H(u)} ,
      \tag{CD1}
   \]
   with the convention that the right side is \(+\infty\) if a denominator is
   zero while the numerator is positive.  Then the canonical budget-thinning
   defect at this opportunity satisfies
   \[
      \Delta(u):=
      \log_+
      \frac{\Gamma(u)}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u)}
      \le
      \log\frac1{1-\kappa\eta^2}
      +
      \mathfrak a_{p,q}(u)
      +
      \mathfrak r_p(u).
      \tag{CD2}
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   If \(\Gamma(u)=0\), the claim is trivial.  Otherwise, by the definition of
   the canonical budget and by \((CD0)\),
   \[
      \frac{\Gamma(u)}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u)}
      \le
      \frac{H_0}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)\overline H(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}.
   \]
   Since \(p\in P[u]\), the identity
   \[
      \frac{H_0}
           {\overline H(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}
      =
      \frac{H_0}{H_p(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}
      \left(\frac{H_p(u)}{\overline H(u)}\right)^\rho
   \]
   holds whenever the quantities are finite, and the stated infinite
   convention covers the remaining cases.  Taking \(\log_+\) and using
   \(\log_+(abc)\le\log a+\log_+b+\log_+c\) for
   \(a=(1-\kappa\eta^2)^{-1}\ge1\) proves \((CD2)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Posterior superlevel cancels resident surprise]
   \label{lem:posterior-superlevel-cancels-surprise}
   Work in the setting and notation of
   \cref{lem:canonical-defect-splitting}, and assume \(D(u)>0\).  For
   \(L\ge0\), define the resident posterior superlevel set
   \[
      C_L(u)
      =
      \{x\in P[u]:H_x(u)\ge e^L\overline H(u)\}.
      \tag{PSU0}
   \]
   Then
   \[
      |C_L(u)|\le e^{-L}|P[u]|.
      \tag{PSU1}
   \]
   Suppose that a retained decoder can open \(C_L(u)\) as a certified child
   inside the same ball, or a smaller certified ball, with recursive charge
   \[
      \Gamma_L(u)\le F_4(|C_L(u)|,R_u).
      \tag{PSU2}
   \]
   If the near witness \(p\) lies in \(C_L(u)\), then the canonical
   budget-thinning defect of this posterior-refined child satisfies
   \[
      \log_+
      \frac{\Gamma_L(u)}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u)}
      \le
      \log\frac1{1-\kappa\eta^2}
      +
      \mathfrak a_{p,q}(u).
      \tag{PSU3}
   \]
   Thus replacing an unstable whole-bucket recentering edge by the appropriate
   posterior superlevel child removes the resident-surprise term from
   \cref{lem:canonical-defect-splitting}; the price is the need to decode that
   superlevel child output-sensitively.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   By definition of \(\overline H(u)=D(u)/|P[u]|\),
   \[
      |C_L(u)|e^L\overline H(u)
      \le
      \sum_{x\in C_L(u)}H_x(u)
      \le
      D(u)
      =
      |P[u]|\overline H(u).
   \]
   Since \(D(u)>0\), also \(\overline H(u)>0\), and \((PSU1)\) follows.
   The monotonicity of \(F_4(m,R)=m^\rho\Psi(R)\) in \(m\) gives
   \[
      \Gamma_L(u)
      \le
      e^{-\rho L}F_4(|P[u]|,R_u).
   \]
   Dividing by the canonical budget
   \[
      \mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u)
      =
      F_4(|P[u]|,R_u)
      \frac{\overline H(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}{H_0}
   \]
   yields
   \[
      \frac{\Gamma_L(u)}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u)}
      \le
      \frac{H_0}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)(e^L\overline H(u))^\rho Q_q(u)^s}.
   \]
   If \(p\in C_L(u)\), then \(H_p(u)\ge e^L\overline H(u)\).  Therefore the
   right side is at most
   \[
      \frac{H_0}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)H_p(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s}.
   \]
   Taking \(\log_+\) proves \((PSU3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Certified threshold handoff]
   \label{cor:certified-threshold-handoff}
   Work in the setting of
   \cref{lem:posterior-superlevel-cancels-surprise}.  Suppose that, for a
   threshold \(L\), a retained reporter lists a certified cover
   \(\mathcal C_L(u)\) of \(C_L(u)\).  Each \(A\in\mathcal C_L(u)\) is opened as
   a child inside the same ball, or inside a smaller certified ball, and the
   cover has charge slack \(\Xi\ge1\):
   \[
      \sum_{A\in\mathcal C_L(u)} F_4(|A|,R_A)
      \le
      \Xi\,F_4(|C_L(u)|,R_u),
      \tag{CTH0}
   \]
   where \(R_A\le R_u\) is the radius certificate of \(A\).  If the near
   witness \(p\) lies in some reported child \(A_p\), then that child has defect
   \[
      \log_+
      \frac{F_4(|A_p|,R_{A_p})}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u)}
      \le
      \log\Xi
      +
      \log\frac1{1-\kappa\eta^2}
      +
      \mathfrak a_{p,q}(u).
      \tag{CTH1}
   \]
   Hence any output-sensitive soft list decoder whose threshold cover has
   polylogarithmic charge slack converts resident surprise into only an
   \(O(\log\log m)\) toll.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Since \(p\in A_p\),
   \[
      F_4(|A_p|,R_{A_p})
      \le
      \sum_{A\in\mathcal C_L(u)}F_4(|A|,R_A)
      \le
      \Xi F_4(|C_L(u)|,R_u).
   \]
   Apply the proof of
   \cref{lem:posterior-superlevel-cancels-surprise}, with the extra factor
   \(\Xi\), and take \(\log_+\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the lattice decoder must decode]
   \label{rem:posterior-superlevel-list-decoder-target}
   \Cref{cor:certified-threshold-handoff} is the local form of the
   code/lattice interpretation.  The score tree is a generic way to enumerate
   the superlevels of the resident posterior \(H_x(u)\).  A structured
   replacement should decode the same set
   \(C_L(u)=\{x:H_x(u)\ge e^L\overline H(u)\}\), not a nearest raw centroid.
   If \(\log H_x(u)\) factors into block reliabilities, this is exactly a
   soft-threshold list-recovery problem.  If the factors are Euclidean block
   scores, it is a Euclidean list-decoding problem after the usual lifting by
   branch prices.  Construction-A/D quotients, Barnes--Wall or Barnes--Sloane
   recursions, polar list decoders, trellises, GMD/Chase sweeps, and ordered
   statistics decoders are therefore useful only through a certified reporter
   satisfying \((CTH0)\), followed by exact metric filtering.

   This also explains how the standard certified ball recursion is absorbed
   into the same branching-score process.  A dense ball is opened because its
   posterior score is high relative to the resident average; the smaller
   resident count of that posterior superlevel pays for the recursive
   \(m^\rho\) charge.  The remaining proof obligation is not to control
   resident surprise again, but to prove a small cumulative pair-affinity
   defect and to provide a compact reporter for these posterior superlevels.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Affinity-cut posterior handoff]
   \label{cor:affinity-cut-posterior-handoff}
   Fix a near pair \(p,q\) and a port skeleton in the setting of
   \cref{lem:port-skeleton-saturated-comparison}.  For every nonunit skeleton
   edge \(e:s\to t\), suppose the exit port lies at a score prefix \(u_e\)
   containing \(p\), and the child entered by the near route is obtained from a
   certified posterior-superlevel cover as in
   \cref{cor:certified-threshold-handoff}.  Let \(\Gamma_e\) be the charge of
   the activated object containing this child, either the child itself or the
   whole reported cover, and assume the cover has charge slack \(\Xi_e\ge1\):
   \[
      \Gamma_e\le \Xi_eF_4(|C_L(u_e)|,R_{u_e})
      \tag{ACH0}
   \]
   for a threshold \(L\) with \(p\in C_L(u_e)\).  Assume that this object is
   activated by the canonical budget-thinning rule.  Put
   \[
      \omega_e
      =
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{(1-\kappa\eta^2)H_p(u_e)^\rho Q_q(u_e)^s}
              {\Xi_e H_0}
      \right\}.
      \tag{ACH1}
   \]
   For deterministic unit-success skeleton passages set \(\omega_e=1\).
   Let \(A_{\rm aff}\) be the resulting affinity prefix mass on the skeleton:
   \(A_{\rm aff}(s_0)=1\), and
   \(A_{\rm aff}(t)=A_{\rm aff}(s)\omega_e\) on each skeleton edge
   \(e:s\to t\).  If every skeleton route cut \(\mathcal C\) satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{s\in\mathcal C}A_{\rm aff}(s)\ge p_0,
      \tag{ACH2}
   \]
   then the original harmonic route value satisfies
   \[
      W(r_{s_0})\ge\frac{p_0}{L(s_0)}.
      \tag{ACH3}
   \]
   In particular, after posterior-superlevel decoding, the remaining
   near-value target can be stated without a pathwise sum of pair-affinity
   defects: it is enough that the port skeleton have inverse-polylogarithmic
   cut mass under the local affinity weights
   \(H_p(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s/H_0\), up to the decoder slack \(\Xi_e\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Since \(p\in C_L(u_e)\), the proof of
   \cref{lem:posterior-superlevel-cancels-surprise} gives
   \[
      F_4(|C_L(u_e)|,R_{u_e})
      \le
      F_4(|P[u_e]|,R_{u_e})
      \left(\frac{\overline H(u_e)}{H_p(u_e)}\right)^\rho .
   \]
   Combining this with \((ACH0)\) gives the same bound for \(\Gamma_e\), with
   the additional factor \(\Xi_e\).
   By the definition of the canonical mixed budget,
   \[
      \mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u_e)
      =
      F_4(|P[u_e]|,R_{u_e})
      \frac{\overline H(u_e)^\rho Q_q(u_e)^s}{H_0}.
   \]
   Therefore the canonical activation probability on this edge is at least
   \[
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u_e)}
              {\Gamma_e}
      \right\}
      \ge
      \omega_e .
   \]
   Unit-success passages have activation probability \(1=\omega_e\).  Hence
   the true skeleton prefix mass \(A_{\rm sk}\) dominates \(A_{\rm aff}\) at
   every skeleton state.  The cut hypothesis \((ACH2)\) therefore implies
   \[
      \sum_{s\in\mathcal C}A_{\rm sk}(s)\ge p_0
   \]
   for every skeleton cut.  Applying
   \cref{cor:skeleton-cut-conductance} proves \((ACH3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Affinity cut-energy certificate]
   \label{cor:affinity-cut-energy}
   Work in the setting of \cref{cor:affinity-cut-posterior-handoff}.  Let
   \(\Lambda\) be a probability law on the good terminal port-skeleton routes,
   and write \(\Lambda(s)\) for the probability that the sampled route passes
   through skeleton state \(s\).  If every skeleton route cut \(\mathcal C\)
   satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{s\in\mathcal C}
      \frac{\Lambda(s)^2}{A_{\rm aff}(s)}
      \le M ,
      \tag{ACE0}
   \]
   with the usual convention that a positive numerator over zero is infinite,
   then
   \[
      W(r_{s_0})\ge\frac{1}{M\,L(s_0)}.
      \tag{ACE1}
   \]

   Equivalently, delete zero-\(\Lambda\) subtrees and write
   \(\lambda_e=\Pr_\Lambda[t\mid s]\) for \(e:s\to t\).  At positive
   \(\Lambda\) states define the affinity square toll
   \[
      \chi_{\rm aff}(s)
      =
      \sum_{e:s\to t}\frac{\lambda_e^2}{\omega_e}.
      \tag{ACE2}
   \]
   If the square-biased spine generated with transition probabilities
   proportional to \(\lambda_e^2/\omega_e\) satisfies, up to every skeleton
   route cut,
   \[
      \sum\log_+\chi_{\rm aff}(s)\le B ,
      \tag{ACE3}
   \]
   on every stopped spine realization, then
   \[
      W(r_{s_0})\ge \frac{e^{-B}}{L(s_0)} .
      \tag{ACE4}
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Since \(\Lambda\) crosses every skeleton cut exactly once,
   Cauchy's inequality and \((ACE0)\) give
   \[
      1
      =
      \left(\sum_{s\in\mathcal C}\Lambda(s)\right)^2
      \le
      \left(\sum_{s\in\mathcal C}A_{\rm aff}(s)\right)
      \left(\sum_{s\in\mathcal C}
      \frac{\Lambda(s)^2}{A_{\rm aff}(s)}\right).
   \]
   Hence every cut has \(A_{\rm aff}\)-mass at least \(1/M\), and
   \cref{cor:affinity-cut-posterior-handoff} proves \((ACE1)\).

   The spine identity is exactly \cref{lem:frontier-square-toll} applied to
   the skeleton tree with edge probabilities \(\omega_e\).  The toll bound
   \((ACE3)\) implies \((ACE0)\) with \(M=e^B\), giving \((ACE4)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Affinity-proportional route law]
   \label{cor:affinity-proportional-route-law}
   Work in the setting of \cref{cor:affinity-cut-energy}.  Let
   \(\mathcal S\) be a support subtree of good terminal skeleton routes.  At
   every nonterminal skeleton state \(s\in\mathcal S\) with positive
   \(\Lambda(s)\), choose a nonempty set \(E_s\) of outgoing edges whose
   descendants remain in \(\mathcal S\), and put
   \[
      \Omega_s=\sum_{e\in E_s}\omega_e .
      \tag{APR0}
   \]
   Assume \(\Omega_s>0\), and define the local route law by
   \[
      \lambda_e=
      \begin{cases}
         \omega_e/\Omega_s,& e\in E_s,\\
         0,& e\notin E_s .
      \end{cases}
      \tag{APR1}
   \]
   Then the affinity square toll at \(s\) is exactly
   \[
      \chi_{\rm aff}(s)=\frac1{\Omega_s}.
      \tag{APR2}
   \]
   Moreover, the square-biased spine from \cref{cor:affinity-cut-energy}
   has the same transition probabilities as this route law.  Consequently, if
   up to every skeleton route cut the stopped affinity-proportional spine
   satisfies
   \[
      \sum_s\log_+\frac1{\Omega_s}\le B
      \tag{APR3}
   \]
   on every realization, then
   \[
      W(r_{s_0})\ge\frac{e^{-B}}{L(s_0)}.
      \tag{APR4}
   \]

   Thus, after posterior-superlevel decoding, the local route-law choice
   carries no additional branching penalty: the entire second-moment toll is
   the cumulative deficit in total outgoing affinity \(\Omega_s\).  A
   structured code, lattice, trellis, or facility decoder can therefore be
   analyzed by the affinity mass of the decoded port list, followed by exact
   certification of the reported children.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \((APR1)\),
   \[
      \chi_{\rm aff}(s)
      =
      \sum_{e:s\to t}\frac{\lambda_e^2}{\omega_e}
      =
      \sum_{e\in E_s}
      \frac{\omega_e^2}{\Omega_s^2\omega_e}
      =
      \frac1{\Omega_s}.
   \]
   The square-biased spine transition is proportional to
   \(\lambda_e^2/\omega_e=\omega_e/\Omega_s^2\) on \(E_s\), hence after
   normalization it is \(\omega_e/\Omega_s=\lambda_e\).  The stopped toll
   condition \((APR3)\) is therefore exactly \((ACE3)\), and
   \cref{cor:affinity-cut-energy} gives \((APR4)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Surprise-defect route-law certificate]
   \label{cor:surprise-defect-route-law}
   Consider a candidate near-route trie in the canonical budget-thinned
   process.  Deterministic score edges and budget-stable recentering edges have
   success probability \(1\), and every remaining required recentering edge
   \(e\) leaves a prefix \(u(e)\) satisfying the local charge bound
   \[
      \Gamma_e\le F_4(|P[u(e)]|,R_{u(e)}).
      \tag{SD0}
   \]
   Fix a near witness \(p\) and a probability law \(\Lambda\) on successful
   routes for that witness, supported on prefixes containing \(p\).  Suppose
   every route uses at most \(H\) remaining required recentering edges and
   \[
      \E_{v\sim\Lambda}
      \sum_{e\preceq v}
      \bigl(
         \mathfrak a_{p,q}(u(e))
         +
         \mathfrak r_p(u(e))
      \bigr)
      \le B ,
      \tag{SD1}
   \]
   where the two defects are defined in \((CD1)\).  Then the saturated route
   value satisfies
   \[
      U(\varnothing)
      \ge
      \frac12
      \exp\!\left(
         -2B
         -2H\log\frac1{1-\kappa\eta^2}
      \right).
      \tag{SD2}
   \]
   In particular, if
   \[
      B+H\eta^2=O_c(\log\log n),
      \tag{SD3}
   \]
   then the budget-thinned value delivered to this route family is
   inverse-polylogarithmic.  After contracting unit-success regions, the same
   statement applies to the port skeleton; \cref{cor:skeleton-cut-conductance}
   then loses only the polylogarithmic skeleton depth denominator.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   On deterministic score and budget-stable edges the canonical defect from
   \cref{cor:canonical-budget-defect-toll} is zero.  On every remaining edge
   \(e\), apply \cref{lem:canonical-defect-splitting} at \(u(e)\).  Along any
   sampled route,
   \[
      \sum_{e\preceq v}\Delta_e
      \le
      \sum_{e\preceq v}
      \bigl(
         \mathfrak a_{p,q}(u(e))
         +
         \mathfrak r_p(u(e))
      \bigr)
      +
      H\log\frac1{1-\kappa\eta^2}.
   \]
   Taking expectation and using \((SD1)\), the expected canonical defect is at
   most
   \[
      B+H\log\frac1{1-\kappa\eta^2}.
   \]
   The expectation form of \cref{cor:canonical-budget-defect-toll} gives
   \((SD2)\).  Since
   \(\log(1/(1-\kappa\eta^2))=O(\eta^2)\) for the small \(\eta\) used in the
   construction, \((SD3)\) gives inverse-polylogarithmic value.  The final
   port-skeleton statement is exactly the unit-success contraction and cut
   comparison from \cref{lem:port-skeleton-saturated-comparison} and
   \cref{cor:skeleton-cut-conductance}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Work can be bought by thinning]\label{rem:budget-thinning}
   \Cref{cor:budget-gated-splits} is deterministic: a split is live only when
   the local budget pays its whole charge.  \Cref{lem:budget-stable-prefix-list}
   and \cref{cor:canonical-budget-stable-decoder} add the list-decoding
   implementation: budget-stable split prefixes can be found by a stack
   decoder whose admissible score is the canonical budget itself.  Thus the
   deterministic part of the recentering process no longer needs activation
   pattern tables or all-child reporting.

   \Cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities} is the corresponding randomized
   rule for the remaining budget-unstable opportunities. It converts any
   prefix-supermartingale budget, in particular the canonical mixed budget of
   \cref{lem:canonical-mixed-budget}, into an unconditional expected bound for
   the activated geometric work.

   In a homogeneous balanced prefix at depth $\ell$, the canonical budget is smaller than the local recursive charge by about $B^{(1-\rho)\ell}$, so the activation probability is about $B^{-(1-\rho)\ell}$. This is exactly the missing factor in the naive sum over the $B^\ell$ query prefixes. Thus the balanced-prefix obstruction is no longer a work blow-up; it becomes a near-lineage question: prove that the tame-good near paths supplied by \cref{lem:adaptive-continuation} contain enough budget-thinned first opportunities that one complete radius-decreasing lineage survives with inverse-polylogarithmic probability.

   \Cref{lem:canonical-defect-splitting} makes that near-lineage question more
   local.  The loss at an unstable port is the sum of two interpretable
   quantities: a pair-score affinity defect
   \(\log_+(H_0/(H_p^\rho Q_q^s))\), and a resident-surprise defect
   \(\rho\log_+(H_p/\overline H)\).  The first is a continuation-mass
   analogue of the spherical near-pair affinity calculation.  The second says
   that the near point is atypically heavy inside the resident bucket.  High
   resident surprise is exactly the regime where posterior/rate-coverage
   buckets or list-decoded facility proposals should help: they need only cover
   the exposed high-posterior prefixes, while low-surprise prefixes are already
   paid for by the canonical budget.
   \Cref{lem:posterior-superlevel-cancels-surprise} makes this precise: if a
   decoder can expose the resident posterior superlevel containing the near
   witness, the smaller child size cancels the resident-surprise factor
   algebraically and the edge pays only the pair-score affinity defect.
   \Cref{cor:affinity-cut-posterior-handoff} gives the corresponding min-cut
   version: the edge activation probability is lower-bounded by the local
   affinity weight \(H_p(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s/H_0\), divided only by the
   decoder's charge slack.  Thus the port-skeleton value proof can target
   affinity mass on every cut, rather than a cumulative log-affinity loss on a
   single route.  \Cref{cor:affinity-cut-energy} gives the stopped
   second-moment version of the same target: it is enough to construct a law
   on good port routes whose \(\chi^2\) energy against these affinity weights
   is polylogarithmic on every skeleton cut.
   \Cref{cor:affinity-proportional-route-law} gives the canonical local
   normalization of that law: once a decoder exposes a port list \(E_s\),
   branch proportionally to \(\omega_e\).  The square-biased spine is then the
   same process, and the only toll is
   \(\sum_s\log_+(1/\sum_{e\in E_s}\omega_e)\).  Thus the list-decoding or
   lattice-decoding theorem should prove high total outgoing affinity of the
   decoded port list, not an additional route-law inequality.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Activated good-route moments]\label{lem:activated-good-route-moments}
   Fix the realized score tree in one budget-thinned geometric block and a near
   pair $(p,q)$. Let $\mathcal G(p,q)$ be any finite family of candidate good
   routes for this pair. For each route $v\in\mathcal G(p,q)$, let
   $\mathcal O(v)$ be the set of budget-thinned cap opportunities that must
   activate for that route to continue toward the near point. Suppose each
   opportunity $u$ has an independent activation coin of probability $\pi(u)$,
   conditionally on the realized score tree. Define
   \[
      Z_{\rm act}
      =
      \sum_{v\in\mathcal G(p,q)}
      \mathbf 1\{\text{all opportunities in }\mathcal O(v)\text{ activate}\}.
   \]
   Then, conditionally on the realized score tree,
   \[
      \E[Z_{\rm act}]
      =
      \sum_{v\in\mathcal G(p,q)}
      \prod_{u\in\mathcal O(v)}\pi(u),
   \]
   and
   \[
      \E[Z_{\rm act}^2]
      =
      \sum_{v,w\in\mathcal G(p,q)}
      \prod_{u\in\mathcal O(v)\cup\mathcal O(w)}\pi(u).
   \]
   Consequently, if for the random score tree
   \[
      \E Z_{\rm act}\ge \log^{-A_c}n,
      \qquad
      \E Z_{\rm act}^2\le \log^{A_c}n\,(\E Z_{\rm act})^2,
   \]
   then at least one good route is activated with probability
   $\log^{-O_c(1)}n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a fixed route $v$, the required activation event is the intersection of
   the independent coin events indexed by $\mathcal O(v)$, so its conditional
   probability is the displayed product. Summing over routes gives the first
   moment. For the second moment, a pair $(v,w)$ succeeds exactly when every
   coin in the union $\mathcal O(v)\cup\mathcal O(w)$ succeeds; shared
   opportunities are paid once, giving the second display. The final statement
   is Paley--Zygmund:
   \[
      \Prb[Z_{\rm act}>0]\ge
      \frac{(\E Z_{\rm act})^2}{\E Z_{\rm act}^2}.
   \]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Fractional value-delivery moments]\label{cor:fractional-value-delivery}
   Fix the realized score tree in one geometric block and a near pair $(p,q)$.
   Let $\mathcal G(p,q)$ be a finite family of candidate good routes. For each
   route $v$, let $\mathcal O(v)$ be the set of fractional child calls or
   geometric continuation opportunities required by that route. An opportunity
   $u$ has child work surrogate $W_u$, allocated budget $b_u$, and one-call
   conditional success probability at least $\sigma_u$ if the true near point is
   in the corresponding child. Run the opportunity independently with success
   probability
   \[
      \gamma(u)
      =
      \min\left\{1,\frac{b_u}{W_u}\right\}\sigma_u .
   \]
   Define
   \[
      Z_{\rm val}
      =
      \sum_{v\in\mathcal G(p,q)}
      \mathbf 1\{\text{all opportunities in }\mathcal O(v)\text{ succeed}\}.
   \]
   Then, conditionally on the realized score tree,
   \[
      \E[Z_{\rm val}]
      =
      \sum_{v\in\mathcal G(p,q)}
      \prod_{u\in\mathcal O(v)}\gamma(u),
   \]
   and
   \[
      \E[Z_{\rm val}^2]
      =
      \sum_{v,w\in\mathcal G(p,q)}
      \prod_{u\in\mathcal O(v)\cup\mathcal O(w)}\gamma(u).
   \]
   If
   \[
      \E Z_{\rm val}\ge\log^{-A_c}n,
      \qquad
      \E Z_{\rm val}^2\le\log^{A_c}n\,(\E Z_{\rm val})^2,
   \]
   then the fractional value-delivery layer reaches a valid route with
   probability $\log^{-O_c(1)}n$. Its expected work is at most
   \[
      \sum_u b_u,
   \]
   up to the already-accounted local score work.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The moment identities are exactly those of
   \cref{lem:activated-good-route-moments}, with $\gamma(u)$ replacing the
   binary activation probability. The only point to check is work. By
   \cref{lem:fractional-value-budget}, opportunity $u$ costs expected work at
   most $b_u$. Linearity of expectation gives the displayed total. The
   Paley--Zygmund step is identical to
   \cref{lem:activated-good-route-moments}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[LCA value-flow certificate]\label{lem:lca-value-flow}
   Suppose the route family in \cref{cor:fractional-value-delivery} is
   represented by a rooted prefix tree $\mathcal T$ whose leaves are the
   candidate good routes. Each opportunity is attached to one edge of
   $\mathcal T$, with independent success probability $\gamma_e$, and two
   routes share exactly the opportunities on the path to their lowest common
   ancestor. For a node $u$, write
   \[
      A(u)=\prod_{e\preceq u}\gamma_e
   \]
   for the success weight of the prefix from the root to $u$, and
   \[
      V(u)=
      \sum_{\substack{v\text{ leaf}\\ v\succeq u}}
      \prod_{u\prec e\preceq v}\gamma_e
   \]
   for the remaining delivered value below $u$. Then
   \[
      \E Z_{\rm val}=V(\varnothing)
   \]
   and
   \[
      \E Z_{\rm val}^2
      \le
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal T} A(u)V(u)^2 .
   \]
   Consequently, if
   \[
      V(\varnothing)\ge \log^{-A_c}n,
      \qquad
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal T}A(u)V(u)^2
      \le
      \log^{A_c}n\,V(\varnothing)^2,
   \]
   then the fractional value-delivery layer reaches a valid route with
   probability $\log^{-O_c(1)}n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The first identity is the first-moment identity from
   \cref{cor:fractional-value-delivery} written with edge products. For two
   leaves $v,w$ with lowest common ancestor $u$, the opportunities on the
   common prefix are paid once and the two suffixes are paid separately, so
   their contribution to the second moment is
   \[
      A(u)
      \left(\prod_{u\prec e\preceq v}\gamma_e\right)
      \left(\prod_{u\prec e\preceq w}\gamma_e\right).
   \]
   Summing over pairs whose lowest common ancestor is exactly $u$ is at most
   $A(u)V(u)^2$. Summing over $u$ gives the displayed second-moment bound. The
   final statement is Paley--Zygmund.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Unit-capped value-flow certificate]\label{lem:unit-capped-value-flow}
   Work in the route trie of \cref{lem:lca-value-flow}, but allow a fractional
   terminal weight $a_v\in[0,1]$ on every leaf route $v$. For a node $u$, put
   \[
      V(u)=
      \sum_{\substack{v\text{ leaf}\\ v\succeq u}}
      a_v\prod_{u\prec e\preceq v}\gamma_e .
   \]
   Suppose every root-to-leaf route has length at most $H$ and the fractional
   route weights have unit suffix capacity,
   \[
      V(u)\le1
      \qquad\text{for every node }u.
   \]
   Let
   \[
      Z_a=\sum_{v\text{ leaf}}a_v\,
      \mathbf 1\{\text{all opportunities on the route to }v\text{ succeed}\}.
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \E Z_a=V(\varnothing),
      \qquad
      \E Z_a^2\le (H+1)V(\varnothing).
   \]
   Consequently,
   \[
      \Prb[Z_a>0]\ge \frac{V(\varnothing)}{H+1}.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The first moment is the same leaf-product identity as in
   \cref{lem:lca-value-flow}. The LCA second-moment calculation also remains
   valid with fractional terminal weights, giving
   \[
      \E Z_a^2
      \le
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal T} A(u)V(u)^2 .
   \]
   Since $V(u)\le1$, this is at most
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal T} A(u)V(u).
   \]
   For a fixed node $u$,
   \[
      A(u)V(u)
      =
      \sum_{\substack{v\text{ leaf}\\ v\succeq u}}
      a_v\prod_{e\preceq v}\gamma_e .
   \]
   Summing over all nodes counts the contribution of each leaf once for each
   ancestor, hence at most $H+1$ times. Therefore
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal T} A(u)V(u)
      \le
      (H+1)
      \sum_{v\text{ leaf}}a_v\prod_{e\preceq v}\gamma_e
      =
      (H+1)V(\varnothing).
   \]
   Paley--Zygmund gives the displayed success lower bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Saturated value recursion]\label{lem:saturated-value-recursion}
   Fix a finite route trie with edge success probabilities
   $\gamma_{u,w}\in[0,1]$. Define the saturated suffix value $U$ by
   \[
      U(v)=1\quad\text{at leaves},
      \qquad
      U(u)=
      \min\left\{1,\sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \gamma_{u,w}U(w)\right\}
   \]
   at internal nodes. Then $U(\varnothing)$ is exactly the largest root value
   achievable by fractional terminal weights satisfying the unit suffix
   capacity condition of \cref{lem:unit-capped-value-flow}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   First, any unit-capped fractional terminal weights have value at most $U$ at
   every node. This is immediate at leaves, since terminal weights are at most
   one. If it holds for the children of $u$, then
   \[
      V(u)=\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}V(w)
      \le
      \sum_w\gamma_{u,w}U(w),
   \]
   while unit suffix capacity gives $V(u)\le1$. Hence $V(u)\le U(u)$.

   Conversely, we prove by induction that for every node $u$ and every
   $0\le t\le U(u)$, there are fractional terminal weights inside the subtree
   of $u$ whose suffix value at $u$ is exactly $t$ and whose suffix value at
   every descendant is at most one. At a leaf, set its terminal weight to $t$.
   At an internal node, write
   \[
      S(u)=\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}U(w).
   \]
   Since $t\le U(u)\le S(u)$ unless $S(u)=0$, choose numbers
   $0\le t_w\le U(w)$ with
   \[
      \sum_w\gamma_{u,w}t_w=t
   \]
   by a greedy allocation over the children. Apply the induction hypothesis in
   each child subtree with target $t_w$. The resulting terminal weights have
   value $t$ at $u$, and all descendant suffix values remain at most one.
   Taking $u=\varnothing$ and $t=U(\varnothing)$ proves attainability.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Saturated value min-cut]\label{lem:saturated-value-mincut}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion}, put
   \[
      A(\varnothing)=1,
      \qquad
      A(w)=A(u)\gamma_{u,w}
      \quad\text{for every edge }u\to w .
   \]
   A route cut is an antichain $\mathcal C$ of trie nodes meeting every
   root-to-leaf route. Then
   \[
      U(\varnothing)
      =
      \min_{\mathcal C}
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}A(u).
   \]
   More generally, for every node $u$,
   \[
      A(u)U(u)
      =
      \min_{\mathcal C\text{ cuts all leaves below }u}
      \sum_{z\in\mathcal C}A(z).
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $W(u)=A(u)U(u)$. At a leaf, $W(u)=A(u)$. At an internal node,
   \[
      W(u)
      =
      A(u)\min\left\{1,\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}U(w)\right\}
      =
      \min\left\{A(u),\sum_w A(w)U(w)\right\}
      =
      \min\left\{A(u),\sum_w W(w)\right\}.
   \]
   The minimum route-cut cost below $u$ satisfies the same recursion: either
   cut immediately at $u$, paying $A(u)$, or cut each child subtree and take
   the union of those child cuts. The leaf boundary condition is also the same.
   Backward induction proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Dominated route-law certificate]\label{lem:dominated-route-law}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion}, and write
   $A(u)$ for the prefix-success mass from
   \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut}. For $p\in(0,1]$, the following are
   equivalent.
   \begin{enumerate}
      \item The saturated value satisfies $U(\varnothing)\ge p$.
      \item There is a probability distribution $\Lambda$ on the leaf routes
      such that, for every trie node $u$,
      \[
         \Lambda(u):=\Lambda\{v\text{ leaf}:v\succeq u\}
         \le \frac{A(u)}{p}.
      \]
   \end{enumerate}
   Consequently, a cutset lower bound may be proved by constructing a route
   law whose prefix probabilities are dominated by the corresponding
   activation prefix probabilities up to the single global factor $p^{-1}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Suppose first that $U(\varnothing)\ge p$. By
   \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion}, choose unit-capped fractional
   terminal weights $a_v$ with root value $p$. Define
   \[
      \Lambda(v)=\frac{a_vA(v)}{p}
   \]
   on leaves. These weights sum to one because the root value is
   $\sum_v a_vA(v)=p$. For any node $u$,
   \[
      \Lambda(u)
      =
      \frac1p
      \sum_{v\succeq u}a_vA(v)
      =
      \frac{A(u)V(u)}p
      \le
      \frac{A(u)}p,
   \]
   where $V(u)\le1$ is the unit suffix capacity.

   Conversely, suppose such a law $\Lambda$ is given. For a leaf $v$, set
   \[
      a_v=\frac{p\,\Lambda(v)}{A(v)}
   \]
   when $A(v)>0$, and $a_v=0$ when $A(v)=0$. The leaf-prefix domination gives
   $a_v\le1$. For a node $u$ with $A(u)>0$, the induced suffix value is
   \[
      V(u)
      =
      \sum_{v\succeq u}a_v\frac{A(v)}{A(u)}
      =
      \frac{p\,\Lambda(u)}{A(u)}
      \le1.
   \]
   Nodes with $A(u)=0$ have zero contribution. Thus the terminal weights are
   unit-capped and their root value is $p$. By
   \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion}, $U(\varnothing)\ge p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Cut-energy route-law certificate]\label{lem:cut-energy-route-law}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion}, and write
   $A(u)$ for the prefix-success mass from
   \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut}. Suppose there is a probability
   distribution $\Lambda$ on the leaf routes such that every route cut
   $\mathcal C$ satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}\frac{\Lambda(u)^2}{A(u)}\le M,
   \]
   with the convention that the summand is $+\infty$ when
   $\Lambda(u)>0$ and $A(u)=0$. Then
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge \frac1M .
   \]
   Thus it is enough to control the $\chi^2$ energy of $\Lambda$ on every
   stopping frontier; one need not sum the energy over all prefixes.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $\mathcal C$ be any route cut. Since $\mathcal C$ is an antichain meeting
   every leaf route, a route sampled from $\Lambda$ passes through exactly one
   node of $\mathcal C$. Hence
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}\Lambda(u)=1 .
   \]
   Cauchy's inequality gives
   \[
      1
      =
      \left(\sum_{u\in\mathcal C}\Lambda(u)\right)^2
      \le
      \left(\sum_{u\in\mathcal C}A(u)\right)
      \left(\sum_{u\in\mathcal C}\frac{\Lambda(u)^2}{A(u)}\right)
      \le
      M\sum_{u\in\mathcal C}A(u).
   \]
   Therefore every route cut has prefix-success mass at least $1/M$. The
   min-cut dual \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut} gives the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Frontier square-toll identity]\label{lem:frontier-square-toll}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:cut-energy-route-law}, after deleting
   zero-$\Lambda$ subtrees. For an edge $u\to w$, write
   \[
      \lambda_{u,w}=\Pr_\Lambda[w\mid u],
      \qquad
      \gamma_{u,w}=\frac{A(w)}{A(u)}
   \]
   whenever $\Lambda(u)>0$ and $A(u)>0$, and assume the displayed quantities
   below are finite. Put
   \[
      \chi_\Lambda(u)=
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2}{\gamma_{u,w}}
   \]
   at every internal positive-$\Lambda$ node, and define the square-biased
   transition law
   \[
      P_u(w)=
      \frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2/\gamma_{u,w}}{\chi_\Lambda(u)} .
   \]
   Let $(U_0,U_1,\ldots)$ be the resulting spine from the root, and let
   $\tau_{\mathcal C}$ be its first hitting time of a route cut
   $\mathcal C$. Then
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}\frac{\Lambda(u)^2}{A(u)}
      =
      \E\left[
         \prod_{i<\tau_{\mathcal C}}\chi_\Lambda(U_i)
      \right].
   \]
   Consequently, if every route cut $\mathcal C$ satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{i<\tau_{\mathcal C}}\log_+\chi_\Lambda(U_i)\le B
      \qquad
      \text{on every square-biased spine realization,}
   \]
   then
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge e^{-B}.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a node $u\in\mathcal C$, multiply the transition identities along the
   unique root-to-$u$ path:
   \[
      \Pr[U_{\tau_{\mathcal C}}=u]
      =
      \prod_{y\to z\preceq u}
      \frac{\lambda_{y,z}^2/\gamma_{y,z}}{\chi_\Lambda(y)} .
   \]
   Therefore
   \[
      \Pr[U_{\tau_{\mathcal C}}=u]
      \prod_{y\prec u}\chi_\Lambda(y)
      =
      \prod_{y\to z\preceq u}\frac{\lambda_{y,z}^2}{\gamma_{y,z}}
      =
      \frac{\Lambda(u)^2}{A(u)} .
   \]
   Summing over the cut proves the identity. If the positive square toll is at
   most $B$ on every stopped spine, the cut energy is at most $e^B$ for every
   route cut. Apply \cref{lem:cut-energy-route-law}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Route-law toll certificate]\label{cor:route-law-toll}
   Let $\Lambda$ be a probability distribution on leaf routes. For an edge
   $u\to w$, write
   \[
      \lambda_{u,w}=\Pr_{\Lambda}[w\mid u]
      =
      \frac{\Lambda(w)}{\Lambda(u)}
   \]
   when $\Lambda(u)>0$. Suppose every positive-$\Lambda$ root-to-leaf route
   satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{u\to w\preceq v}
      \log_+\frac{\lambda_{u,w}}{\gamma_{u,w}}
      \le B,
   \]
   with the convention that the summand is $+\infty$ if
   $\lambda_{u,w}>0$ and $\gamma_{u,w}=0$. Then
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge e^{-B}.
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Along any prefix $u$ of any positive-$\Lambda$ route, the displayed toll
   bound gives
   \[
      \Lambda(u)
      =
      \prod_{e\preceq u}\lambda_e
      \le
      e^B\prod_{e\preceq u}\gamma_e
      =
      e^BA(u).
   \]
   Apply \cref{lem:dominated-route-law} with $p=e^{-B}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Typical route-law toll certificate]\label{cor:typical-route-law-toll}
   Let $\Lambda$ be a probability distribution on leaf routes, and define the
   prefix domination height of a leaf $v$ by
   \[
      T_\Lambda(v)
      =
      \max_{u\preceq v}
      \log_+\frac{\Lambda(u)}{A(u)},
   \]
   with $T_\Lambda(v)=+\infty$ if $v$ has a prefix $u$ with
   $\Lambda(u)>0$ and $A(u)=0$. If
   \[
      \Lambda\{v:T_\Lambda(v)\le B\}\ge h,
   \]
   then
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge h e^{-B}.
   \]
   In particular, if for $\Lambda$-a.e. leaf $v$ the cumulative edge toll
   \[
      T_{\rm edge}(v)=
      \sum_{u\to w\preceq v}
      \log_+\frac{\lambda_{u,w}}{\gamma_{u,w}}
   \]
   has expectation at most $B$, then
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge \frac12 e^{-2B}.
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Let $E=\{v:T_\Lambda(v)\le B\}$ and condition $\Lambda$ on $E$. The
   conditional law $\Lambda_E$ satisfies, for every trie node $u$,
   \[
      \Lambda_E(u)
      =
      \frac{\Lambda(E\cap\{v:v\succeq u\})}{\Lambda(E)}
      \le
      \frac{\Lambda(u)}{h}
      \le
      \frac{e^BA(u)}{h}.
   \]
   Applying \cref{lem:dominated-route-law} with
   $p=he^{-B}$ proves the first claim.

   For every prefix of $v$,
   \[
      \frac{\Lambda(u)}{A(u)}
      =
      \prod_{e\preceq u}\frac{\lambda_e}{\gamma_e}
      \le
      \exp(T_{\rm edge}(v)),
   \]
   so $T_\Lambda(v)\le T_{\rm edge}(v)$. Markov's inequality gives
   $\Lambda\{T_{\rm edge}\le2B\}\ge1/2$ when $B>0$; if $B=0$ this event has
   probability one. The first claim with $h=1/2$ and threshold $2B$ gives the
   displayed bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Canonical budget-defect toll certificate]
   \label{cor:canonical-budget-defect-toll}
   Consider a candidate near-route trie in the canonical budget-thinned
   process.  Deterministic score edges and budget-stable recentering edges have
   success probability \(1\).  Every remaining required recentering edge \(e\)
   is offered at some prefix \(u(e)\), has certified recursive charge
   \(\Gamma_e\), and is activated with probability \(\gamma_e\) satisfying
   \[
      \gamma_e\ge
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{(1-\kappa\eta^2)
               \mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u(e))}
              {\Gamma_e}
      \right\}.
   \]
   When \(\Gamma_e=0\), the displayed lower bound is interpreted as \(1\).
   On remaining required recentering edges, define the canonical budget defect
   by
   \[
      \Delta_e
      =
      \log_+
      \frac{\Gamma_e}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u(e))},
   \]
   with the convention that the ratio is \(+\infty\) when
   \(\Gamma_e>0\) and \(\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u(e))=0\), and is \(0\) when
   \(\Gamma_e=0\).
   Put \(\Delta_e=0\) on deterministic score and budget-stable edges.
   Let \(\Lambda\) be any probability law on leaf routes, and for a route \(v\)
   set
   \[
      \Delta(v)=\sum_{e\preceq v}\Delta_e .
   \]
   If
   \[
      \Lambda\{v:\Delta(v)\le B\}\ge h,
   \]
   then the saturated route value satisfies
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge h e^{-B}.
   \]
   In particular, if \(\E_\Lambda \Delta(v)\le B\), then
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge \frac12e^{-2B}.
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Let \(\lambda_e\) be the transition probability of the route law on edge
   \(e\).  On deterministic score and budget-stable edges, \(\gamma_e=1\), so
   \(\log_+(\lambda_e/\gamma_e)=0\) because \(\lambda_e\le1\).  On a
   budget-thinned edge,
   \[
      \frac{\lambda_e}{\gamma_e}
      \le
      \frac1{\gamma_e}
      \le
      \max\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{\Gamma_e}
              {(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u(e))}
      \right\},
   \]
   hence
   \[
      \log_+\frac{\lambda_e}{\gamma_e}\le\Delta_e .
   \]
   Thus the cumulative edge toll of \cref{cor:typical-route-law-toll} is at
   most \(\Delta(v)\) for every route \(v\).  Applying that corollary gives the
   high-probability statement.  Markov's inequality gives the final displayed
   bound from the expectation assumption.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Pruned budget-defect toll certificate]
   \label{cor:pruned-budget-defect-toll}
   Consider the setting of \cref{cor:canonical-budget-defect-toll}, but allow
   each remaining required recentering edge \(e\) to use a certified pruning
   multiplier \(r_e\ge0\).  Suppose its activation probability satisfies
   \[
      \gamma_e\ge
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{(1-\kappa\eta^2)r_e
               \mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u(e))}
              {\Gamma_e}
      \right\},
   \]
   with the same \(\Gamma_e=0\) convention. Define the pruned canonical defect
   by
   \[
      \Delta^{(r)}_e
      =
      \log_+
      \frac{\Gamma_e}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)r_e\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u(e))},
   \]
   with ratio \(+\infty\) when \(\Gamma_e>0\) and the denominator is zero, and
   ratio \(0\) when \(\Gamma_e=0\). Put \(\Delta^{(r)}_e=0\) on deterministic
   score and budget-stable edges. If a route law \(\Lambda\) satisfies
   \[
      \Lambda\left\{v:\sum_{e\preceq v}\Delta^{(r)}_e\le B\right\}\ge h,
   \]
   then \(U(\varnothing)\ge he^{-B}\). If
   \[
      \E_\Lambda\sum_{e\preceq v}\Delta^{(r)}_e\le B,
   \]
   then \(U(\varnothing)\ge \frac12e^{-2B}\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The proof of \cref{cor:canonical-budget-defect-toll} applies with
   \(r_e\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u(e))\) in place of
   \(\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u(e))\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Allocation-defect toll certificate]
   \label{cor:allocation-defect-toll}
   Consider a candidate near-route trie in which deterministic score edges and
   budget-stable recentering edges have success probability \(1\). Every
   remaining required recentering edge \(e\) has certified recursive charge
   \(\Gamma_e\), receives a certified activation allocation \(Y_e\ge0\), and
   is activated with probability \(\gamma_e\) satisfying
   \[
      \gamma_e\ge
      \min\left\{
         1,\,
         \frac{(1-\kappa\eta^2)Y_e}{\Gamma_e}
      \right\},
   \]
   with the convention that the displayed lower bound is \(1\) when
   \(\Gamma_e=0\). Define the allocation defect by
   \[
      \Delta^{(Y)}_e
      =
      \log_+
      \frac{\Gamma_e}{(1-\kappa\eta^2)Y_e},
   \]
   with ratio \(+\infty\) when \(\Gamma_e>0\) and \(Y_e=0\), and ratio \(0\)
   when \(\Gamma_e=0\). Put \(\Delta^{(Y)}_e=0\) on deterministic score and
   budget-stable edges. If a route law \(\Lambda\) satisfies
   \[
      \Lambda\left\{v:\sum_{e\preceq v}\Delta^{(Y)}_e\le B\right\}\ge h,
   \]
   then \(U(\varnothing)\ge he^{-B}\). If
   \[
      \E_\Lambda\sum_{e\preceq v}\Delta^{(Y)}_e\le B,
   \]
   then \(U(\varnothing)\ge \frac12e^{-2B}\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   This is \cref{cor:typical-route-law-toll} applied exactly as in
   \cref{cor:canonical-budget-defect-toll}, with \(Y_e\) replacing the local
   budget.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Water-filled optimal activation allocation]
   \label{lem:waterfilled-allocation}
   Let \(E\) be a finite set of required opportunities for one query state.
   A route law uses edge \(e\) with occupation weight
   \(\ell_e\ge0\), and the edge has normalized charge \(a_e\ge0\).  For an
   allocation \(y_e\ge0\) with \(\sum_e y_e\le1\), define the expected defect
   \[
      \mathcal D(y)
      =
      \sum_e \ell_e\log_+\frac{a_e}{y_e},
   \]
   with the conventions that terms with \(\ell_e=0\) or \(a_e=0\) contribute
   \(0\), while \(\ell_e>0\), \(a_e>0\), \(y_e=0\) contributes \(+\infty\).
   If
   \[
      \sum_{\ell_e>0}a_e\le1,
   \]
   then \(\inf_y\mathcal D(y)=0\).  Otherwise let \(t>0\) be chosen so that
   \[
      \sum_{\ell_e>0}\min\{a_e,t\ell_e\}=1 .
   \]
   Then the allocation
   \[
      y_e^*=
      \begin{cases}
         \min\{a_e,t\ell_e\},&\ell_e>0,\\
         0,&\ell_e=0,
      \end{cases}
   \]
   minimizes \(\mathcal D(y)\), and the minimum value is
   \[
      \mathcal D^*
      =
      \sum_e \ell_e\log_+\frac{a_e}{t\ell_e}.
   \]
   The same zero-term conventions apply in the last display.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Edges with \(\ell_e=0\) do not affect the objective and never need budget.
   If the occupied charge sum is at most one, allocate \(y_e=a_e\) on occupied
   edges and obtain zero defect.

   Assume the occupied charge sum is larger than one.  The function
   \(f_e(y)=\ell_e\log_+(a_e/y)\) is convex and nonincreasing on \(y>0\), with
   derivative \(-\ell_e/y\) when \(0<y<a_e\) and derivative \(0\) when
   \(y>a_e\).  Let \(\lambda=1/t\).  We claim that for every occupied edge and
   every \(y\ge0\),
   \[
      f_e(y)\ge f_e(y_e^*)-\lambda(y-y_e^*) .
   \]
   If \(y_e^*<a_e\), then \(y_e^*=t\ell_e\) and the displayed inequality is
   the tangent-line inequality for the convex function \(\ell_e\log(a_e/y)\).
   If \(y_e^*=a_e\), then \(a_e\le t\ell_e\), so \(\lambda\le\ell_e/a_e\).
   For \(y<a_e\),
   \[
      \ell_e\log\frac{a_e}{y}
      \ge
      \ell_e\left(1-\frac{y}{a_e}\right)
      \ge
      \lambda(a_e-y),
   \]
   while for \(y\ge a_e\) the right-hand side is nonpositive.  This proves the
   claim in all cases.

   Summing the claim over occupied edges and using
   \(\sum_e y_e^*=1\) gives, for every feasible allocation \(y\),
   \[
      \mathcal D(y)
      \ge
      \mathcal D(y^*)-\lambda\left(\sum_e y_e-1\right)
      \ge
      \mathcal D(y^*) .
   \]
   Thus \(y^*\) is optimal, and substituting it into the objective gives the
   displayed value.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Water-filled allocation defect certificate]
   \label{cor:waterfilled-allocation-defect}
   In the setting of \cref{cor:allocation-defect-toll}, fix a route law
   \(\Lambda\) and let
   \[
      \ell_e=\Pr_{V\sim\Lambda}[e\preceq V],
      \qquad
      a_e=\frac{\Gamma_e}{(1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R)} .
   \]
   Let \(\mathcal D^*\) be the water-filled optimum from
   \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation}.  If the corresponding allocation is
   query-decodable and \(\mathcal D^*\le B\), then the saturated route value
   satisfies
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge \frac12e^{-2B}.
   \]
   In particular, \(B=O_c(\log\log n)\) gives inverse-polylogarithmic route
   value.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Use the water-filled allocation \(Y_e=F_4(m,R)y_e^*\).  The expected
   allocation defect under \(\Lambda\) is exactly \(\mathcal D^*\).  Apply the
   expectation form of \cref{cor:allocation-defect-toll}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Transition-aware water-filled certificate]
   \label{cor:transition-waterfilled-allocation}
   In the setting of \cref{cor:allocation-defect-toll}, fix a route law
   \(\Lambda\).  For a required edge \(e=u\to w\), write
   \[
      \lambda_e=\Pr_\Lambda[w\mid u]
   \]
   when \(\Lambda(u)>0\), and put \(\lambda_e=0\) otherwise.  Let
   \[
      \ell_e=\Pr_{V\sim\Lambda}[e\preceq V],
      \qquad
      a_e=\frac{\Gamma_e}{(1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R)},
      \qquad
      b_e=\lambda_ea_e .
   \]
   For any allocation \(y\), define the transition-aware defect
   \[
      \mathcal D_{\rm tr}(y)
      =
      \sum_e\ell_e\log_+\frac{b_e}{y_e},
   \]
   with the same zero and infinity conventions as in
   \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation}.  If \(y\) is query-decodable and
   \(\mathcal D_{\rm tr}(y)\le B\), then the saturated route value satisfies
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge \frac12e^{-2B}.
   \]
   In particular, applying \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation} with occupation
   weights \(\ell_e\) and transition-aware charges \(b_e\) gives the best such
   bound; write the optimum as \(\mathcal D^*_{\rm tr}\).

   Moreover, if \(H=\sum_e\ell_e\le H_0\), then
   \[
      \mathcal D^*_{\rm tr}
      \le
      \sum_{\substack{e:\ \ell_e>0\\ \lambda_ea_e>0}}
      \ell_e\log_+\frac{H_0\lambda_ea_e}{\ell_e}.
   \]
   Since \(\ell_e=\Lambda(u)\lambda_e\) on every positive-\(\Lambda\) edge
   \(e=u\to w\), this bound may be written as
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{e=u\to w:\ \ell_e>0\\ a_e>0}}
      \ell_e\log_+\frac{H_0a_e}{\Lambda(u)}.
   \]
   Thus \(H_0=O_c(\log\log n)\) and the prefix-density condition
   \[
      H_0a_e\le C\Lambda(u)
      \qquad(e=u\to w\text{ used by }\Lambda)
   \]
   give \(\mathcal D^*_{\rm tr}=O_c(\log\log n)\) for \(C=O_c(1)\).  If
   \[
      \sum_{\ell_e>0}\lambda_ea_e\le1,
   \]
   then \(\mathcal D^*_{\rm tr}=0\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Fix any allocation \(y\) and use \(Y_e=F_4(m,R)y_e\) in
   \cref{cor:allocation-defect-toll}.  The activation probability satisfies
   \[
      \gamma_e\ge\min\{1,y_e/a_e\},
   \]
   with the usual convention when \(a_e=0\).  If \(y_e\ge a_e\), then
   \(\gamma_e=1\) and \(\lambda_e/\gamma_e\le1\).  If \(y_e<a_e\), then
   \[
      \frac{\lambda_e}{\gamma_e}
      \le
      \frac{\lambda_ea_e}{y_e}.
   \]
   If \(b_e=0\), the right-hand side is interpreted as zero and the inequality
   is trivial because \(\lambda_e/\gamma_e\le1\).  Hence, in all cases,
   \[
      \log_+\frac{\lambda_e}{\gamma_e}
      \le
      \log_+\frac{\lambda_ea_e}{y_e}.
   \]
   Averaging over \(V\sim\Lambda\), the expected edge toll is at most
   \[
      \sum_e\ell_e\log_+\frac{b_e}{y_e}.
   \]
   Thus any allocation with transition-aware defect at most \(B\) has expected
   edge toll at most \(B\), and the expectation form of
   \cref{cor:typical-route-law-toll} gives the displayed route value.  The
   optimality statement is exactly \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation} applied to
   the charges \(b_e\).

   The raw logarithmic estimate is
   \cref{lem:waterfilled-raw-moment-certificate} applied to the same
   occupation weights and charges \(b_e\), using \(\tau=1/H_0\).  The
   simplification follows from
   \[
      \ell_e=\Pr_\Lambda[u]\Pr_\Lambda[w\mid u]=\Lambda(u)\lambda_e .
   \]
   The prefix-density and zero-defect conclusions are immediate from the
   displayed raw bound and the zero-defect case of
   \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Relation to square-energy tolls]
   The transition-aware profile is the allocation form of square-benign
   branching.  If the query can afford \(y_e\ge\lambda_ea_e\) on every outgoing
   required edge, then \(\gamma_e\ge\lambda_e\).  Consequently the domination
   toll on that edge is zero and the local square multiplier satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{w}\frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2}{\gamma_{u,w}}
      \le
      \sum_w\lambda_{u,w}
      \le1 .
   \]
   Water-filling is therefore the budget-constrained, extreme-pruning version
   of the ideal square-nonexpansive profile.  The remaining loss measures only
   how far the decodable activation budget is from the transition-proportional
   charge \(\lambda_ea_e\), rather than from the full edge charge \(a_e\).
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Antichain prefix-density flow]
   \label{lem:antichain-prefix-density-flow}
   Let \(\mathcal T\) be a finite rooted trie whose leaves are successful
   routes, and let \(d(u)\ge0\) be a demand on trie nodes.  There is a
   probability law \(\Lambda\) on the leaves such that
   \[
      \Lambda(u)\ge d(u)
      \qquad\text{for every node }u,
   \]
   if and only if every antichain \(\mathcal C\) of trie nodes satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}d(u)\le1.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Necessity is immediate: an antichain is prefix-free, so a leaf route passes
   through at most one node of \(\mathcal C\), and hence
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}d(u)
      \le
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}\Lambda(u)
      \le1.
   \]

   For sufficiency, define the required mass \(r(u)\) of a subtree recursively.
   At a leaf put \(r(u)=d(u)\).  At an internal node put
   \[
      r(u)=
      \max\left\{
         d(u),\,
         \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}r(w)
      \right\}.
   \]
   By induction, \(r(u)\) is the largest total demand of an antichain contained
   in the subtree rooted at \(u\): such an antichain either contains \(u\), or
   is the disjoint union of antichains from the child subtrees.  Therefore the
   hypothesis gives \(r(\varnothing)\le1\).

   We now assign leaf mass recursively.  Give the root mass \(m(\varnothing)=1\).
   Suppose a node \(u\) has mass \(m(u)\ge r(u)\).  If \(u\) is a leaf, put
   this mass on that leaf.  If \(u\) is internal, first assign each child
   \(w\) mass \(r(w)\).  The remaining mass
   \(m(u)-\sum_wr(w)\ge0\) is distributed arbitrarily among the children.
   Then every child receives mass at least its \(r(w)\), and the child masses
   sum to \(m(u)\), so the recursion continues.  The induced probability law
   on leaves has \(\Lambda(u)=m(u)\ge r(u)\ge d(u)\) at every node.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Local potential certificate for antichain charge]
   \label{lem:local-antichain-charge-potential}
   Let \(\mathcal T\) be a finite rooted trie and let \(b(u)\ge0\) be a charge
   on trie nodes.  Suppose there is a nonnegative potential \(\Phi(u)\) such
   that, for every node \(u\),
   \[
      b(u)\le\Phi(u),
      \qquad
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\Phi(w)\le\Phi(u).
   \]
   Then every antichain \(\mathcal C\) contained in the subtree rooted at \(v\)
   satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C} b(u)\le\Phi(v).
   \]
   In particular, if \(\Phi(\varnothing)\le C/H_0\), then
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}b(u)\le \frac{C}{H_0}
   \]
   for every antichain of \(\mathcal T\).

   A useful stronger sufficient condition is the potential-drop inequality
   \[
      b(u)+\sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\Phi(w)\le\Phi(u)
      \qquad(u\in\mathcal T),
   \]
   which implies the two displayed hypotheses.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   We prove the subtree statement by induction from the leaves upward.  If
   \(v\) is a leaf, every antichain in its subtree is either empty or
   \(\{v\}\), so the claim follows from \(b(v)\le\Phi(v)\).

   Let \(v\) be internal.  If an antichain \(\mathcal C\) in the subtree of
   \(v\) contains \(v\), then it contains no strict descendant of \(v\), and
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}b(u)=b(v)\le\Phi(v).
   \]
   Otherwise \(\mathcal C\) is the disjoint union of its intersections with
   the child subtrees.  By induction,
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}b(u)
      \le
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}\Phi(w)
      \le
      \Phi(v).
   \]
   This proves the claim for every subtree.  The root statement is the case
   \(v=\varnothing\).  The potential-drop condition implies
   \(b(u)\le\Phi(u)\) and
   \(\sum_w\Phi(w)\le\Phi(u)\) because all terms are nonnegative.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Least superharmonic majorant]
   \label{lem:least-superharmonic-majorant}
   Let \(\mathcal T\) be a finite rooted trie and let \(b(u)\ge0\) be a charge
   on trie nodes.  Define \(\Phi^*\) by the backward recursion
   \[
      \Phi^*(u)=b(u)\quad\text{at leaves},
   \]
   and, at internal nodes,
   \[
      \Phi^*(u)=
      \max\left\{
         b(u),\,
         \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\Phi^*(w)
      \right\}.
   \]
   Then \(\Phi^*\) is the pointwise least nonnegative potential satisfying
   \[
      b(u)\le\Phi(u),
      \qquad
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\Phi(w)\le\Phi(u)
   \]
   at every node.  Moreover, for every node \(v\),
   \[
      \Phi^*(v)
      =
      \max_{\mathcal C\subseteq\mathcal T_v\ {\rm antichain}}
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}b(u),
   \]
   where \(\mathcal T_v\) is the subtree rooted at \(v\).  Thus the existence
   of a potential with \(\Phi(\varnothing)\le C/H_0\) is equivalent to the
   single computable inequality
   \[
      \Phi^*(\varnothing)\le \frac{C}{H_0}.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The recursion immediately gives \(b(u)\le\Phi^*(u)\) and
   \(\sum_w\Phi^*(w)\le\Phi^*(u)\), so \(\Phi^*\) is feasible.  If \(\Phi\) is
   any other feasible potential, then at leaves
   \(\Phi(u)\ge b(u)=\Phi^*(u)\).  Inductively, at an internal node,
   \[
      \Phi(u)
      \ge
      b(u),
      \qquad
      \Phi(u)
      \ge
      \sum_w\Phi(w)
      \ge
      \sum_w\Phi^*(w),
   \]
   hence \(\Phi(u)\ge\Phi^*(u)\).  This proves pointwise minimality.

   The antichain formula follows by the same backward induction.  In the
   subtree of a leaf \(v\), the largest antichain charge is \(b(v)\).  At an
   internal node \(v\), an antichain in \(\mathcal T_v\) either contains
   \(v\), giving charge \(b(v)\), or is the disjoint union of antichains in the
   child subtrees, giving maximum total charge
   \(\sum_w\Phi^*(w)\) by induction.  Taking the maximum of these two cases is
   exactly the displayed recursion.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Superharmonic potential route law]
   \label{lem:superharmonic-potential-route-law}
   Let \(\mathcal T\) be a finite rooted trie whose leaves are successful
   routes.  Suppose \(\Phi(u)\ge0\) satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\Phi(w)\le\Phi(u)
      \qquad\text{for every internal node }u,
   \]
   and \(\Phi(\varnothing)\le1\).  Then there is a probability law
