   \(\Lambda\) on the leaves such that
   \[
      \Lambda(u)\ge\Phi(u)
      \qquad\text{for every node }u.
   \]
   More generally, if \(b(u)\ge0\), \(b(u)\le\Phi(u)\), and
   \(\Phi(\varnothing)\le C/H_0\), then there is a leaf law satisfying
   \[
      H_0b(u)\le C\Lambda(u)
      \qquad\text{for every node }u.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   We prove the normalized statement.  Give the root mass
   \(m(\varnothing)=1\).  Suppose an internal node \(u\) has already received
   mass \(m(u)\ge\Phi(u)\).  Assign each child \(w\) a mandatory mass
   \(\Phi(w)\).  The total mandatory child mass is at most \(\Phi(u)\le m(u)\),
   so the leftover
   \[
      m(u)-\sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\Phi(w)
   \]
   is nonnegative and can be distributed arbitrarily among the children.  This
   gives every child mass at least its \(\Phi(w)\), and the child masses sum to
   \(m(u)\).  Recurse until leaves and put the resulting masses on the leaves.
   The induced law satisfies \(\Lambda(u)=m(u)\ge\Phi(u)\) for every node.

   For the scaled statement, apply the normalized construction to
   \[
      \widehat\Phi(u)=\frac{H_0}{C}\Phi(u).
   \]
   Then \(\widehat\Phi(\varnothing)\le1\), and the constructed law satisfies
   \[
      \Lambda(u)\ge\widehat\Phi(u)
      \ge \frac{H_0}{C}b(u),
   \]
   which is the displayed inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Antichain charge certificate for transition water-filling]
   \label{cor:antichain-transition-waterfill}
   Work in the setting of \cref{cor:transition-waterfilled-allocation}.  For
   every internal trie node \(u\), let
   \[
      a_*(u)=\max_{e=u\to w}a_e
   \]
   over the required outgoing edges from \(u\), with \(a_*(u)=0\) when there is
   no such edge.  Suppose every successful route has at most \(H_0\) required
   edges, and suppose there is a constant \(C\ge1\) such that every antichain
   \(\mathcal C\) of trie nodes satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}a_*(u)\le \frac{C}{H_0}.
   \]
   Then there is a route law \(\Lambda\) on successful leaves for which every
   used required edge \(e=u\to w\) satisfies
   \[
      H_0a_e\le C\Lambda(u).
   \]
   Consequently the transition-aware water-filled defect of this route law is
   at most
   \[
      H_0\log C.
   \]
   If the corresponding allocation is query-decodable and
   \(H_0\log C=O_c(\log\log n)\), then the budget-thinned route value is
   inverse-polylogarithmic.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:antichain-prefix-density-flow} with demands
   \[
      d(u)=\frac{H_0a_*(u)}{C}.
   \]
   The antichain hypothesis is exactly the feasibility condition.  Hence there
   is a leaf law \(\Lambda\) with \(\Lambda(u)\ge H_0a_*(u)/C\) for every
   node.  Since \(a_e\le a_*(u)\) for every outgoing edge \(e=u\to w\), the
   displayed prefix-density bound follows.

   Under this route law,
   \[
      \sum_e\ell_e\le H_0,
   \]
   because every sampled route uses at most \(H_0\) required edges.  The raw
   transition-aware bound in \cref{cor:transition-waterfilled-allocation}
   therefore gives
   \[
      \mathcal D^*_{\rm tr}
      \le
      \sum_e\ell_e\log C
      \le H_0\log C.
   \]
   The final statement is the route-value conclusion of
   \cref{cor:transition-waterfilled-allocation}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Existential antichain Fano certificate]
   \label{cor:existential-antichain-fano}
   Fix one query state and a trie whose successful terminal routes all invoke
   certified procedures returning a valid \(cr\)-neighbor for the current ANN
   query.  Work with the normalized charges \(a_e\) and transition-aware
   branch prices of \cref{cor:transition-waterfilled-allocation}.  Let
   \(a_*(u)\) be the maximum outgoing normalized charge at prefix \(u\).  Suppose
   every successful route has at most \(H_0\) required edges and every antichain
   of required prefixes satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}a_*(u)\le \frac{C}{H_0}.
   \]
   Put \(B=H_0\log C\).  Let \(A_q(z)\) and \(\overline A_q(u)\) be raw
   route reliabilities and raw admissible upper certificates.  Assume every
   successful terminal route has raw margin
   \[
      A_q(z)\ge \Theta+2B,
   \]
   and assume the unpriced prefix and terminal exponential moments satisfy
   \[
      \sum_u g_q(u)e^{\beta\overline A_q(u)}
      \le
      e^{\beta(\Theta+2B)}F\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_z Q_z e^{\beta A_q(z)}
      \le
      e^{\beta(\Theta+2B)}F\log^{O_c(1)}m.
   \]
   If the route law and transition-aware water-filled allocation supplied by
   \cref{cor:antichain-transition-waterfill} are query-decodable, then the
   Fano-biased priority decoder at threshold \(\Theta\) lists a valid terminal
   route and has query work
   \[
      e^{2\beta B}F\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   up to the priority-queue logarithm.  In particular,
   \(B=O_c(\log\log m)\) and \(\beta=O_c(1)\) give only polylogarithmic
   overhead over \(F\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \cref{cor:antichain-transition-waterfill}, there is a route law
   \(\Lambda\) supported on successful leaves whose transition-aware
   water-filled defect is at most \(B\).  Use this as a singleton witness in
   \cref{cor:robust-fano-allocation-compiler}, with the Fano branch price
   \(\log_+(\lambda_ea_e/y_e)\) coming from the decodable transition-aware
   allocation.  The raw margin and moment assumptions are exactly the
   hypotheses of that compiler.  Hence the listed successful routes have
   \(\Lambda\)-mass at least \(1/2\), so at least one valid terminal route is
   invoked, and the displayed work bound follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Local-potential antichain Fano certificate]
   \label{cor:local-potential-antichain-fano}
   In the setting of \cref{cor:existential-antichain-fano}, suppose the
   successful-route trie admits a nonnegative potential \(\Phi\) with
   \[
      a_*(u)\le\Phi(u),
      \qquad
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\Phi(w)\le\Phi(u),
      \qquad
      \Phi(\varnothing)\le \frac{C}{H_0}.
   \]
   Then the antichain hypothesis of
   \cref{cor:existential-antichain-fano} holds.  Consequently, under the same
   raw-margin, prefix-moment, terminal-moment, and decodability hypotheses as
   in that corollary, the Fano-biased priority decoder lists a valid terminal
   route with query work
   \[
      e^{2\beta H_0\log C}F\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   up to the priority-queue logarithm.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:local-antichain-charge-potential} with
   \(b(u)=a_*(u)\).  This gives the required antichain charge bound
   \(\sum_{u\in\mathcal C}a_*(u)\le C/H_0\).

   Equivalently, \cref{lem:superharmonic-potential-route-law} constructs a
   leaf law \(\Lambda\) directly from the same potential, with
   \[
      H_0a_*(u)\le C\Lambda(u)
      \qquad(u\in\mathcal T).
   \]
   Therefore every required edge \(e=u\to w\) satisfies
   \(H_0a_e\le C\Lambda(u)\), and the transition-aware water-filled defect is
   at most \(H_0\log C\) as in
   \cref{cor:antichain-transition-waterfill}.  The Fano work and success
   conclusion is \cref{cor:existential-antichain-fano} with
   \(B=H_0\log C\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Offline superharmonic route verifier]
   \label{cor:offline-superharmonic-route-verifier}
   Work in the setting of \cref{cor:local-potential-antichain-fano}.  Suppose
   preprocessing has produced a finite successful-route trie for one query
   signature, with computable charges \(a_*(u)\), raw route reliabilities,
   admissible prefix upper certificates, terminal prices, and child-generation
   rules for the Fano decoder.  Let \(\Phi^*\) be the least superharmonic
   majorant of the charges \(a_*(u)\), computed by
   \cref{lem:least-superharmonic-majorant}.

   If
   \[
      \Phi^*(\varnothing)\le \frac{C}{H_0},
   \]
   and the top-down route law and transition-aware water-filled allocation
   induced by \(\Phi^*\) are query-decodable, then the conclusion of
   \cref{cor:local-potential-antichain-fano} holds with this canonical
   potential.  In particular, when \(H_0\log C=O_c(\log\log m)\), the accepted
   signature has only polylogarithmic overhead over \(F\).

   The verifier is complete for this certificate family: if any feasible
   superharmonic potential has root at most \(C/H_0\), then the computed
   \(\Phi^*\) also has root at most \(C/H_0\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \cref{lem:least-superharmonic-majorant}, \(\Phi^*\) is feasible for the
   local-potential hypotheses and is pointwise no larger at the root than any
   other feasible potential.  Therefore the displayed root test is exactly the
   antichain-charge certificate for the trie.  Applying
   \cref{cor:local-potential-antichain-fano} with \(\Phi=\Phi^*\) gives the
   Fano work and success bound.  The completeness statement is the pointwise
   minimality of \(\Phi^*\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Finite accepted-route-trie compiler]
   \label{cor:finite-accepted-route-trie-compiler}
   Fix one query signature and one finite successful-route trie.  Let
   \(L_m=\log^{A_c}m\), and assume every terminal route has at most \(H_0\)
   required edges.  For every terminal route \(z\), assume the terminal
   procedure is certified to return a valid \(cr\)-neighbor for the current ANN
   query whenever invoked.  Let \(A_q(z)\) be its raw reliability score, \(Q_z\)
   its terminal price, and let \(\overline A_q(u)\) be a raw admissible prefix
   upper certificate.  Let \(a_*(u)\) be the maximum outgoing normalized charge
   at prefix \(u\), and let \(\Phi^*\) be the least superharmonic majorant of
   \(a_*\).  Suppose the following checks all pass:
   \[
      \Phi^*(\varnothing)\le \frac{C}{H_0},
      \tag{RT1}
   \]
   the top-down route law and transition-aware water-filled allocation induced
   by \(\Phi^*\) are query-decodable, and children satisfying the resulting
   Fano threshold can be generated output-sensitively;
   \[
      A_q(z)\ge\Theta+2H_0\log C
      \qquad\text{for every terminal }z,
      \tag{RT2}
   \]
   \[
      \sum_u g_q(u)e^{\beta\overline A_q(u)}
      \le
      e^{\beta(\Theta+2H_0\log C)}F L_m,
      \tag{RT3}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_z Q_z e^{\beta A_q(z)}
      \le
      e^{\beta(\Theta+2H_0\log C)}F L_m.
      \tag{RT4}
   \]
   Then the Fano-biased priority decoder at threshold \(\Theta\) lists a valid
   terminal route and has query work
   \[
      e^{2\beta H_0\log C}F L_m
   \]
   up to the priority-queue logarithm.

   Consequently, if \(\beta=O_c(1)\), \(H_0\log C=O_c(\log\log m)\), and
   \(L_m=\log^{O_c(1)}m\), an accepted trie has only polylogarithmic overhead
   over its local target \(F\).  If preprocessing searches a finite proposal
   family of route tries and retains one passing \((RT1)\)--\((RT4)\), then
   only the retained trie, its canonical potential, and its Fano reporter are
   charged to the index; failed proposals are build-time artifacts.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The root check \((RT1)\) and decodability condition give the hypotheses of
   \cref{cor:offline-superharmonic-route-verifier}.  With
   \(B=H_0\log C\), the raw margin check \((RT2)\) and the moment checks
   \((RT3)\)--\((RT4)\) are exactly the remaining hypotheses of
   \cref{cor:existential-antichain-fano}.  Therefore the Fano decoder lists a
   successful terminal route and has work
   \[
      e^{2\beta B}F L_m
      =
      e^{2\beta H_0\log C}F L_m
   \]
   up to the priority-queue logarithm.  The final preprocessing statement is
   the finite search principle from \cref{lem:finite-pool-optimizer} and
   \cref{cor:offline-pareto-compression}: the verifier is deterministic on
   each finite candidate, and discarded candidates are not stored.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Compressed route-DAG verifier]
   \label{cor:compressed-route-dag-verifier}
   In the setting of \cref{cor:finite-accepted-route-trie-compiler}, suppose
   that the proposed successful routes are represented by a finite acyclic
   directed graph rather than by an explicitly materialized trie.  The graph has
   a source \(s\), terminal vertices, and a topological order.  A route is a
   source-to-terminal path, every route has at most \(H_0\) required edges, and
   a query prefix is a source-to-current-vertex path together with its
   accumulated additive score and price.  Assume that the graph state contains
   all information needed by the child generator and by the raw upper
   certificate, or else that the displayed quantities below are valid upper
   bounds over all prefixes reaching the state.

   For every nonterminal state \(v\), let \(a_*(v)\) upper-bound the maximum
   outgoing normalized charge of any required edge from any prefix ending at
   \(v\).  Suppose there is a nonnegative state potential \(\Phi\) satisfying
   \[
      a_*(v)\le\Phi(v),
      \qquad
      \sum_{e=(v,w)}\Phi(w)\le\Phi(v),
      \qquad
      \Phi(s)\le \frac{C}{H_0}.
      \tag{RD1}
   \]
   Suppose also that the path unfolding of the graph satisfies the raw margin,
   prefix-moment, and terminal-moment checks \((RT2)\)--\((RT4)\), with prefix
   sums over source-prefix paths and terminal sums over source-terminal paths,
   and that the Fano child rule can enumerate the admissible outgoing edges of
   a live compressed state output-sensitively.  Then the Fano-biased decoder on
   the compressed graph lists a valid terminal route with query work
   \[
      e^{2\beta H_0\log C}F L_m
   \]
   up to the priority-queue logarithm.

   Thus a code, trellis, product-code, or lattice decoder may be certified in
   compressed form: the route trie used by the proof is the unfolding of the
   decoder DAG, while the retained index stores only the graph state space, the
   potential \(\Phi\), and the output-sensitive child reporters.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Unfold the acyclic graph into the tree of all source-prefix paths.  Give each
   unfolded prefix \(u\) ending at graph state \(v\) the potential
   \(\widehat\Phi(u)=\Phi(v)\).  The first two inequalities in \((RD1)\) imply
   \[
      a_*(u)\le\widehat\Phi(u),
      \qquad
      \sum_{u'\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\widehat\Phi(u')
      \le\widehat\Phi(u),
   \]
   because the children of \(u\) are exactly the outgoing graph edges from
   \(v\), with multiplicity.  The root bound in \((RD1)\) is the root bound for
   the unfolded trie.  Hence \(\widehat\Phi\) is a feasible superharmonic
   potential on the unfolding, so the accepted-route-trie compiler applies to
   that unfolded trie using the assumed pathwise checks \((RT2)\)--\((RT4)\).

   The unfolding is only an analysis device.  The priority decoder never needs
   to store it: a live prefix is represented by its compressed graph state and
   accumulated additive quantities, and the assumed child reporter generates
   exactly the outgoing edges whose Fano upper score remains above threshold.
   Therefore the same list and work bound are achieved by the compressed
   decoder.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Moment-dominated superharmonic potential]
   \label{lem:moment-dominated-superharmonic}
   Let \(\mathcal T\) be a finite rooted trie.  Let \(b(u)\ge0\) be node
   charges and let \(w(u)\ge0\) be prefix weights.  Define the suffix weight
   \[
      W(u)=\sum_{v\succeq u}w(v).
   \]
   Suppose there is a constant \(\zeta\ge0\) such that
   \[
      b(u)\le \zeta W(u)
      \qquad(u\in\mathcal T).
      \tag{MD1}
   \]
   In particular, the pointwise bound \(b(u)\le\zeta w(u)\) is sufficient.
   Then \(\Phi(u)=\zeta W(u)\) is a superharmonic majorant of \(b\):
   \[
      b(u)\le\Phi(u),
      \qquad
      \sum_{x\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\Phi(x)\le\Phi(u).
   \]
   In particular, if \(\sum_uw(u)\le M\), then every antichain
   \(\mathcal C\) satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}b(u)\le \zeta M .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The first inequality is \((MD1)\).  Also
   \[
      \sum_{x\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}W(x)
      =
      W(u)-w(u)
      \le W(u),
   \]
   and multiplying by \(\zeta\) gives the superharmonic inequality.  The
   antichain statement is \cref{lem:local-antichain-charge-potential} applied
   to \(\Phi\), with \(\Phi(\varnothing)=\zeta\sum_uw(u)\le\zeta M\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Prefix-moment accepted-route compiler]
   \label{cor:prefix-moment-accepted-route-compiler}
   Work in the setting of
   \cref{cor:finite-accepted-route-trie-compiler}.  Suppose the raw margin
   \((RT2)\), prefix moment \((RT3)\), terminal moment \((RT4)\), and
   Fano child-generation hypotheses hold.  In place of the separate
   superharmonic root check \((RT1)\), assume that the suffix-weight potential
   constructed below has a query-decodable top-down route law and
   transition-aware water-filled allocation, and assume the suffix
   charge-density bound
   \[
      W_q(u):=
      \sum_{v\succeq u}
      \frac{
         g_q(v)e^{\beta\overline A_q(v)}
      }{
         e^{\beta(\Theta+2H_0\log C)}F L_m
      },
      \qquad
      a_*(u)
      \le
      \frac{C}{H_0}\,W_q(u)
      \qquad(u\in\mathcal T).
      \tag{MD2}
   \]
   It is enough, in particular, to prove the stronger same-prefix domination
   obtained by replacing \(W_q(u)\) with the single summand indexed by \(u\).
   Then the local-potential hypotheses hold automatically, and the Fano-biased
   priority decoder lists a valid terminal route with query work
   \[
      e^{2\beta H_0\log C}F L_m
   \]
   up to the priority-queue logarithm.

   Equivalently, a route trie is accepted if its local required charge is
   dominated by the normalized Fano prefix-moment mass below that prefix, its
   raw reliable terminals have margin \(2H_0\log C\), and its terminal prices
   satisfy \((RT4)\).  The antichain route law and transition-aware
   water-filled allocation are then supplied by the same prefix moment that
   bounds the stack size.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Put
   \[
      w(u)=
      \frac{
         g_q(u)e^{\beta\overline A_q(u)}
      }{
         e^{\beta(\Theta+2H_0\log C)}F L_m
      } .
   \]
   By \((RT3)\), \(\sum_uw(u)\le1\).  The density condition \((MD2)\) is
   \(a_*(u)\le(C/H_0)\sum_{v\succeq u}w(v)\).  Applying
   \cref{lem:moment-dominated-superharmonic} with
   \(\zeta=C/H_0\) gives a superharmonic majorant with root at most \(C/H_0\).
   The assumed decodability of this potential supplies the route law,
   transition-aware allocation, and Fano child reporter.  Applying
   \cref{cor:local-potential-antichain-fano} with this potential, and using
   \((RT2)\)--\((RT4)\), gives the stated work bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite convex-linear minimax]\label{lem:finite-convex-minimax}
   Let \(X\subset\R^d\) be compact and convex, and let
   \(\phi_i:X\to\R\), \(i\in I\), be finitely many continuous convex
   functions. Then
   \[
      \min_{x\in X}\max_{i\in I}\phi_i(x)
      =
      \max_{\alpha\in\Delta_I}
      \min_{x\in X}\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_i\phi_i(x),
   \]
   where \(\Delta_I=\{\alpha_i\ge0:\sum_i\alpha_i=1\}\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The left-hand side is at least the right-hand side because, for every
   \(x\) and every \(\alpha\in\Delta_I\),
   \[
      \max_i\phi_i(x)\ge\sum_i\alpha_i\phi_i(x).
   \]

   For the reverse inequality, put
   \(m=\min_x\max_i\phi_i(x)\), and fix \(r<m\).  Let
   \[
      C=\{z\in\R^I:\exists x\in X\text{ with }z_i\ge\phi_i(x)\text{ for all }i\}
   \].
   The set \(C\) is convex because the \(\phi_i\) are convex, and it is closed
   because \(X\) is compact and the \(\phi_i\) are continuous.  The point
   \(r\mathbf 1\) is not in \(C\).  By the finite separating hyperplane
   theorem, there are a nonzero vector \(\alpha\) and a scalar \(\beta\) such
   that
   \[
      \inf_{z\in C}\alpha\cdot z
      \ge \beta
      >
      \alpha\cdot(r\mathbf 1).
   \]
   Since \(C\) is closed upward in every coordinate, this inequality can hold
   only when \(\alpha_i\ge0\) for every \(i\).  Normalize so that
   \(\sum_i\alpha_i=1\).  Then \(\alpha\cdot(r\mathbf 1)=r\), and for every
   \(x\in X\), \((\phi_i(x))_i\in C\), so
   \[
      \sum_i\alpha_i\phi_i(x)\ge r .
   \]
   Hence
   \[
      \max_{\alpha\in\Delta_I}\min_x\sum_i\alpha_i\phi_i(x)\ge r .
   \]
   Letting \(r\uparrow m\) proves the reverse inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Robust water-filled allocation minimax]
   \label{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax}
   Let \(I\) be a finite family of route-law occupation profiles
   \(\ell^i=(\ell^i_e)_{e\in E}\) on a finite opportunity set \(E\), and let
   \(a_e\ge0\) be normalized edge charges.  For an allocation \(y\), define
   \[
      \mathcal D_i(y)=
      \sum_e\ell^i_e\log_+\frac{a_e}{y_e}
   \]
   with the same zero and infinity conventions as in
   \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation}.  For
   \(\alpha\in\Delta_I\), put
   \[
      \ell^\alpha_e=\sum_i\alpha_i\ell^i_e,
   \]
   and let \(\mathcal D^*(\ell^\alpha)\) be the water-filled optimum from
   \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation} applied to the aggregate occupation
   profile \(\ell^\alpha\).  Then
   \[
      \inf_{\sum_e y_e\le1,\ y_e\ge0}\max_{i\in I}\mathcal D_i(y)
      =
      \sup_{\alpha\in\Delta_I}\mathcal D^*(\ell^\alpha).
   \]
   Consequently, if every mixture \(\alpha\) has
   \(\mathcal D^*(\ell^\alpha)\le B\), then for every \(\varepsilon>0\) there
   is one allocation \(y\), independent of \(i\), such that
   \[
      \mathcal D_i(y)\le B+\varepsilon
      \qquad\text{for all }i\in I.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The lower bound is immediate: for every allocation \(y\) and every
   \(\alpha\in\Delta_I\),
   \[
      \max_i\mathcal D_i(y)
      \ge
      \sum_i\alpha_i\mathcal D_i(y)
      =
      \sum_e\ell^\alpha_e\log_+\frac{a_e}{y_e}
      \ge
      \mathcal D^*(\ell^\alpha).
   \]

   For the upper bound, delete edges with \(a_e=0\) or
   \(\max_i\ell^i_e=0\), since they never affect any defect.  If no edge
   remains, the claim is trivial.  Put
   \(L=\max_i\sum_e\ell^i_e\).  If
   \(\sup_\alpha\mathcal D^*(\ell^\alpha)=+\infty\), there is nothing to
   prove, so assume it is finite.  Fix \(\varepsilon>0\), and choose
   \(\delta>0\) with \(|E|\delta<1/2\) and
   \[
      L\log\frac1{1-|E|\delta}\le\varepsilon .
   \]
   Let
   \[
      X_\delta=\{y:y_e\ge\delta\text{ for all }e,\ \sum_e y_e=1\}.
   \]
   On \(X_\delta\), the functions \(\mathcal D_i\) are continuous and convex,
   so \cref{lem:finite-convex-minimax} gives
   \[
      \min_{y\in X_\delta}\max_i\mathcal D_i(y)
      =
      \max_{\alpha\in\Delta_I}
      \min_{y\in X_\delta}
      \sum_e\ell^\alpha_e\log_+\frac{a_e}{y_e}.
   \]

   Fix \(\alpha\).  Let \(y^*\) be a water-filled optimizer for
   \(\ell^\alpha\) with total mass one; when the optimum value is zero and the
   occupied charges use less than unit mass, spend the leftover mass
   arbitrarily.  Let \(T=\{e:y^*_e<\delta\}\).  Raise every coordinate in
   \(T\) to \(\delta\), and multiply the remaining coordinates of \(y^*\) by
   the common factor
   \[
      c=\frac{1-|T|\delta}{1-\sum_{e\in T}y^*_e}.
   \]
   Since \(\sum_{e\in T}y^*_e<|T|\delta\), this factor lies in \((0,1]\), and
   \(c\ge1-|E|\delta\).  The modified allocation lies in \(X_\delta\).  Raising
   the coordinates in \(T\) cannot increase the defect; scaling the other
   coordinates by \(c\) increases it by at most
   \[
      \left(\sum_e\ell^\alpha_e\right)
      \log\frac1c
      \le
      L\log\frac1{1-|E|\delta}
      \le\varepsilon .
   \]
   Thus its aggregate defect is at most
   \(\mathcal D^*(\ell^\alpha)+\varepsilon\).
   Therefore
   \[
      \min_{y\in X_\delta}\max_i\mathcal D_i(y)
      \le
      \sup_{\alpha\in\Delta_I}\mathcal D^*(\ell^\alpha)+\varepsilon .
   \]
   Since \(X_\delta\) is a subset of the full allocation simplex, the full
   robust infimum is no larger.  Letting \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\) and using
   the lower bound proves the equality.  The final statement is the same
   argument with the chosen \(\varepsilon\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Layer-cake form of water-filled defect]
   \label{lem:waterfilled-layercake}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation}, and assume the
   occupied charge sum is larger than one, so the water level \(t\) is finite.
   For edges with \(\ell_e>0\) and \(a_e>0\), put
   \[
      R_e=\frac{a_e}{t\ell_e}.
   \]
   Edges with \(\ell_e=0\) or \(a_e=0\) contribute zero and may be ignored.
   Define the occupation tail
   \[
      L(s)=\sum_{e:\ R_e>e^s}\ell_e,\qquad s\ge0 .
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \mathcal D^*=\int_0^\infty L(s)\,ds .
   \]
   Equivalently, for any \(\Delta>0\),
   \[
      \Delta\sum_{j\ge1}L(j\Delta)
      \le
      \mathcal D^*
      \le
      \Delta\sum_{j\ge0}L(j\Delta).
   \]
   Thus it is enough to bound the occupation mass of edges whose normalized
   charge-to-occupation ratio exceeds the water level by each dyadic factor.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The layer-cake identity
   \[
      x=\int_0^\infty \mathbf 1\{x>s\}\,ds
   \]
   for \(x\ge0\), applied to \(x=\log_+R_e\), gives
   \[
      \mathcal D^*
      =
      \sum_e\ell_e\log_+R_e
      =
      \int_0^\infty
      \sum_{e:\log R_e>s}\ell_e\,ds
      =
      \int_0^\infty L(s)\,ds .
   \]
   The dyadic inequalities are the corresponding lower and upper Riemann sums
   for the nonincreasing function \(L\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Positive-moment certificate for water-filled defect]
   \label{lem:waterfilled-moment-certificate}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:waterfilled-layercake}.  For \(p>0\),
   define
   \[
      M_p^+=\sum_e\ell_e(R_e^p-1)_+,
      \qquad
      \widetilde M_p=\sum_{e:\ R_e>1}\ell_eR_e^p .
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \mathcal D^*
      \le \frac{M_p^+}{p},
      \qquad
      \mathcal D^*
      \le \frac{\widetilde M_p}{pe}.
   \]
   Moreover, if the occupation tail satisfies \(L(s)\le C e^{-ps}\) for all
   \(s\ge0\), then
   \[
      \mathcal D^*\le \frac Cp .
   \]
   Thus an \(O_c(1)\) bound on either positive moment at
   \(p=\Omega_c(1/\log\log n)\) gives the
   \(O_c(\log\log n)\) water-filled defect needed for inverse-polylogarithmic
   route value.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For \(x\ge1\),
   \[
      \log x=\frac1p\log x^p\le \frac{x^p-1}{p}.
   \]
   Applying this to \(x=R_e\) and summing with weights \(\ell_e\) gives the
   first bound.  Also, the function \(x\mapsto(\log x)/x^p\) on \(x\ge1\) is
   maximized at \(x=e^{1/p}\), with maximum value \(1/(pe)\).  Hence
   \[
      \log_+R_e\le \frac{R_e^p}{pe}
   \]
   for every \(e\), and summing gives the second bound.

   The tail statement is immediate from \cref{lem:waterfilled-layercake}:
   \[
      \mathcal D^*=\int_0^\infty L(s)\,ds
      \le
      C\int_0^\infty e^{-ps}\,ds
      =
      \frac Cp .
   \]
   The final sentence substitutes \(p=\Omega_c(1/\log\log n)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Water-level-free moment certificate]
   \label{lem:waterfilled-raw-moment-certificate}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation}, and assume the
   occupied charge sum is larger than one.  Let \(t\) be the water level from
   \cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation}.  If \(\tau>0\) satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{\ell_e>0}\min\{a_e,\tau\ell_e\}\le1,
   \]
   then \(\tau\le t\), and
   \[
      \mathcal D^*
      \le
      \sum_{\substack{e:\ \ell_e>0\\ a_e>0}}
      \ell_e
      \log_+\frac{a_e}{\tau\ell_e},
   \]
   Consequently, for every \(p>0\),
   \[
      \mathcal D^*
      \le
      \frac1p
      \sum_{\substack{e:\ \ell_e>0\\ a_e>0}}
      \ell_e
      \left(
      \left(\frac{a_e}{\tau\ell_e}\right)^p-1
      \right)_+ .
   \]
   In particular, if
   \[
      H=\sum_e\ell_e<\infty,
   \]
   then the choice \(\tau=1/H\) is feasible and gives
   \[
      \mathcal D^*
      \le
      \sum_{\substack{e:\ \ell_e>0\\ a_e>0}}
      \ell_e
      \log_+\frac{H a_e}{\ell_e},
   \]
   as well as
   \[
      \mathcal D^*
      \le
      \frac1p
      \sum_{\substack{e:\ \ell_e>0\\ a_e>0}}
      \ell_e
      \left(
      \left(\frac{H a_e}{\ell_e}\right)^p-1
      \right)_+ .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Put
   \[
      S(u)=\sum_{\ell_e>0}\min\{a_e,u\ell_e\}.
   \]
   The function \(S\) is nondecreasing and \(t\) is chosen so that
   \(S(t)=1\).  Hence \(S(\tau)\le1\) implies \(\tau\le t\).  Therefore, for
   every occupied edge,
   \[
      \frac{a_e}{t\ell_e}\le \frac{a_e}{\tau\ell_e}.
   \]
   Summing the resulting logarithmic inequality gives the first displayed
   bound. Combining it with
   \[
      \log_+x\le\frac{(x^p-1)_+}{p}
   \]
   proves the moment bound.

   Finally,
   \[
      S(1/H)
      =
      \sum_{\ell_e>0}\min\{a_e,\ell_e/H\}
      \le
      \sum_{\ell_e>0}\frac{\ell_e}{H}
      =
      1,
   \]
   so \(\tau=1/H\) is feasible, and substituting it proves the last two
   bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Robust water-filled tail certificate]
   \label{cor:robust-waterfilled-tail}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax}.  Suppose that
   for every mixture \(\alpha\in\Delta_I\), either
   \[
      \sum_{\ell^\alpha_e>0}a_e\le1,
   \]
   or, for the water level \(t_\alpha\) solving
   \[
      \sum_{\ell^\alpha_e>0}\min\{a_e,t_\alpha\ell^\alpha_e\}=1,
   \]
   the tails
   \[
      L_\alpha(s)
      =
      \sum_{e:\ a_e>e^s t_\alpha\ell^\alpha_e}
      \ell^\alpha_e
   \]
   satisfy
   \[
      \int_0^\infty L_\alpha(s)\,ds\le B .
   \]
   Then for every \(\varepsilon>0\) there is one allocation \(y\), independent
   of the witness profile \(i\), with
   \[
      \mathcal D_i(y)\le B+\varepsilon
      \qquad\text{for all }i\in I.
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \cref{lem:waterfilled-layercake}, each mixture has water-filled defect at
   most \(B\), with defect zero in the first case.  Apply
   \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Robust water-filled moment certificate]
   \label{cor:robust-waterfilled-moment}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax}.  Fix
   \(p>0\) and \(M\ge0\).  Suppose that for every mixture
   \(\alpha\in\Delta_I\), either
   \[
      \sum_{\ell^\alpha_e>0}a_e\le1,
   \]
   or, for the water level \(t_\alpha\) solving
   \[
      \sum_{\ell^\alpha_e>0}\min\{a_e,t_\alpha\ell^\alpha_e\}=1,
   \]
   the positive moment
   \[
      M_{p,\alpha}^+
      =
      \sum_{\substack{e:\ \ell^\alpha_e>0\\ a_e>0}}
      \ell^\alpha_e
      \left(
      \left(\frac{a_e}{t_\alpha\ell^\alpha_e}\right)^p-1
      \right)_+
   \]
   is at most \(M\).  Then for every \(\varepsilon>0\) there
   is one allocation \(y\), independent of the witness profile \(i\), with
   \[
      \mathcal D_i(y)\le \frac Mp+\varepsilon
      \qquad\text{for all }i\in I.
   \]
   In particular, if \(M/p=O_c(\log\log n)\), then the robust allocation loses
   only an inverse-polylogarithmic factor in route value.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \cref{lem:waterfilled-moment-certificate}, every nontrivial mixture has
   water-filled defect at most \(M/p\), and the mixtures in the first case have
   defect zero.  Apply \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax}.  The final
   sentence is the same substitution into \cref{cor:allocation-defect-toll} for
   each witness route law.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Robust raw-log certificate]
   \label{cor:robust-waterfilled-raw-log}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax}.  Fix
   \(H_0\ge1\) and \(B\ge0\).  Suppose that for every mixture
   \(\alpha\in\Delta_I\), either
   \[
      \sum_{\ell^\alpha_e>0}a_e\le1,
   \]
   or both
   \[
      \sum_e\ell^\alpha_e\le H_0
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{e:\ \ell^\alpha_e>0\\ a_e>0}}
      \ell^\alpha_e
      \log_+\frac{H_0a_e}{\ell^\alpha_e}
      \le B .
   \]
   Then for every \(\varepsilon>0\) there is one allocation \(y\), independent
   of the witness profile \(i\), with
   \[
      \mathcal D_i(y)\le B+\varepsilon
      \qquad\text{for all }i\in I.
   \]
   In particular, if \(H_0=O_c(\log\log n)\) and
   \(H_0a_e\le C\ell^\alpha_e\) for every occupied edge of every nontrivial
   mixture, with \(C=O_c(1)\), then the robust defect is
   \(O_c(\log\log n)\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   For a nontrivial mixture \(\alpha\), put \(\tau=1/H_0\). Since
   \(\sum_e\ell^\alpha_e\le H_0\),
   \[
      \sum_{\ell^\alpha_e>0}\min\{a_e,\tau\ell^\alpha_e\}
      \le
      \tau\sum_e\ell^\alpha_e
      \le1.
   \]
   The raw logarithmic bound in
   \cref{lem:waterfilled-raw-moment-certificate} therefore gives water-filled
   defect at most \(B\).  The mixtures in the first case have defect zero, and
   \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax} gives the common allocation.  Under
   the bounded-density hypothesis the displayed logarithmic sum is at most
   \(H_0\log C\), which is \(O_c(\log\log n)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Robust raw-moment certificate]
   \label{cor:robust-waterfilled-raw-moment}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax}.  Fix
   \(p>0\), \(H_0\ge1\), and \(M\ge0\).  Suppose that for every mixture
   \(\alpha\in\Delta_I\), either
   \[
      \sum_{\ell^\alpha_e>0}a_e\le1,
   \]
   or both
   \[
      \sum_e\ell^\alpha_e\le H_0
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{e:\ \ell^\alpha_e>0\\ a_e>0}}
      \ell^\alpha_e
      \left(
      \left(\frac{H_0a_e}{\ell^\alpha_e}\right)^p-1
      \right)_+
      \le M .
   \]
   Then for every \(\varepsilon>0\) there is one allocation \(y\), independent
   of the witness profile \(i\), with
   \[
      \mathcal D_i(y)\le \frac Mp+\varepsilon
      \qquad\text{for all }i\in I.
   \]
   Thus, if route laws have polylogarithmic expected opportunity count and the
   displayed raw moment has \(M/p=O_c(\log\log n)\), the robust allocation is
   sufficient for inverse-polylogarithmic route value without explicitly
   estimating any water level.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   For a nontrivial mixture \(\alpha\), put
   \(\tau=1/H_0\).  Since \(\sum_e\ell^\alpha_e\le H_0\),
   \[
      \sum_{\ell^\alpha_e>0}\min\{a_e,\tau\ell^\alpha_e\}
      \le
      \tau\sum_e\ell^\alpha_e
      \le1.
   \]
   Hence \cref{lem:waterfilled-raw-moment-certificate} bounds the water-filled
   defect of this mixture by \(M/p\).  The mixtures in the first case have
   defect zero.  Apply \cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Robust transition-envelope raw-log certificate]
   \label{cor:robust-transition-envelope-raw-log}
   Work with a finite family \(I\) of witness route laws on the same required
   opportunity set.  Let \(\ell^i_e\) be the occupation profile of witness
   \(i\), let \(\lambda^i_e\) be its transition probability on edge \(e\), and
   let \(a_e\ge0\) be the normalized edge charges.  Suppose there is a
   decodable envelope \(\bar\lambda_e\in[0,1]\) such that
   \[
      \lambda^i_e\le\bar\lambda_e
      \qquad\text{whenever }\ell^i_e>0,
   \]
   and put \(\bar a_e=\bar\lambda_ea_e\).  Fix \(H_0\ge1\) and \(B\ge0\).
   Suppose that for every mixture \(\alpha\in\Delta_I\), either
   \[
      \sum_{\ell^\alpha_e>0}\bar a_e\le1,
   \]
   or both
   \[
      \sum_e\ell^\alpha_e\le H_0
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{e:\ \ell^\alpha_e>0\\ \bar a_e>0}}
      \ell^\alpha_e
      \log_+\frac{H_0\bar a_e}{\ell^\alpha_e}
      \le B .
   \]
   Then for every \(\varepsilon>0\) there is one allocation \(y\), independent
   of the witness profile, such that every witness has transition-aware defect
   \[
      \sum_e
      \ell^i_e
      \log_+\frac{\lambda^i_ea_e}{y_e}
      \le B+\varepsilon .
   \]
   Consequently, if the resulting allocation is query-decodable, the same
   allocation gives inverse-polylogarithmic route value for all witnesses
   whenever \(B=O_c(\log\log n)\).

   In the important special case where all witnesses share the same local
   transition rule \(\lambda_e\), one may take
   \(\bar\lambda_e=\lambda_e\).  For an edge \(e=u\to w\), the mixed
   occupation is then
   \[
      \ell^\alpha_e=\Lambda^\alpha(u)\lambda_e,
   \]
   and the raw logarithmic density becomes
   \[
      \frac{H_0\bar a_e}{\ell^\alpha_e}
      =
      \frac{H_0a_e}{\Lambda^\alpha(u)}
      \qquad(\lambda_e>0).
   \]
   Thus the robust target is prefix charge density, not child-edge occupation
   density.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Since \(\lambda^i_e\le\bar\lambda_e\),
   \[
      \log_+\frac{\lambda^i_ea_e}{y_e}
      \le
      \log_+\frac{\bar a_e}{y_e}.
   \]
   Apply \cref{cor:robust-waterfilled-raw-log} with the common charges
   \(\bar a_e\) to obtain one allocation \(y\) for which
   \[
      \sum_e\ell^i_e\log_+\frac{\bar a_e}{y_e}
      \le B+\varepsilon
      \qquad\text{for every }i.
   \]
   The previous display gives the transition-aware defect bound.  The route
   value statement follows from \cref{cor:transition-waterfilled-allocation}
   applied to each witness route law.  The shared-transition simplification is
   just \(\ell^\alpha_e=\Lambda^\alpha(u)\lambda_e\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Prefix-energy route-law certificate]\label{cor:route-law-prefix-energy}
   Let $\Lambda$ be a probability distribution on leaf routes and let $A(u)$
   be the activation prefix mass. Define the prefix $\chi^2$ energy
   \[
      \mathcal E_2(\Lambda\|A)
      =
      \sum_{u}
      \frac{\Lambda(u)^2}{A(u)},
   \]
   with the convention that the summand is $+\infty$ if $\Lambda(u)>0$ and
   $A(u)=0$. If
   \[
      \mathcal E_2(\Lambda\|A)\le M,
   \]
   then
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge \frac{1}{4M}.
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   For a random leaf $V\sim\Lambda$, put
   \[
      X(V)=\sum_{u\preceq V}\frac{\Lambda(u)}{A(u)}.
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \E_\Lambda X(V)
      =
      \sum_u
      \frac{\Lambda(u)}{A(u)}
      \Pr_\Lambda[V\succeq u]
      =
      \sum_u\frac{\Lambda(u)^2}{A(u)}
      \le M.
   \]
   By Markov's inequality, $\Lambda\{X\le2M\}\ge1/2$. On this event every
   prefix of $V$ has $\Lambda(u)/A(u)\le2M$, so
   $T_\Lambda(V)\le\log(2M)$. Applying
   \cref{cor:typical-route-law-toll} with $h=1/2$ and
   $B=\log(2M)$ proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Local prefix-energy certificate]\label{lem:local-prefix-energy}
   Work in the setting of \cref{cor:route-law-prefix-energy}. For an edge
   $u\to w$, write
   \[
      \lambda_{u,w}=\Pr_\Lambda[w\mid u],
      \qquad
      \gamma_{u,w}=\frac{A(w)}{A(u)}
   \]
   whenever $\Lambda(u)>0$ and $A(u)>0$, with the convention that
   $\lambda_{u,w}^2/\gamma_{u,w}=+\infty$ if
   $\lambda_{u,w}>0$ and $\gamma_{u,w}=0$, while terms with
   $\lambda_{u,w}=0$ contribute zero. Suppose there is a nonnegative function
   $M$ on trie nodes such that $M(v)\ge1$ at every leaf and, at every internal
   node with $\Lambda(u)>0$,
   \[
      1+
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2}{\gamma_{u,w}}\,M(w)
      \le M(u).
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \mathcal E_2(\Lambda\|A)\le M(\varnothing).
   \]
   Consequently, if $M(\varnothing)=\log^{O_c(1)}n$, then the saturated value
   satisfies
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge\log^{-O_c(1)}n .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a node $u$ with $\Lambda(u)>0$, define the normalized subtree energy
   \[
      \mathcal E(u)
      =
      \sum_{z\succeq u}
      \frac{(\Lambda(z)/\Lambda(u))^2}{A(z)/A(u)} .
   \]
   Finite $M(u)$ forces $A(u)>0$ on every positive-$\Lambda$ node, so the
   expression is well-defined under the stated convention. At a leaf,
   $\mathcal E(u)=1\le M(u)$. At an internal node,
   \[
      \mathcal E(u)
      =
      1+
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2}{\gamma_{u,w}}\,\mathcal E(w).
   \]
   Backward induction using the displayed local inequality gives
   $\mathcal E(u)\le M(u)$ for all positive-$\Lambda$ nodes. At the root,
   $\Lambda(\varnothing)=A(\varnothing)=1$, so
   $\mathcal E(\varnothing)=\mathcal E_2(\Lambda\|A)$. The final statement is
   \cref{cor:route-law-prefix-energy}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Prefix-energy spine toll]\label{lem:prefix-energy-spine-toll}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:local-prefix-energy}, after deleting
   zero-$\Lambda$ subtrees. If some positive-$\Lambda$ edge has
   $\gamma_{u,w}=0$, the energy is infinite and the upper-bound hypotheses
   below are false, so assume all displayed quantities are finite. For every
   internal node $u$, put
   \[
      \chi_\Lambda(u)=
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2}{\gamma_{u,w}},
   \]
   and, when $\chi_\Lambda(u)>0$, define the energy-spine transition law
   \[
      P_u(w)=
      \frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2/\gamma_{u,w}}{\chi_\Lambda(u)} .
   \]
   Let $(U_0,U_1,\ldots,U_T)$ be the random root-to-leaf spine generated by
   these transitions. Then
   \[
      \mathcal E_2(\Lambda\|A)
      =
      \E\left[
         \sum_{j=0}^{T}
         \prod_{i<j}\chi_\Lambda(U_i)
      \right].
   \]
   Consequently, if every root-to-leaf route has length at most $H$ and every
   energy-spine realization satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{i<T}\log_+\chi_\Lambda(U_i)\le B,
   \]
   then
   \[
      \mathcal E_2(\Lambda\|A)\le(H+1)e^B
   \]
   and hence
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge \frac{1}{4(H+1)e^B}.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a nonroot node $z$, multiply the identities
   \[
      \frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2}{\gamma_{u,w}}
      =
      \chi_\Lambda(u)P_u(w)
   \]
   along the root-to-$z$ path. This gives
   \[
      \frac{\Lambda(z)^2}{A(z)}
      =
      \left(\prod_{u\prec z}\chi_\Lambda(u)\right)
      \Prb[U_{|z|}=z],
   \]
   with the empty product at the root. Summing over all depths gives the
   displayed identity. If the positive toll is at most $B$, every partial
   product is at most $e^B$, and there are at most $H+1$ summands. The
   saturated-value lower bound follows from
   \cref{cor:route-law-prefix-energy}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Square-benign route laws]\label{cor:square-benign-route-laws}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:local-prefix-energy}, suppose every
   root-to-leaf route has length at most $H$ and every internal
   positive-$\Lambda$ node satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2}{\gamma_{u,w}}\le1.
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \mathcal E_2(\Lambda\|A)\le H+1
   \]
   and
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge\frac{1}{4(H+1)}.
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:prefix-energy-spine-toll} with $B=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Optimal prefix-energy route law]\label{lem:optimal-prefix-energy-law}
   Fix a finite route trie with activation edge probabilities
   $\gamma_{u,w}\in[0,1]$. For a node $u$, let
   $\mathfrak E(u)$ be the infimum of the normalized prefix energy
   \[
      \sum_{z\succeq u}
      \frac{\Lambda_u(z)^2}{A(z)/A(u)}
   \]
   over all probability laws $\Lambda_u$ on leaves below $u$, where
   $A(z)/A(u)$ is the activation mass from $u$ to $z$. Then
   \[
      \mathfrak E(v)=1\quad\text{at leaves},
   \]
   and, at an internal node,
   \[
      \mathfrak E(u)
      =
      1+
      \left(
         \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
         \frac{\gamma_{u,w}}{\mathfrak E(w)}
      \right)^{-1},
   \]
   with the convention that the inverse of zero is $+\infty$ and
   $\gamma_{u,w}/\infty=0$. When the displayed denominator is positive, an
   optimal law is obtained by using the transition probabilities
   \[
      \lambda_{u,w}
      =
      \frac{\gamma_{u,w}/\mathfrak E(w)}
           {\sum_y\gamma_{u,y}/\mathfrak E(y)}
   \]
   and then recursing independently inside the selected child.

   Equivalently, the harmonic route value
   $W(u)=1/\mathfrak E(u)$ satisfies
   \[
      W(v)=1\quad\text{at leaves},
      \qquad
      W(u)=\frac{S(u)}{1+S(u)},
      \qquad
      S(u)=\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}W(w).
   \]
   If $W(\varnothing)\ge\log^{-O_c(1)}n$, then the saturated value satisfies
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge\log^{-O_c(1)}n .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   We prove the recursion by backward induction. At a leaf, the only route law
   has energy one. At an internal node $u$, any law first chooses child $w$
   with probability $\lambda_w$ and then uses a conditional law below $w$.
   If a positive-$\lambda_w$ child has $\gamma_{u,w}=0$, the energy is
   infinite, so such children may be ignored when minimizing finite energy.
   For finite choices, the normalized energy decomposes as
   \[
      1+
      \sum_w
      \frac{\lambda_w^2}{\gamma_{u,w}}\,
      \mathcal E_w,
   \]
   where $\mathcal E_w$ is the normalized energy of the conditional law below
   $w$. By the induction hypothesis, the best choice has
   $\mathcal E_w=\mathfrak E(w)$, so it remains to minimize
   \[
      \sum_w \lambda_w^2\frac{\mathfrak E(w)}{\gamma_{u,w}}
      \qquad\text{subject to}\qquad
      \sum_w\lambda_w=1,\quad \lambda_w\ge0 .
   \]
   Cauchy's inequality gives
   \[
      1
      =
      \left(\sum_w\lambda_w\right)^2
      \le
      \left(\sum_w\lambda_w^2
      \frac{\mathfrak E(w)}{\gamma_{u,w}}\right)
      \left(\sum_w\frac{\gamma_{u,w}}{\mathfrak E(w)}\right).
   \]
   Equality holds for
   $\lambda_w\propto\gamma_{u,w}/\mathfrak E(w)$, which proves the displayed
   recursion and the optimizer. Taking reciprocals gives the harmonic
   $W$-recursion. The final implication follows from
   \cref{cor:route-law-prefix-energy}, applied to the optimal root law.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Harmonic resistance normal form]
   \label{lem:harmonic-resistance-normal-form}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:optimal-prefix-energy-law}, put
   \[
      R(u)=\frac1{W(u)}
   \]
   with the convention \(1/0=\infty\), and define the child conductance
   \[
      C(u)=\sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\frac{\gamma_{u,w}}{R(w)} .
   \]
   Then leaves have \(R(u)=1\), while internal nodes satisfy
   \[
      R(u)=1+\frac1{C(u)}
      \tag{RN1}
   \]
   with \(1/0=\infty\).  Thus child conductances add in parallel, and a
   one-child edge of positive success probability \(\gamma\) has the series rule
   \[
      R(u)=1+\frac{R(w)}{\gamma}.
      \tag{RN2}
   \]
   If \(\gamma=0\), the one-child resistance is infinite unless the child is
   unused.
   More generally, if children \(w_i\) have downstream resistances \(R_i\),
   then the entire child layer is equivalent, for the parent recursion, to one
   conductance \(\sum_i\gamma_i/R_i\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The harmonic recursion in \cref{lem:optimal-prefix-energy-law} is
   \(W(u)=S(u)/(1+S(u))\), where
   \(S(u)=\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}W(w)\).  Since \(W(w)=1/R(w)\), the quantity
   \(S(u)\) is exactly \(C(u)\).  Taking reciprocals gives \((RN1)\), and
   \((RN2)\) is the special case with one child.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Harmonic and saturated values differ only by depth]\label{lem:harmonic-saturated-comparison}
   Fix a finite route trie with edge probabilities $\gamma_{u,w}\in[0,1]$.
   Let $U$ be the saturated value from
   \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion}, let $W$ be the harmonic route value
   from \cref{lem:optimal-prefix-energy-law}, and let $h(u)$ be the maximum
   number of edges from $u$ to a descendant leaf. Then, for every node $u$,
   \[
      \frac{U(u)}{h(u)+1}\le W(u)\le U(u).
   \]
   Consequently, if every route has length at most $H$ and
   $U(\varnothing)\ge p$, then the optimal prefix-energy route law has
   \[
      \mathcal E_2(\Lambda\|A)
      \le
      \frac{H+1}{p}.
   \]
   In particular, on polylogarithmic-depth route tries, the harmonic-value
   target and the saturated min-cut target are equivalent up to a
   polylogarithmic factor.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The upper bound is by induction. At leaves equality holds. At an internal
   node, the induction hypothesis gives
   \[
      \sum_w\gamma_{u,w}W(w)
      \le
      \sum_w\gamma_{u,w}U(w).
   \]
   Since $x/(1+x)\le\min\{1,x\}$ for $x\ge0$,
   \[
      W(u)
      =
      \frac{\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}W(w)}
           {1+\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}W(w)}
      \le
      \min\left\{1,\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}U(w)\right\}
      =
      U(u).
   \]

   For the lower bound, the leaf case is again equality. At an internal node,
   put
   \[
      x=\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}U(w),
      \qquad
      h=h(u).
   \]
   Each child has $h(w)+1\le h$, so the induction hypothesis gives
   \[
      \sum_w\gamma_{u,w}W(w)
      \ge
      \frac{x}{h}.
   \]
   Therefore
   \[
      W(u)\ge \frac{x/h}{1+x/h}=\frac{x}{h+x}.
   \]
   If $x\le1$, then $U(u)=x$ and
   \[
      \frac{x}{h+x}\ge\frac{x}{h+1}=\frac{U(u)}{h(u)+1}.
   \]
   If $x\ge1$, then $U(u)=1$ and
   \[
      \frac{x}{h+x}\ge\frac1{h+1}=\frac{U(u)}{h(u)+1}.
   \]

   The energy statement follows because
   $\mathcal E_2(\Lambda\|A)=1/W(\varnothing)$ for the optimal law from
   \cref{lem:optimal-prefix-energy-law}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Unit-success skeleton contraction]
   \label{lem:unit-success-skeleton-contraction}
   Let \(W\) be the harmonic route value from
   \cref{lem:optimal-prefix-energy-law} on a finite route trie.  Fix a node
   \(r\) and a route cut \(\mathcal F\) in the subtree below \(r\).  Assume
   every edge on every path from \(r\) to \(\mathcal F\), not including edges
   below \(\mathcal F\), has success probability \(1\).  For a node
   \(u\preceq z\) with \(z\in\mathcal F\), let
   \[
      T_{\mathcal F}(u)
      =
      \sum_{\substack{z\in\mathcal F\\ z\succeq u}} W(z)
   \]
   and let \(h_{\mathcal F}(u)\) be the maximum number of edges from \(u\) to
   a descendant in \(\mathcal F\).  Then
   \[
      W(u)
      \ge
      \frac{T_{\mathcal F}(u)}
           {1+h_{\mathcal F}(u)T_{\mathcal F}(u)}
      \qquad
      (u\preceq\mathcal F).
      \tag{USC1}
   \]
   Equivalently, when \(T_{\mathcal F}(u)>0\),
   \[
      \frac1{W(u)}
      \le
      \frac1{T_{\mathcal F}(u)}+h_{\mathcal F}(u).
      \tag{USC2}
   \]
   In particular, if \(h_{\mathcal F}(r)\le H\), then
   \[
      W(r)\ge \frac{\min\{T_{\mathcal F}(r),1\}}{H+1}.
      \tag{USC3}
   \]
   Thus a maximal block of ordinary score-only or otherwise deterministic
   unit-success branching contributes only its depth as harmonic resistance
   before the first genuinely thinned skeleton frontier.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   We prove \((USC1)\) by backward induction on \(h_{\mathcal F}(u)\).  If
   \(u\in\mathcal F\), then \(h_{\mathcal F}(u)=0\) and
   \(T_{\mathcal F}(u)=W(u)\).

   Let \(u\notin\mathcal F\), put \(h=h_{\mathcal F}(u)\), and write
   \(T_w=T_{\mathcal F}(w)\) for the children \(w\) of \(u\).  By the unit
   success hypothesis, the harmonic recursion at \(u\) is
   \[
      W(u)=\frac{S}{1+S},
      \qquad
      S=\sum_w W(w).
   \]
   Every child with \(T_w>0\) has \(h_{\mathcal F}(w)\le h-1\), so the
   induction hypothesis gives
   \[
      W(w)\ge \frac{T_w}{1+(h-1)T_w}.
   \]
   The function \(f_a(t)=t/(1+at)\), \(a\ge0\), is subadditive in the form
   \[
      \sum_i f_a(t_i)\ge f_a\!\left(\sum_i t_i\right)
      \qquad(t_i\ge0),
   \]
   because the two-term difference is
   \[
      f_a(x)+f_a(y)-f_a(x+y)
      =
      \frac{axy(2+a(x+y))}
           {(1+ax)(1+ay)(1+a(x+y))}
      \ge0,
   \]
   and the general case follows by induction.  Hence,
   with \(T=T_{\mathcal F}(u)=\sum_wT_w\),
   \[
      S\ge \frac{T}{1+(h-1)T}.
   \]
   Since \(x\mapsto x/(1+x)\) is increasing,
   \[
      W(u)\ge
      \frac{T/(1+(h-1)T)}
           {1+T/(1+(h-1)T)}
      =
      \frac{T}{1+hT}.
   \]
   This proves \((USC1)\).  The reciprocal form \((USC2)\) is the same
   inequality rearranged.  Finally, if \(T_{\mathcal F}(r)\le1\), then
   \((USC1)\) gives \(W(r)\ge T_{\mathcal F}(r)/(H+1)\); if
   \(T_{\mathcal F}(r)\ge1\), it gives \(W(r)\ge1/(H+1)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Harmonic skeleton certificate]
   \label{cor:harmonic-skeleton-certificate}
   In a budget-thinned route trie, contract every maximal region whose edges
   all have success probability \(1\) until the first frontier containing a
   thinned geometric opportunity, a fractional-delivery opportunity, or a
   terminal route.  At the entrance \(r\) of any contracted region with exit
   frontier \(\mathcal F\), it is enough that
   \[
      \sum_{z\in\mathcal F} W(z)\ge \log^{-A_c}n ;
   \]
   if the region depth is \(\log^{O_c(1)}n\), then
   \(W(r)\ge\log^{-O_c(1)}n\).  Therefore ordinary score branching,
   deterministic residual routing, and budget-stable deterministic recentering
   do not create a polynomial near-value loss.  A skeleton proof only needs to
   lower-bound the downstream harmonic mass presented to these unit-success
   regions; the nontrivial value loss is isolated to genuinely thinned
   opportunities.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:unit-success-skeleton-contraction} to the contracted region.
   The displayed lower bound is exactly \((USC3)\) with
   \(T_{\mathcal F}(r)\ge\log^{-A_c}n\) and polylogarithmic
   \(h_{\mathcal F}(r)\).  The final statement is just the definition of the
   contracted skeleton: all nontrivial edge probabilities are attached to the
   thinned or fractional-delivery frontier edges.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Ported unit-region contraction]
   \label{lem:ported-unit-region-contraction}
   Let \(W\) be the harmonic route value on a finite route trie.  Let
   \(\mathcal R\) be a prefix-closed region with entrance \(r\).  Every edge
   whose tail and head both lie in \(\mathcal R\) has success probability
   \(1\).  Let \(\partial^+\mathcal R\) be the set of exit edges
   \(e=(a,b)\) with \(a\in\mathcal R\) and \(b\notin\mathcal R\), and let
   \(\gamma_e\) be the success probability of \(e\).  For \(u\in\mathcal R\),
   write \(\partial^+_u\mathcal R\) for the exit edges whose tail is
   reachable from \(u\) by internal edges of \(\mathcal R\), and put
   \[
      T_{\mathcal R}(u)
      =
      \sum_{e=(a,b)\in\partial^+_u\mathcal R}
      \gamma_e W(b).
   \]
   Let \(h_{\mathcal R}(u)\) be the maximum number of internal edges from
   \(u\) to the tail of an exit in \(\partial^+_u\mathcal R\), with the
   convention that the claim is trivial when \(\partial^+_u\mathcal R\) is
   empty.  Then
   \[
      W(u)
      \ge
      \frac{T_{\mathcal R}(u)}
           {1+(h_{\mathcal R}(u)+1)T_{\mathcal R}(u)}
      \qquad(u\in\mathcal R).
      \tag{PU1}
   \]
   Equivalently, when \(T_{\mathcal R}(u)>0\),
   \[
      \frac1{W(u)}
      \le
      \frac1{T_{\mathcal R}(u)}+h_{\mathcal R}(u)+1.
      \tag{PU2}
   \]
   Hence a whole deterministic score block followed by many thinned exits
   loses only its internal depth plus one unit of resistance, and the exits
   combine by the single conductance
   \(\sum_{e=(a,b)}\gamma_eW(b)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   We prove \((PU1)\) by backward induction on \(h_{\mathcal R}(u)\).  If
   \(\partial^+_u\mathcal R\) is empty, then \(T_{\mathcal R}(u)=0\) and there
   is nothing to prove.  Suppose first that \(h_{\mathcal R}(u)=0\).  Then
   every relevant exit edge leaves directly from \(u\).  In the harmonic
   recursion
   \[
      W(u)=\frac{S}{1+S},
   \]
   the sum \(S\) contains the exit contribution \(T_{\mathcal R}(u)\), and all
   other child contributions are nonnegative.  Thus
   \(W(u)\ge T_{\mathcal R}(u)/(1+T_{\mathcal R}(u))\), which is \((PU1)\).

   Now let \(h=h_{\mathcal R}(u)>0\).  Let \(P\) be the total conductance of
   exits leaving directly from \(u\), and let \(T_w=T_{\mathcal R}(w)\) for
   the internal children \(w\in\mathcal R\) of \(u\).  Then
   \(T_{\mathcal R}(u)=P+\sum_wT_w\).  For every child with \(T_w>0\), the
   induction hypothesis and \(h_{\mathcal R}(w)+1\le h\) give
   \[
      W(w)\ge\frac{T_w}{1+hT_w}.
   \]
   Therefore
   \[
      S\ge
      P+\sum_w\frac{T_w}{1+hT_w}.
   \]
   The function \(f_h(t)=t/(1+ht)\) is subadditive in the form
   \[
      f_h(x)+f_h(y)\ge f_h(x+y)
      \qquad(x,y\ge0),
   \]
   and \(P\ge f_h(P)\).  Iterating the two inequalities gives
   \[
      S\ge \frac{T_{\mathcal R}(u)}
                 {1+hT_{\mathcal R}(u)} .
   \]
   Monotonicity of \(x/(1+x)\) then yields
   \[
      W(u)\ge
      \frac{T_{\mathcal R}(u)/(1+hT_{\mathcal R}(u))}
           {1+T_{\mathcal R}(u)/(1+hT_{\mathcal R}(u))}
      =
      \frac{T_{\mathcal R}(u)}
           {1+(h+1)T_{\mathcal R}(u)} .
   \]
   This proves \((PU1)\), and \((PU2)\) is the reciprocal form.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Ported skeleton certificate]
   \label{cor:ported-skeleton-certificate}
   In a budget-thinned route trie, contract a maximal unit-success region
   \(\mathcal R\) but leave the first thinned or fractional-delivery edges as
   exit ports.  If its depth is \(\log^{O_c(1)}n\) and
   \[
      \sum_{e=(a,b)\in\partial^+\mathcal R}\gamma_eW(b)
      \ge\log^{-A_c}n ,
   \]
   then the harmonic value at the entrance of the region is
   \(\log^{-O_c(1)}n\).  Thus the near-value proof may work entirely on the
   skeleton of nonunit ports: deterministic score branching merely routes
   conductance to the ports, while the ports themselves are the only places
   where activation probability can create nontrivial resistance.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:ported-unit-region-contraction} at the entrance \(r\).  The
   displayed lower bound is \(T_{\mathcal R}(r)\ge\log^{-A_c}n\), and the
   region depth is polylogarithmic.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Port-skeleton saturated comparison]
   \label{lem:port-skeleton-saturated-comparison}
   Let a finite route trie be decomposed into a rooted port skeleton tree
   \(\mathcal S\) with root state \(s_0\).  Each skeleton state \(s\) has an
   entrance trie node
   \(r_s\).  If \(s\) is terminal, then \(r_s\) is a trie leaf.  If \(s\) is
   nonterminal, it has a prefix-closed unit-success region
   \(\mathcal R_s\) rooted at \(r_s\).  Every exit edge
   \(e=(a_e,r_t)\in\partial^+\mathcal R_s\) points to the entrance of a child
   skeleton state \(t\), has success probability \(\gamma_e\), and the child
   subregions partition the route subtrees below the exits.  Put
   \[
      d_s=h_{\mathcal R_s}(r_s)+1
   \]
   for nonterminal \(s\), where \(h_{\mathcal R_s}\) is the internal
   exit-tail depth from \cref{lem:ported-unit-region-contraction}.

   Define the skeleton saturated value by
   \[
      U_{\rm sk}(s)=1
      \quad\text{at terminal states},
      \qquad
      U_{\rm sk}(s)=
      \min\left\{1,\sum_{e:s\to t}\gamma_eU_{\rm sk}(t)\right\}
   \]
   at nonterminal states.  Also define the skeleton depth denominator
   \[
      L(s)=1
      \quad\text{at terminal states},
      \qquad
      L(s)=d_s+\max_{e:s\to t}L(t)
   \]
   at nonterminal states with at least one exit edge, and \(L(s)=\infty\) if a
   nonterminal state has no exit edge.  Then the original harmonic route
   value \(W\) satisfies
   \[
      W(r_s)\ge\frac{U_{\rm sk}(s)}{L(s)}
      \qquad(s\in\mathcal S).
      \tag{PSC1}
   \]
   Consequently, if
   \[
      L(s_0)=\log^{O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      U_{\rm sk}(s_0)\ge\log^{-A_c}n,
   \]
   then the original route trie has inverse-polylogarithmic harmonic value at
   its root.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   First define the compressed harmonic lower bound \(Y\) on the skeleton by
   \(Y(s)=1\) at terminal states and, at nonterminal states,
   \[
      Y(s)=\frac{T_s}{1+d_sT_s},
      \qquad
      T_s=\sum_{e:s\to t}\gamma_eY(t).
      \tag{PSC2}
   \]
   We claim \(W(r_s)\ge Y(s)\) for every skeleton state.  This is immediate at
   terminal states.  At a nonterminal state \(s\), the induction hypothesis
   gives \(W(r_t)\ge Y(t)\) for every child \(t\).  Hence the port conductance
   in \cref{lem:ported-unit-region-contraction} is at least \(T_s\).  Applying
   that lemma to \(\mathcal R_s\) gives
   \[
      W(r_s)\ge\frac{T_s}{1+d_sT_s}=Y(s).
   \]

   It remains to compare \(Y\) with \(U_{\rm sk}\).  We prove
   \(Y(s)\ge U_{\rm sk}(s)/L(s)\) by backward induction.  The terminal case is
   equality.  If a nonterminal state has no exit edges, then
   \(Y(s)=U_{\rm sk}(s)=0\), so the claim is trivial.  Otherwise let \(s\) be
   nonterminal and put
   \[
      M_s=\max_{e:s\to t}L(t),
      \qquad
      x_s=\sum_{e:s\to t}\gamma_eU_{\rm sk}(t).
   \]
   By induction,
   \[
      T_s=\sum_{e:s\to t}\gamma_eY(t)
      \ge
      \frac{x_s}{M_s}.
   \]
   Since \(z/(1+d_sz)\) is increasing,
   \[
      Y(s)\ge
      \frac{x_s/M_s}{1+d_sx_s/M_s}
      =
      \frac{x_s}{M_s+d_sx_s}.
   \]
   If \(x_s\le1\), this is at least \(x_s/(M_s+d_s)=U_{\rm sk}(s)/L(s)\).
   If \(x_s\ge1\), then \(T_s\ge1/M_s\), and directly from \((PSC2)\),
   \[
      Y(s)\ge\frac{1/M_s}{1+d_s/M_s}
      =
      \frac1{M_s+d_s}
      =
      \frac{U_{\rm sk}(s)}{L(s)}.
   \]
   This proves \((PSC1)\).  The inverse-polylogarithmic consequence follows
   immediately.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Skeleton cut conductance certificate]
   \label{cor:skeleton-cut-conductance}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:port-skeleton-saturated-comparison}, let
   \(A_{\rm sk}\) be the skeleton prefix mass,
   \(A_{\rm sk}(s_0)=1\) and
   \(A_{\rm sk}(t)=A_{\rm sk}(s)\gamma_e\) for a skeleton edge \(e:s\to t\).
   Suppose every skeleton route cut \(\mathcal C\) satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{s\in\mathcal C}A_{\rm sk}(s)\ge p .
      \tag{SCC1}
   \]
   Then the original route trie has
   \[
      W(r_{s_0})
      \ge
      \frac{p}{L(s_0)}.
   \]
   In particular, a polylogarithmic skeleton depth denominator and an
   inverse-polylogarithmic skeleton cut mass imply the aggregate value-delivery
   hypothesis needed by the budget-thinned endpoint.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut} to the skeleton tree with edge
   probabilities \(\gamma_e\).  The cut hypothesis \((SCC1)\) gives
   \(U_{\rm sk}(s_0)\ge p\).  Now use
   \cref{lem:port-skeleton-saturated-comparison}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Cutset value delivery]\label{cor:cutset-value-delivery}
   In the setting of \cref{cor:fractional-value-delivery}, suppose every
   route has length at most $H$ and every route cut $\mathcal C$ in the
   candidate trie has prefix-success mass
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}A(u)\ge p .
   \]
   Then the fractional value-delivery layer reaches a valid route with
   probability at least $p/(H+1)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut}, $U(\varnothing)\ge p$. Apply
   \cref{cor:saturated-value-delivery}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Capped-flow value delivery]\label{cor:capped-flow-delivery}
   In the setting of \cref{cor:fractional-value-delivery}, suppose the
   candidate route trie admits fractional terminal weights $a_v\in[0,1]$ such
   that every suffix value is at most one and
   \[
      V(\varnothing)\ge\log^{-A_c}n .
   \]
   If every route has length $H=\log^{O_c(1)}n$, then the fractional
   value-delivery layer reaches a valid route with probability
   $\log^{-O_c(1)}n$, and its expected work is still bounded by the allocated
   budget.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:unit-capped-value-flow}. The event $Z_a>0$ implies that at
   least one positive-weight route has all of its required opportunities
   succeed, so a valid route is reached. The work bound is unchanged from
   \cref{cor:fractional-value-delivery}, because fractional terminal pruning
   only discards successes and does not open additional opportunities.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Saturated value delivery]\label{cor:saturated-value-delivery}
   In the setting of \cref{cor:fractional-value-delivery}, let $U$ be the
   saturated value of the candidate route trie. If every route has length
   $H=\log^{O_c(1)}n$ and
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge\log^{-A_c}n,
   \]
   then the fractional value-delivery layer reaches a valid route with
   probability $\log^{-O_c(1)}n$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion}, the saturated root value is
   achieved by unit-capped fractional terminal weights. Apply
   \cref{cor:capped-flow-delivery}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Local LCA-energy certificate]\label{lem:local-lca-energy}
   Work in the route trie of \cref{lem:lca-value-flow}. For a child edge
   $u\to w$, write $\gamma_{u,w}$ for its success probability, and let
   $\operatorname{ch}(u)$ be the children of $u$. Suppose there is a
   nonnegative function $M$ on trie nodes such that $M(v)\ge1$ at every leaf
   and, at every internal node,
   \[
      V(u)^2+
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \gamma_{u,w}M(w)V(w)^2
      \le
      M(u)V(u)^2 .
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal T}A(u)V(u)^2
      \le
      M(\varnothing)V(\varnothing)^2 .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a node $u$, let
   \[
      \mathcal E(u)=
      \sum_{z\succeq u}
      \left(\prod_{u\prec e\preceq z}\gamma_e\right)V(z)^2
   \]
   be the LCA energy of the subtree below $u$, normalized so that the prefix
   weight at $u$ itself is one. We prove by backward induction that
   $\mathcal E(u)\le M(u)V(u)^2$. At a leaf, $\mathcal E(u)=V(u)^2=1$ and this
   follows from $M(u)\ge1$. At an internal node,
   \[
      \mathcal E(u)=
      V(u)^2+
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \gamma_{u,w}\mathcal E(w).
   \]
   Applying the induction hypothesis to the children and then the displayed
   local inequality gives $\mathcal E(u)\le M(u)V(u)^2$. Taking $u$ to be the
   root proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Energy-spine toll decomposition]\label{lem:energy-spine-toll}
   Work in the route trie of \cref{lem:lca-value-flow} after deleting
   zero-value subtrees. For every internal node $u$, define its local
   square-energy toll
   \[
      \chi(u)=
      \frac{\sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \gamma_{u,w}V(w)^2}{V(u)^2}.
   \]
   Define the energy-spine transition law
   \[
      P_u(w)=
      \frac{\gamma_{u,w}V(w)^2}
           {\sum_{y\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}\gamma_{u,y}V(y)^2}.
   \]
   Let $(U_0,U_1,\ldots,U_T)$ be the random root-to-leaf spine generated by
   these transitions, with $U_0=\varnothing$. Then
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal T}A(u)V(u)^2
      =
      V(\varnothing)^2
      \E\left[
         \sum_{j=0}^{T}
         \prod_{i<j}\chi(U_i)
      \right].
   \]
   Consequently, if every root-to-leaf route has length at most $H$ and every
   energy-spine realization satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{i<T}\log_+\chi(U_i)\le B,
   \]
   then
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal T}A(u)V(u)^2
      \le
      (H+1)e^B V(\varnothing)^2 .
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a nonroot node $z$, multiply the identities
   \[
      \gamma_{u,w}V(w)^2
      =
      \chi(u)V(u)^2P_u(w)
   \]
   along the root-to-$z$ path. This gives
   \[
      A(z)V(z)^2
      =
      V(\varnothing)^2
      \left(\prod_{u\prec z}\chi(u)\right)
      \Prb[U_{|z|}=z],
   \]
   with the empty product for the root. Summing over all nodes at all depths
   yields the displayed identity. If the positive toll along every spine has
   logarithm at most $B$, then every partial product is at most $e^B$, and
   there are at most $H+1$ terms.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Square-nonexpansive route tries]\label{cor:square-nonexpansive-routes}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:lca-value-flow}, suppose every root-to-leaf route
   has at most $H$ edges and every internal node satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{w\in\operatorname{ch}(u)}
      \gamma_{u,w}V(w)^2
      \le
      V(u)^2 .
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal T}A(u)V(u)^2
      \le
      (H+1)V(\varnothing)^2 .
   \]
   Hence, if $H=\log^{O_c(1)}n$ and
   $V(\varnothing)\ge\log^{-O_c(1)}n$, the fractional value-delivery layer
   succeeds with inverse-polylogarithmic probability.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Let $h(u)$ be the maximum number of edges from $u$ to a descendant leaf and
   set $M(u)=h(u)+1$. For a child $w$ of $u$, $M(w)\le h(u)$. Therefore
   \[
      V(u)^2+
      \sum_w\gamma_{u,w}M(w)V(w)^2
      \le
      V(u)^2+h(u)\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}V(w)^2
      \le
      (h(u)+1)V(u)^2
      =
      M(u)V(u)^2 .
   \]
   Apply \cref{lem:local-lca-energy} and then
   \cref{lem:lca-value-flow}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The concrete route-moment target]\label{rem:lca-value-target}
   \Cref{lem:lca-value-flow} is the route-level analogue of the spherical LCA
   calculation in \cref{lem:tame-second-moment}. It separates the remaining
   near-side problem into two measurable quantities on the adaptive score tree:
   the root delivered value $V(\varnothing)$, and the LCA energy
   $\sum_uA(u)V(u)^2$. A proof of polylogarithmic overhead through the
   budget-thinned route can now aim to show
   \[
      V(\varnothing)\ge\log^{-O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      \sum_uA(u)V(u)^2
      \le \log^{O_c(1)}n\,V(\varnothing)^2
   \]
   for the trie of tame near routes that are not already handled by a cap-free
   residual scan. This is more checkable than a single-path product condition:
   shared early opportunities are charged once through $A(u)$, and branching is
   charged only through the LCA energy, exactly as shared score prefixes are
   charged in the spherical second moment.

   \Cref{lem:local-lca-energy} gives a local way to prove the same bound. The
   cleanest sufficient condition is the square-nonexpansion in
   \cref{cor:square-nonexpansive-routes}: ordinary score branching with
   $\gamma_{u,w}=1$ automatically has this property because
   $\sum_wV(w)^2\le(\sum_wV(w))^2$. Thus any remaining LCA-energy growth must
   come from genuinely thinned geometric or fractional-budget opportunities.
   The harmonic version is even more explicit:
   \cref{lem:harmonic-resistance-normal-form} says that child values combine as
   parallel conductances, while
   \cref{lem:ported-unit-region-contraction} shows that an entire
   deterministic score region loses only its depth before handing the aggregate
   conductance \(\sum_{e=(a,b)}\gamma_eW(b)\) to the first nonunit ports.
   \Cref{lem:port-skeleton-saturated-comparison} and
   \cref{cor:skeleton-cut-conductance} push this through a whole compressed
   skeleton: it is enough to prove inverse-polylogarithmic prefix mass on every
   skeleton cut, paying only the total unit-region depth denominator.
   \Cref{lem:energy-spine-toll} makes this exact: the whole loss is the
   accumulated positive toll
   \[
      \sum\log_+
      \frac{\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}V(w)^2}{V(u)^2}
   \]
   along an energy-biased route spine. The next theorem to prove is therefore
   local: either show that the actual tame-route value split is
   square-nonexpansive after aggregating alternative routes, construct an
   explicit Bellman multiplier $M$ with $M(\varnothing)=\log^{O_c(1)}n$, or
   prune the route family to a unit-capped fractional flow as in
   \cref{lem:unit-capped-value-flow}. The last formulation removes the
   second-moment target entirely: the remaining statement becomes a max-flow
   lower bound saying that inverse-polylogarithmic root value survives after
   imposing capacity $V(u)\le1$ at every prefix. By
   \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion}, this is equivalently a lower bound on
   the saturated Bellman value
   \[
      U(u)=\min\{1,\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}U(w)\}.
   \]
   By \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut}, it is also equivalent to proving that
   every prefix cut through the tame-route trie has inverse-polylogarithmic
   prefix-success mass $\sum_{u\in\mathcal C}A(u)$.
   \Cref{lem:dominated-route-law} gives the dual probabilistic form of the same
   target: construct a law on tame routes whose chance of passing through any
   prefix $u$ is at most a polylogarithmic factor times the activation prefix
   mass $A(u)$. Equivalently, by \cref{cor:route-law-toll}, prove only
   $O_c(\log\log n)$ positive domination toll
   $\log_+(\lambda_{u,w}/\gamma_{u,w})$ along the chosen route law. The
   typical-route version \cref{cor:typical-route-law-toll} is the form most
   compatible with the adaptive-continuation proof: it is enough to prove
   $O_c(\log\log n)$ expected positive toll and then discard the bad tail of
   the route law. The natural candidate is the tilted tame-good route law from
   \cref{lem:adaptive-continuation}; in homogeneous balanced prefixes its
   prefix probabilities are smaller than the canonical activation factors, so
   positive toll can only come from atypical geometric opportunities.
   \Cref{cor:canonical-budget-defect-toll} makes this target concrete for the
   canonical budget-thinned process: budget-stable recentering edges have zero
   toll, and every remaining edge pays only
   \[
      \log_+
      \frac{\Gamma(u)}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u)} .
   \]
   \Cref{lem:canonical-defect-splitting} decomposes this toll into the
   pair-score affinity defect
   \(\log_+(H_0/(H_p^\rho Q_q^s))\), the resident-surprise defect
   \(\rho\log_+(H_p/\overline H)\), and the harmless
   \(O(\eta^2)\) activation slack.  Hence
   \cref{cor:surprise-defect-route-law} gives a more geometric sufficient
   theorem: prove \(O_c(\log\log n)\) expected cumulative affinity-plus-surprise
   along a tame route law.  Low-surprise prefixes are paid for by the canonical
   budget; high-surprise prefixes are precisely the exposed posterior prefixes
   that should be handled by structured rate-coverage or list-decoded facility
   buckets.  \Cref{lem:posterior-superlevel-cancels-surprise} formalizes this
   handoff: a decoded resident posterior superlevel child has size smaller by
   \(e^{-L}\), and that power-size saving cancels the resident-surprise factor
   \(e^{\rho L}\) in the canonical defect.
   The stronger cut form
   \cref{cor:affinity-cut-posterior-handoff} removes the need to sum the
   remaining pair-affinity defect along one route: after the posterior
   handoff, it is enough that every cut of the port skeleton have
   inverse-polylogarithmic mass under the local weights
   \(H_p(u)^\rho Q_q(u)^s/H_0\), up to the structured-decoder slack.
   By \cref{cor:affinity-cut-energy}, the matching second-moment formulation
   is to bound
   \[
      \sum_{s\in\mathcal C}
      \frac{\Lambda(s)^2}{A_{\rm aff}(s)}
   \]
   on every stopped skeleton cut, or equivalently to bound the stopped
   square-spine toll
   \(\sum\log_+\chi_{\rm aff}\).
   \Cref{cor:affinity-proportional-route-law} removes the arbitrary choice of
   local route law from this formulation: for any decoded outgoing port list
   \(E_s\), the affinity-proportional transition
   \(\lambda_e=\omega_e/\sum_{f\in E_s}\omega_f\) makes
   \(\chi_{\rm aff}(s)=1/\sum_{f\in E_s}\omega_f\).  Hence the clean
   code/lattice target is a decoded port list with only
   \(O_c(\log\log n)\) cumulative outgoing-affinity deficit before every cut.
   Thus one sufficient geometric theorem is to prove this affinity cut mass;
   another is to construct a tame route law whose expected cumulative
   canonical budget defect is \(O_c(\log\log n)\).
   The pruned version \cref{cor:pruned-budget-defect-toll} allows an
   additional multiplier profile \(r\), provided
   \cref{lem:pruned-budget-thinning} certifies its expected work.  This is a
   strictly more flexible target: prove a decodable profile with
   \[
      \E\sum_{u\in\mathcal A(q)}r_q(u)\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}(u)
      \le F_4(m,R)
   \]
   and only \(O_c(\log\log n)\) expected pruned defect along the tilted tame
   route law.
   The allocation form \cref{lem:budget-allocation-thinning} and
   \cref{cor:allocation-defect-toll} remove even this parameterization.  Write
   a certified allocation as
   \(Y_q(e)=F_4(m,R)y_q(e)\), where \(y_q\) is a subprobability distribution on
   the first-opportunity edges decoded by the query.  A fully canonical
   remaining theorem would be: for every query state, construct one decodable
   \(y_q\), independent of which near point will witness the query, such that
   for every unresolved near point \(p\) there is a tame route law with edge
   occupation probabilities \(\ell^p_e\) satisfying
   \[
      \sum_e\ell^p_e
      \log_+
      \frac{\Gamma_e}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R)y_q(e)}
      =
      O_c(\log\log n).
   \]
   This per-witness statement is stronger than the ANN guarantee requires.
   The ANN-native version replaces the family of laws \(\{\ell^p\}\) by a
   single law on successful routes whose terminal leaf may contain any point in
   the unresolved near set \(N(q)\).  It is enough to prove
   \[
      \E_{V\sim\ell^{\exists}}
      \sum_{e\preceq V}
      \log_+
      \frac{\Gamma_e}
           {(1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R)y_q(e)}
      =
      O_c(\log\log n)
   \]
   along such an existential successful route law whenever \(N(q)\ne\emptyset\).
   Singleton near sets still force the old per-witness behavior, but dense or
   multi-witness queries may spend all route mass on the easiest valid neighbor.
   This is the exact analogue of optimizing a sphere-decoder pruning profile:
   the build phase may search for the profile, but the query must decode the
   retained allocation and its total mass must be certified.
   \Cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation} removes the last arbitrary choice in this
   profile once a route law is fixed: the optimal allocation is the
   water-filled vector \(y_e=\min\{a_e,t\ell_e\}\), where
   \(\ell_e\) is the route-law occupation measure and \(a_e\) is the normalized
   recursive charge.  Thus the near-side theorem can be phrased as bounding
   one scalar water-filled defect, plus proving that this allocation is
   query-decodable.
   \Cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax} gives the corresponding all-witness
   version: one query allocation works for a finite family of witness route
   laws iff every mixture of their occupation measures has small water-filled
   defect.  This is the allocation analogue of list-decoding a received word
   against a whole candidate list rather than against one known codeword.
   \Cref{lem:waterfilled-layercake} and
   \cref{cor:robust-waterfilled-tail} turn that scalar defect into a tail
   estimate: for every mixture, bound the occupation mass of opportunities
   whose normalized charge \(a_e/\ell_e\) exceeds the water level by a factor
   \(e^s\), and integrate over \(s\).  This is the form closest to the
   branching-random-walk proof, where high-score prefixes are controlled by
   summable tail bounds.
   \Cref{lem:cut-energy-route-law} is the sharpest form for a stopping-frontier
   proof: it is enough to show that, for every route cut $\mathcal C$,
   \[
      \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}\frac{\Lambda(u)^2}{A(u)}
      \le \log^{O_c(1)}n .
   \]
   This avoids paying for intermediate prefixes that are not used by the cut.
   By \cref{lem:frontier-square-toll}, this cut energy is exactly a
   square-biased spine expectation stopped at $\mathcal C$.  Therefore another
   sufficient local target is: along the square-biased tame-route spine, every
   adversarial route cut is hit after only $O_c(\log\log n)$ positive square
   toll
   \[
      \sum\log_+\chi_\Lambda(u),
      \qquad
      \chi_\Lambda(u)=
      \sum_w\frac{\lambda_{u,w}^2}{\gamma_{u,w}} .
   \]
   The stronger but often easier all-prefix condition
   \cref{cor:route-law-prefix-energy} asks for
   $\sum_u\Lambda(u)^2/A(u)=\log^{O_c(1)}n$, which is exactly the kind of
   LCA-summed second-moment estimate proved for tame spherical leaves.
   \Cref{lem:local-prefix-energy} and
   \cref{lem:prefix-energy-spine-toll} make this target local: it is enough
   to bound the edge square toll
   $\chi_\Lambda(u)=\sum_w\lambda_{u,w}^2/\gamma_{u,w}$. Ordinary score
   branching with unthinned edges has $\gamma_{u,w}=1$, and therefore
   $\chi_\Lambda(u)\le1$ for every route law. Thus any prefix-energy growth is
   isolated to the budget-thinned or fractional-delivery opportunities where
   activation probabilities are genuinely smaller than the route-law split.
   Finally, \cref{lem:optimal-prefix-energy-law} removes the need to guess the
   route law: the optimal prefix-energy law is computed by the harmonic
   Bellman recursion
   \[
      W(u)=
      \frac{\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}W(w)}
           {1+\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}W(w)}.
   \]
   \Cref{lem:unit-success-skeleton-contraction} makes the harmless part
   precise: a whole score-only unit-success region contributes only its depth
   as harmonic resistance.  An underfunded geometric opportunity, by contrast,
   is visible through the local sum
