   $\sum_w\gamma_{u,w}W(w)$. Thus a fully explicit sufficient target is to
   prove inverse-polylogarithmic harmonic value at the root of the tame-route
   trie. By \cref{lem:harmonic-saturated-comparison}, this is equivalent to
   the saturated min-cut target up to the polylogarithmic route depth.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The remaining aggregate value target]\label{rem:value-delivery-target}
   \Cref{lem:canonical-budget-activation} makes the near-lineage part of the
   budget-thinned route quantitative. Along a candidate near route, every
   necessary cap edge contributes the usual geometric thinning factor from
   \cref{lem:thin-lineage} and, only when the canonical budget is locally
   insufficient, the additional activation factor $\beta_q(u)$. The work side
   is already handled by optional stopping in
   \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities}.

   The right remaining statement should be aggregate, not single-path. A fixed
   path sufficient condition would ask for one tame route with
   $\prod_{u\in\mathcal O(v)}\beta_q(u)\ge\log^{-O_c(1)}n$. This can be too
   strong. In a homogeneous balanced prefix, the canonical factor at depth
   $\ell$ is about $B^{-(1-\rho)\ell}$; requiring many underfunded opportunities
   on one distinguished route would multiply these factors even though different
   good routes share early opportunities and split only after their lowest
   common ancestors.

   One valid target is a first/second moment statement for the fractional value
   variable $Z_{\rm val}$ in \cref{cor:fractional-value-delivery}, through the
   LCA value-flow certificate of \cref{lem:lca-value-flow}. The first moment
   sums the $\beta_q$-weighted delivered value over all tame good routes, while
   the second moment charges two routes by the union of their required
   opportunity sets, exactly as the spherical proof charges two accepting
   leaves by their LCA. If that moment estimate holds with
   inverse-polylogarithmic first moment and polynomial-logarithmic
   second-moment ratio, then the budget-thinned route delivers value to the
   near point with inverse-polylogarithmic probability.

   \Cref{lem:unit-capped-value-flow} gives an even cleaner sufficient target.
   Instead of proving the LCA energy directly, prune the fractional route
   family so that every prefix has remaining conditional value at most one.
   If the capped root value is still inverse-polylogarithmic, then
   \cref{cor:capped-flow-delivery} gives the required success probability
   automatically. This is the right way to state the integer-materialization
   problem: a tiny-probability mandatory edge may lead to many downstream
   routes, but it should contribute at most its own probability to the capped
   root value. Equivalently, by \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion} and
   \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut}, prove that the saturated value
   $U(\varnothing)$ of the tame-route trie is inverse-polylogarithmic, or that
   every route cut has inverse-polylogarithmic prefix-success mass. This is the
   form used in \cref{asm:aggregate-value-delivery}. An equivalent
   computable sufficient condition, up to the route-depth factor, is the
   harmonic value
   $W(\varnothing)$ from \cref{lem:optimal-prefix-energy-law}: it directly
   optimizes the prefix-energy route law and is within a factor
   $H+1$ of the saturated value by
   \cref{lem:harmonic-saturated-comparison}.
   Between these two formulations is the cut-energy certificate of
   \cref{lem:cut-energy-route-law}.  It asks for a route law whose
   $\chi^2$ energy is polylogarithmic on every stopping cut, not on the union of
   all prefixes.  This is the formulation most directly aligned with the
   branching continuation process: a cap-discovery frontier, a resident-scan
   frontier, or an adversarial route cut is exactly one antichain.
   \Cref{lem:frontier-square-toll} rewrites this certificate as an optional
   stopping estimate for the square-biased route spine, again without the
   route-depth loss that appears in all-prefix energy.
\end{remark}

\begin{assumption}[Saturated fractional value delivery]\label{asm:aggregate-value-delivery}
   Run the randomized one-tree construction with
   \[
      \eta=\Theta_c\!\left(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\right),
      \qquad
      \delta=\Theta_c(\eta^{2/\rho_c}),
   \]
   and use the canonical budget-thinned activation rule of
   \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities} at every geometric opportunity.
   For every near pair $(p,q)$, after excluding routes on which a cap-free
   residual scan already exposes $p$, there is a family
   $\mathcal G(p,q)$ of tame candidate routes and a fractional budget
   allocation whose total expected work is charged by
   \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities} and
   \cref{lem:fractional-value-budget}. The corresponding route trie has
   polylogarithmic depth and edge success probabilities $\gamma_{u,w}$ for
   these additional budget or fractional-delivery opportunities, excluding the
   ordinary cap-edge thinning handled separately in
   \cref{lem:thin-lineage}. Let $U$ be its saturated value, equivalently the
   min-cut value from \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut}. Then
   \[
      U(\varnothing)\ge \log^{-A_c}n
   \]
   for a constant $A_c$ depending only on $c$. Each route in
   $\mathcal G(p,q)$ either reaches a resident scan containing $p$ or follows
   certified cap children containing $p$ until such a scan is reached.
\end{assumption}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional budget-thinned polylog endpoint]\label{prop:budget-thinned-polylog}
   Under \cref{asm:aggregate-value-delivery}, the canonical budget-thinned
   one-tree construction gives worst-case Euclidean ANN bounds
   \[
      Q(n,d)=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      S(n,d)=dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n .
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Work after bounded-diameter localization, so the initial radius ratio is
   $R_0/r=\log^{O_c(1)}n$.  With
   \(\eta=\Theta_c((\log\log n)/\log n)\) and
   \(\delta=\Theta_c(\eta^{2/\rho_c})\),
   \cref{prop:budget-thinned-work} gives expected query work
   \[
      dF_4(n,R_0)\log^{O_c(1)}n
      =
      d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n .
   \]

   Storage is controlled by the deterministic storage part of
   \cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction} and the stored-prefix bounds for the score
   blocks, giving $dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n$ after summing over the
   polylogarithmic localization copies.

   For success, \cref{asm:aggregate-value-delivery} and
   \cref{cor:saturated-value-delivery} imply that with probability
   $\log^{-O_c(1)}n$ there is a successful delivered route for the true near pair,
   unless a cap-free residual scan has already exposed the near point. Along a
   successful delivered cap route, the ordinary geometric thinning probabilities telescope
   by \cref{lem:thin-lineage}; with the displayed choice of $\delta$ and the
   above generation bound, this costs only another $\log^{-O_c(1)}n$ factor.
   Hence one copy succeeds with inverse-polylogarithmic probability.
   Polylogarithmic independent copies, followed by standard truncation of the
   expected work, give the capped constant-success bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why normalized continuation budgets are too small]\label{rem:query-only-budget}
   The most obvious query-only budget,
   \[
      \mathcal B_q(u)=F_4(m,R)\frac{H_q(u)}{H_q(\varnothing)},
   \]
   is a normalized query continuation-mass martingale, but it cannot pay for early geometric splits by itself. The canonical mixed budget in \cref{lem:canonical-mixed-budget} has the same scale in the homogeneous balanced case. At an early level $\ell$, before the continuation barrier prunes many children, a typical query prefix has budget about $F_4(m,R)B^{-\ell}$. The corresponding bucket has size about $mB^{-\ell}$, so its recursive power charge is
   \[
      F_4(mB^{-\ell},R)
      \approx
      F_4(m,R)B^{-\rho\ell}.
   \]
   The ratio is $B^{(1-\rho)\ell}$. Thus the budget certificate cannot be query-only; it must include data-aware information about how the bucket mass contracts under the score process, or else the algorithm must gate early cap splits until such a budget is available.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional randomized one-tree polylog endpoint]\label{prop:randomized-polylog}
   Under \cref{asm:mixed-continuation-operator}, the randomized one-tree construction with predictable almost-total cap tests gives worst-case Euclidean ANN bounds
   \[
      Q(n,d)=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      S(n,d)=dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Work in one bounded-diameter localized instance and put
   \[
      L=\log n,
      \qquad
      \ell=\log\log n,
      \qquad
      \eta=\Theta_c(\ell/L),
      \qquad
      \delta=\delta_0\eta^{2/\rho_c}.
   \]
   Choose $\delta_0$ small enough that \cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction} applies to every almost-total cap split. Also
   \[
      \alpha^{-1}=(\eta\delta)^{-1}=\eta^{-1-2/\rho_c}\delta_0^{-1}=\log^{O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      \exp(O_c(\eta L))=\log^{O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      \eta^{-O_c(1)}=\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
   Let
   \[
      A(n)=\eta^{-O_c(1)}\alpha^{-1}\exp(O_c(\eta L))\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
   By \cref{lem:cap-free-scan-accounting}, the expected local score work and cap-free terminal scans at a state are at most $A(n)F_4(m,R)$, while \cref{asm:mixed-continuation-operator} bounds the expected sum of recursive child potentials by $(1-\kappa\eta^2)F_4(m,R)$. Unrolling over geometric generations gives total expected query work at most
   \[
      O(\eta^{-2})A(n)F_4(n,R_0).
   \]
   Since $F_4(n,R_0)=n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n$, the displayed parameter choice makes this $n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n$.

   The cap-edge thinning along the true near lineage still costs only a polylogarithmic factor. Indeed, the number of radius-reducing cap edges is
   \[
      H=O_c\!\left(\eta^{-2}\log\frac{R_0}{r}\right)
      =
      O_c(\eta^{-2}\ell),
   \]
   and
   \[
      H\delta=O_c(\eta^{-2+2/\rho_c}\ell)=o(1)
   \]
   for the displayed choice of $\delta$. Thus \cref{lem:thin-lineage} gives lineage-thinning probability at least $\log^{-O_c(1)}n$. The score depth is $K=O_c(L)$, so \cref{lem:adaptive-continuation} gives a common near score path with probability $K^{-O_c(1)}=\log^{-O_c(1)}n$. Polylogarithmic amplification and the truncation paragraph in the preliminaries give the query bound with constant success probability. The storage bound follows from the storage contraction in \cref{lem:thin-cap-contraction}, the stored-prefix part of \cref{lem:adaptive-continuation}, and bounded-diameter localization.
\end{proof}

\section{A structured posterior route}

\Cref{prop:randomized-polylog} keeps the cap geometry and asks for a branching first-split work estimate. There is another possible route: replace cap discovery by posterior-superlevel children of a stochastic score mechanism. This is closer to the LSH-forest negative-moment calculus and to the local surrogate-success viewpoint of Andoni and Beaglehole~\cite{AB22}. The advantage is that the recursion accounting becomes almost algebraic.

The important distinction is between explicit enumeration and structured decoding. The negative-moment recursion itself does not impose a coarse child mass. The coarse scale appears only when every possible outcome must be explicitly evaluated before the few useful ones can be found.

\begin{lemma}[Explicit-enumeration barrier]\label{lem:explicit-enumeration-barrier}
   Fix $\rho>0$ and a node of size $m$. Consider a one-scale posterior recursion whose useful children have mass
   \[
      \xi=m^{-\gamma}.
   \]
   Suppose one independent group must evaluate
   \[
      B\ge \xi^{-1}\log^{-O(1)}m
   \]
   possible outcomes, and that turning $\xi$-scale negative-moment value into constant hit probability requires
   \[
      T\ge \xi^{-\rho}\log^{-O(1)}m
   \]
   independent groups. If the local query budget is $m^\rho\log^{O(1)}m$, then
   \[
      \gamma\le \frac{\rho}{1+\rho}+O\!\left(\frac{\log\log m}{\log m}\right).
   \]
   In particular, for $\rho=\rho_c=1/(2c^2-1)$ the explicit balanced scale is
   \[
      m^{-\rho/(1+\rho)}=m^{-1/(2c^2)}
   \]
   up to polylogarithmic factors. If a structured decoder lists only active outcomes and avoids the factor $B$, the repetition cost $\xi^{-\rho}$ fits the $m^\rho$ budget for every nonempty scale $\xi\ge1/m$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Explicit evaluation contributes at least
   \[
      BT\ge \xi^{-(1+\rho)}\log^{-O(1)}m
      =
      m^{\gamma(1+\rho)}\log^{-O(1)}m
   \]
   units of local work. Requiring this to be at most $m^\rho\log^{O(1)}m$ gives the displayed bound on $\gamma$. The identity
   \[
      \frac{\rho_c}{1+\rho_c}=\frac1{2c^2}
   \]
   is immediate from the definition of $\rho_c$. Without explicit enumeration, the one-scale amplification cost is only $\xi^{-\rho}$, and $\xi\ge1/m$ implies $\xi^{-\rho}\le m^\rho$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Oblivious sparsification barrier]\label{lem:oblivious-sparsification-barrier}
   The scale-normalized moment bounds alone do not imply that the ideal
   continuous process can be replaced by a small query-oblivious panel. More
   precisely, fix $\rho\in(0,1)$ and a scale $\xi\in(0,1)$ with
   $M=\lfloor 1/\xi\rfloor$. There is an abstract one-scale instance with
   $M$ disjoint children $C_1,\ldots,C_M$, each of mass $\xi$, satisfying
   the active and stored moment bounds of \cref{lem:scale-window-compiler}
   with $A=1$ and value $h=1$ for $M$ different promised queries
   $q_1,\ldots,q_M$, such that $q_i$ can be hit only by child $C_i$.

   Consequently, consider any replacement that samples query-independent
   panels of children and whose query algorithm can return only children
   appearing in the sampled panels. If
   $T=\lceil c_0\xi^{-\rho}\rceil$ independent panels are used, and every
   promised query must hit its child with probability at least $1/2$, then the
   expected total number of distinct panel children over the $T$ repetitions is
   $\Omega(\xi^{-1})$. In particular, at fine scales the moment bounds by
   themselves cannot justify the $\xi^{-\rho}$ work of structured decoding; the
   decoder must use query-dependent structure, or one must prove an additional
   geometry-specific sensitivity bound.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let the children be disjoint sets of mass $\xi$. Give each child one stored
   copy, so the stored moment is
   \[
      \sum_{i=1}^M \xi^{1+\rho}\le \xi^\rho .
   \]
   For query $q_i$, set the active intensity of $C_i$ to $\xi^\rho$ and the
   active intensity of every other child to zero. Then the active
   $\rho$-moment for $q_i$ is
   \[
      \xi^\rho\cdot \xi^\rho=\xi^{2\rho}\le\xi^\rho ,
   \]
   while the negative-moment value is
   \[
      \xi^\rho\cdot \xi^{-\rho}=1 .
   \]
   Thus the hypotheses used by the scale-window compiler are all satisfied.

   Let $U$ be the union of the children appearing in all sampled panels. Since
   $q_i$ can be hit only if $C_i\in U$, the average hit probability over a
   uniformly random $i\in[M]$ is at most $\E |U|/M$. If every $q_i$ succeeds
   with probability at least $1/2$, then $\E |U|\ge M/2=\Omega(\xi^{-1})$.
   Counting panel entries with multiplicity can only be larger than $|U|$.
\end{proof}

Thus the structured target is not a finite quantization theorem. It is an output-sensitive decoding theorem: the outcome space may be implicit or enormous, provided the query lists only the active children and the listed children obey the moment bounds below. One must still remember the scale. A raw lower bound on
\[
   \sum_{C\ni p}\lambda_q(C)\mu(C)^{-\rho}
\]
is a value statement, not by itself a hit-probability statement; a singleton child hit with one-group intensity $m^{-\rho}$ already contributes constant negative-moment value but has only $m^{-\rho}$ hit intensity before amplification. The dyadic amplification lemmas below keep the scale $\xi$ explicit and insert exactly the $\xi^{-\rho}$ repetitions needed to convert value into constant success probability.
\Cref{lem:oblivious-sparsification-barrier} shows why this is not merely a
sampling issue: an oblivious panel can be forced to cover $\Omega(\xi^{-1})$
disjoint possible witnesses, whereas a structured decoder is allowed to list the
one or few witnesses relevant to the query.

We first write the posterior identity in the finite notation, because it is the cleanest algebraic form. Let a node contain a set $S$ of $m$ points. A finite score channel assigns probabilities $K_x(j)$, $j\in[B]$, to every $x\in S$, with $\sum_jK_x(j)=1$. Put
\[
   \mu_j=\frac1m\sum_{x\in S}K_x(j),
   \qquad
   R_j(x)=\frac{K_x(j)}{\mu_j}.
\]
For a threshold $u$, the posterior-superlevel child is
\[
   C_{j,u}=\{x\in S:R_j(x)\ge u\},
   \qquad
   \Phi_j(u)=\frac{|C_{j,u}|}{m}.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Posterior superlevel compiler]\label{lem:posterior-superlevel-compiler}
   Fix $0<\beta<\rho$ and put $s_\beta=1-\beta$. Store child $(j,u)$ with Poisson intensity
   \[
      \mu_j\,\beta u^{\beta-1}\Phi_j(u)^\rho\,du,
   \]
   and let query $q$ assign each stored copy weight $R_j(q)^{s_\beta}$ in the ideal fractional calculation. Equivalently, one may materialize active copies from a query-dependent Poisson intensity multiplied by this factor. For every data point $p$,
   \[
      \E\sum_{\substack{C\text{ active}\\p\in C}}
      \left(\frac{|C|}{m}\right)^{-\rho}
      =
      \sum_jK_p(j)^\beta K_q(j)^{s_\beta}.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The negative moment cancels the stored factor $\Phi_j(u)^\rho$:
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      \E\sum_{\substack{C\text{ active}\\p\in C}}
      \left(\frac{|C|}{m}\right)^{-\rho}
      &=
      \sum_j\mu_jR_j(q)^{s_\beta}
      \int_0^{R_j(p)}\beta u^{\beta-1}\,du\\
      &=
      \sum_j\mu_jR_j(p)^\beta R_j(q)^{s_\beta}\\
      &=
      \sum_jK_p(j)^\beta K_q(j)^{s_\beta},
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   since $\beta+s_\beta=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Scale-window negative-moment amplification]\label{lem:scale-window-compiler}
   Fix a scale $\xi\in[1/m,1]$ and a value lower bound $h>0$. Consider one independent structured channel group whose active children are generated by a query-dependent Poisson process on certified sets $C\subseteq S$. Write
   \[
      \mu(C)=\frac{|C|}{m}.
   \]
   Suppose every active child has
   \[
      \xi/2\le\mu(C)\le\xi,
   \]
   and let $\lambda_q(C)$ be its active intensity for query $q$. Assume that, for a near pair $(p,q)$ not handled by the resident guard,
   \[
      \sum_{C\ni p}\lambda_q(C)\mu(C)^{-\rho}\ge h.
   \]
   Also assume one group has output-sensitive decoding cost $d\log^{O_c(1)}m$ plus the listed output, and satisfies the scale-normalized moment bounds
   \[
      \sum_C\lambda_q(C)\mu(C)^\rho
      \le A\xi^\rho,
      \qquad
      \sum_C\sigma(C)\mu(C)^{1+\rho}
      \le A\xi^\rho,
   \]
   where $\sigma(C)$ is the preprocessing intensity with which child $C$ is stored. Then
   \[
      T=\left\lceil 2^{\rho+1}\xi^{-\rho}h^{-1}\right\rceil
   \]
   independent groups give a constant-probability hit of some active child containing $p$, while their total active-child $\rho$-moment and stored-child $(1+\rho)$-moment are both $O(Ah^{-1})$. Their decoding cost is
   \[
      d\,\xi^{-\rho}h^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   which is at most $d\,m^\rho h^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let
   \[
      \Lambda_p(q)=\sum_{C\ni p}\lambda_q(C)
   \]
   be the one-group Poisson intensity of active children containing $p$. Since $\mu(C)\ge\xi/2$,
   \[
      \mu(C)^{-\rho}\le 2^\rho\xi^{-\rho}.
   \]
   The negative-moment lower bound therefore implies
   \[
      \Lambda_p(q)\ge 2^{-\rho}\xi^\rho h.
   \]
   Across $T$ independent groups, the number of active children containing $p$ is Poisson with mean at least $2$, so the hit probability is at least $1-e^{-2}$.

   The active and stored moment bounds multiply by $T$:
   \[
      T A\xi^\rho=O(Ah^{-1}).
   \]
   The decoding bound is the same multiplication. Finally $\xi\ge1/m$ gives $\xi^{-\rho}\le m^\rho$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Dyadic structured amplification]\label{lem:dyadic-structured-amplification}
   Let $\mathcal I$ be dyadic scales $\xi\in[1/m,\eta]$. At each scale $\xi$, suppose one independent structured group has children with
   \[
      \xi/2\le\mu(C)\le\xi,
   \]
   output-sensitive decoding cost $d\log^{O_c(1)}m$ plus output, and moment bounds
   \[
      \sum_{C\text{ at scale }\xi}\lambda_q(C)\mu(C)^\rho
      \le A\xi^\rho,
      \qquad
      \sum_{C\text{ at scale }\xi}\sigma(C)\mu(C)^{1+\rho}
      \le A\xi^\rho .
   \]
   Suppose that, for a near pair $(p,q)$, the total negative-moment value over all scales is
   \[
      \sum_{\xi\in\mathcal I}
      \sum_{\substack{C\text{ at scale }\xi\\p\in C}}
      \lambda_q(C)\mu(C)^{-\rho}
      \ge h .
   \]
   If scale $\xi$ is repeated
   \[
      T_\xi=\left\lceil 2^{\rho+1}\xi^{-\rho}h^{-1}\right\rceil
   \]
   times, then the probability of activating some child containing $p$ is at least $1-e^{-2}$. The total decoding and active-output work is
   \[
      d\,m^\rho h^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   and the total expected number of active children is at most the same bound without the factor $d$. If each stored child is represented by $O(1)$ words, the total expected stored-child descriptions over all repetitions are at most
   \[
      m^{1+\rho}h^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let $V_\xi$ be the negative-moment contribution at scale $\xi$ and let $\Lambda_\xi$ be the one-group active intensity of children at that scale containing $p$. Since $\mu(C)\le\xi$ and $\mu(C)\ge\xi/2$ on the scale,
   \[
      \Lambda_\xi
      =
      \sum_{C\ni p}\lambda_q(C)
      \ge
      2^{-\rho}\xi^\rho V_\xi .
   \]
   After $T_\xi$ repetitions, the total hit intensity over all scales is at least
   \[
      \sum_\xi T_\xi\Lambda_\xi
      \ge
      2h^{-1}\sum_\xi V_\xi
      \ge2,
   \]
   so the miss probability is at most $e^{-2}$.

   The decoding cost is proportional to
   \[
      \sum_{\xi\in\mathcal I}\xi^{-\rho}h^{-1}
      \le
      m^\rho h^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   because the dyadic sum is dominated by the smallest scale $1/m$. The one-group expected number of active children at scale $\xi$ is at most
   \[
      (\xi/2)^{-\rho}\sum_C\lambda_q(C)\mu(C)^\rho
      \le
      2^\rho A,
   \]
   so the same dyadic sum bounds the active output.

   Finally, the one-group expected number of stored child descriptions at scale $\xi$ is at most
   \[
      (\xi/2)^{-(1+\rho)}
      \sum_C\sigma(C)\mu(C)^{1+\rho}
      \le
      2^{1+\rho}A\xi^{-1}.
   \]
   Multiplying by $T_\xi$ and summing $\xi^{-(1+\rho)}$ over dyadic scales gives the stated $m^{1+\rho}h^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m$ bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Continuous likelihood-ball geometry]\label{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls}
   Let a node contain $S\subset\R^d$, $|S|=m$, and let $\gamma_t$ denote $N(0,tI_d)$. For $Y\sim\gamma_t$ define
   \[
      L_Y(x)=\exp\!\left(\ip{Y}{x}-\frac t2\|x\|^2\right),
      \qquad
      C_{Y,u}=\{x\in S:L_Y(x)\ge u\},
      \qquad
      \Phi_Y(u)=\frac{|C_{Y,u}|}{m}.
   \]
   The set $C_{Y,u}$ is a Euclidean ball. For $0<\alpha<\rho$ and $s_\alpha=1-\alpha$,
   \[
      \int L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}\mathbf 1_{\{p\in C_{Y,u}\}}\Phi_Y(u)^{-\rho}
      \,d\gamma_t(Y)\,\alpha u^{\alpha-1}\Phi_Y(u)^\rho\,du
      =
      \exp\!\left(-\frac{\alpha s_\alpha t}{2}\|p-q\|^2\right).
   \]

   Moreover fix $c>1$ and $\eta\in[1/m,1]$. Let $p,q$ satisfy $\|p-q\|\le r$, and suppose fewer than $\eta m/2$ data points are within distance $cr$ of $q$. With
   \[
      \alpha=\kappa/\log m,
      \qquad
      t=C_c\frac{\log m}{r^2}
   \]
   for sufficiently small $\kappa=\kappa(c)>0$ and sufficiently large $C_c=C_c(c)$, the pure restricted value
   \[
      \mathcal P_\eta(p,q)
      =
      \int d\gamma_t(Y)L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}
      \int_0^{L_Y(p)}
      \alpha u^{\alpha-1}
      \mathbf 1_{\{\Phi_Y(u)\le\eta,\ C_{Y,u}\subseteq \Ball(q,cr)\}}\,du
   \]
   is at least a positive constant $\delta_c$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
   The level-set identity
   \[
      L_Y(x)\ge u
      \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
      \left\|x-\frac Yt\right\|^2
      \le
      \left\|\frac Yt\right\|^2-\frac{2\log u}{t}
   \]
   shows that $C_{Y,u}$ is a ball. The negative moment cancels $\Phi_Y(u)^\rho$, and
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      \int d\gamma_t(Y)L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}
      \int_0^{L_Y(p)}\alpha u^{\alpha-1}\,du
      &=
      \int L_Y(p)^\alpha L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}\,d\gamma_t(Y)\\
      &=
      \exp\!\left(-\frac{\alpha s_\alpha t}{2}\|p-q\|^2\right),
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   by completing the square for the Gaussian shift family.

   For the pure bound, translate so that $q=0$ and write $d=\|p-q\|\le r$. Under the tilted measure proportional to
   \[
      L_Y(p)^\alpha L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}\,d\gamma_t(Y),
   \]
   the vector $Y$ has distribution $N(t\alpha p,tI_d)$, up to the affinity factor displayed above. For an invalid point $x$ with $\|x-q\|>cr$, put
   \[
      \ell_x=\log\frac{L_Y(x)}{L_Y(p)}.
   \]
   Under this tilted law, $\ell_x$ is Gaussian with variance $t\|x-p\|^2$ and mean
   \[
      -\frac t2\Delta_x,
      \qquad
      \Delta_x
      =
      \alpha\|x-p\|^2+s_\alpha(\|x-q\|^2-d^2).
   \]
   Since $\|x-q\|\ge cr$ and $d\le r$,
   \[
      \Delta_x\ge s_\alpha(c^2-1)r^2,
      \qquad
      \frac{\Delta_x}{\|x-p\|}\ge \|x-q\|-d\ge(c-1)r .
   \]
   Therefore, for
   \[
      h_0=\frac{ts_\alpha(c^2-1)r^2}{4},
   \]
   a Gaussian tail bound gives
   \[
      \Pr[\ell_x\ge-h_0]\le
      \exp\!\left(-\Omega_c(tr^2)\right)\le m^{-3}
   \]
   after increasing $C_c$. A union bound over $S$ shows that, with probability $1-O(m^{-2})$ under the tilted law, every invalid point has $L_Y(x)<e^{-h_0}L_Y(p)$.

   On this event, every threshold $u\in[e^{-h_0}L_Y(p),L_Y(p)]$ produces a child containing $p$ and no invalid point. Since fewer than $\eta m/2$ points are valid, such a child also has mass at most $\eta$. Thus
   \[
      \int_0^{L_Y(p)}
      \alpha u^{\alpha-1}
      \mathbf 1_{\{\Phi_Y(u)\le\eta,\ C_{Y,u}\subseteq \Ball(q,cr)\}}\,du
      \ge
      L_Y(p)^\alpha(1-e^{-\alpha h_0})
   \]
   except on a tilted event of probability $O(m^{-2})$. Integrating back gives
   \[
      \mathcal P_\eta(p,q)
      \ge
      \exp\!\left(-\frac{\alpha s_\alpha tr^2}{2}\right)
      (1-e^{-\alpha h_0})(1-O(m^{-2})).
   \]
   The choices $\alpha=\kappa/\log m$ and $t=C_c\log m/r^2$ make both $\alpha tr^2$ and $\alpha h_0$ positive constants depending only on $c$, proving the claim after fixing $\kappa,C_c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Ideal likelihood scale profile]\label{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile}
   In the notation of \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls}, fix $0<\alpha<\rho$ and $s_\alpha=1-\alpha$. For a dyadic scale $\xi\in[1/m,1]$, restrict the continuous children to
   \[
      \xi/2\le \Phi_Y(u)\le \xi
   \]
   and give child $(Y,u)$ preprocessing intensity
   \[
      d\sigma(Y,u)=d\gamma_t(Y)\,\alpha u^{\alpha-1}\Phi_Y(u)^\rho\,du
   \]
   and query-active intensity
   \[
      d\lambda_q(Y,u)=L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}\,d\sigma(Y,u).
   \]
   Then, uniformly in the data set and query,
   \[
      \int_{\xi/2\le\Phi_Y(u)\le\xi}
      \Phi_Y(u)^\rho\,d\lambda_q(Y,u)
      \le
      C_{\rho,\alpha}\xi^\rho,
   \]
   and
   \[
      \int_{\xi/2\le\Phi_Y(u)\le\xi}
      \Phi_Y(u)^{1+\rho}\,d\sigma(Y,u)
      \le
      C_{\rho,\alpha}\xi^\rho.
   \]
   Moreover, under the guard-failure hypothesis of \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls}, the ideal pure multiscale value satisfies
   \[
      \sum_{\xi\le\eta}
      \int_{\substack{\xi/2\le\Phi_Y(u)\le\xi\\
                      p\in C_{Y,u},\ C_{Y,u}\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)}}
      \Phi_Y(u)^{-\rho}\,d\lambda_q(Y,u)
      \ge
      \delta_c,
   \]
   where the sum ranges over dyadic $\xi\in[1/m,\eta]$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For $a>\alpha$, put
   \[
      Z_Y=\frac1m\sum_{x\in S}L_Y(x),
      \qquad
      R_Y(x)=\frac{L_Y(x)}{Z_Y},
   \]
   and let $F_Y(v)=m^{-1}|\{x:R_Y(x)\ge v\}|$. Since $R_Y(X)$ has mean one for a uniform random data point $X$, Markov's inequality gives
   \[
      F_Y(v)\le\min\{1,v^{-1}\}.
   \]
   Therefore
   \[
      \int_0^\infty \alpha u^{\alpha-1}\Phi_Y(u)^a\,du
      =
      Z_Y^\alpha
      \int_0^\infty \alpha v^{\alpha-1}F_Y(v)^a\,dv
      \le
      C_{a,\alpha}Z_Y^\alpha.
   \]

   Jensen's inequality gives
   \[
      Z_Y^\alpha\le\frac1m\sum_{x\in S}L_Y(x)^\alpha.
   \]
   Hence
   \[
      \int L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}Z_Y^\alpha\,d\gamma_t(Y)
      \le
      \frac1m\sum_{x\in S}
      \int L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}L_Y(x)^\alpha\,d\gamma_t(Y)
      \le1,
   \]
   by the same Gaussian affinity identity used in \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls}. Similarly,
   \[
      \int Z_Y^\alpha\,d\gamma_t(Y)
      \le
      \frac1m\sum_{x\in S}\int L_Y(x)^\alpha\,d\gamma_t(Y)
      \le1.
   \]

   On the scale $\Phi_Y(u)\le\xi$,
   \[
      \Phi_Y(u)^{2\rho}\le\xi^\rho\Phi_Y(u)^\rho.
   \]
   Taking $a=\rho$ in the previous bound proves
   \[
      \int_{\xi/2\le\Phi_Y(u)\le\xi}
      \Phi_Y(u)^\rho\,d\lambda_q(Y,u)
      =
      \int_{\xi/2\le\Phi_Y(u)\le\xi}
      L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}\alpha u^{\alpha-1}\Phi_Y(u)^{2\rho}\,du\,d\gamma_t(Y)
      \le
      C_{\rho,\alpha}\xi^\rho .
   \]
   Likewise, on the same scale
   \[
      \Phi_Y(u)^{1+2\rho}\le\xi^\rho\Phi_Y(u)^{1+\rho},
   \]
   and taking $a=1+\rho$ gives the stored-child moment bound.

   Finally, for any child containing $p$, its mass is at least $1/m$. Thus summing the dyadic scales up to $\eta$ partitions the pure event in \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls}. Since
   \[
      \Phi_Y(u)^{-\rho}\,d\lambda_q(Y,u)
      =
      L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}\alpha u^{\alpha-1}\,du\,d\gamma_t(Y),
   \]
   the displayed pure multiscale value is exactly $\mathcal P_\eta(p,q)$, and \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls} lower-bounds it by $\delta_c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Rank-prefix form of likelihood balls]\label{lem:likelihood-rank-prefix}
   In the notation of \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls}, fix $Y$. Let
   \[
      r_1>r_2>\cdots>r_M>0
   \]
   be the distinct values taken by $L_Y$ on $S$, set $r_{M+1}=0$, and put
   \[
      P_i(Y)=\{x\in S:L_Y(x)\ge r_i\},
      \qquad
      k_i(Y)=|P_i(Y)|.
   \]
   For every $a\ge0$ and every nonnegative functional $G$ of the nonempty
   superlevel set,
   \[
      \int_0^{r_1}\alpha u^{\alpha-1}\Phi_Y(u)^a
      G(C_{Y,u})\,du
      =
      \sum_{i=1}^M
      \left(\frac{k_i(Y)}m\right)^a
      \left(r_i^\alpha-r_{i+1}^\alpha\right)G(P_i(Y)).
   \]
   Consequently, the ideal active process at dyadic scale $\xi$ can be written
   as a Poisson process over score prefixes $(Y,i)$ with
   \[
      \xi/2\le k_i(Y)/m\le\xi
   \]
   and query intensity
   \[
      d\lambda_q(Y,i)
      =
      L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}
      \left(\frac{k_i(Y)}m\right)^\rho
      \left(r_i^\alpha-r_{i+1}^\alpha\right)\,d\gamma_t(Y).
   \]
   The negative-moment contribution of such a prefix is obtained by multiplying
   by $(k_i(Y)/m)^{-\rho}$, so the factor $(k_i(Y)/m)^\rho$ cancels exactly.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For $u\in(r_{i+1},r_i]$, the superlevel set is $C_{Y,u}=P_i(Y)$ and
   $\Phi_Y(u)=k_i(Y)/m$. Integrating $\alpha u^{\alpha-1}$ over this interval
   gives $r_i^\alpha-r_{i+1}^\alpha$. Summing over the intervals proves the
   identity. The Poisson-process statement is the same identity applied to the
   preprocessing intensity
   $\alpha u^{\alpha-1}\Phi_Y(u)^\rho du\,d\gamma_t(Y)$ and then multiplied by
   the query weight $L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Pure-prefix leader quotient]\label{lem:pure-prefix-leader-quotient}
   Fix a dyadic scale \(\xi\in[1/m,1]\).  Let \(\mathcal A\) be a possibly
   implicit label family.  Each label \(a\in\mathcal A\) has a nonempty
   certified set \(C_a\subseteq S\), an analysis mass
   \(\mu_a=|C_a|/m\in[\xi/2,\xi]\), and a reported point
   \(\ell(a)\in C_a\).  For query \(q\), suppose one group activates labels
   according to a Poisson process of intensities \(\lambda_q(a)\), with
   output-sensitive work proportional to the number of distinct reported
   points after labels with the same \(\ell(a)\) are quotient-merged.

   If, for a near pair \((p,q)\),
   \[
      \sum_{\substack{a:\ p\in C_a\\ C_a\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)}}
      \lambda_q(a)\mu_a^{-\rho}\ge h,
      \tag{LQ1}
   \]
   then
   \[
      T=\left\lceil 2^{\rho+1}\xi^{-\rho}h^{-1}\right\rceil
   \]
   independent groups report a valid \(cr\)-neighbor with probability at least
   \(1-e^{-2}\).  Moreover quotienting by the reported point can only decrease
   the active output size relative to listing the labels themselves, and it
   can only decrease any stored metadata that is charged per reported bucket.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let \(\mathcal P\) be the set of pure labels in \((LQ1)\).  Since
   \(\mu_a\ge\xi/2\),
   \[
      \sum_{a\in\mathcal P}\lambda_q(a)
      \ge
      2^{-\rho}\xi^\rho h .
   \]
   Across \(T\) independent groups, the total number of activated pure labels
   is Poisson with mean at least \(2\), so some pure label appears with
   probability at least \(1-e^{-2}\).  If \(a\) is pure, then
   \(\ell(a)\in C_a\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)\), and verification of the reported
   point succeeds.

   Quotienting labels by their reported point replaces each nonempty active
   class by one output record.  Therefore the number of reported records is at
   most the number of active labels.  The same monotonicity holds for any
   retained metadata charged per output bucket.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Likelihood prefixes need only their leader]
   \label{cor:likelihood-prefix-leader}
   In the rank-prefix process of \cref{lem:likelihood-rank-prefix}, fix a score
   direction \(Y\) and let
   \[
      \ell(Y)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x\in S} L_Y(x)
   \]
   with an arbitrary deterministic tie-breaking rule.  Map every prefix
   \(P_i(Y)\) to the singleton reported bucket \(\{\ell(Y)\}\).  Then the pure
   multiscale value in \cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile} is still a
   valid success certificate for ANN: whenever a sampled prefix satisfies
   \(p\in P_i(Y)\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)\), the reported leader \(\ell(Y)\) is
   itself a valid \(cr\)-neighbor of \(q\).

   Consequently, a structured posterior implementation does not have to store
   or output a representative for every dyadic rank prefix.  It may aggregate
   all prefix intensities that report the same leader and sample or list only
   those leader buckets.  The dyadic amplification and moment accounting are
   unchanged, while active candidate verification is charged per reported
   leader rather than per prefix.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   The leader \(\ell(Y)\) belongs to every nonempty prefix \(P_i(Y)\).  Thus if
   \(P_i(Y)\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)\), the leader is also in \(\Ball(q,cr)\).
   Applying \cref{lem:pure-prefix-leader-quotient} at each dyadic scale gives
   the same hit probability as the prefix process.  The query and storage
   moment bounds from \cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile} are properties
   of the underlying prefix intensities; quotienting their reported buckets can
   only reduce the number of reported candidates and bucket records.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Dyadic leader-quotient amplification]
   \label{lem:dyadic-leader-quotient}
   Let \(\mathcal I\) be dyadic scales \(\xi\in[1/m,\eta]\).  At each scale
   \(\xi\), suppose one independent group has a possibly implicit family of
   hidden labels \(a\in\mathcal A_\xi\).  Each label has a nonempty certified
   set \(C_a\subseteq S\), mass
   \[
      \mu_a=|C_a|/m\in[\xi/2,\xi],
   \]
   and reported point \(\ell(a)\in C_a\).  For query \(q\), labels are
   activated by a Poisson process of intensities \(\lambda_q(a)\).  Assume the
   one-group query routine reports the distinct points \(\ell(a)\) appearing
   among activated labels in work \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\) plus the number of
   reported points, and that
   \[
      \sum_{a\in\mathcal A_\xi}\lambda_q(a)\mu_a^\rho
      \le A\xi^\rho
      \qquad(\xi\in\mathcal I).
      \tag{LQA1}
   \]
   Suppose that, for a near pair \((p,q)\), the pure hidden-label value is
   \[
      \sum_{\xi\in\mathcal I}
      \sum_{\substack{a\in\mathcal A_\xi:\\
                      p\in C_a,\ C_a\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)}}
      \lambda_q(a)\mu_a^{-\rho}
      \ge h .
      \tag{LQA2}
   \]
   Repeat scale \(\xi\)
   \[
      T_\xi=\left\lceil2^{\rho+1}\xi^{-\rho}h^{-1}\right\rceil
   \]
   times independently.  Then the query reports a valid \(cr\)-neighbor with
   probability at least \(1-e^{-2}\).  Its total expected number of reported
   points is at most
   \[
      A\,m^\rho h^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   and its decoding work is
   \[
      d\,m^\rho h^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
   The compiler charges no additional per-hidden-label or per-prefix storage:
   the hidden sets are analysis objects, and the stored space is only whatever
   is needed to support the leader reporter for the repeated groups.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Let \(V_\xi\) be the contribution of scale \(\xi\) to the pure value in
   \((LQA2)\), and let
   \[
      \Lambda_\xi
      =
      \sum_{\substack{a\in\mathcal A_\xi:\\
                      p\in C_a,\ C_a\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)}}
      \lambda_q(a)
   \]
   be the one-group intensity of pure labels at that scale.  Since
   \(\mu_a\ge\xi/2\),
   \[
      \Lambda_\xi\ge2^{-\rho}\xi^\rho V_\xi .
   \]
   After \(T_\xi\) independent repetitions at every scale, the total activated
   pure-label count is Poisson with mean at least
   \[
      \sum_{\xi\in\mathcal I}T_\xi\Lambda_\xi
      \ge
      2h^{-1}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal I}V_\xi
      \ge2 .
   \]
   Hence a pure label is activated with probability at least \(1-e^{-2}\).  If
   such a label \(a\) appears, its reported point satisfies
   \[
      \ell(a)\in C_a\subseteq\Ball(q,cr),
   \]
   so metric verification accepts it.

   The expected number of reported points is no larger than the expected number
   of activated labels.  By \((LQA1)\),
   \[
      \sum_{a\in\mathcal A_\xi}\lambda_q(a)
      \le
      (\xi/2)^{-\rho}
      \sum_{a\in\mathcal A_\xi}\lambda_q(a)\mu_a^\rho
      \le
      2^\rho A .
   \]
   Multiplying by \(T_\xi\) and summing the dyadic scales gives
   \[
      O(Ah^{-1})\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal I}\xi^{-\rho}
      \le
      A\,m^\rho h^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m .
   \]
   The fixed decoding overhead satisfies the same dyadic bound with the factor
   \(d\log^{O_c(1)}m\).  Since the output buckets are reported leaders, the
   proof never materializes or stores a record for each hidden label; any
   storage bound must come from the implementation of the leader reporter
   itself.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Poisson leader superposition]
   \label{lem:poisson-leader-superposition}
   Let \(\mathcal A\) be a finite or countable hidden label family.  Each
   label \(a\) has an activation intensity \(\lambda(a)\ge0\), a reported
   point \(\ell(a)\in S\), and optionally an analysis certificate \(C_a\).
   Run independent Poisson clocks of means \(\lambda(a)\), and report the
   distinct points \(\ell(a)\) whose class has at least one activated label.
   For every \(x\in S\), define the aggregate leader intensity
   \[
      \Lambda(x)=\sum_{a:\ell(a)=x}\lambda(a).
   \]
   Then the reported set has exactly the same law as the following quotient
   process: independently for each \(x\), report \(x\) with probability
   \(1-e^{-\Lambda(x)}\).

   Moreover, fix a query \(q\) and a near witness \(p\).  Let
   \[
      G_{p,q}=\{\ell(a):p\in C_a,\ C_a\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)\}.
   \]
   Then every point in \(G_{p,q}\) is a valid \(cr\)-neighbor of \(q\), and the
   success probability of the quotient process is at least
   \[
      1-\exp\!\left(
      -\sum_{x\in G_{p,q}}\Lambda(x)
      \right)
      \ge
      1-\exp\!\left(
      -\sum_{\substack{a:\ p\in C_a\\ C_a\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)}}\lambda(a)
      \right).
   \]
   The same statement holds for measurable label families after replacing
   sums by the corresponding disintegrated intensities over the reported point.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For a fixed point \(x\), the total number of activated labels reporting
   \(x\) is the sum of independent Poisson variables with means
   \(\lambda(a)\) over the class \(\ell(a)=x\).  Hence it is Poisson with mean
   \(\Lambda(x)\).  Disjoint leader classes involve disjoint sets of label
   clocks, so these aggregate Poisson variables are independent.  Reporting
   \(x\) iff its aggregate count is positive gives probability
   \(1-e^{-\Lambda(x)}\).

   If \(x\in G_{p,q}\), then \(x=\ell(a)\in C_a\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)\) for some
   pure certificate \(a\), so \(x\) is valid.  The probability that no valid
   leader in \(G_{p,q}\) is reported is
   \[
      \exp\!\left(-\sum_{x\in G_{p,q}}\Lambda(x)\right).
   \]
   Finally, the aggregate intensity on \(G_{p,q}\) includes the intensity of
   every pure label in the displayed sum, possibly together with additional
   nonpure labels that report the same already-valid leader.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Likelihood leader-intensity law]
   \label{cor:likelihood-leader-intensity}
   In the rank-prefix process of \cref{lem:likelihood-rank-prefix}, write
   \[
      \ell(Y)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x\in S}L_Y(x)
   \]
   with deterministic tie-breaking.  For a dyadic scale \(\xi\), define the
   rank-gap weight
   \[
      B_\xi(Y)
      =
      \sum_{\substack{i:\ \xi/2\le k_i(Y)/m\le\xi}}
      \left(\frac{k_i(Y)}m\right)^\rho
      \bigl(r_i^\alpha-r_{i+1}^\alpha\bigr).
   \]
   After quotienting all prefixes by their leader, the one-group likelihood
   process at scale \(\xi\) is exactly a Poisson reporter on data points with
   aggregate intensity
   \[
      \Lambda_{q,\xi}(x)
      =
      \int_{\{Y:\ell(Y)=x\}}
      L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}B_\xi(Y)\,d\gamma_t(Y).
      \tag{LI}
   \]
   Its expected number of reported leaders is at most the expected number of
   active prefixes and is therefore controlled by the moment bound in
   \cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile}.

   If a hidden prefix \(P_i(Y)\) is pure for \((p,q)\), meaning
   \(p\in P_i(Y)\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)\), then its leader contribution in
   \((LI)\) reports a valid point.  Thus the multiscale posterior target can be
   stated entirely as a leader-intensity reporting problem: implement the
   point measures \(\Lambda_{q,\xi}\) output-sensitively, with the hidden
   rank-prefixes used only to certify the intensity and the near-pair pure
   value.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   For fixed \(Y\), every prefix \(P_i(Y)\) has the same reported point
   \(\ell(Y)\).  Summing the prefix intensities from
   \cref{lem:likelihood-rank-prefix} over the scale window gives precisely
   \(L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}B_\xi(Y)d\gamma_t(Y)\).  Disintegrating this measure by
   the value of \(\ell(Y)\) gives \((LI)\), and
   \cref{lem:poisson-leader-superposition} proves equality of the quotient
   reporter law.

   Quotienting can only merge prefix outputs, so the expected leader count is
   no larger than the expected active-prefix count.  The latter is bounded by
   \((\xi/2)^{-\rho}\) times the active \(\rho\)-moment from
   \cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile}.  If
   \(p\in P_i(Y)\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)\), then
   \(\ell(Y)\in P_i(Y)\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)\), so the reported leader is valid.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Poissonizing a normalized leader sampler]
   \label{lem:poissonized-normalized-leader-sampler}
   Let \(\mathcal A\) be a finite or countable hidden label family.  Each
   label \(a\) has a leader \(\ell(a)\in S\) and a query intensity
   \(\lambda_q(a)\ge0\).  Put
   \[
      W_q=\sum_{a\in\mathcal A}\lambda_q(a)<\infty .
   \]
   Suppose a query routine can compute \(W_q\) and, when \(W_q>0\), sample
   \(a\) from the normalized law
   \[
      \nu_q(a)=\lambda_q(a)/W_q .
   \]
   The following procedure reports exactly the same leader set as independent
   Poisson clocks of means \(\lambda_q(a)\), after quotienting labels by their
   leader:
   draw \(N\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(W_q)\), draw
   \(a_1,\ldots,a_N\) independently from \(\nu_q\), and report the distinct
   leaders among \(\ell(a_1),\ldots,\ell(a_N)\).

   In particular, if one normalized sample costs \(D_q\) arithmetic operations
   and computing \(W_q\) costs \(D_q^0\), the expected work is
   \(D_q^0+W_qD_q+O(W_q)\) plus candidate verification, and the expected number
   of reported leaders is at most \(W_q\).  The same statement holds for a
   measurable label space by replacing the sums by the corresponding finite
   intensity measure.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Conditioned on \(N\), the sampled labels are iid from \(\nu_q\).  By the
   Poisson splitting identity, the number of times a fixed label \(a\) appears
   is an independent Poisson random variable with mean
   \(W_q\nu_q(a)=\lambda_q(a)\), and the counts for disjoint label classes are
   independent.  Thus the sampled multiset has the same law as the independent
   label clocks.  Applying \cref{lem:poisson-leader-superposition} gives the
   quotient leader law.  The work and output estimates follow from
   \(\E N=W_q\) and from the fact that quotienting can only reduce the number
   of outputs.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Ideal likelihood leader-law certificate]
   \label{lem:ideal-likelihood-leader-law}
   Work in the notation of \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls},
   \cref{lem:likelihood-rank-prefix}, and
   \cref{cor:likelihood-leader-intensity}.  For a dyadic scale \(\xi\), let
   \[
      W_{q,\xi}=\sum_{x\in S}\Lambda_{q,\xi}(x)
      =
      \int L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}B_\xi(Y)\,d\gamma_t(Y)
   \]
   be the total leader intensity.  Then, uniformly over the data set, query,
   and scale,
   \[
      W_{q,\xi}\le C_c .
      \tag{ILL1}
   \]

   Moreover, choose
   \[
      \alpha=\kappa/\log m,
      \qquad
      t=C_c\log m/r^2
   \]
   as in \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls}, and let
   \(\eta\in[1/m,1]\).  If \(\|p-q\|\le r\) and fewer than \(\eta m/2\)
   resident points lie in \(\Ball(q,cr)\), then the ideal leader law has
   constant scale-weighted valid intensity:
   \[
      \sum_{\xi\le\eta}\xi^{-\rho}
      \int_{\substack{Y,i:\ \xi/2\le k_i(Y)/m\le\xi\\
                      p\in P_i(Y)\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)}}
      L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}
      \left(\frac{k_i(Y)}m\right)^\rho
      \bigl(r_i^\alpha-r_{i+1}^\alpha\bigr)\,d\gamma_t(Y)
      \ge c_c .
      \tag{ILL2}
   \]
   Every hidden label counted in \((ILL2)\) reports a leader in
   \(\Ball(q,cr)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The identity for \(W_{q,\xi}\) is \((LI)\) summed over leaders.  Before the
   quotient, it is exactly the total active-prefix intensity at scale \(\xi\).
   Since every prefix on this scale has mass
   \(\mu_i(Y)=k_i(Y)/m\ge\xi/2\),
   \[
      W_{q,\xi}
      =
      \int_{\xi/2\le\mu_i(Y)\le\xi}d\lambda_q(Y,i)
      \le
      (\xi/2)^{-\rho}
      \int_{\xi/2\le\mu_i(Y)\le\xi}\mu_i(Y)^\rho\,d\lambda_q(Y,i).
   \]
   The last integral is the active \(\rho\)-moment bounded by
   \cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile}; hence \((ILL1)\), after
   absorbing the fixed dependence on \(c,\rho,\alpha\) into \(C_c\).

   Let \(V_\xi\) be the pure negative-moment contribution at scale \(\xi\):
   \[
      V_\xi=
      \int_{\substack{Y,i:\ \xi/2\le\mu_i(Y)\le\xi\\
                      p\in P_i(Y)\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)}}
      L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}
      \bigl(r_i^\alpha-r_{i+1}^\alpha\bigr)\,d\gamma_t(Y).
   \]
   By \cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile} and
   \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls},
   \(\sum_{\xi\le\eta}V_\xi\ge\delta_c\).  On the scale \(\xi\),
   \(\mu_i(Y)^\rho\ge(\xi/2)^\rho\), so the contribution of the same pure
   labels to the left-hand side of \((ILL2)\) is at least
   \(2^{-\rho}V_\xi\).  Summing over scales gives
   \(c_c=2^{-\rho}\delta_c\).  If the counted prefix is pure, then its leader
   belongs to \(P_i(Y)\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Scale-amplified likelihood collapses to max-tilted leaders]
   \label{lem:scale-amplified-alpha-leader}
   In the notation of \cref{lem:likelihood-rank-prefix}, let
   \[
      M_Y=\max_{x\in S}L_Y(x),
      \qquad
      \ell(Y)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x\in S}L_Y(x)
   \]
   with deterministic tie-breaking.  Let the dyadic scale windows
   \([\xi/2,\xi]\), \(\xi\in[1/m,1]\), partition the possible prefix masses.
   Then, for every fixed \(Y\),
   \[
      2^{-\rho}M_Y^\alpha
      \le
      \sum_{\xi}\xi^{-\rho}B_\xi(Y)
      \le
      M_Y^\alpha .
      \tag{AL1}
   \]
   Thus the scale-amplified quotient posterior process is, up to constants
   depending only on \(\rho\), the max-tilted leader process with point
   intensities
   \[
      \widetilde\Lambda_q(x)
      =
      \int_{\{\ell(Y)=x\}}
      L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}M_Y^\alpha\,d\gamma_t(Y).
      \tag{AL2}
   \]

   If
   \[
      \alpha=\kappa/\log m,\qquad
      t=C_c\log m/r^2
   \]
   with \(\kappa=\kappa(c)\) sufficiently small and \(C_c=C_c(c)\)
   sufficiently large, then the total mass of \((AL2)\) is bounded by a
   constant:
   \[
      \widetilde W_q=\sum_x\widetilde\Lambda_q(x)
      =
      \int L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}M_Y^\alpha\,d\gamma_t(Y)
      \le C_c .
      \tag{AL3}
   \]
   Moreover, for every near pair \(\|p-q\|\le r\),
   \[
      \sum_{x\in S\cap\Ball(q,cr)}\widetilde\Lambda_q(x)\ge c_c .
      \tag{AL4}
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   For fixed \(Y\), write
   \(\mu_i(Y)=k_i(Y)/m\) and
   \(\Delta_i(Y)=r_i^\alpha-r_{i+1}^\alpha\).  Each rank prefix belongs to a
   unique dyadic window \([\xi/2,\xi]\), and on that window
   \[
      2^{-\rho}\le \xi^{-\rho}\mu_i(Y)^\rho\le1 .
   \]
   Let \(\xi(i)\) denote this unique dyadic scale for prefix \(i\).  Therefore
   \[
      \sum_{\xi}\xi^{-\rho}B_\xi(Y)
      =
      \sum_i \xi(i)^{-\rho}\mu_i(Y)^\rho\Delta_i(Y)
   \]
   lies between \(2^{-\rho}\sum_i\Delta_i(Y)\) and
   \(\sum_i\Delta_i(Y)\).  The latter sum telescopes to
   \(r_1^\alpha=M_Y^\alpha\), proving \((AL1)\).  Since every prefix reports
   the same leader \(\ell(Y)\), multiplying the scale-\(\xi\) process by
   \(\xi^{-\rho}\) and summing over scales gives \((AL2)\) up to the constants
   in \((AL1)\).

   For the total mass, put \(Z_Y=m^{-1}\sum_{x\in S}L_Y(x)\).  Since
   \(M_Y\le mZ_Y\),
   \[
      M_Y^\alpha\le m^\alpha Z_Y^\alpha .
   \]
   The Jensen/affinity calculation in
   \cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile} gives
   \[
      \int L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}Z_Y^\alpha\,d\gamma_t(Y)\le1.
   \]
   Because \(m^\alpha=e^\kappa\), this proves \((AL3)\).

   For the valid mass, use the tilted probability measure proportional to
   \[
      L_Y(p)^\alpha L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}\,d\gamma_t(Y).
   \]
   Its normalizing constant is
   \[
      A_{p,q}
      =
      \exp\!\left(-\frac{\alpha s_\alpha t}{2}\|p-q\|^2\right)
      \ge c_c
   \]
   for the displayed choice of \(\alpha,t\).  The same Gaussian margin
   calculation as in \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls} shows that, after
   increasing \(C_c\), with tilted probability at least \(1-O(m^{-2})\) every
   invalid point \(z\notin\Ball(q,cr)\) satisfies \(L_Y(z)<L_Y(p)\).  On this
   event the leader \(\ell(Y)\) is valid and \(M_Y^\alpha\ge L_Y(p)^\alpha\).
   Hence
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      \sum_{x\in S\cap\Ball(q,cr)}\widetilde\Lambda_q(x)
      &=
      \int L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}M_Y^\alpha
      \mathbf 1_{\{\ell(Y)\in\Ball(q,cr)\}}\,d\gamma_t(Y)\\
      &\ge
      A_{p,q}(1-O(m^{-2}))
      \ge c_c ,
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   after adjusting the constant.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Voronoi-capture form of the max-tilted leader law]
   \label{lem:alpha-leader-voronoi-capture}
   Work in the notation of \cref{lem:scale-amplified-alpha-leader}, and let
   \(s_\alpha=1-\alpha\).  For a point \(x\in S\), let
   \[
      \mathcal V_x
      =
      \{Y:\ell(Y)=x\}
   \]
   be its score-leader cell, using the same deterministic tie-breaking as
   before.  Then the max-tilted leader intensity has the exact form
   \[
      \widetilde\Lambda_q(x)
      =
      \int_{\mathcal V_x}
      L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}L_Y(x)^\alpha\,d\gamma_t(Y)
      \tag{VC1}
   \]
   and, after writing \(Z=Y/t\),
   \[
      \widetilde\Lambda_q(x)
      =
      \exp\!\left(
         -\frac{\alpha s_\alpha t}{2}\|x-q\|^2
      \right)
      \Pr\!\left[
         x=\operatorname{NN}_S(Z)
      \right],
      \tag{VC2}
   \]
   where
   \[
      Z\sim
      N\!\left(s_\alpha q+\alpha x,\frac1t I_d\right)
   \]
   and \(\operatorname{NN}_S\) denotes exact nearest neighbor in \(S\) with the
   same tie-breaking.  Consequently,
   \[
      \widetilde\Lambda_q(x)
      \le
      \exp\!\left(
         -\frac{\alpha s_\alpha t}{2}\|x-q\|^2
      \right).
      \tag{VC3}
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   On the cell \(\mathcal V_x\), the maximum likelihood is \(M_Y=L_Y(x)\).
   Substituting this into \((AL2)\) gives \((VC1)\).

   The Gaussian affinity identity used in
   \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls} gives, for every measurable event
   \(E\),
   \[
      \int_E L_Y(q)^{s_\alpha}L_Y(x)^\alpha\,d\gamma_t(Y)
      =
      \exp\!\left(
         -\frac{\alpha s_\alpha t}{2}\|x-q\|^2
      \right)
      \Pr_{Y\sim N(t(s_\alpha q+\alpha x),tI_d)}[Y\in E].
   \]
   Apply this with \(E=\mathcal V_x\).  Finally,
   \[
      L_Y(z)
      =
      \exp\!\left(
         \frac{t}{2}\|Y/t\|^2
         -\frac{t}{2}\|z-Y/t\|^2
      \right),
   \]
   so maximizing \(L_Y(z)\) over \(z\in S\) is exactly nearest-neighbor
   search for \(Z=Y/t\) with the same tie-breaking.  This proves \((VC2)\).
   The upper bound \((VC3)\) follows because the probability in \((VC2)\) is
   at most one.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Query-centered nearest-cell reweighting]
   \label{cor:query-centered-cell-reweighting}
   Suppose the localized node lies in dimension \(D\).  Let
   \[
      H\ge\max\{D,\log m,2\},\qquad
      \alpha=\kappa/H,\qquad
      t=C H/r^2 ,
      \tag{QC0}
   \]
   where \(\kappa=\kappa(c)>0\) is sufficiently small and
   \(C=C(c)>0\) is sufficiently large.  Then the total-mass and valid-mass
   conclusions \((AL3)\)--\((AL4)\) of
   \cref{lem:scale-amplified-alpha-leader} remain true for the max-tilted law
   \((AL2)\) with these parameters.

   Let \(Z\sim N(q,I_D/t)\), and let
   \[
      N_S(Z)=\operatorname{NN}_S(Z)
   \]
   be the exact nearest resident point with the fixed tie-breaking.  Define
   \[
      R_q(Z)
      =
      \exp\!\left(
         \frac{\alpha t}{2}
         \bigl(\|Z-q\|^2-\|Z-N_S(Z)\|^2\bigr)
      \right).
      \tag{QC1}
   \]
   For every \(A\subseteq S\),
   \[
      \sum_{x\in A}\widetilde\Lambda_q(x)
      =
      \E_{Z\sim N(q,I_D/t)}
      \left[
         \mathbf 1_{\{N_S(Z)\in A\}}R_q(Z)
      \right].
      \tag{QC2}
   \]
   Moreover, for every \(L\ge0\),
   \[
      \E\left[
         R_q(Z)\mathbf 1_{\{\log R_q(Z)>L\}}
      \right]
      \le
      e^{2\kappa}e^{-L}.
      \tag{QC3}
   \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Reinspect the proofs of \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls} and
   \cref{lem:scale-amplified-alpha-leader}.  They use
   \(\alpha t r^2=\Theta_c(1)\), \(t r^2\ge C_c\log m\), and
   \(m^\alpha=O_c(1)\).  The parameter choice \((QC0)\) has all three
   properties: \(\alpha t r^2=\kappa C\),
   \(t r^2\ge C\log m\), and \(m^\alpha\le e^\kappa\).  Taking \(C\) large
   enough for the Gaussian separation tail and \(\kappa\) small enough for the
   affinity constants therefore gives \((AL3)\)--\((AL4)\) with adjusted
   constants.

   To prove \((QC2)\), use \((VC2)\) and compare the Gaussian law
   \(N(q+\alpha(x-q),I_D/t)\) with \(N(q,I_D/t)\).  For
   \(u=x-q\), the density ratio at \(Z\) is
   \[
      \exp\!\left(
         \alpha t\langle Z-q,u\rangle
         -\frac{\alpha^2t}{2}\|u\|^2
      \right).
   \]
   Multiplying by the affinity factor
   \(\exp(-\alpha(1-\alpha)t\|u\|^2/2)\) from \((VC2)\) gives
   \[
      \exp\!\left(
         \alpha t\langle Z-q,u\rangle
         -\frac{\alpha t}{2}\|u\|^2
      \right)
      =
      \exp\!\left(
         \frac{\alpha t}{2}
         (\|Z-q\|^2-\|Z-x\|^2)
      \right).
   \]
   On the event \(N_S(Z)=x\), this is exactly \(R_q(Z)\).  Summing over
   \(x\in A\) proves \((QC2)\).

   Finally,
   \[
      \log R_q(Z)
      \le
      \frac{\alpha t}{2}\|Z-q\|^2
      =
      \frac{\alpha}{2}\chi_D^2 ,
   \]
   where \(\chi_D^2\) denotes a chi-square random variable with \(D\) degrees
   of freedom.  Since
   \[
      R_q(Z)\mathbf 1_{\{\log R_q(Z)>L\}}
      \le e^{-L}R_q(Z)^2 ,
   \]
   we get
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      \E\left[R_q(Z)\mathbf 1_{\{\log R_q(Z)>L\}}\right]
      &\le
      e^{-L}
      \E\exp\!\left(\alpha\chi_D^2\right)\\
      &=
      e^{-L}(1-2\alpha)^{-D/2}
      \le
      e^{2\kappa}e^{-L},
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   after decreasing \(\kappa\) so that \(\alpha\le1/4\), and using
   \(\alpha D\le\kappa\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Truncated nearest-cell sampler compiler]
   \label{cor:truncated-nearest-cell-compiler}
   Work in the setting and with the dimension-aware parameters of
   \cref{cor:query-centered-cell-reweighting}.  Suppose a retained query
   primitive can return \(N_S(Z)\) exactly for any query point \(Z\), with
   query work \(T_{\rm cell}(m)\) and retained space \(S_{\rm cell}(m)\).
   Let \(B=\log^{A_c}m\), with \(A_c\) large enough.  One trial of the
   following rejection procedure costs \(T_{\rm cell}(m)+O(D)\):
   sample \(Z\sim N(q,I_D/t)\), compute \(x=N_S(Z)\), put
   \[
      \Delta(Z)=
      \frac{\alpha t}{2}
      \bigl(\|Z-q\|^2-\|Z-x\|^2\bigr),
   \]
   and accept \(x\) with probability \(e^{\Delta(Z)}/B\) if
   \(\Delta(Z)\le\log B\), rejecting otherwise.

   If a near pair exists in the localized node, then
   \(O_c(B)\) independent trials, followed by ordinary metric verification of
   accepted points, return a valid \(cr\)-neighbor with constant probability.
   The query work is
   \[
      O_c\!\left(B(T_{\rm cell}(m)+D)\right)
   \]
   and the retained space is \(S_{\rm cell}(m)+dm\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   By \((QC2)\), the untruncated acceptance numerator over any set
   \(A\subseteq S\) is exactly \(\sum_{x\in A}\widetilde\Lambda_q(x)\).  The
   truncation discards only the mass where \(\log R_q(Z)>\log B\).  By
   \((QC3)\), the total discarded mass is at most \(e^{2\kappa}/B\).  Taking
   \(A_c\) large makes this less than half of the valid mass lower bound
   \(c_c\) in \((AL4)\).  Thus one trial accepts a valid point with probability
   at least
   \[
      \frac{c_c/2}{B}.
   \]
   Repeating \(O_c(B)\) times gives constant probability of at least one
   accepted valid point.  Every accepted point is verified in the original
   metric, so invalid accepted points do not cause false positives.  The work
   and space bounds are immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Smoothed witness-list compiler]
   \label{lem:smoothed-witness-list-compiler}
   Work in the setting and with the dimension-aware parameters of
   \cref{cor:query-centered-cell-reweighting}.  Fix a query \(q\), a near
   witness \(p\in S\cap\Ball(q,r)\), and a retained candidate-list routine
   which, on input \((q,Z)\), returns a set
   \[
      \mathcal L(q,Z)\subseteq S .
   \]
   Put
   \[
      R_{p,q}(Z)
      =
      \exp\!\left(
         \frac{\alpha t}{2}
         \bigl(\|Z-q\|^2-\|Z-p\|^2\bigr)
      \right),
      \qquad Z\sim N(q,I_D/t).
      \tag{WL0}
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \E R_{p,q}(Z)
      =
      \exp\!\left(
         -\frac{\alpha(1-\alpha)t}{2}\|p-q\|^2
      \right)
      \ge a_c>0 ,
      \tag{WL1}
   \]
   where \(a_c\) depends only on \(c\).  Suppose further that the list routine
   has tilted witness loss
   \[
      \E\left[
         R_{p,q}(Z)\mathbf 1_{\{p\notin\mathcal L(q,Z)\}}
      \right]
      \le a_c/4 .
      \tag{WL2}
   \]
   Let \(B=\log^{A_c}m\), with \(A_c\) large enough.  One trial of the
   following procedure succeeds with probability at least
   \(\Omega_c(1/B)\): sample \(Z\sim N(q,I_D/t)\), form
   \(\mathcal L(q,Z)\), verify all listed candidates in the original metric,
   and for every verified valid \(x\in\mathcal L(q,Z)\cap\Ball(q,cr)\) define
   \[
      \Delta_x(Z)=
      \frac{\alpha t}{2}
      \bigl(\|Z-q\|^2-\|Z-x\|^2\bigr).
   \]
   Let
   \[
      W_{\rm val}(Z)
      =
      \sum_{\substack{x\in\mathcal L(q,Z)\cap\Ball(q,cr)\\
                      \Delta_x(Z)\le\log B}}
      e^{\Delta_x(Z)} .
   \]
   Accept with probability \(\min\{1,W_{\rm val}(Z)/B\}\); conditional on
   accepting, return a listed valid point with probability proportional to
   \(e^{\Delta_x(Z)}\) over the capped valid candidates.  Thus
   \(O_c(B)\) independent trials return a valid \(cr\)-neighbor with constant
   probability.  The query work is \(O_c(B)\) times the cost of producing and
   verifying one list.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The identity \((WL1)\) is the same Gaussian affinity calculation used above:
   multiplying the density of \(N(q,I_D/t)\) by \(R_{p,q}\) gives the density
   of \(N((1-\alpha)q+\alpha p,I_D/t)\), times the normalizing factor
   displayed in \((WL1)\).  Since \(\|p-q\|\le r\) and
   \(\alpha t r^2=\Theta_c(1)\), this factor is bounded below by a positive
   constant \(a_c\).

   As in the proof of \((QC3)\),
   \[
      \Delta_p(Z)
      \le
      \frac{\alpha t}{2}\|Z-q\|^2
      =
      \frac{\alpha}{2}\chi_D^2 ,
   \]
   and therefore
   \[
      \E\left[
         R_{p,q}(Z)\mathbf 1_{\{\Delta_p(Z)>\log B\}}
      \right]
      \le e^{2\kappa}/B .
      \tag{WL3}
   \]
   On the event \(p\in\mathcal L(q,Z)\) and \(\Delta_p(Z)\le\log B\), the
   candidate \(p\) is valid and contributes \(e^{\Delta_p(Z)}\) to
   \(W_{\rm val}(Z)\).  Because \(e^{\Delta_p(Z)}\le B\),
   \[
      \min\{1,W_{\rm val}(Z)/B\}
      \ge
      B^{-1}R_{p,q}(Z)
      \mathbf 1_{\{p\in\mathcal L(q,Z),\,\Delta_p(Z)\le\log B\}} .
   \]
   Taking expectations and using \((WL1)\)--\((WL3)\) gives one-trial success
   probability at least
   \[
      \frac1B\left(a_c-a_c/4-e^{2\kappa}/B\right)
      =
      \Omega_c(1/B),
   \]
   after choosing \(A_c\) large.  The returned point is explicitly verified,
   so false positives cannot occur.  Independent repetition gives constant
   success probability.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Facility-bucket witness form]
   \label{cor:facility-bucket-witness}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:smoothed-witness-list-compiler}, suppose the
   retained structure consists of bucket sets \(\{S_a:a\in\mathcal A\}\) and a
   query reporter which, on input \((q,Z)\), outputs labels
   \(\mathcal A(q,Z)\subseteq\mathcal A\).  Let
   \[
      \mathcal L(q,Z)=\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal A(q,Z)}S_a .
   \]
   If, for every near pair \(p,q\),
   \[
      \E\left[
         R_{p,q}(Z)
         \mathbf 1_{\{p\notin\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal A(q,Z)}S_a\}}
      \right]
      \le a_c/4,
      \tag{FB1}
   \]
   and the reported bucket union has expected scan size
   \(m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m\), then \(O_c(\log^{A_c}m)\) trials of the bucket
   procedure return a valid neighbor with constant probability using expected
   scan work \(D\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m\), up to the cost of reporting the
   labels.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply \cref{lem:smoothed-witness-list-compiler} to the candidate list
   \(\mathcal L(q,Z)\).  The scan-size assumption bounds the verification work,
   and \((FB1)\) is exactly the tilted witness-loss condition \((WL2)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Poisson multi-assignment rate coverage]
   \label{lem:poisson-rate-coverage}
   Work in the setting of \cref{lem:smoothed-witness-list-compiler}.  Let
   \(\mathcal A\) be a finite label universe.  For every point \(x\in S\), let
   \(\mu_x\) be a probability distribution on \(\mathcal A\), and let
   \(a_x\ge0\) be its replication budget.  During preprocessing, draw a
   Poisson number \(N_x\) of labels with mean \(a_x\), independently over
   points, and place \(x\) into each sampled label bucket.  A query reporter,
   on input \((q,Z)\), returns a set of labels
   \(\mathcal A(q,Z)\subseteq\mathcal A\), and the candidate list is the union
   of the reported buckets.

   For a label set \(B\subseteq\mathcal A\), write
   \[
      \mu_x(B)=\sum_{a\in B}\mu_x(a).
   \]
   Conditional on \(\mathcal A(q,Z)=B\), the probability over preprocessing
   that a fixed point \(p\) is absent from the reported bucket union is exactly
   \[
      \exp(-a_p\mu_p(B)).
      \tag{RC0}
   \]
   Consequently, if for every near pair \(p,q\),
   \[
      \E_{Z\sim N(q,I_D/t)}
      \left[
         R_{p,q}(Z)
         \exp(-a_p\mu_p(\mathcal A(q,Z)))
      \right]
      \le a_c/4 ,
      \tag{RC1}
   \]
   then the randomized bucket structure satisfies the tilted witness-loss
   condition \((WL2)\) in expectation over preprocessing.  If in addition, for
   every query \(q\),
   \[
      \E_{Z\sim N(q,I_D/t)}
      \sum_{x\in S}a_x\mu_x(\mathcal A(q,Z))
      \le
      m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m ,
      \tag{RC2}
   \]
   then the expected reported incidence count per trial is
   \(m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m\).  The expected stored incidence count is
   \[
      \sum_{x\in S}a_x .
      \tag{RC3}
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Poisson thinning gives \((RC0)\): among the \(N_p\) independent labels drawn
   for \(p\), the number landing in \(B\) is Poisson with mean
   \(a_p\mu_p(B)\).  Thus no copy of \(p\) appears in a reported bucket with
   probability \(\exp(-a_p\mu_p(B))\).

   Multiplying this conditional miss probability by the tilted weight
   \(R_{p,q}(Z)\) and averaging over \(Z\) gives \((RC1)\), which is exactly the
   expectation form of \((WL2)\).  For the load, a point \(x\) contributes to
   the reported incidence count with expected multiplicity
   \(a_x\mu_x(\mathcal A(q,Z))\) conditional on \(Z\).  Summing over points and
   averaging gives \((RC2)\).  Finally, the expected total number of stored
   incidences is the sum of the Poisson means, proving \((RC3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Intensity form of rate coverage]
   \label{cor:rate-coverage-intensity}
   The normalized laws \(\mu_x\) in \cref{lem:poisson-rate-coverage} are only a
   parametrization.  Equivalently, assign a nonnegative intensity
   \(b_{x,a}\ge0\) to every point-label pair and, during preprocessing, place
   \(x\) into bucket \(a\) with independent Poisson multiplicity of mean
   \(b_{x,a}\).  For \(B\subseteq\mathcal A\), write
   \[
      b_x(B)=\sum_{a\in B}b_{x,a}.
   \]
   Then the miss probability for \(p\) from reported labels \(B\) is
   \[
      \exp(-b_p(B)),
      \tag{RI0}
   \]
   the expected reported incidence count for a query outcome \(B\) is
   \[
      \sum_{x\in S}b_x(B),
      \tag{RI1}
   \]
   and the expected stored incidence count is
   \[
      \sum_{x\in S}\sum_{a\in\mathcal A}b_{x,a}.
      \tag{RI2}
   \]
   Thus the rate-coverage conditions are exactly
   \[
      \E_Z\left[
         R_{p,q}(Z)\exp(-b_p(\mathcal A(q,Z)))
      \right]\le a_c/4
      \tag{RI3}
   \]
   for every near pair \(p,q\), and
   \[
      \E_Z
      \sum_{x\in S}b_x(\mathcal A(q,Z))
      \le m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \tag{RI4}
   \]
   for every promised query \(q\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Apply Poisson thinning independently to each point-label pair.  The total
   number of copies of \(p\) whose labels fall in \(B\) is Poisson with mean
   \(b_p(B)\), giving \((RI0)\).  Linearity of expectation gives
   \((RI1)\) and \((RI2)\).  The displayed conditions are \((RC1)\)--\((RC2)\)
   after writing \(b_{x,a}=a_x\mu_x(a)\), with the convention that points with
   \(a_x=0\) have arbitrary \(\mu_x\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite-signature rate-coverage envelope]
   \label{lem:finite-signature-rate-coverage}
   Let \(\mathcal Q\) be an arbitrary, possibly continuous, query domain for
   one localized node, and let \(\Lambda\) be a finite label universe.  A
   retained reporter maps each query and random outcome to a label set
   \[
      B(q,\omega)=A(q,\omega)\subseteq\Lambda .
   \]
   All probabilities and expectations below are with respect to the reporter
   outcome law for the queried point \(q\).
   Suppose preprocessing supplies a finite signature set \(\Sigma\) and a map
   \(\sigma:\mathcal Q\to\Sigma\).  For each signature \(\sigma_0\), let
   \(N_{\sigma_0}\subseteq S\) contain every possible near witness for queries
   of that signature:
   \[
      \{p\in S:\exists q\in\mathcal Q,\ \sigma(q)=\sigma_0,\ \|p-q\|\le r\}
      \subseteq N_{\sigma_0}.
      \tag{FS1}
   \]
   Assume that for every signature \(\sigma_0\), every label set
   \(B\subseteq\Lambda\), and every \(p\in N_{\sigma_0}\), preprocessing has
   certified finite upper envelopes
   \[
      U_{p,\sigma_0,B}
      \ge
      \E_{\omega}\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)\,
         \mathbf 1\{B(q,\omega)=B\}
      \right]
      \tag{FS2}
   \]
   for all \(q\in\mathcal Q\) with \(\sigma(q)=\sigma_0\) and
   \(\|p-q\|\le r\), and
   \[
      V_{\sigma_0,B}
      \ge
      \Pr_{\omega}[B(q,\omega)=B]
      \tag{FS3}
   \]
   for all \(q\in\mathcal Q\) with \(\sigma(q)=\sigma_0\).  Let
   \(b_{x,a}\ge0\) be point-label intensities and write
   \(b_x(B)=\sum_{a\in B}b_{x,a}\).  If the finite inequalities
   \[
      \sum_{B\subseteq\Lambda}
      U_{p,\sigma_0,B}\exp(-b_p(B))
      \le \tau
      \qquad(\sigma_0\in\Sigma,\ p\in N_{\sigma_0})
      \tag{FS4}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{B\subseteq\Lambda}
      V_{\sigma_0,B}
      \sum_{x\in S}b_x(B)
      \le \ell
      \qquad(\sigma_0\in\Sigma)
      \tag{FS5}
   \]
   hold, then the continuous-query rate-coverage conditions
   \[
      \E_{\omega}\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)\exp(-b_p(B(q,\omega)))
      \right]\le\tau
      \tag{FS6}
   \]
   for every promised near pair \(\|p-q\|\le r\), and
   \[
      \E_{\omega}\sum_{x\in S}b_x(B(q,\omega))\le\ell
      \tag{FS7}
   \]
   for every \(q\in\mathcal Q\), both hold.  The storage bound remains
   \(\sum_{x,a}b_{x,a}\).

   Thus a continuous query domain can be certified without a metric net: it is
   enough to enumerate or decode finitely many reporter signatures and to prove
   finite upper envelopes for their tilted miss and load coefficients.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Fix a promised near pair \((p,q)\), and put
   \(\sigma_0=\sigma(q)\).  By \((FS1)\), \(p\in N_{\sigma_0}\).  Decompose the
   expectation according to the finite label set \(B(q,\omega)\):
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      \E_\omega\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)e^{-b_p(B(q,\omega))}
      \right]
      &=
      \sum_{B\subseteq\Lambda}
      e^{-b_p(B)}
      \E_\omega\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)\mathbf 1\{B(q,\omega)=B\}
      \right]  \\
      &\le
      \sum_{B\subseteq\Lambda}
      U_{p,\sigma_0,B}e^{-b_p(B)}
      \le \tau .
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   This proves \((FS6)\).  The load bound is identical:
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      \E_\omega\sum_xb_x(B(q,\omega))
      &=
      \sum_{B\subseteq\Lambda}
      \Pr[B(q,\omega)=B]\sum_xb_x(B)\\
      &\le
      \sum_{B\subseteq\Lambda}
      V_{\sigma_0,B}\sum_xb_x(B)
      \le\ell .
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   Poisson materialization gives the same stored incidence count as in
   \cref{cor:rate-coverage-intensity}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Finite decoder-transcript rate-coverage envelope]
   \label{cor:finite-transcript-rate-coverage}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:finite-signature-rate-coverage}, suppose the
   retained reporter is implemented by a finite transcript decoder.  That is,
   for each query signature \(\sigma_0\) there is a finite transcript set
   \(\mathcal Z_{\sigma_0}\), and the reporter maps each outcome to
   \[
      \zeta(q,\omega)\in\mathcal Z_{\sigma(q)} .
   \]
   For each transcript \(\zeta\in\mathcal Z_{\sigma_0}\), the decoder reports
   a fixed label set \(B_{\sigma_0,\zeta}\subseteq\Lambda\) and spends at most
   \(d_{\sigma_0,\zeta}\ge0\) extra work.  Assume preprocessing certifies
   \[
      U_{p,\sigma_0,\zeta}
      \ge
      \E_\omega\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)\,
         \mathbf 1\{\zeta(q,\omega)=\zeta\}
      \right]
      \tag{DT1}
   \]
   for every \(q\) of signature \(\sigma_0\) with \(\|p-q\|\le r\), and
   \[
      V_{\sigma_0,\zeta}
      \ge
      \Pr_\omega[\zeta(q,\omega)=\zeta]
      \tag{DT2}
   \]
   for every \(q\) of signature \(\sigma_0\).  If
   \[
      \sum_{\zeta\in\mathcal Z_{\sigma_0}}
      U_{p,\sigma_0,\zeta}
      \exp\!\bigl(-b_p(B_{\sigma_0,\zeta})\bigr)
      \le \tau
      \qquad(\sigma_0\in\Sigma,\ p\in N_{\sigma_0})
      \tag{DT3}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{\zeta\in\mathcal Z_{\sigma_0}}
      V_{\sigma_0,\zeta}
      \left(
         d_{\sigma_0,\zeta}
         +\sum_{x\in S}b_x(B_{\sigma_0,\zeta})
      \right)
      \le \ell
      \qquad(\sigma_0\in\Sigma),
      \tag{DT4}
   \]
   then the tilted miss bound \((FS6)\) holds and the expected reporter-plus-scan
   work is at most \(\ell\) for every query.

   Thus a code, trellis, polar, or lattice quotient need not expose all
   subsets of the label universe.  It is enough that its possible decoder
   transcripts are finite or compactly decodable, and that preprocessing proves
   transcript-level envelopes.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Fix a promised near pair \((p,q)\), and put \(\sigma_0=\sigma(q)\).  By
   \((FS1)\), \(p\in N_{\sigma_0}\).  Decomposing over decoder transcripts
   gives
   \[
      \E_\omega\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)
         e^{-b_p(B_{\sigma_0,\zeta(q,\omega)})}
      \right]
      \le
      \sum_{\zeta\in\mathcal Z_{\sigma_0}}
      U_{p,\sigma_0,\zeta}
      e^{-b_p(B_{\sigma_0,\zeta})}
      \le\tau .
   \]
   For an arbitrary query \(q\) with signature \(\sigma_0=\sigma(q)\), the
   expected reporter-plus-scan work is bounded by
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      &\E_\omega\!\left[
         d_{\sigma_0,\zeta(q,\omega)}
         +\sum_xb_x(B_{\sigma_0,\zeta(q,\omega)})
      \right]  \\
      &\qquad\le
      \sum_{\zeta\in\mathcal Z_{\sigma_0}}
      V_{\sigma_0,\zeta}
      \left(
         d_{\sigma_0,\zeta}
         +\sum_xb_x(B_{\sigma_0,\zeta})
      \right)
      \le\ell .
   \end{aligned}
   \]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Compressed decoder-DAG rate-coverage envelope]
   \label{cor:compressed-decoder-dag-rate-coverage}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:finite-signature-rate-coverage}, suppose the
   retained reporter for each signature \(\sigma_0\) is represented by a finite
   acyclic decoder graph \(G_{\sigma_0}=(V_{\sigma_0},E_{\sigma_0})\) with
   source \(s_{\sigma_0}\) and terminal set \(T_{\sigma_0}\).  For every query
   \(q\) with \(\sigma(q)=\sigma_0\) and outcome \(\omega\), the decoder visits
   one source-to-terminal path
   \[
      \Pi(q,\omega)\subseteq V_{\sigma_0},
      \qquad
      t(q,\omega)\in T_{\sigma_0}.
   \]
   A terminal \(t\) reports the fixed label set \(B_t\subseteq\Lambda\), and a
   visited state \(v\) costs at most \(d_v\ge0\) units of reporter work.

   Assume preprocessing certifies state-reach envelopes
   \[
      U_{p,\sigma_0,v}
      \ge
      \E_\omega\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)\mathbf 1\{v\in\Pi(q,\omega)\}
      \right]
      \tag{DG1}
   \]
   for every \(q\) of signature \(\sigma_0\) with \(\|p-q\|\le r\), and
   \[
      V_{\sigma_0,v}
      \ge
      \Pr_\omega[v\in\Pi(q,\omega)]
      \tag{DG2}
   \]
   for every \(q\) of signature \(\sigma_0\).  If
   \[
      \sum_{t\in T_{\sigma_0}}
      U_{p,\sigma_0,t}\exp(-b_p(B_t))
      \le \tau
      \qquad(\sigma_0\in\Sigma,\ p\in N_{\sigma_0})
      \tag{DG3}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{v\in V_{\sigma_0}}V_{\sigma_0,v}d_v
      +
      \sum_{t\in T_{\sigma_0}}
      V_{\sigma_0,t}\sum_{x\in S}b_x(B_t)
      \le \ell
      \qquad(\sigma_0\in\Sigma),
      \tag{DG4}
   \]
   then the tilted miss bound \((FS6)\) holds and the expected
   reporter-plus-scan work is at most \(\ell\) for every query.

   Thus the proof object can be the compact state graph of a list-recovery,
   trellis, polar, or lattice-enumeration decoder.  The full transcript tree is
   only an analysis unfolding; the retained certificate stores state reach
   envelopes and terminal bucket sets.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
   Fix a promised near pair \((p,q)\), let \(\sigma_0=\sigma(q)\), and write
   \(t=t(q,\omega)\).  Since \(p\in N_{\sigma_0}\), terminal decomposition gives
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      \E_\omega\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)e^{-b_p(B_{t(q,\omega)})}
      \right]
      &=
      \sum_{t\in T_{\sigma_0}}
      e^{-b_p(B_t)}
      \E_\omega\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)\mathbf 1\{t(q,\omega)=t\}
      \right]  \\
      &\le
      \sum_{t\in T_{\sigma_0}}
      U_{p,\sigma_0,t}e^{-b_p(B_t)}
      \le\tau .
   \end{aligned}
   \]
   For the work bound, the acyclic path visits every state at most once, so
   \[
   \begin{aligned}
      &\E_\omega\!\left[
         \sum_{v\in\Pi(q,\omega)}d_v
         +\sum_xb_x(B_{t(q,\omega)})
      \right]  \\
      &\qquad=
      \sum_{v\in V_{\sigma_0}}d_v\Pr[v\in\Pi(q,\omega)]
      +
      \sum_{t\in T_{\sigma_0}}
      \Pr[t(q,\omega)=t]\sum_xb_x(B_t)  \\
      &\qquad\le
      \sum_{v\in V_{\sigma_0}}V_{\sigma_0,v}d_v
      +
      \sum_{t\in T_{\sigma_0}}
      V_{\sigma_0,t}\sum_xb_x(B_t)
      \le\ell .
   \end{aligned}
   \]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Output-sensitive decoder-DAG rate coverage]
   \label{cor:output-sensitive-decoder-dag-rate-coverage}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:finite-signature-rate-coverage}, suppose the
   retained reporter for each signature \(\sigma_0\) is represented by a finite
   acyclic decoder graph with state set \(V_{\sigma_0}\) and terminal-output
   set \(T_{\sigma_0}\).  For query \(q\) and outcome \(\omega\), the decoder
   visits a state set
   \[
      W(q,\omega)\subseteq V_{\sigma(q)}
   \]
   and reports a nonempty terminal set
   \[
      O(q,\omega)\subseteq T_{\sigma(q)} .
   \]
   A no-output outcome is represented by a dummy terminal \(t_\emptyset\) with
   \(B_{t_\emptyset}=\emptyset\).  Each terminal \(t\) has a fixed label set
   \(B_t\subseteq\Lambda\), and each visited state \(v\) costs at most
   \(d_v\ge0\).  The reported query label set is
   \[
      B(q,\omega)=\bigcup_{t\in O(q,\omega)}B_t .
      \tag{OD0}
   \]
   Finally, let \(\alpha_t(q,\omega)\in[0,1]\) be terminal responsibility
   weights supported on \(O(q,\omega)\), with
   \[
      \sum_{t\in T_{\sigma(q)}}\alpha_t(q,\omega)=1 .
      \tag{OD1}
   \]

   Assume preprocessing certifies
   \[
      U_{p,\sigma_0,t}
      \ge
      \E_\omega\!\left[
         R_{p,q}(\omega)\alpha_t(q,\omega)
      \right]
      \tag{OD2}
   \]
   for every \(q\) of signature \(\sigma_0\) with \(\|p-q\|\le r\), and
   \[
      V^{\rm state}_{\sigma_0,v}
      \ge
      \Pr_\omega[v\in W(q,\omega)],
      \qquad
      V^{\rm out}_{\sigma_0,t}
      \ge
      \Pr_\omega[t\in O(q,\omega)]
      \tag{OD3}
   \]
   for every \(q\) of signature \(\sigma_0\).  If
   \[
      \sum_{t\in T_{\sigma_0}}
      U_{p,\sigma_0,t}\exp(-b_p(B_t))
      \le\tau
      \qquad(\sigma_0\in\Sigma,\ p\in N_{\sigma_0})
      \tag{OD4}
   \]
   and
   \[
      \sum_{v\in V_{\sigma_0}}V^{\rm state}_{\sigma_0,v}d_v
      +
      \sum_{t\in T_{\sigma_0}}
      V^{\rm out}_{\sigma_0,t}\sum_{x\in S}b_x(B_t)
      \le\ell
      \qquad(\sigma_0\in\Sigma),
      \tag{OD5}
   \]
   then \((FS6)\) holds and the expected reporter-plus-scan work is at most
   \(\ell\) for every query.

   The responsibility weights are only an analysis certificate.  They let the
   miss proof charge one useful reported terminal, or a convex mixture of useful
   terminals, while the actual scan cost remains charged to every reported
   bucket.  This is the natural rate-coverage interface for output-sensitive
   list recovery, polar list decoding, and pruned lattice sphere enumeration.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
