   event, every point $\widehat x$ satisfying
   \[
      A_Y(\widehat x)\ge\max_{z\in S}A_Y(z)-\frac12\delta_c t r^2
   \]
   is a valid $cr$-neighbor of $q$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Write $G=Y-tq$. Up to constants independent of $x$,
   \[
      A_Y(x)
      =
      \ip{G}{x-q}-\frac t2\|x-q\|^2+\mathrm{const}(Y,q).
   \]
   Thus we may translate so that $q=0$ and write $Y$ for
   $G\sim N(0,tI_d)$. For an invalid point $x$, put
   \[
      \ell_x=A_Y(x)-A_Y(p).
   \]
   Since $Y\sim N(0,tI_d)$, this is a Gaussian random variable with variance
   $t\|x-p\|^2$ and mean
   \[
      -\frac t2(\|x\|^2-\|p\|^2).
   \]
   Also
   \[
      \|x\|^2-\|p\|^2\ge(c^2-1)r^2,
      \qquad
      \frac{\|x\|^2-\|p\|^2}{\|x-p\|}
      \ge
      \|x\|-\|p\|
      \ge(c-1)r .
   \]
   Taking $\delta_c=(c^2-1)/4$ and
   \[
      \delta_c t r^2=\frac14t(c^2-1)r^2
   \]
   and applying a Gaussian tail bound gives
   \[
      \Pr\!\left[\ell_x\ge-\delta_c t r^2\right]
      \le
      \exp(-\Omega_c(tr^2))
      \le
      m^{-3}
   \]
   after increasing $C_c$. A union bound over $S$ proves the simultaneous
   score gap.

   On that event, every invalid point has score at most
   $A_Y(p)-\delta_c t r^2$. Since $\max_{z\in S}A_Y(z)\ge A_Y(p)$, an additive
   $\frac12\delta_c t r^2$ maximizer cannot be invalid.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Score maximization is additive nearest neighbor]\label{lem:score-max-additive-nn}
   Fix $t>0$ and write $y=Y/t$. For every $\varepsilon>0$, a point
   $\widehat x\in S$ satisfies
   \[
      A_Y(\widehat x)
      \ge
      \max_{z\in S}A_Y(z)-\varepsilon t r^2
   \]
   if and only if
   \[
      \|\widehat x-y\|^2
      \le
      \min_{z\in S}\|z-y\|^2+2\varepsilon r^2.
   \]
   Thus \cref{asm:smoothed-score-max} is exactly a smoothed additive nearest
   neighbor primitive with additive squared-distance error $\delta_c r^2$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Since $Y=ty$,
   \[
      A_Y(x)
      =
      t\ip{y}{x}-\frac t2\|x\|^2
      =
      \frac t2\|y\|^2-\frac t2\|x-y\|^2.
   \]
   The first term is independent of $x$, so losing $\varepsilon t r^2$ in
   score is equivalent to increasing squared distance from $y$ by
   $2\varepsilon r^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Smoothed dominated kernel sampling suffices]\label{lem:smoothed-gibbs-suffices}
   In the setting of \cref{lem:smoothed-max-score-margin}, define the Gibbs law
   on the resident points by
   \[
      \mathsf G_Y(x)
      =
      \frac{\exp(A_Y(x))}
           {\sum_{z\in S}\exp(A_Y(z))}.
   \]
   Equivalently, for $y=Y/t$,
   \[
      \mathsf G_Y(x)
      =
      \frac{\exp(-t\|x-y\|^2/2)}
           {\sum_{z\in S}\exp(-t\|z-y\|^2/2)} .
   \]
   After increasing the constant $C_c$ in the lower bound on $t$, with
   probability at least $1-m^{-2}$ over $Y$ the Gibbs mass of invalid points is
   at most $m^{-3}$.

   Consequently, let $Q_Y$ be any output law satisfying the one-sided
   domination
   \[
      Q_Y(x)\le L_m\,\mathsf G_Y(x)
      \qquad\text{for all }x\in S.
   \]
   Then $Q_Y$ returns an invalid point with probability at most
   $L_m m^{-3}$ on this event. In particular $L_m=\log^{O_c(1)}m$ gives a
   valid $cr$-neighbor with probability $1-o(1)$. A sampler within total
   variation distance $\varepsilon$ from $\mathsf G_Y$ also returns a valid
   neighbor with probability at least $1-\varepsilon-m^{-3}$.

   Finally, exact Gibbs sampling is the same as Gumbel-perturbed score
   maximization: if $(\Gamma_x)_{x\in S}$ are independent standard Gumbel
   variables, then
   \[
      \operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x\in S}\{A_Y(x)+\Gamma_x\}
   \]
   has law $\mathsf G_Y$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   The kernel identity follows from
   \[
      A_Y(x)=\frac t2\|y\|^2-\frac t2\|x-y\|^2 .
   \]
   On the event of \cref{lem:smoothed-max-score-margin}, every invalid point
   $x$ satisfies
   \[
      \exp(A_Y(x))\le \exp(A_Y(p))\exp(-\delta_c t r^2).
   \]
   Hence the total invalid Gibbs weight is at most
   \[
      \frac{m\exp(A_Y(p))\exp(-\delta_c t r^2)}
           {\exp(A_Y(p))}
      =
      m\exp(-\delta_c t r^2)
      \le m^{-3}
   \]
   after increasing $C_c$ so that $\delta_c t r^2\ge4\log m$. The dominated
   and total-variation statements are immediate.

   For the Gumbel representation, let $(E_x)_{x\in S}$ be independent
   exponential random variables with rates $\exp(A_Y(x))$. The probability
   that $E_x$ is the minimum equals its rate divided by the sum of all rates,
   namely $\mathsf G_Y(x)$. Since $E_x=\exp(-A_Y(x))E'_x$ with
   $E'_x\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1)$, minimizing $E_x$ is the same as maximizing
   $A_Y(x)-\log E'_x$, and $-\log E'_x$ is standard Gumbel.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Captured Gibbs mass gives domination]\label{lem:captured-gibbs-domination}
   Fix $Y$ and a distribution $\mathsf G_Y$ on $S$. Suppose a randomized
   reporter outputs a nonempty set $L_Y\subseteq S$ and, for some
   $\theta>0$, always satisfies
   \[
      \mathsf G_Y(L_Y)\ge\theta.
   \]
   If the final output is sampled from $\mathsf G_Y$ conditioned on $L_Y$,
   then its law $Q_Y$ satisfies
   \[
      Q_Y(x)\le \theta^{-1}\mathsf G_Y(x)
      \qquad\text{for every }x\in S.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   Averaging over the reporter randomness,
   \[
      Q_Y(x)
      =
      \E\left[
         \mathbf 1_{\{x\in L_Y\}}
         \frac{\mathsf G_Y(x)}{\mathsf G_Y(L_Y)}
      \right]
      \le
      \theta^{-1}\mathsf G_Y(x).
   \]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Additive Gumbel optimization gives domination]\label{lem:gumbel-additive-domination}
   Fix real scores $(a_x)_{x\in S}$ and let $(\Gamma_x)_{x\in S}$ be
   independent standard Gumbel variables. Let
   \[
      G(x)=\frac{\exp(a_x)}{\sum_{z\in S}\exp(a_z)}.
   \]
   Suppose a certified algorithm, given the realized Gumbel marks, outputs
   $I\in S$ satisfying
   \[
      a_I+\Gamma_I\ge
      \max_{z\in S}(a_z+\Gamma_z)-\Delta
   \]
   always. Then its output law obeys the pointwise domination
   \[
      \Pr[I=x]\le e^\Delta G(x)
      \qquad\text{for every }x\in S.
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   If $I=x$, then
   \[
      a_x+\Delta+\Gamma_x
      \ge
      \max_{z\ne x}(a_z+\Gamma_z).
   \]
   The right-hand event is exactly the event that $x$ wins the Gumbel-max race
   after its log-weight is increased by $\Delta$. Hence
   \[
      \Pr[I=x]
      \le
      \frac{\exp(a_x+\Delta)}
           {\exp(a_x+\Delta)+\sum_{z\ne x}\exp(a_z)}
      \le
      e^\Delta G(x).
   \]
\end{proof}

\begin{assumption}[Certified additive Gumbel-MIPS]\label{asm:gumbel-mips}
   Fix $c>1$ and $\rho=\rho_c$. For every bounded-diameter localized node with
   $m$ resident points there is a data structure for one independent marked
   trial. During preprocessing it assigns independent standard Gumbel marks
   $(\Gamma_x)_{x\in S}$. For every query $q$, the query algorithm chooses
   \[
      C_c\log m/r^2\le t\le \log^{O_c(1)}m/r^2,
      \qquad
      Y\sim N(tq,tI_d),
   \]
   and returns a point $I\in S$ with the certified additive guarantee
   \[
      A_Y(I)+\Gamma_I
      \ge
      \max_{x\in S}\{A_Y(x)+\Gamma_x\}
      -O_c(\log\log m)
   \]
   with probability at least $\log^{-O_c(1)}m$, using query time
   \[
      d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   and space
   \[
      m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   over all localized nodes. The additive guarantee is certified or Las Vegas:
   undetected failure is not counted as success.
\end{assumption}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional additive Gumbel endpoint]\label{prop:gumbel-mips-polylog}
   Under \cref{asm:gumbel-mips}, worst-case Euclidean ANN has query time
   \[
      Q(n,d)=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n
   \]
   and space
   \[
      S(n,d)=dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Work after bounded-diameter localization and repeat the primitive a
   polylogarithmic number of times in a good localized copy containing a near
   pair. For a successful certified call, apply
   \cref{lem:gumbel-additive-domination} with $a_x=A_Y(x)$. Since
   $\Delta=O_c(\log\log m)$, the output law is pointwise dominated by
   $\log^{O_c(1)}m\,\mathsf G_Y$. By
   \cref{lem:smoothed-gibbs-suffices}, the returned point is a valid
   $cr$-neighbor with probability $1-o(1)$ over $Y$ and the Gumbels.
   Polylogarithmic repetition amplifies the inverse-polylogarithmic certified
   success probability to a constant. The query and space bounds sum over the
   localized copies exactly as in \cref{prop:smoothed-gibbs-polylog}.
\end{proof}

\begin{assumption}[Smoothed Gaussian guard-or-capture reporter]\label{asm:smoothed-gibbs}
   Fix $c>1$ and $\rho=\rho_c$. For every bounded-diameter localized node with
   $m$ resident points, and for every query $q$, there is a data structure with
   the following guarantee. Choose a parameter
   \[
      C_c\log m/r^2\le t\le \log^{O_c(1)}m/r^2,
   \]
   sample $Y\sim N(tq,tI_d)$, and either report failure or report a nonempty
   list $L_Y\subseteq S$. For every near pair $(p,q)$ in the node, at least one
   of the following alternatives holds.
   \begin{itemize}
      \item The resident guard density is inverse-polylogarithmic at the
      $m^{-\rho}$ scale:
      \[
         \frac1m|\{x\in S:\|x-q\|\le cr\}|
         \ge
         m^{-\rho}\log^{-O_c(1)}m .
      \]
      \item With probability at least $\log^{-O_c(1)}m$ over $Y$ and the
      reporter randomness, the returned list satisfies, for
   \[
      W_Y(A)=\sum_{x\in A}\exp(A_Y(x)),
   \]
      one has
   \[
      W_Y(L_Y)\ge \log^{-O_c(1)}m\,W_Y(S).
   \]
   \end{itemize}
   One trial uses query time
   \[
      d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   and space
   \[
      m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   over all localized nodes.
\end{assumption}

The reporter need not certify which alternative holds, certify the displayed
mass inequality, or estimate $W_Y(S)$. It only has to output the list when it
does not fail; the final restricted Gibbs sample uses the explicitly computable
weights on that list. The inverse-polylogarithmic success probabilities are
amplified in the next proposition.

\begin{proposition}[Conditional smoothed guard-or-capture endpoint]\label{prop:smoothed-gibbs-polylog}
   Under \cref{asm:smoothed-gibbs}, worst-case Euclidean ANN has query time
   \[
      Q(n,d)=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n
   \]
   and space
   \[
      S(n,d)=dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Apply the projected bounded-diameter localization of \cref{lem:localization}
   and work in a good localized copy containing a true near pair. First sample
   a uniform resident guard of size $m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m$ and verify every
   sampled point in the original metric. If the guard-density alternative in
   \cref{asm:smoothed-gibbs} holds, this finds a valid point with constant
   probability after increasing the logarithmic constant.

   Otherwise call the smoothed mass-capture reporter a polylogarithmic number
   of times. Whenever a trial returns a list $L_Y$, sample one point from the
   weights $\exp(A_Y(x))$ restricted to $L_Y$, and verify it in the original
   metric. On a mass-capturing trial, \cref{lem:captured-gibbs-domination}
   makes this output law pointwise dominated by a $\log^{O_c(1)}m$ multiple of
   $\mathsf G_Y$. By \cref{lem:smoothed-gibbs-suffices}, such a trial returns a
   valid point with probability $1-o(1)$. Since the trial is mass-capturing
   with inverse-polylogarithmic probability, polylogarithmic repetition gives
   constant success probability.

   The guard, reporter, and localization costs all fit
   $d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n$, and the assumed local space bounds sum over
   the polylogarithmic localization copies as before.
\end{proof}

\begin{assumption}[Smoothed additive score maximization]\label{asm:smoothed-score-max}
   Fix $c>1$ and $\rho=\rho_c$. For every bounded-diameter localized node with
   $m$ resident points, and for every query $q$, there is a data structure that
   chooses a parameter
   \[
      C_c\log m/r^2\le t\le \log^{O_c(1)}m/r^2,
   \]
   samples $Y\sim N(tq,tI_d)$, and returns a point $\widehat x\in S$ satisfying
   \[
      A_Y(\widehat x)
      \ge
      \max_{z\in S}A_Y(z)-\delta_c t r^2/2
   \]
   with probability at least $\log^{-O_c(1)}m$, using query time
   \[
      d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   and space
   \[
      m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   over all localized nodes.
\end{assumption}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional smoothed score-max endpoint]\label{prop:smoothed-score-max-polylog}
   Under \cref{asm:smoothed-score-max}, worst-case Euclidean ANN has query time
   \[
      Q(n,d)=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n
   \]
   and space
   \[
      S(n,d)=dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Apply the projected bounded-diameter localization of \cref{lem:localization}.
   In each localized node, call the smoothed score-max structure a
   polylogarithmic number of times and verify every returned point in the
   original metric. For any true near pair in a good localized copy,
   \cref{lem:smoothed-max-score-margin} says that an additive
   $\delta_c t r^2/2$ maximizer is valid with probability $1-m^{-2}$ over the
   sampled score direction. The oracle success probability is inverse
   polylogarithmic by assumption, so polylogarithmic amplification gives a
   valid answer with constant probability.

   The assumed local query and space bounds sum over the polylogarithmic
   localization copies exactly as in \cref{prop:multiscale-structured-polylog}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What this shortcut does and does not prove]\label{rem:score-max-shortcut}
   \Cref{lem:smoothed-max-score-margin} is the $\alpha=0$ endpoint of the
   likelihood-ball geometry: if one can approximately maximize the smoothed
   score $A_Y$ for a query-centered Gaussian score direction, the hierarchy,
   dyadic scales, and representative materialization are unnecessary. This is
   a sharper possible route than finite channels.

   The lemma is not itself an ANN data structure. By
   \cref{lem:score-max-additive-nn}, the open algorithmic question becomes
   whether this smoothed additive nearest-neighbor problem has an
   optimal-exponent, output-sensitive implementation. Without such an
   implementation, \cref{asm:smoothed-score-max} should be viewed as a clean
   alternative target, not as a proof of the polylogarithmic endpoint.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why ordinary ANN does not implement the score-max target]\label{rem:score-max-ordinary-ann}
   One can force the perturbed center $y=Y/t$ to be close to $q$ by taking
   $t$ large. In dimension $D$, if
   \[
      t\ge C(D+\log m)/(a^2r^2),
   \]
   then $\|y-q\|\le ar$ with probability at least $1-m^{-3}$. On that event,
   any true near point is within $(1+a)r$ of $y$, while every invalid point is
   farther than $(c-a)r$ from $y$. Thus an ordinary
   \[
      \kappa(a)=\frac{c-a}{1+a}
   \]
   approximate-nearest-neighbor oracle for query center $y$ would return a
   valid $cr$-neighbor after verification.

   This observation is only a sanity check. Since
   $\rho_{\kappa(a)}=\rho_c+O_c(a)$, keeping the exponent loss polylogarithmic
   requires $a=O_c((\log\log m)/\log m)$. The resulting subproblem is then just
   ANN at approximation $c-O_c(a)$ with essentially the same optimal exponent.
   Hence a black-box ordinary ANN call would make the score-max route circular;
   a proof of \cref{asm:smoothed-score-max} must exploit the smoothed
   maximum-score structure itself.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why multiplicative ANN does not implement Gumbel-MIPS]\label{rem:gumbel-mips-not-ann}
   The Gumbel primitive needs an additive score guarantee, not a multiplicative
   distance guarantee. If $B\ge\max_x\Gamma_x$, one may lift
   \[
      X_x=\left(x,\sqrt{2(B-\Gamma_x)/t}\right),
      \qquad
      Y'=(y,0),
   \]
   so that minimizing $\|X_x-Y'\|^2$ is equivalent to maximizing
   $A_Y(x)+\Gamma_x$. However, a $c$-ANN answer in the lifted space gives only
   \[
      \|X_I-Y'\|^2
      \le
      c^2\min_x\|X_x-Y'\|^2,
   \]
   which can translate into additive score error
   \[
      \frac t2(c^2-1)\min_x\|X_x-Y'\|^2.
   \]
   Under the smoothing distribution, this minimum can contain the common
   $\|y-q\|^2$ term, of order $d/t$, so the additive score error can be
   $\Theta(d)$ rather than $O(\log\log m)$.

   The obstruction is real even without Gumbel marks. Take $p=q=0$ and
   $x=Lr e_1$ with $L>c$. For $y=q+g$ and $g\sim N(0,I_d/t)$,
   \[
      \|x-y\|^2-\|p-y\|^2=L^2r^2-2Lrg_1.
   \]
   With high probability this is $\Omega(L^2r^2)$, so the Gaussian weight of
   $x$ is polynomially smaller than that of $p$ when $t=\Theta(\log m/r^2)$.
   In high dimension, nevertheless, the shared orthogonal term
   $\|g_\perp\|^2$ can dominate both distances, making
   $\|x-y\|/\|p-y\|$ arbitrarily close to one. A multiplicative ANN oracle may
   then return $x$ with constant probability, which would violate the
   pointwise domination required by \cref{lem:gumbel-additive-domination}.
\end{remark}

A stronger all-listing sufficient form of the same posterior target is captured
by the following assumption.  It is useful as a baseline because it allows an
implicit channel, a signature tree, a multiprobe score index, or a direct
sampled-prefix reporter, but \cref{asm:normalized-likelihood-leader-sampler}
is the lighter sampler target.

\begin{assumption}[Multiscale structured likelihood decoder]\label{asm:multiscale-structured-likelihood}
   Fix $c>1$ and $\rho=\rho_c$. For every bounded-diameter localized node with $m$ resident points there is a structured, output-sensitive posterior decoder over dyadic mass scales
   \[
      \xi\in[1/m,\eta_m],
      \qquad
      \eta_m=m^{-\rho}\log^{A_c}m,
   \]
   with the following properties.
   \begin{itemize}
      \item At scale $\xi$, children are certified subsets $C\subseteq S$ with $\xi/2\le |C|/m\le\xi$, and each stored child has a representative point.
      \item One independent group at one scale can be decoded for a query in $d\log^{O_c(1)}m$ work plus output work.
      \item For every query $q$ and every scale $\xi$, the one-group active-child and stored-child moments satisfy
      \[
         \sum_{C\text{ at scale }\xi}\lambda_q(C)\mu(C)^\rho
         \le
         \xi^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m,
         \qquad
         \sum_{C\text{ at scale }\xi}\sigma(C)\mu(C)^{1+\rho}
         \le
         \xi^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m.
      \]
      \item For every near pair $(p,q)$ in the node, either a uniform resident guard of size $m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m$ finds a valid $cr$-neighbor with constant probability, or the pure multiscale value is inverse-polylogarithmic:
      \[
         \sum_{\xi\le\eta_m}
         \sum_{\substack{C\text{ at scale }\xi\\p\in C,\ C\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)}}
         \lambda_q(C)\mu(C)^{-\rho}
         \ge
         \log^{-O_c(1)}m.
      \]
   \end{itemize}
\end{assumption}

The last bullet is deliberately a pre-amplification value condition. At a fixed
scale $\xi$, value $V_\xi$ only implies hit intensity on the order of
$\xi^\rho V_\xi$ in one independent group; the query algorithm below supplies
$\xi^{-\rho}$ groups at that scale. This is the scale-aware version of the
structured-channel condition and is the reason the assumption does not impose a
coarse lower bound such as $\xi\ge m^{-1/(2c^2)}$.

\begin{proposition}[Conditional multiscale structured endpoint]\label{prop:multiscale-structured-polylog}
   Under \cref{asm:multiscale-structured-likelihood}, worst-case Euclidean ANN has query time
   \[
      Q(n,d)=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n
   \]
   and space
   \[
      S(n,d)=dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   Work after bounded-diameter localization. At a node of size $m$, first sample the resident guard from \cref{asm:multiscale-structured-likelihood}. If it fails, apply \cref{lem:dyadic-structured-amplification} with
   \[
      h=\log^{-O_c(1)}m,
      \qquad
      \eta=\eta_m=m^{-\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m.
   \]
   The smallest dyadic scale is $1/m$, so the total local decoding and active-output work is
   \[
      d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m.
   \]
   With constant probability, the decoder activates a stored child $C$ containing the true near point and satisfying $C\subseteq\Ball(q,cr)$. Returning and verifying the stored representative of $C$ therefore succeeds. The guard case also succeeds by definition.

   The expected number of stored child descriptions over all repeated scales is
   \[
      m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   by \cref{lem:dyadic-structured-amplification}. Adding the original data and the bounded-diameter localization copies gives the displayed space bound. Standard truncation and a constant number of independent copies give capped query bounds with constant success probability.
\end{proof}

For comparison, the coarse finite-channel route is recovered by collapsing the continuum of thresholds to one explicit top-$\xi$ child per outcome. Let $\xi\in(0,1)$ and let $C_j^\xi$ be the top $\lceil \xi m\rceil$ points by posterior ratio $R_j(x)$. If $r_{j,1}\ge\cdots\ge r_{j,m}$ are these ratios, put
\[
   \Delta_j=r_{j,1}^{\beta}-r_{j,\lceil\xi m\rceil+1}^{\beta}.
\]
Store $C_j^\xi$ with Poisson mean
\[
   \Lambda_j=\xi^\rho\mu_j^\beta\Delta_j
\]
and activate each copy with probability $K_q(j)^{s_\beta}$. Then the single top-$\xi$ child dominates the restricted part of the negative moment coming from all thresholds with $\Phi_j(u)\le\xi$. Moreover $\mu_j^\beta\Delta_j\le1$, so the expected number of active top-$\xi$ children in one channel group is at most
\[
   \xi^\rho\sum_jK_q(j)^{s_\beta}
   \le
   \xi^\rho B^\beta .
\]
Thus if $B\le\xi^{-1}\log^{O(1)}m$ and $\beta=O(1/\log m)$, one group has only $O_c(\xi^\rho)$ expected active children. This is precisely where explicit enumeration imposes the coarse scale $\xi=m^{-\rho/(1+\rho)}$: evaluating all $B\simeq\xi^{-1}$ outcomes in $\xi^{-\rho}$ repetitions costs $\xi^{-(1+\rho)}$, so fitting the $m^\rho$ budget forces $\xi\ge m^{-\rho/(1+\rho)}$. This finite specialization is therefore a baseline and diagnostic, not the structured target.

\begin{assumption}[Finite restricted channel]\label{asm:finite-restricted-channel}
   Fix $c>1$ and let
   \[
      \rho=\rho_c,
      \qquad
      \vartheta=\frac{\rho}{1+\rho}=\frac1{2c^2},
      \qquad
      \xi=m^{-\vartheta},
      \qquad
      \beta=\Theta_c(1/\log m).
   \]
   There is a query-decodable finite channel with
   \[
      B\le\xi^{-1}\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   outcomes, evaluable on the node in $dB\log^{O_c(1)}m$ work, such that for every near pair $p,q$ in the node, either a uniformly sampled resident guard block of size $O(\xi^{-1}\log n)$ returns a valid $cr$-neighbor with constant probability, or the restricted top-$\xi$ posterior-superlevel negative moment for $p,q$ is at least $\log^{-O_c(1)}m$.
\end{assumption}

\Cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls} proves the geometric part of the posterior approach: likelihood level sets are Euclidean balls, and unless a resident guard succeeds there is constant negative-moment value on children that contain the near point and no invalid point. \Cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile} proves that the ideal continuous measure also has the exact dyadic query and storage moments required for $m^\rho$ amplification. What is not yet proved is the structured decoding step in \cref{asm:multiscale-structured-likelihood}: an implicit decoder must materialize stored representatives and preserve that pure value across dyadic mass scales with output-sensitive listing. The finite special case below remains useful as a coarse diagnostic; there the missing statement is balanced, query-decodable compilation with only $B\simeq\xi^{-1}$ outcomes.

The corresponding finite special-case diagnostic is to measure the left-hand side of \cref{asm:finite-restricted-channel}. For a proposed finite channel, let $\tau_j$ be the top-$\xi$ posterior threshold of $R_j(x)$ over the data, and for each planted near pair $(p,q)$ compute
\[
   \mathcal H_\xi(p,q)
   =
   \sum_j
   \mu_j^\beta K_q(j)^{1-\beta}
   \left(R_j(p)^\beta-\tau_j^\beta\right)_+ .
\]
Also compute the resident-guard density
\[
   g(q)=\frac1m|\{x\in S:\|x-q\|\le cr\}|.
\]
The conjecture predicts that the lower quantiles of $g(q)+\mathcal H_\xi(p,q)$ should be at worst inverse-polylogarithmic in $m$, and ideally bounded below by a constant.

\begin{lemma}[Posterior-rank margin certificate]\label{lem:posterior-rank-margin}
   Let
   \[
      \mathsf A_\beta(p,q)=\sum_j K_p(j)^\beta K_q(j)^{1-\beta}
   \]
   If $\mathsf A_\beta(p,q)=0$, then the right-hand side below is interpreted as $0$. Otherwise let $W_{p,q}$ be the tilted outcome law
   \[
      W_{p,q}(j)=\frac{K_p(j)^\beta K_q(j)^{1-\beta}}{\mathsf A_\beta(p,q)}.
   \]
   For $\Delta\ge0$, define
   \[
      G_\Delta(p)=\{j:R_j(p)\ge e^\Delta\tau_j\}.
   \]
   Then
   \[
      \mathcal H_\xi(p,q)
      \ge
      (1-e^{-\beta\Delta})\,
      \mathsf A_\beta(p,q)\,
      W_{p,q}(G_\Delta(p)).
   \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
   On $G_\Delta(p)$,
   \[
      R_j(p)^\beta-\tau_j^\beta
      \ge (1-e^{-\beta\Delta})R_j(p)^\beta.
   \]
   Multiplying by $\mu_j^\beta K_q(j)^{1-\beta}$ gives
   \[
      (1-e^{-\beta\Delta})K_p(j)^\beta K_q(j)^{1-\beta}.
   \]
   Summing over $G_\Delta(p)$ proves the claim.
\end{proof}

Thus the finite special case would follow from a posterior-rank margin statement: for every near pair not handled by the resident guard, a non-negligible fraction of the tilted affinity must lie on outcomes for which $p$ is inside the top-$\xi$ posterior child by enough margin. The diagnostic should therefore report not only $\mathcal H_\xi$, but also $\mathsf A_\beta(p,q)$, $W_{p,q}(G_\Delta(p))$, and the certified lower bound in \cref{lem:posterior-rank-margin}, for a margin such as $\Delta=1/\beta$. For the balanced-softmax candidate
\[
   K_x(j)=
   \frac{\exp(\lambda_{\rm sm}\ip{a_j}{x}+b_j)}
   {\sum_\ell\exp(\lambda_{\rm sm}\ip{a_\ell}{x}+b_\ell)},
\]
the offsets $b_j$ should first be balanced so that $\mu_j\approx1/B$, and the inverse temperature $\lambda_{\rm sm}$ should be swept rather than fixed by an arbitrary normalization of the panel. Writing
\[
   A(x)=\log\sum_j\exp(\lambda_{\rm sm}\ip{a_j}{x}+b_j),
\]
the unrestricted affinity has the exact form
\[
   \sum_j K_p(j)^\beta K_q(j)^{1-\beta}
   =
   \exp\!\left(A(z)-\beta A(p)-(1-\beta)A(q)\right),
   \qquad z=\beta p+(1-\beta)q.
\]
Moreover the tilted law $W_{p,q}$ is exactly $K_z$. Thus $\lambda_{\rm sm}$ controls both the local curvature $\nabla^2A(x)=\lambda_{\rm sm}^2\operatorname{Cov}_{K_x}(a_j)$ and the effective support of the certificate distribution $K_z$. The diagnostic should compare Gaussian, whitened Gaussian, cross-polytope, and learned panels; report lower quantiles rather than only averages; and track the effective support and Hessian condition number of $K_z$. These measurements distinguish the two hoped-for regimes: well-conditioned softmax curvature gives score separation, while concentrated $K_z$ should give posterior contraction by placing most tilted mass on $G_\Delta(p)$.
One should also separate the resident-guard regime from the genuine finite-channel regime. On the critical random sphere, the guard density can already be constant, so $g(q)$ masks the posterior term. A sharper stress test uses isolated planted pairs with smaller radius, where $g(q)\simeq1/m$ and the lower quantiles of $\mathcal H_\xi(p,q)$ carry the conjectural content.

\begin{proposition}[Conditional finite-channel polylog endpoint]\label{prop:finite-channel-polylog}
   Under \cref{asm:finite-restricted-channel}, the posterior-superlevel recursion gives worst-case Euclidean ANN bounds
   \[
      Q(n,d)=d\,n^{\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n,
      \qquad
      S(n,d)=dn+n^{1+\rho_c}\log^{O_c(1)}n.
   \]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
   At a node of size $m$, repeat the finite channel
   \[
      R=\xi^{-\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m
   \]
   times. By \cref{asm:finite-restricted-channel} and the top-$\xi$ collapse, a true near point enters at least one active child with inverse-polylogarithmic probability, unless the resident guard already succeeds. The local channel-evaluation work is
   \[
      dRB\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \le
      d\,\xi^{-(1+\rho)}\log^{O_c(1)}m
      =
      d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   because $\vartheta(1+\rho)=\rho$.

   The expected number of active recursive children over all repetitions is polylogarithmic, since one group contributes $O_c(\xi^\rho)$ active children. Every recursive child has size at most $\xi m=m^{1-\vartheta}$. Hence the query recurrence is
   \[
      Q(m)\le d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m+\log^{O_c(1)}m\,Q(m^{1-\vartheta}),
   \]
   which solves to $d\,m^\rho\log^{O_c(1)}m$ over $O_c(\log\log m)$ macro levels.

   For space, each stored child has size at most $\xi m$. The total stored-child $(1+\rho)$ power over all repeated channel groups is bounded by
   \[
      R\cdot B\cdot(\xi m)^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m
      \le
      m^{1+\rho}\log^{O_c(1)}m,
   \]
   again using $RB=\xi^{-(1+\rho)}\log^{O_c(1)}m$. Summing over macro levels and adding the original data storage gives the displayed space bound. Polylogarithmic amplification and the standard truncation paragraph convert the inverse-polylogarithmic local success into constant success probability.
\end{proof}

\section{Audit trail and extensions}

The main self-contained proof path is the restartable slice route of \cref{prop:slice-two-thirds}. The following notes explain which pieces are closed, which one-tree milestones are conditional, and which older formulations remain useful as comparisons or possible extensions.

\paragraph{Spherical stage.}
The continuation-mass spherical decoder is proved in \cref{lem:continuation-work}, \cref{lem:good-first-moment}, \cref{lem:tame-second-moment}, and \cref{cor:spherical-survival}. The one-sided analytical upper barrier controls prolific early prefixes in the proof, while the algorithm itself enforces only continuation-mass pruning. Independently, \cref{thm:smooth-product-terminal} gives a constant-recall terminal spherical engine by smooth product filtering. The adaptive decoder is for carrying score through localization; the smooth product filter is for the final localized spherical search. \Cref{lem:flat-shell-obstruction} explains why this terminal filter cannot simply be applied to all radial shells of the bounded-diameter instance: without recentering to the critical normalization, the exponent becomes $1/c^2$ rather than $\rho_c$.

\paragraph{Restartable \(2/3\) stage.}
\Cref{prop:slice-two-thirds} proves the restartable $2/3$ rung and is the proof of \cref{thm:main}. It reaches a subpolynomial overhead using deliberate restarts and leaf-conditioned slices.

\paragraph{Randomized \(1/3\) stage.}
\Cref{prop:one-third} records the cube-root parameter choice from the one-tree notes. The direct tube calculation is kept only as intuition; the formal proposition uses \cref{lem:adaptive-continuation} for whole-lineage score survival and assumes only the cube-root geometric frontier inequality \cref{asm:mixed-operator}. Thus the central open issue is already the work-side first-hit accounting, not near-pair score survival.

\paragraph{Randomized polylog stage.}
\Cref{lem:adaptive-continuation} replaces the tube proof in the predictable-score setting, so near-pair survival is no longer the obstacle for the randomized one-tree endpoint. \Cref{asm:mixed-continuation-operator} is the direct remaining work-side target after \cref{lem:cap-free-scan-accounting}: it must prove thinned cap-split contraction over branching cap-discovery frontiers. Conditional on that operator, \cref{prop:randomized-polylog} shows that choosing $\eta\asymp(\log\log n)/\log n$ gives polylogarithmic query and space overhead. The sharper budget-thinned variant proves the activated geometric work bound by optional stopping in \cref{lem:canonical-mixed-budget} and \cref{lem:budget-thinned-opportunities}. It now has its own conditional endpoint, \cref{prop:budget-thinned-polylog}: the only remaining assumption is aggregate fractional value delivery. For laminar tame-route families, \cref{lem:lca-value-flow} reduces the second-moment version to the two quantities $V(\varnothing)$ and $\sum_uA(u)V(u)^2$, while \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion} and \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut} give a simpler sufficient target: every route cut should have inverse-polylogarithmic prefix-success mass. The route-law cut-energy certificate \cref{lem:cut-energy-route-law} gives the most stopping-time-compatible sufficient condition for this min-cut target.

\paragraph{Certified-cover route.}
\Cref{lem:certified-cover-compiler} abstracts the empirical $k$-means
multi-assignment mechanism as an overlapping certified filter cover. This gives
a deterministic routing guarantee by triangle inequality, but a flat
inflated-ball cover can be circular unless its children are genuinely smaller
and can be useless on concentrated high-dimensional nodes where most inflated
balls are stabbed. The stronger \cref{asm:power-cover} is the additive-cover
target: construct a query-decodable cover whose inflated-ball power stabbing
contracts by $1-\Theta(\eta^2)$ and whose storage power does not grow.
Conditional on that cover theorem, \cref{prop:power-cover-polylog} gives the
desired polylogarithmic ANN bound.

The more promising practical-theory interface is the score-cover variant:
\cref{lem:cap-slack-cover}, \cref{lem:lipschitz-score-cover}, and
\cref{lem:shell-normalized-cap-cover} turn overlapping cap or Lipschitz-score
filters into deterministic certified covers with the same active-power
accounting. Thus $k$-means is best viewed as an initializer or fallback for a
recursive fixed-overlap angular score-filter tree, not as a worst-case theorem
by itself.  Since preprocessing time is deliberately not a scarce resource,
\cref{lem:finite-pool-optimizer} and
\cref{cor:offline-pareto-compression} allow the build stage to enumerate,
sample, or learn a huge proposal pool and then keep only the certified cover on
the desired query-memory Pareto frontier.  The fractional LP and
pattern-complete rounding remain useful proof devices, but their logarithmic
rounding factor is not inherent to preprocessing: an exact integral certificate
would pay no such index overhead.  With
\cref{cor:offline-center-score-saturation}, even the continuous score-filter
parameters reduce to finite seed data: a proposed center-score family is closed
under all data thresholds, data radii, and retained submemberships before
certification, so the stored cover can be much smaller than the build-time
candidate universe.  \Cref{cor:seed-saturate-certify} makes this the canonical
offline build model: $k$-means++, spherical \(D^2\), dual-weighted, learned, or
exhaustive seeds are saturated and certified first, and only the selected
retained family is charged.  The closed-form $F_4$ charge is also only a
sufficient price certificate: \cref{lem:priced-cover-compiler} and
\cref{cor:offline-recursive-pareto-pricing} allow the offline verifier to price
each candidate child by its actual certified descendant Pareto cost.  This
folds ordinary certified ball recursion, score-filter recursion, residual
scans, and terminal samplers into the same priced cover process, and can only
lower the retained query-memory frontier relative to the surrogate analysis.
The all-reporting obstruction is also separated from the recursive compiler:
\cref{lem:sampled-priced-cover} allows a retained family to be used through a
query sampler, paying expected sampled child price and requiring only
inverse-polylogarithmic route mass on children that contain the true near point.
Thus middle-density singletons and posterior prefixes should be handled as
sampling/value objects whenever listing them would violate active power.
\Cref{lem:finite-sampled-cover-dual} gives the finite obstruction to such a
sampler: a bad query carries route-demand prices on unresolved near points, and
the remaining theorem must find an active child whose priced success mass beats
its query price plus the normalization price.
\Cref{lem:existential-sampled-priced-cover} weakens the sufficient condition to
the actual ANN objective: if the unresolved near set is nonempty, the sampler
only needs inverse-polylogarithmic mass on children that contain at least one
of those near points.  \Cref{lem:finite-existential-sampled-cover-dual} gives
the corresponding one-constraint dual.
\Cref{lem:finite-sampled-route-verifier} turns this into an all-query
certificate by grouping queries according to their activation pattern and
unresolved near set; the cost of storing or implicitly decoding those signatures
is now explicit.
\Cref{lem:finite-existential-sampled-route-verifier} gives the same signature
certificate with existential near-set mass instead of per-witness mass.
\Cref{lem:two-child-existential-sampler} then collapses each exact existential
signature sampler to two useful children plus no-op mass; the \(\log |N|\)
cohort factor is needed only for the stronger per-witness materialization.
\Cref{lem:activation-sampled-route-verifier} gives the clean activation-only
variant: if the route LP is feasible for every activation pattern against the
union of all compatible near points, then the query decoder no longer needs the
near-set coordinate at all.
\Cref{lem:sampled-route-cohort} removes the next fractional artifact: once a
signature LP is feasible, a polylogarithmic cohort of child identifiers
implements its sampler with only constant-factor loss in price and route mass.
\Cref{lem:activation-cohort-sampled-compiler} packages these two reductions
into a directly stored activation-pattern sampler, with no-op records carrying
the leftover subprobability mass.
\Cref{cor:activation-cohort-resources} records the exact query-memory test:
the LP/cohort search may be arbitrarily expensive at build time, but the
retained cohort table and pattern decoder must fit the local storage budget.
\Cref{lem:prefix-list-decoder}, \cref{lem:priority-prefix-list-decoder},
\cref{lem:exponential-list-size}, and \cref{cor:prefix-list-resources} record
the deterministic list-decoding
alternative suggested by the lattice viewpoint: a stack decoder enumerates
exactly the prefixes whose certified upper score can still reach the decoding
threshold, and an exponential-moment certificate turns this into a short-list
and query-work bound.  If the score tree admits such an output-sensitive prefix
enumerator, routing reduces to a list-size theorem followed by ordinary metric
verification.
\Cref{lem:fano-biased-prefix-list} and
\cref{cor:waterfilled-fano-bias} make the sequential-decoding bias explicit:
subtracting a nonnegative branch price from the reliability score preserves
admissibility, can only shrink the exponential list-size moment, and turns an
\(O_c(\log\log n)\) water-filled allocation defect into only a polylogarithmic
threshold loss.  \Cref{cor:robust-fano-allocation-compiler} adds the
multi-witness form: one robust water-filled allocation, tested against all
finite witness mixtures, gives one Fano-biased stack decoder that lists a valid
route for every witness as soon as the raw routes have the corresponding
\(O_c(\log\log n)\) reliability margin.  Thus a code or lattice replacement
should expose Fano-biased threshold reporters, not just nearest-center
reporters.
\Cref{lem:continuation-list-certificate} and
\cref{cor:continuation-as-list-decoding} show that ordinary continuation-mass
pruning is already the \(\beta=1\) case of this certificate; the unresolved
part is to construct an equally tight admissible prefix potential after
geometric recentering and budget-thinned child generation.
\Cref{asm:flat-spherical-code-decoder} and
\cref{prop:flat-spherical-code-terminal} isolate the flatter code/lattice
alternative: replace the terminal score tree by one structured spherical-code
panel whose cap list is output-sensitive, whose point and far-pair loads match
the random Gaussian spectrum, and whose near-pair hit probability is
inverse-polylogarithmic.  This is the theorem a Construction-A lattice,
group-orbit spherical code, or concatenated soft list-recovery construction
would have to prove.  The product-code filtering construction of
Becker--Ducas--Gama--Laarhoven is the closest existing algorithmic template:
it turns an enormous implicit filter family into a decoding problem, so the
missing worst-case statement becomes a prefix-list moment bound and a near-route
hit bound for a structured product panel.  \Cref{lem:soft-list-recovery-reporter}
makes the coding-theoretic interface explicit: any certified soft list-recovery
or lattice enumeration block is enough to supply the threshold reporters used
by the product-panel decoder.  \Cref{cor:euclidean-list-decoder-reporter}
records the direct Euclidean-metric case, where a code or lattice list decoder
already reports the needed soft-score superlevel set.  For non-Euclidean
bounded scores, \cref{lem:hard-list-recovery-lift} gives a self-contained
reduction to ordinary hard list-recovery constraints by repeating each
coordinate across score levels.
\Cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep} gives the cheaper GMD special case:
one hard symbol and one confidence per coordinate produce only \(O(h)\)
erasure transcripts, and the weighted-agreement inequality \((GMD)\) is enough
to guarantee that the near route is decoded.
\Cref{lem:soft-score-gmd-margin} turns this into an ordinary soft-score
certificate: top-symbol gaps define the confidences, and any codeword above
the resulting score threshold is included by the same \(O(h)\)-call sweep.
\Cref{cor:gmd-block-certificate} packages the usable block condition for
finite-signature and decoder-DAG rate-coverage certificates.
\Cref{lem:chase-gmd-reporter} and
\cref{cor:chase-gmd-block-certificate} add the polylogarithmic ambiguity
version: branch over a few low-confidence coordinates, promote the guessed
symbols to confidence \(1\), and then use the same GMD sweep.  The branch
product is charged explicitly as decoder work.
\Cref{cor:top-list-chase-completeness} removes the need to branch over the
full alphabet: top lists are complete whenever omitted symbols have enough
score deficit to fall below the reporting threshold.
\Cref{lem:information-set-threshold-reporter} and
\cref{cor:ordered-statistics-information-set} record the complementary OSD
normal form from code and lattice decoding.  A retained systematic chart lists
only high-score information words, extends them through the code or quotient
basis, and exact-filters the resulting candidates.  This can be strictly
cheaper than Chase-GMD when a reliable information set exists, because the
online branch count is the ordered-statistics discrepancy list rather than the
full ambiguity product.
\Cref{lem:min-entropy-information-set} identifies the best chart for a linear
block: assign each coordinate its soft local entropy
\(\lambda_i=\log\sum_a\exp(\beta(\omega_i(a)-m_i))\), then choose a
minimum-weight information set.  For MDS blocks this is just the \(k\) smallest
coordinate entropies; for general linear blocks it is a greedy minimum-basis
computation.  Thus the decoder overhead target reduces to the scalar margin
\(\beta(\sum_im_i-\Theta)+\Lambda_*=O_c(\log\log m)\).
\Cref{lem:superset-product-panel-decoder} handles the fact that OSD and lattice
enumerators often produce a certified superset before exact filtering.
\Cref{cor:osd-superset-block-reporter} gives the resulting product-panel query
certificate: an OSD block contributes the ordinary prefix moment multiplied by
only \(e^{\Lambda_j(q)}\) at the block being decoded.
\Cref{cor:superset-product-panel-rate-bookkeeping} packages this as a
blockwise rate test: the OSD query factor is
\((1+A_j+e^{\Lambda_j})e^{\beta(M_j-\theta_{Q,j})}\), together with the usual
partition factor \(e^{-\beta\theta_{Q,j}}Z_j^Q\).  Thus the remaining product
panel theorem can state query overhead, storage, far load, and near hit as four
ordinary multiplicative block-rate inequalities.
The remaining work is spectrum and rate bookkeeping rather than Lloyd-style
convergence analysis.
\Cref{lem:additive-product-panel-decoder}
makes the decoder side self-contained: once block threshold reporters and
suffix upper scores are available, the live stack is controlled by the product
partition certificate \(P_\beta\), and terminal scan work is controlled by
\(T_\beta\).  The remaining geometric theorem for such panels is therefore
only the random-like spectrum.  \Cref{prop:product-panel-spectrum-compiler}
states the exact certificates: one-panel decoder work \((PP1)\), storage
moment \((PP2)\), far co-load moment \((PP3)\), and near-pair hit probability
\((PP4)\).  The two load conditions are pure exponential-moment inequalities
and factor block-by-block for product panels.
\Cref{lem:product-panel-tensorization} records that reduction explicitly:
after splitting the thresholds by block, the decoder partition, storage
partition, far co-load partition, and a coordinatewise near-hit event all
tensorize.  The remaining code/lattice theorem can therefore be attacked one
block at a time, then multiplied.
\Cref{cor:product-panel-rate-bookkeeping} is the exponent form: prove one-block
rates \(u_j,v_j,w_j,b_j\) with total query and storage rates at most \(\rho\),
total far-separation rate at least \(1-\rho\), and total near-loss
\(O_c(\log\log m)\).  This is the precise product-code analogue of the
spherical exponent calculation.
\Cref{prop:sampled-structured-panel} records the corresponding sampler
alternative.  A structured code or lattice panel need not list all active
labels if it can sample labels from a certified Gibbs or discrete-Gaussian law:
stored redundancy, far sampled co-load, and near sampled mass are enough.  This
is a cleaner theoretical version of the empirical multi-assignment lesson and
a possible way around all-reporting overhead in middle-density regimes.
\Cref{cor:gibbs-sampled-product-panel} makes the full-product case algebraic:
far sampled load is a ratio of mixed and query partition functions, while the
near condition is Gibbs mass on labels that store the near point.  These
quantities tensorize block-by-block, so the sampled lattice/code target has the
same rate-bookkeeping shape as the reporting target but no active-list term.
\Cref{cor:gibbs-sampled-rate-bookkeeping} is the exponent form: prove one-block
rates for storage, sampled far separation, and near Gibbs mass with total
storage rate at most \(\rho\), total far-separation rate at least \(1-\rho\),
and total near loss \(O_c(\log\log m)\).  No query-list rate appears.
\Cref{lem:gibbs-near-moment-lower-bound} makes the near condition itself
checkable by a positive tilted-moment margin: under the query Gibbs law, the
storage score of the near point must have a first exponential moment just above
the storage threshold and a controlled second moment.  Thus the sampled
lattice/code route is reduced to three block partition-function estimates.
\Cref{lem:critical-tilted-gaussian-sampled-spectrum} computes the ideal
continuous target at the critical Euclidean normalization: an intensity-\(m\)
Gaussian filter family with query tilt \(N(\beta q,I)\) has storage rate
\(\rho_c\), sampled far-separation rate \(1-\rho_c\), and constant near sampled
mass.  A compact code or lattice sampler would be a finite quadrature for this
calculation.
\Cref{lem:naive-gaussian-quadrature-barrier} explains why this quadrature
cannot be an iid Gaussian label pool analyzed by ordinary importance-sampling
concentration: the label entropy needed to stabilize the tilted partition
function is polynomially larger than the entropy allowed by the storage and far
load exponents.  The finite object must therefore exploit structure, not just
more random labels.
\Cref{lem:construction-a-theta-quotient} records the cleanest Construction-A
interface: the discrete-Gaussian law on the lattice quotients exactly to a soft
theta-reliability law on the residue code, and close-point threshold reporting
reduces to a certified soft list-recovery superset of residues followed by
ordinary integer-lift enumeration.  Thus a Construction-A panel should be
analyzed through residue-code list recovery and tilted partition functions, not
as a black-box nearest-lattice oracle.
\Cref{cor:query-centered-cell-reweighting} gives the posterior analogue of
this decoding normal form.  After the scale-amplified leader law has collapsed
to one max-tilted measure, choosing
\(\alpha=\Theta_c(1/H)\) with \(H\ge\max\{D,\log m\}\) rewrites that measure as
the exact nearest resident cell of a query-centered Gaussian perturbation,
size-biased by a scalar weight whose tail is killed by a polylogarithmic cap.
\Cref{cor:truncated-nearest-cell-compiler} then turns any compact structured
nearest-cell decoder into the max-tilted posterior sampler with only a
polylogarithmic rejection overhead.  \Cref{rem:nearest-cell-structured-decoding}
is the important caveat: this is not a black-box exact-NN assumption, but a
code/lattice/trellis decoder target for smoothed Voronoi or quotient cells.
\Cref{lem:smoothed-witness-list-compiler} weakens this again: the query need
not identify the exact nearest cell of the perturbation, only list the true near
witness with constant tilted mass.  \Cref{cor:facility-bucket-witness} is the
corresponding facility or \(k\)-means++-style form: retained buckets are useful
when their reported union has small scan load and high tilted witness capture.
\Cref{lem:poisson-rate-coverage} turns this into the scalar rate-coverage
certificate \((RC1)\)--\((RC3)\), and
\cref{lem:finite-signature-rate-coverage} gives a conservative all-query
verification route through finite reporter signatures and coefficient
envelopes, while \cref{cor:finite-transcript-rate-coverage} charges compact
decoder transcripts and their reporter work directly.
\Cref{cor:compressed-decoder-dag-rate-coverage} compresses those transcripts to
acyclic decoder state graphs with state-reach envelopes.
\Cref{cor:output-sensitive-decoder-dag-rate-coverage} gives the corresponding
output-sensitive list-enumerator form, with responsibility weights for miss
coverage and ordinary output probabilities for scan load.
\cref{lem:scalar-redundancy-waterfill} gives the optimal scalar redundancy
rule once a reporter and point assignment law are fixed.
\cref{lem:finite-rate-coverage-convex} gives the offline convex verifier and
dual obstruction for a proposed finite panel.  The reduced-cost,
whole-bucket-gain, and column-generation lemmas,
\cref{lem:rate-coverage-column-gain}, \cref{cor:whole-bucket-reduced-gain}, and
\cref{lem:finite-rate-coverage-column-generation}, make expensive build-time
search exact for any finite candidate label family.
\Cref{lem:reduced-gain-variational-separation} turns the nonlinear
whole-bucket objective into a signed weighted coverage problem over label
events, parameterized by candidate intensities.
\Cref{lem:layer-cake-reduced-gain-separation} then reduces positive-gain
exposure to one subset layer and, after truncation, a logarithmic dyadic search
over layer weights.  \Cref{cor:pointwise-reduced-cost-separation} identifies
the exact completeness oracle as a point-label weighted coverage score.
\Cref{cor:soft-list-decoding-reduced-cost-separator} then shows that any
coordinatewise soft list decoder for that score is already an exact implicit
separator after filtering.
\Cref{cor:threshold-event-reduced-cost} gives the exact normal form when the
reporter itself is threshold-decoded: the offline separator is signed
threshold coverage over the reporter tests, not necessarily one additive
codeword score.
\Cref{cor:positive-coverage-screen} then removes the signed part from
candidate generation: list labels with enough positive coverage and use the
negative tests only for exact reduced-cost filtering.
\Cref{cor:dyadic-positive-list-recovery} reduces the weighted positive
coverage lister to dyadic unweighted agreement/list-recovery calls plus exact
filtering.
\Cref{cor:unified-soft-decoded-rate-coverage} packages the desired retained
object: the compact query decoder handles online reporter lists, an exact
offline separator drives preprocessing, and the convex certificate charges
empty decoder output, reported incidences, and storage separately.  The
seeded search
compiler \cref{lem:seeded-reduced-gain-search} isolates the useful
code/lattice contribution: a proposal sampler or list decoder only has to
expose a positive reduced-gain label against each surviving dual obstruction;
the finite verifier then decides whether to retain it.  The implicit separator
\cref{lem:implicit-reduced-gain-separation} further shows that a compact
code/lattice quotient does not have to be enumerated in advance: no-positive-gain
separation is itself an infeasibility certificate for every finite-support
panel in that universe.
\Cref{prop:rate-coverage-polylog} records the resulting posterior endpoint.
\Cref{cor:weighted-gaussian-quadrature-panel} records the exact positive
finite certificate: a weighted retained label set only has to pass storage,
sampled far-load, and near-mass checks under the tilted query sampler.  The
weights may be chosen by arbitrary preprocessing; only retained buckets and the
query sampler are charged.
\Cref{cor:bucket-quotient-quadrature} removes a further artificial charge:
labels with the same stored point set may be quotient-merged, so storage
depends on distinct buckets while the query samples their aggregate tilted
weight.  This is the natural form for trellises, code cosets, lattice shells,
and rank-prefix reporters.
\Cref{lem:compressed-aggregate-sampler} gives the corresponding compact
decoder interface: a path-weighted DAG samples the quotient bucket law by a
backward partition-function recursion, so the charged object is graph size and
bucket spectrum rather than raw label count.
\Cref{lem:tilted-weight-linear-feasibility} shows that, for a fixed retained
pool, choosing those weights is just a linear feasibility problem after
clearing the tilted partition functions.  Failure has a concrete dual
certificate assigning prices to bad queries and near witnesses; a structured
label family must provide a nonpositive-reduced-obstruction label for every
such dual.
\Cref{lem:continuous-gaussian-finite-witness} checks the corresponding
positive fact for the unrestricted Gaussian label universe: every finite test
set has feasible tilted weights with strict slack.  Hence the missing theorem
is not weight existence in the ideal spectrum, but a finite low-storage,
query-decodable quadrature for that spectrum.
\Cref{cor:small-signature-gaussian-quadrature} adds the useful boundary case:
if the all-query constraints collapse to at most \(m^\rho\polylog m\) exact
signatures, the Caratheodory support itself is small enough to store and to
sample by a direct scan.  The remaining obstruction is therefore signature
entropy and compact decoding, not the finite weighted Gaussian calculation.
\Cref{rem:metric-net-signature-barrier} rules out the most naive source of
such signatures: a metric net fine enough to preserve the critical tilted near
mass has size \(m^{\Theta(\log\log m)}\) in logarithmic dimension.  The
signatures must therefore be reliability or decoder states, not uniform
spherical cells.
\Cref{rem:outer-list-recovery-codebook} records the stronger alternative
suggested by list-decoding codes and lattices: instead of decoding the full
Cartesian product, retain a sparse outer code or lattice coset and use a
certified decoder to enumerate high-score global codewords.  Direct
Euclidean-metric decoding avoids the repeated-coordinate lift when the score is
an embedded squared distance; otherwise the lift remains a polylogarithmic
fallback for bounded scores.  This can reduce the index description and query
enumeration while keeping the same certification interface, provided the
retained panel satisfies the random-like spectrum.
\Cref{lem:reliability-erasure-sweep} isolates the lowest-overhead version of
that idea: a GMD-style sweep gives a compact \(O(h)\)-transcript reporter when
the useful route has enough confidence-weighted hard agreement.
\Cref{lem:soft-score-gmd-margin} reduces that agreement condition to a
top-gap soft-score margin, while Chase-style low-confidence branching gives
the corresponding small-list extension.
\Cref{cor:gmd-block-certificate} is the resulting retained-block certificate:
high-margin signatures can use ordinary errors-and-erasures decoding instead
of a full soft list-recovery oracle.
\Cref{cor:chase-gmd-block-certificate} extends this to signatures with a
polylogarithmic ambiguity list: a few unreliable coordinates may be guessed
explicitly, and the remaining coordinates only need the stronger top-gap
margin outside that guessed set.
\Cref{lem:budget-stable-prefix-list} and
\cref{cor:canonical-budget-stable-decoder} take one step in that direction:
the canonical mixed Bellman budget itself is an admissible list-decoding
potential for budget-stable recentering opportunities, leaving only
budget-unstable opportunities for the sampled value-delivery layer.
\Cref{cor:canonical-budget-defect-toll} then rewrites the sampled layer's
route-law toll as the cumulative log deficit
\(\log_+(\Gamma/\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix})\) of those unstable opportunities.
\Cref{lem:pruned-budget-thinning} and
\cref{cor:pruned-budget-defect-toll} add the lattice extreme-pruning analogue:
an offline-certified multiplier profile may reallocate activation probability
toward reliable near-route prefixes, so the relevant toll becomes the pruned
defect \(\log_+(\Gamma/(r\mathcal B_q^{\rm mix}))\).
\Cref{lem:budget-allocation-thinning} and
\cref{cor:allocation-defect-toll} simplify this further: the algorithm only
needs a directly certified activation allocation \(Y\), and the toll becomes
\(\log_+(\Gamma/Y)\).  Thus the remaining profile problem can be stated as a
single decodable subprobability allocation over first opportunities, rather
than as a multiplier of the canonical budget.
\Cref{lem:waterfilled-allocation} and
\cref{cor:waterfilled-allocation-defect} make the allocation normal form
canonical for any fixed route law: allocate \(y_e=\min\{a_e,t\ell_e\}\), where
\(\ell_e\) is the route-law occupation probability and \(a_e\) is the
normalized recursive charge.  A polylogarithmic endpoint through this route now
requires only a decodable representation of that water-filled allocation and an
\(O_c(\log\log n)\) bound on its scalar defect.
\Cref{cor:transition-waterfilled-allocation} sharpens this by keeping the
route-law transition probability \(\lambda_e\) that the coarser defect bound
discarded.  The exact edge toll is controlled by
\(\log_+(\lambda_ea_e/y_e)\), so the water-filled charge should be
\(\lambda_ea_e\), not \(a_e\).  In the raw-log certificate this cancels the
child branch probability:
for \(e=u\to w\),
\[
   \ell_e=\Lambda(u)\lambda_e
   \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
   \frac{H_0\lambda_ea_e}{\ell_e}=\frac{H_0a_e}{\Lambda(u)} .
\]
The concrete target is therefore bounded prefix charge density
\(H_0a_e\le C\Lambda(u)\), rather than the stronger edge-density condition
\(H_0a_e\le C\ell_e\).  This is also the bridge to the square-energy view:
the ideal allocation \(y_e=\lambda_ea_e\) gives \(\gamma_e\ge\lambda_e\), so
the local square multiplier is at most one; water-filling is the
budget-constrained version of that square-benign profile.  The robust envelope
form \cref{cor:robust-transition-envelope-raw-log} preserves this improvement
whenever the witness route laws share, or admit a decodable upper envelope for,
their local transition probabilities.
\Cref{lem:antichain-prefix-density-flow} removes another arbitrary choice: a
route law with the required prefix densities exists exactly when the
corresponding node demands have total mass at most one on every antichain.
Applied with demand \(H_0a_*(u)/C\), where \(a_*(u)\) is the largest outgoing
normalized charge at prefix \(u\), this gives
\cref{cor:antichain-transition-waterfill}: it is enough to prove the cut-style
charge bound
\[
   \sum_{u\in\mathcal C}a_*(u)\le C/H_0
\]
for every antichain of required prefixes.  This is closer to the existing
min-cut and stopping-frontier certificates: instead of guessing a route law,
one proves that no prefix-free frontier carries too much maximum local charge,
then the law and the transition-aware water-filled allocation are forced by a
finite flow argument.
\Cref{lem:local-antichain-charge-potential} turns this global frontier
condition into a local proof obligation: find a nonnegative potential
\(\Phi\) with \(a_*(u)\le\Phi(u)\),
\(\sum_w\Phi(w)\le\Phi(u)\), and
\(\Phi(\varnothing)\le C/H_0\).  The stronger drop form
\(a_*(u)+\sum_w\Phi(w)\le\Phi(u)\) is enough and telescopes through every
stopping frontier.  \Cref{lem:superharmonic-potential-route-law} then turns
the same potential into an explicit top-down leaf law whose prefix masses
dominate \(H_0a_*(u)/C\).  Thus a decodable superharmonic potential supplies
both the route law and the transition-aware water-filled allocation; no
separate antichain-flow materialization is needed.  \Cref{cor:local-potential-antichain-fano}
packages this with the Fano decoder, so the remaining geometric theorem can be
attacked by local potential inequalities rather than by explicitly
constructing the route law.
\Cref{lem:least-superharmonic-majorant} makes the certificate canonical: the
least feasible \(\Phi^*\) is computed by a backward dynamic program and
\(\Phi^*(\varnothing)\) is exactly the maximum antichain charge.  Hence
\cref{cor:offline-superharmonic-route-verifier} gives a finite offline
acceptance test for any proposed successful-route trie.  Expensive
preprocessing may search over route tries, score panels, and saturated covers,
but the retained index only needs a decodable accepted trie, its canonical
potential, and the corresponding Fano threshold reporter.
\Cref{cor:finite-accepted-route-trie-compiler} is the resulting local
algorithmic certificate.  A trie is accepted by four finite checks: the
canonical superharmonic root bound, the raw reliability margin, the unpriced
prefix exponential moment, and the terminal-price exponential moment.  Passing
these checks with \(H_0\log C=O_c(\log\log m)\) gives the desired polylogarithmic
overhead for that local state.  Thus the remaining geometric theorem is now
not an allocation theorem or a route-law theorem; it is the construction of
accepted route tries for all unresolved local ANN states.
\Cref{cor:compressed-route-dag-verifier} is the code/lattice form of the same
certificate.  A trellis, recursive code decoder, product-panel stack decoder,
or lattice-enumeration DAG may be verified on its compact state graph as long
as a state potential satisfies the same superharmonic inequalities; the
explicit route trie is only the path unfolding used in the proof.  This is the
right way to import list-decoding ideas: not as a black-box nearest-lattice
oracle, but as a compact Fano-biased decoder whose state potential and path
moments pass the accepted-route checks.
\Cref{lem:moment-dominated-superharmonic} and
\cref{cor:prefix-moment-accepted-route-compiler} remove one remaining
duplication in this certificate.  If the maximum outgoing recursive charge at
each prefix is dominated by the normalized prefix-moment mass in its descendant
subtree, then the antichain/superharmonic root test follows from that same
moment bound.  Thus a future geometric construction does not need to prove an
independent route-law theorem: it may instead prove a suffix charge-density
inequality against the list-decoder prefix measure.  Same-prefix domination is
only a convenient stronger sufficient condition.
\Cref{lem:harmonic-resistance-normal-form} writes the optimal route law as an
ordinary series/parallel resistance calculation.  In particular,
\cref{lem:unit-success-skeleton-contraction} removes the ordinary score-only
part from the harmonic target: a deterministic unit-success region with exit
frontier \(\mathcal F\) satisfies
\[
   \frac1{W(r)}
   \le
   \frac1{\sum_{z\in\mathcal F}W(z)}
   +h_{\mathcal F}(r).
\]
\Cref{lem:ported-unit-region-contraction} gives the sharper ported form: the
first thinned or fractional-delivery exits contribute only through the single
conductance \(\sum_{e=(a,b)}\gamma_eW(b)\), plus the unit-region depth.  Thus
score branching, deterministic residual edges, and budget-stable recenterings
add only polylogarithmic harmonic resistance.  The remaining near-value theorem
can be stated on the contracted skeleton of genuinely thinned geometric or
fractional-delivery ports.  \Cref{lem:port-skeleton-saturated-comparison} and
\cref{cor:skeleton-cut-conductance} give the exact skeleton-level sufficient
condition: every cut of that port skeleton must carry inverse-polylogarithmic
activation mass, and the only additional loss is the sum of contracted
unit-region depths along a skeleton route.
\Cref{cor:existential-antichain-fano} composes this with the Fano decoder in
the ANN-correct existential form: if the union of valid terminal routes has the
same antichain charge bound and every such route has \(O_c(\log\log n)\) raw
reliability margin, then one deterministic biased stack decoder lists a valid
route with polylogarithmic overhead.  The remaining geometric theorem can
therefore be stated as an antichain charge bound plus a raw-margin/list-moment
bound for the successful-route trie, rather than as a separate allocation,
sampling, or cohort-materialization problem.
\Cref{lem:finite-convex-minimax} and
\cref{lem:robust-waterfilled-minimax} upgrade this from one route law to a
finite witness family: a single query allocation is optimal exactly when tested
against mixtures of the witness occupation profiles, so the remaining near-set
problem is a robust water-filled defect bound rather than a separate profile
for each possible near point.
\Cref{lem:waterfilled-layercake} and
\cref{cor:robust-waterfilled-tail} restate that robust defect as a tail
condition on high charge-to-occupation ratios above the water level.  The next
geometric proof target is therefore a uniform summable tail estimate for those
ratios over all finite mixtures of unresolved near-route laws.
\Cref{lem:waterfilled-moment-certificate} and
\cref{cor:robust-waterfilled-moment} give a more compact sufficient target:
it is enough to bound a small positive moment
\(\sum \ell^\alpha((a/(t_\alpha\ell^\alpha))^p-1)_+\) uniformly over
mixtures, with \(M/p=O_c(\log\log n)\).  Thus the remaining estimate can be
attacked as a low-temperature energy bound for the charge/occupation ratios,
rather than by proving every dyadic tail separately.
\Cref{lem:waterfilled-raw-moment-certificate} and
\cref{cor:robust-waterfilled-raw-moment} remove the implicit water level from
that target: if every mixture has total occupation at most \(H_0\), then
\(\tau=1/H_0\) is a valid lower bound on the water level, and it is enough to
bound the raw logarithmic defect
\[
   \sum_e\ell^\alpha_e
   \log_+\frac{H_0a_e}{\ell^\alpha_e},
\]
or more strongly the raw positive moment
\[
   \sum_e\ell^\alpha_e
   \left(
   \left(\frac{H_0a_e}{\ell^\alpha_e}\right)^p-1
   \right)_+
\]
with \(M/p=O_c(\log\log n)\).  A particularly concrete sufficient condition
is bounded charge density \(H_0a_e\le C\ell^\alpha_e\) on occupied unstable
opportunities, together with \(H_0=O_c(\log\log n)\); then the defect is at
most \(H_0\log C\).  For \(H_0=\log^{O_c(1)}n\), taking
\(p\asymp1/\log\log n\) still keeps the moment normalization \(H_0^p\)
constant, so the remaining theorem can be stated without solving for
\(t_\alpha\).
The guard refinement
\cref{lem:low-guard-shadow} further removes points whose entire near-query
neighborhood is already high-density: the finite dual now prices only the
low-guard shadow, so the remaining score-cover theorem is about sparse exposed
regions rather than dense clusters that resident sampling already solves.
\Cref{cor:shadow-finite-pool-dual} makes this formal in the LP: proposals are
intersected with the exposed shadow before their storage and active weights are
computed, and point prices vanish outside that shadow.
\Cref{lem:shadow-price-density-compiler} packages the exact remaining
score-cover theorem: every surplus shadow dual must expose a certified parent
proposal whose highest-price exposed prefix has positive reduced gain.  If this
separation holds for a finite low-guard pattern universe, column generation and
pattern-complete materialization produce the stored cover required by
\cref{asm:weak-offline-score-cover}.

\paragraph{Structured posterior route.}
\Cref{lem:posterior-superlevel-compiler} records the negative-moment identity suggested by the latest notes. \Cref{lem:explicit-enumeration-barrier} makes the coarse-child obstruction precise: the scale $\xi=m^{-1/(2c^2)}$ is the price of explicitly enumerating $B\simeq\xi^{-1}$ outcomes, not a geometric limit. \Cref{lem:oblivious-sparsification-barrier} adds the complementary warning that the moment bounds alone still permit $\Omega(\xi^{-1})$ disjoint possible witnesses, so direct random sparsification cannot replace structured decoding without an additional sensitivity theorem. \Cref{lem:scale-window-compiler} and \cref{lem:dyadic-structured-amplification} isolate the important arithmetic: if mass windows can be decoded output-sensitively, then $\xi^{-\rho}$ repetitions at each dyadic scale convert negative-moment value into constant hit probability, and summing all scales down to $1/m$ still fits the $m^\rho$ query budget. This scale-aware amplification is essential; a raw negative-moment diagnostic alone does not imply that the near point is actually listed with inverse-polylogarithmic probability.

The continuous likelihood-ball family now proves both analytic sides of the desired posterior decoder: \cref{thm:continuous-likelihood-balls} gives the guard-or-pure-child value, and \cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-scale-profile} gives the scale-normalized query and storage moments. \Cref{lem:likelihood-rank-prefix} further collapses the threshold continuum to rank gaps of a random score order. \Cref{cor:likelihood-prefix-leader} removes another artificial materialization cost: for ANN, every pure prefix may report the same score leader, so the decoder can aggregate prefix mass by leader buckets instead of storing one representative per prefix. \Cref{lem:dyadic-leader-quotient} records the full amplification in this hidden-label form and contributes no per-prefix storage term, and \cref{cor:likelihood-leader-intensity} identifies the exact quotient law as the rank-gap weighted measure over score leaders. \Cref{lem:ideal-likelihood-leader-law} further proves that this quotient law has constant total intensity per scale and constant scale-weighted valid intensity under guard failure. \Cref{lem:poissonized-normalized-leader-sampler} removes the remaining all-listing requirement from a sampled implementation: computing the total intensity and sampling from the normalized tilted law gives the exact Poisson leader process. Finally, \cref{lem:scale-amplified-alpha-leader} shows that the dyadic repetitions cancel the rank powers, collapsing the whole amplified posterior to the max-tilted leader law \((AL2)\), with constant total mass and constant valid mass for every near pair. The clean measure target is therefore \cref{asm:alpha-leader-sampler}; conditional on it, \cref{prop:alpha-leader-polylog} gives the desired polylogarithmic ANN bounds. \Cref{lem:alpha-leader-voronoi-capture} gives an equivalent geometric normal form for that same target: the max-tilted intensity of \(x\) is a Gaussian Voronoi-cell capture probability for the interpolated center \((1-\alpha)q+\alpha x\), multiplied by the explicit distance tilt \(\exp(-\alpha(1-\alpha)t\|x-q\|^2/2)\). \Cref{cor:query-centered-cell-reweighting} and \cref{cor:truncated-nearest-cell-compiler} then reduce max-tilted sampling to query-centered nearest-cell decoding plus scalar rejection. The weakest current algorithmic posterior target is \cref{asm:smoothed-witness-list}: list the true near witness with constant tilted mass and scan the verified list. Conditional on it, \cref{prop:witness-list-polylog} gives the same polylogarithmic ANN bound. The facility form \cref{cor:facility-bucket-witness} is the version most directly connected to \(k\)-means++ or other build-time bucket proposals. \Cref{lem:poisson-rate-coverage}, \cref{lem:finite-signature-rate-coverage}, \cref{cor:finite-transcript-rate-coverage}, \cref{cor:compressed-decoder-dag-rate-coverage}, \cref{cor:output-sensitive-decoder-dag-rate-coverage}, \cref{lem:scalar-redundancy-waterfill}, and \cref{asm:rate-coverage-panel} sharpen this further into the redundancy theorem object: point-label intensities, query label sets, finite coefficient envelopes, transcript-level and state-DAG decoder work, responsibility-weighted enumerator coverage, the marginal replication water level, the tilted miss bound, and the scan-load bound. \Cref{lem:finite-rate-coverage-convex} gives the finite convex feasibility test and dual obstruction for proposed panels. Conditional on that finite rate-coverage panel, \cref{prop:rate-coverage-polylog} gives the same endpoint. The scale-resolved sampler \cref{asm:ideal-likelihood-leader-law-sampler}, the hidden-label sampler \cref{asm:normalized-likelihood-leader-sampler}, the structured nearest-cell decoder \cref{asm:smoothed-nearest-cell}, and the older all-listing \cref{asm:multiscale-structured-likelihood} remain stronger sufficient implementation forms.

\Cref{lem:seeded-reduced-gain-search} is the build-time bridge from this
finite certificate to list-decoding and lattice-decoding proposals: the
proposal mechanism only has to expose a positive reduced-gain label against
the current dual obstruction, because the retained panel is accepted by the
same exact convex verifier.  \Cref{lem:implicit-reduced-gain-separation}
turns this into a true separation-oracle statement for implicit quotient
families: if a code, lattice, trellis, or facility universe cannot expose
positive gain, the dual obstruction rules out every finite-support panel from
that universe.  \Cref{lem:reduced-gain-variational-separation} gives the
search form of the oracle: after choosing tentative point intensities, the
label generator only solves a signed weighted coverage problem, and exact
verification converts any positive surplus into a valid column.
\Cref{lem:layer-cake-reduced-gain-separation} removes the remaining continuous
profile from that search, replacing it by a single point subset and scalar
layer, with a dyadic scalar grid whenever the retained intensities are capped.
\Cref{cor:pointwise-reduced-cost-separation} sharpens the final separator:
to certify completeness it is enough to find one point and one implicit label
whose residual tilted coverage exceeds its query-load and storage price.
\Cref{cor:soft-list-decoding-reduced-cost-separator} converts this into the
standard soft-decoding interface when the score is coordinatewise additive:
threshold-list codewords, lattice residues, or trellis paths by the dual-priced
reliability score, then exactly filter the returned candidates.
\Cref{cor:threshold-event-reduced-cost} records the corresponding exact target
for ordinary threshold reporters: solve a signed weighted coverage problem over
the satisfied threshold tests.
\Cref{cor:positive-coverage-screen} reduces candidate generation to the
nonnegative part of that coverage objective; signed terms are checked only
after a candidate label is returned.
\Cref{cor:dyadic-positive-list-recovery} then converts arbitrary nonnegative
dual weights into dyadic unweighted list-recovery calls, with the exact
weighted reduced cost checked only at the filter step.
\Cref{cor:unified-soft-decoded-rate-coverage} is the corresponding full panel
contract: offline separation is a build-time concern, while online decoder
output, candidate incidence load, and stored replication are charged as three
separate resources.

\Cref{lem:smoothed-max-score-margin} gives a still simpler non-channel target: a query-centered Gaussian score direction separates every invalid point from the true near point by an additive $\Theta_c(tr^2)$ score margin, with high probability. The softest resulting primitive is \cref{asm:smoothed-gibbs}: with inverse-polylogarithmic probability, report a list carrying inverse-polylogarithmic mass under the Gaussian Gibbs kernel on data points. Sampling from the kernel restricted to that list gives a pointwise dominated output law by \cref{lem:captured-gibbs-domination}, and \cref{lem:smoothed-gibbs-suffices} then returns a valid answer because invalid points have negligible Gibbs mass and cannot be oversampled too much. A more exact sampling route is certified additive Gumbel-MIPS: \cref{lem:gumbel-additive-domination} shows that an $O(\log\log m)$ additive optimizer for the Gumbel-perturbed scores gives precisely the required one-sided Gibbs domination, yielding \cref{prop:gumbel-mips-polylog}. A stronger but different implementation route is smoothed additive nearest-neighbor search for the perturbed center $Y/t$ with additive squared-distance error $\Theta_c(r^2)$, exactly equivalent to additive score maximization by \cref{lem:score-max-additive-nn}. These oracles would bypass dyadic scales and representatives entirely, as recorded in \cref{prop:smoothed-gibbs-polylog}, \cref{prop:gumbel-mips-polylog}, and \cref{prop:smoothed-score-max-polylog}. They are targets, not implemented theorems; \cref{rem:gumbel-mips-not-ann} explains why black-box multiplicative ANN does not supply the Gumbel primitive. The older finite restricted-channel compiler \cref{asm:finite-restricted-channel} and \cref{prop:finite-channel-polylog} remain as a coarse explicit-enumeration special case and as a useful diagnostic framework.

\paragraph{Cohort-or-leaf.}
\Cref{asm:cohort-or-leaf} is the original full-child route-level condition for the broader posterior-cohort endpoint, but \cref{lem:prefix-slice-accounting} shows that a prefix-conditioned formulation is cleaner. Work accounting for opened children is no longer the hard part: \cref{lem:vote-budget} pays opened recursive calls from realized detector votes. The preferred remaining target for that more general route is \cref{asm:prefix-slice-survival}: prove that one cumulative near-pair score path either reaches a cap-free residual scan or receives enough detector votes to follow prefix-conditioned certified slices to a resident scan. \Cref{lem:detector-inflation} and \cref{lem:off-spine-inflation} reduce the detector-shadowing part of this target to early activation prefixes with large enough continuation mass $H(v)$ and logarithmic spine-ratio sum $\sum H(u^+)/H(u)$; \cref{rem:linear-spine-ratio} explains why long score routes need an aggregate detector-mass argument instead. This should be tested against the hard cases: an isolated near point next to a massive far cluster, a near point on a cluster boundary, a tiny child that becomes heavy only late, and symmetric far shells. The proof must show a whole-lineage inverse-polylogarithmic event, not merely an inverse-polylogarithmic event at each level.

\section{Conclusion}

The algorithmic target is a cumulative score-and-ball tree with query time
\[
   d\,n^{1/(2c^2-1)}\log^{O_c(1)}n
\]
and space
\[
   dn+n^{1+1/(2c^2-1)}\log^{O_c(1)}n.
\]
The exact pieces now aligned for the randomized cap-thinning route are: continuation-mass pruning gives optimal expected spherical work, adaptive continuation gives whole-lineage near survival, thinned cap splitting gives radius-potential contraction, and cap-free terminal scans are paid by low-correlation collisions.
