For the randomized cap-thinning route, one remaining task is the branching first-split work estimate in \cref{asm:mixed-continuation-operator}: prove that the thinned geometric child potentials contract over random cap-discovery frontiers without replacing the branching decoder by a normalized path law. The newer budget-thinned variant proves the expected work bound from a canonical prefix supermartingale and packages the remaining near-side issue as \cref{asm:aggregate-value-delivery}: prove that the saturated value, or equivalently every route-cut prefix mass, is inverse-polylogarithmic rather than tracking a product along one distinguished path. For laminar route tries, \cref{lem:lca-value-flow} turns the second-moment version into the concrete LCA target
\[
   V(\varnothing)\ge\log^{-O_c(1)}n,
   \qquad
   \sum_uA(u)V(u)^2
   \le
   \log^{O_c(1)}n\,V(\varnothing)^2 .
\]
\Cref{lem:local-lca-energy} further reduces this to a local Bellman multiplier, and \cref{cor:square-nonexpansive-routes} shows that square-nonexpansive value branching costs only the polylogarithmic route depth. Equivalently, \cref{lem:energy-spine-toll} says that it is enough to prove only $O_c(\log\log n)$ positive square-energy toll along the energy-biased route spine. The capped-flow certificate \cref{lem:unit-capped-value-flow}, the Bellman recursion \cref{lem:saturated-value-recursion}, and the min-cut dual \cref{lem:saturated-value-mincut} give a still simpler sufficient formulation: prove that every route cut has inverse-polylogarithmic prefix-success mass. The equivalent dominated-law form in \cref{lem:dominated-route-law} says to construct a tilted law on tame routes whose prefix probabilities are dominated by activation prefix masses up to a polylogarithmic factor; by \cref{cor:typical-route-law-toll}, it is enough to prove only $O_c(\log\log n)$ expected positive domination toll under that route law and prune the bad tail. The cut-energy form \cref{lem:cut-energy-route-law} is closer to stopping-time proofs: it asks only for polylogarithmic $\sum_{u\in\mathcal C}\Lambda(u)^2/A(u)$ on each route cut $\mathcal C$. By \cref{lem:frontier-square-toll}, this is equivalent to bounding the positive square toll of the square-biased route spine up to each stopped frontier, with no all-prefix depth summation. The stronger all-prefix bound in \cref{cor:route-law-prefix-energy} remains an LCA-compatible sufficient condition. \Cref{lem:unit-success-skeleton-contraction} contracts ordinary score-only regions into additive harmonic resistance, so the remaining value theorem can be stated on the skeleton of genuinely thinned opportunities. Conditional on either aggregate value statement, \cref{prop:budget-thinned-polylog} gives the desired polylogarithmic ANN theorem. This is a fully non-channel route; the smooth product terminal filter can be used after localization and no longer contributes an inverse-polylogarithmic table-recall penalty. The structured posterior route replaces the cap-frontier obstacle by the tilted witness-list target \cref{asm:smoothed-witness-list}: prove a compact reporter whose verified candidate list contains the true near point with constant mass under the query-centered tilted Gaussian law. The rate-coverage panel \cref{asm:rate-coverage-panel} is the most concrete facility version of this target: a multi-assignment scheme is certified by the closed-form miss term \(\exp(-a_p\mu_p(B))\), the reported-load bound, and the total replication budget. The stronger max-tilted sampler \cref{asm:alpha-leader-sampler} and nearest-cell decoder \cref{asm:smoothed-nearest-cell} remain clean implementation normal forms, but k-means++-style or lattice/code buckets only need the rate-coverage certificates. The smoothed sampling route replaces both by \cref{asm:smoothed-gibbs} or \cref{asm:gumbel-mips}: prove either an optimal-exponent reporter that captures inverse-polylogarithmic Gaussian Gibbs mass, or a certified $O(\log\log n)$-additive Gumbel-MIPS primitive. Smoothed additive nearest neighbor is another stronger sufficient primitive. The detector-vote cohort-or-leaf route is a separate possible generalization to arbitrary certified child hierarchies, where the remaining issue is near-lineage coupling between ordinary continuation paths and detector activations.
\Cref{cor:affinity-proportional-route-law} gives the cleanest current
list-decoding interpretation of the posterior handoff: once the reporter
decodes an outgoing port list, branch by local affinity and pay only the
cumulative deficit in the list's total affinity.  Thus the score-tree/lattice
analogy has become a concrete theorem target: prove that the decoded port
lists have inverse-polylogarithmic affinity across every skeleton cut.

\appendix

\section{More detail on the spherical exponent calculation}

For completeness, we verify the algebra leading to $\rho(a,b)$. Let $B$ be the branching factor and $\ell_B=\log B$. With
\[
   t^2=(1+a)\ell_B
\]
and
\[
   k=
   \frac{2(1+b)\log m}
   {(1+a)(1-b)\ell_B}
   +O(1),
\]
the expected number of accepting leaves for one point has logarithm
\begin{align*}
   k\ell_B-\frac{kt^2}{2}+O(\log k)
                                                                                                                  &=
                                                                                                                  \frac{k\ell_B(1-a)}{2}+O(\log k)\\
                                                                                                                                                                                                 &=
                                                                                                                                                                                                 \frac{(1-a)(1+b)}{(1+a)(1-b)}\log m+O(\log\log m).
\end{align*}
Thus it equals
\[
   m^{\rho(a,b)}\log^{O(1)}m.
\]

For a pair of correlation at most $b$, the expected number of common accepting leaves has logarithm at most
\begin{align*}
   k\ell_B-\frac{kt^2}{1+b}
                                                                                                                  &=
                                                                                                                  k\ell_B\left(1-\frac{1+a}{1+b}\right)\\
                                                                                                                                                                                                 &=-\frac{k\ell_B(a-b)}{1+b}\\
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           &=-\frac{2(a-b)}{(1+a)(1-b)}\log m+O(1).
\end{align*}
A direct calculation shows
\[
   1-\rho(a,b)
   =
   \frac{2(a-b)}{(1+a)(1-b)}.
\]
Hence one ordinary far point contributes at most
\[
   m^{-(1-\rho(a,b))}\log^{O(1)}m
\]
common leaves, and all $m$ ordinary far points contribute at most
\[
   m^{\rho(a,b)}\log^{O(1)}m.
\]

\section{Bellman cancellation algebra}

We record the cancellation with no surrounding notation. Let $s=1-\rho$, let a parent contain $m$ points in radius $R$, and let a child have relative size $\alpha$ and relative radius $\lambda$. With
\[
   F(m,R)=m^\rho\left(\frac Rr\right)^{2s}
\]
and activation threshold $\tau=\lambda^2$, the first-hit bound charges at most $(\alpha/\lambda^2)^s$ query mass to the child. The recursive charge is therefore
\begin{align*}
   \left(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^2}\right)^s
   F(\alpha m,\lambda R)
   &=
   \alpha^s\lambda^{-2s}
   \alpha^\rho m^\rho
   \lambda^{2s}\left(\frac Rr\right)^{2s}\\
   &=
   \alpha F(m,R).
\end{align*}
Thus the radius improvement pays exactly for the posterior threshold, and disjoint children sum to at most the parent potential.

\section{Bibliographic note}

The one-sided spherical proof is motivated by the Ahle--Knudsen manuscript supplied with this project, but it uses continuation mass and an analytical upper barrier instead of the two-sided tube proof there. The score-and-ball tree follows the geometric ideas of Andoni and Razenshteyn while replacing dense-cap restart accounting by randomized cap thinning inside one cumulative score process. The local Bellman-game viewpoint is inspired by the robust learned hashing framework of Andoni and Beaglehole. The posterior-cohort route is retained as a possible extension to more general certified child hierarchies.
